# Dependent prepositions: verb patterns

> The prepositions you do not choose — the ones a particular verb simply requires, so that *depend* takes *on*, *rely* takes *on*, and *discuss* takes nothing at all.

[← The grammar of prepositions](../README.md) &middot; [All categories](README.md)

Every other file in this dictionary treats prepositions as words with meanings of their own — *in* is a container, *under* is a position, *since* is a starting point. This file treats them as **grammar fixed by the word in front of them**. Nothing about the meaning of *on* explains why English says *depend on* and not ✗ *depend of*, and nothing about the meaning of *of* explains why *accuse* takes it while *charge* takes *with*; these pairings are arbitrary, they differ from language to language, and they have to be learned one at a time the way irregular verbs are. What follows is the American English inventory of verb patterns, with the complement each one allows, the frames it appears in, and — the field to read first — what changes when you swap the preposition for another one.

**One rule governs half the errors in this file.** A preposition is followed by a noun, and when you want a verb in that slot it must become a **gerund**, the *-ing* form. That is why *look forward to* takes *hearing* and never ✗ *hear*, why *object to* takes *being questioned*, why *resort to* takes *lying*, and why *succeed in* takes *raising* rather than ✗ *to raise*. The test that settles every case: put a plain noun in the slot. If *look forward to the meeting* works, the *to* is a preposition, and any verb after it must end in *-ing*. Where a to-infinitive really is correct after one of these patterns — *depend on him **to** be late*, *wait for her **to** finish*, *count on them **to** show up* — it is licensed by the **person** sitting between the preposition and the verb, never by the preposition itself.

### The gerund family, in full

Any preposition in this file can be followed by a gerund. But the trap only bites where the preposition **looks like the infinitive marker *to***, and those entries are worth learning as one block: [look forward to](#look-forward-to), [object to](#object-to), [resort to](#resort-to), [confess to](#confess-to), [admit to](#admit-to), [commit to](#commit-to), [dedicate to / devote to](#dedicate-to-devote-to), [get used to / get accustomed to](#get-used-to-get-accustomed-to), [get around to](17-phrasal-g.md#get-around-to), [adapt to](#adapt-to), [react to](#react-to), [contribute to](#contribute-to), [lead to](18-phrasal-h-l.md#lead-to). Every one of them takes *-ing* after the *to* and never an infinitive: ✗ *look forward to hear from you*, ✗ *object to be questioned*, ✗ *get used to work nights*, ✗ *resort to lie*, ✗ *committed to lower emissions*.

Four more prepositions run the same trap without the disguise. *In*: [succeed in](#succeed-in) takes *raising*, never ✗ *to raise*. *Of*: [take care of](#take-care-of) takes *getting it notarized*, never ✗ *to get it notarized*. *On*: [insist on](#insist-on) and [plan on](#plan-on) take *paying* and *staying*, never ✗ *to pay*, ✗ *to stay*. *From*: [prevent from](#prevent-from), [discourage from](#discourage-from), and their whole family take *-ing* where their positive counterparts take an infinitive — *encourage her **to** apply* but *discourage her **from** applying*.

Two entries look like members and are not. [Prefer to](#prefer-to) uses *to* for a comparison between two like things (*prefer walking to driving*) and separately takes a plain infinitive with no comparison at all (*I'd prefer to walk*). [Subscribe to](#subscribe-to) takes a noun and nothing else — ✗ *subscribe to getting the paper*. And [hope for](#hope-for) is the exact inverse of the whole family: it refuses the gerund, so you must switch frames and say *hope to win*.

The *-ed* words built on the same frame — *accustomed to*, *used to*, *opposed to*, *committed to*, *dedicated to*, *devoted to* — behave identically (*accustomed to working nights*, *opposed to raising the fee*) but are adjectives rather than verbs, so they are catalogued next door in **[dependent prepositions: adjective patterns](../../06-prepositions/catalog/15-dependent-adjectives.md)** rather than repeated here. The verbs that produce them are the entries above.

### The sets worth learning first

Nine of these entries are not single patterns but families, where changing the preposition changes what the sentence claims. Each carries a full contrast table.

[hear about / from / of](#hear-about-hear-from-hear-of) &middot; [agree with / to / on](#agree-with-agree-to-agree-on) &middot; [look at / for / after / into](#look-at-look-for-look-after-look-into) &middot; [think about / of](#think-about-think-of) &middot; [talk to / with / about](#talk-to-talk-with-talk-about) &middot; [wait for / on](#wait-for-wait-on) &middot; [care about / for](#care-about-care-for) &middot; [worry about / by](#worry-about) &middot; [ask for / about](#ask-for-ask-about)

Three more meaning-changing sets belong to adjectives and live in the companion file: [good at / for / with](../../06-prepositions/catalog/15-dependent-adjectives.md#good-at), [sorry for / about](../../06-prepositions/catalog/15-dependent-adjectives.md#sorry-for), and [bored with / of](../../06-prepositions/catalog/15-dependent-adjectives.md#bored-with). The adjective *different from / than / to* is treated here, under [differ from](#differ-from), because the verb and the adjective are constantly confused with each other.

And the last section of the file — [verbs that take no preposition at all](#verbs-that-take-no-preposition-at-all) — is where the errors are most stubborn, because the noun almost always does take one: *a discussion **about** it* but ✗ *discuss about it*, *an approach **to** it* but ✗ *approach to it*, *married **to** her* but ✗ *marry with her*.

## A

### abide by

**Pronunciation:** /əˈbaɪd baɪ/ &middot; *uh-BYDE by* &middot; weak /əˈbaɪd bi/
**Русский:** соблюдать, подчиняться — принять правило или решение и следовать ему
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral with a legal tilt; contracts, court orders, club rules, and any promise to accept a decision
**Complement:** noun phrase naming a **rule, decision, agreement, or outcome** — *the rules*, *the terms*, *the ruling*, *the results*, *our agreement*. **Never a person** (✗ *abide by the referee*; you abide by his *call*), **never a gerund** (✗ *abide by paying on time* — say *abide by the payment schedule*), **never a to-infinitive**, and **never a that-clause** (✗ *abide by that the vote was fair*). The past tense is regular: *abided by*, never ✗ *abode by*.
**Pattern:** *abide by* [a rule / a decision / an agreement] &middot; *agree to abide by* [the terms] &middot; *be required to abide by* [something] &middot; *abide by* [the results of a vote]
**Contrast:** ***Abide by* is not the same verb as bare *abide*.** With no preposition, *abide* means tolerate and lives almost entirely in the negative: *I can't abide him* — and there ✗ *can't abide by him* would be wrong. Against its neighbors, *abide by* accepts a decision or a promise that already exists, often with a note of good sportsmanship; *comply with* meets a requirement someone imposes; *adhere to* sticks to a standard or a plan over time; *conform to* matches a specification. *Abide in*, meaning dwell, is biblical and archaic.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic, naming conduct that follows a decision over time; the progressive is fine (*they are abiding by the terms for now*). Not the same verb as bare stative *abide* meaning tolerate (*I can't abide him*).
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** with an obligatory prepositional object — *abide by the rules*, and never a bare direct object: ✗ *abide the ruling*, ✗ *abide by the referee* (a person is not a rule).
**Passive:** **restricted** — the prepositional passive works in legal and contractual prose (*the arbitrator's decision was abided by without complaint*), but it is stiff, and Americans normally keep the active.

Accept a rule or a decision and act accordingly, even if you did not want it.

1. Every player signs a form promising to *abide by* the league's drug policy.
2. Both parties agreed in advance to *abide by* the arbitrator's decision.
3. If you rent here, you *abide by* the quiet hours like everyone else.
4. The state said it would *abide by* the court's ruling while it prepared an appeal.
5. She lost the primary and *abided by* the result without a word of complaint.
6. Contractors must *abide by* the safety standards written into the bid.

**See also:** [comply with](#comply-with) &middot; [adhere to](#adhere-to) &middot; [conform to, conform with](#conform-to-conform-with) &middot; [agree to](#agree-with-agree-to-agree-on) &middot; [by](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#by)

### absolve of

**Pronunciation:** /əbˈzɑlv əv/ &middot; *ub-ZOLV uv* &middot; weak /əbˈzɑlv ə/
**Русский:** освобождать от (вины, обязанности) — снимать обвинение; юр./книжн.
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal; law, official reports, and religion — an American meets it in a court opinion, in a newspaper sentence like *the report absolved the mayor of any wrongdoing*, or in a church, where a priest absolves a penitent
**Complement:** noun phrase naming **blame, guilt, responsibility, liability, or a charge** — *of all blame*, *of responsibility for the collapse*. A gerund is grammatical but rare (*absolved of having signed it*); **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *absolved him of to pay*) and **never a that-clause** (✗ *absolved him that he was innocent*). The passive carries most of the traffic: *was absolved of*. In the religious sense the preposition can drop entirely: *the priest absolved her*.
**Pattern:** *absolve* [someone] *of* [blame / responsibility] &middot; *be absolved of* [wrongdoing] &middot; *absolve* [someone] *from* [an obligation] &middot; *absolve* [someone] — religious sense, no preposition
**Variants:** *absolve from* — the older form, still standard; American writing prefers *of* for blame and guilt (*absolved of any wrongdoing*) and keeps *from* for duties and obligations you are released from (*absolved from further payments*, *absolved from her promise*). Where the object is guilt, the two are interchangeable and *of* is the safer default.
**Contrast:** **Four verbs clear a person, and they are not interchangeable.** *Acquit of* is what a jury does when a formal charge fails; *absolve of* is broader and often moral, and needs no trial; *clear of* is the neutral journalistic word; *exonerate* stresses that the evidence proved innocence. And do not slide *absolve of* into *relieve of*: you are absolved *of* blame but relieved *of* a duty, a burden, or a command.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic; a declaration made at a moment (*the review absolved the pilot*), religious or legal.
**Transitivity:** **transitive** — the person cleared is an obligatory direct object, with the blame in *of*: *absolve the gym of liability*. ✗ *absolves of any wrongdoing* with nobody absolved is incomplete; only the religious sense drops the preposition (*the priest absolved him*).
**Passive:** **yes**, and the passive carries most of the traffic — *the mayor was absolved of any wrongdoing*, *she was absolved from the remaining debt*.

Declare someone free of blame, guilt, or an obligation.

1. The internal review *absolved* the pilot *of* any responsibility for the landing.
2. Signing the waiver does not *absolve* the gym *of* liability for broken equipment.
3. Nothing in the settlement *absolves* the company *of* the duty to notify customers.
4. He seemed to think an apology *absolved* him *of* everything.
5. The bankruptcy court *absolved* her *from* the remaining medical debt.
6. In the confessional the priest *absolved* him and sent him home.

**See also:** [acquit of](#acquit-of) &middot; [convict of](#convict-of) &middot; [accuse of](#accuse-of) &middot; [charge with](#charge-with-charge-for) &middot; [of](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#of)

### abstain from

**Pronunciation:** /əbˈsteɪn frʌm/ &middot; *ub-STAYN frum* &middot; weak /əbˈsteɪn frəm/
**Русский:** воздерживаться от — намеренно не делать чего-то или не участвовать
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal; parliamentary and corporate voting, medical instructions (*abstain from solid food after midnight*), and moral or religious discourse
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*abstain from commenting*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *abstain from to comment*) and **never a that-clause**. The verb also stands alone with no complement at all in the voting sense — *three directors abstained* — and there it is not a dependent-preposition pattern but a plain intransitive.
**Pattern:** *abstain from* [noun phrase] &middot; *abstain from* [verb-ing] &middot; *abstain* — no complement, in a vote &middot; *abstain from voting on* [a motion]
**Contrast:** ***Abstain from* and *refrain from* split by duration.** *Refrain from* is a single act of holding back, usually on request and usually right now: *please refrain from flash photography*. *Abstain from* is a policy — a vote you sit out, a substance you give up for good: *abstain from alcohol*, and ✗ *please abstain from flash photography* sounds absurdly grand. Further along the same line, *desist from* is what a cease-and-desist letter demands, and *forbear from* is archaic outside legal prose. Note also that *abstain* never takes a direct object: ✗ *abstain the vote*.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic, but a deliberate non-doing: a policy kept up over time (*he has abstained from alcohol since the accident*).
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** — never a direct object (✗ *abstain the vote*), and the *from* phrase is optional: *three directors abstained* is complete.
**Passive:** **no** — there is no object to promote: ✗ *caffeine is abstained from before the test*, ✗ *the vote was abstained from by two members*. Recast in the active or use *avoid*, which does passivize.

Deliberately not do something, or not take part.

1. Two board members *abstained from* the vote on the merger.
2. Patients are asked to *abstain from* caffeine for twelve hours before the test.
3. He has *abstained from* alcohol since the accident.
4. She *abstained from* commenting on her opponent's finances.
5. Observers at the talks *abstained from* taking sides in public.
6. The union urged members to *abstain from* overtime until the contract was signed.

**See also:** [prevent from](#prevent-from) &middot; [discourage from](#discourage-from) &middot; [retire from](#retire-from) &middot; [from](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#from)

### accede to

**Pronunciation:** /əkˈsid tu/ &middot; *uk-SEED too* &middot; weak /əkˈsid tə/
**Русский:** уступать, соглашаться на; присоединяться к (договору); всходить на (престол); книжн.
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal; diplomacy and law above all — a country *accedes to* a treaty, a party *accedes to* a demand, a monarch *accedes to* the throne; an American meets the word in news coverage of negotiations, in contracts, and in history books, essentially never in conversation
**Complement:** noun phrase naming **a request, a demand, a treaty, or an office**. A **gerund** is grammatical and occasionally used (*acceded to releasing the files*), but **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *acceded to release the files*) and **never a that-clause**. There is no direct-object frame: ✗ *acceded the request*.
**Pattern:** *accede to* [a demand / a request] &middot; *accede to* [a treaty / a convention] &middot; *accede to* [the throne / the presidency] &middot; *accede to* [verb-ing]
**Contrast:** **The yielding verbs, from weakest resistance to strongest.** *Agree to* is neutral and everyday; *consent to* grants permission, and is the word medicine and law use; *assent to* formally expresses approval; *accede to* gives in to pressure after resisting; *acquiesce in* accepts without approving and without objecting out loud. Two spelling traps: *accede* is not *exceed* (go beyond) and not *concede* (admit defeat, which takes a direct object or a that-clause, not *to*).
**Verb type:** action — dynamic in all three senses: yielding to pressure, joining a treaty, taking an office.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** with an obligatory *to* phrase — ✗ *acceded the request*. Do not confuse it with *concede*, which does take a direct object or a that-clause.
**Passive:** **restricted** — a prepositional passive turns up in legal and diplomatic prose (*the request was acceded to over the state's objection*), but it is rare in American writing and reads as translationese elsewhere.

Give in to a demand or a request, or formally join a treaty or an office.

1. After three weeks of picketing, management *acceded to* the union's demand for arbitration.
2. Turkey *acceded to* the convention in 2004.
3. The court declined to *accede to* the request for a sealed record.
4. He *acceded to* the chairmanship when his predecessor resigned mid-term.
5. She *acceded to* their wishes and let the story run without her name on it.
6. Congress has never *acceded to* a proposal that broad.

**See also:** [assent to](#assent-to) &middot; [consent to](#consent-to) &middot; [acquiesce in](#acquiesce-in) &middot; [agree to](#agree-with-agree-to-agree-on) &middot; [to](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#to)

### account for

**Pronunciation:** /əˈkaʊnt fɔr/ &middot; *uh-KOWNT for* &middot; weak /əˈkaʊnt fər/
**Русский:** объяснять; быть причиной; составлять (долю от целого)
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral; heavily used in business, science, and reporting
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*how do you account for losing three days?*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *account for to lose three days*) and **never a that-clause** (✗ *account for that sales fell*; say *account for the drop in sales*). A percentage or share is the commonest complement of all: *accounts for 40% of revenue*.
**Pattern:** *account for* [a fact / a discrepancy] &middot; [something] *account for* [a share or percentage] &middot; *be accounted for* — be located and checked off &middot; *account for* [verb-ing] &middot; *that accounts for* [it]
**Contrast:** ***Account for* and *account to* are different sentences.** *For* takes the thing you explain or the share you make up; *to* takes the person or body you owe an explanation to: *the treasurer accounts to the board for every dollar*. Against *explain*, *account for* leans toward being the cause (*the rain accounts for the low turnout*) where *explain* leans toward telling (*explain the rules to me*), and *explain* takes its object bare: ✗ *explain about the delay* in careful writing. And *allow for* is a third thing again — it makes room in advance rather than explaining afterward.
**Verb type:** **both** — action in the explaining sense (*how do you account for the missing hour?*), **stative** in the share sense, where nothing happens and the progressive is odd: *overtime accounts for a fifth of the budget*, ✗ *is accounting for a fifth*.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** with an obligatory *for* phrase — there is no bare object (✗ *account the discrepancy*), and *account for* must not be crossed with *account to*, which takes the person you owe the explanation to.
**Passive:** **yes** — the prepositional passive is idiomatic and in one sense obligatory: *all twelve hikers were accounted for by Sunday night*, *most of the variance is accounted for by age*.

Explain something, be the cause of it, or make up a given share of it.

1. Overtime *accounts for* nearly a fifth of the department's budget.
2. How do you *account for* the missing hour on the timesheet?
3. Two states *account for* most of the country's almond crop.
4. All twelve hikers were *accounted for* by Sunday night.
5. A slow spring *accounted for* the weak first quarter.
6. She could not *account for* having the key in her coat pocket.

**See also:** [account to](#account-to) &middot; [allow for](#allow-for) &middot; [explain to](#explain-to) &middot; [answer for](#answer-for) &middot; [for](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#for)

### account to

**Pronunciation:** /əˈkaʊnt tu/ &middot; *uh-KOWNT too* &middot; weak /əˈkaʊnt tə/
**Русский:** быть подотчётным кому-то — обязан отчитываться о своих действиях; юр./офиц.
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal; corporate governance, fiduciary law, and organizational-chart talk — an American meets it in a partnership agreement, a trust document, or a court opinion about a trustee's duties
**Complement:** noun phrase naming a **person or body you are answerable to** — *to the board*, *to shareholders*, *to Congress*. **Never a gerund**, **never a to-infinitive**, **never a that-clause**. The thing being explained is added with a second preposition, *for*: *account to the partners for every disbursement*.
**Pattern:** *account to* [a person / a body] &middot; *account to* [someone] *for* [something] &middot; *be required to account to* [someone] &middot; *be accountable to* — the adjective frame, far commoner in speech
**Contrast:** ***Account to* takes the audience; [account for](#account-for) takes the explanation.*** *She accounts to the CFO* names her supervisor; *she accounts for the variance* names what she explains — and you can put both in one sentence, in that fixed order, *account to X for Y*. Never ✗ *account for the CFO* when you mean report to him. In ordinary speech Americans say *answer to* (*he answers to the regional manager*) or *report to*; *account to* survives mainly where a legal duty is at stake.
**Verb type:** **stative** — it names a standing obligation, not an act: *the treasurer accounts to the board*, and ✗ *is accounting to the board* says something else (bookkeeping).
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** — the person or body takes *to*, and the matter explained needs a second preposition: *account to the partners for every disbursement*. No bare object: ✗ *account the board*.
**Passive:** **no** — stative and objectless: ✗ *the board is accounted to by the treasurer*. American writing uses the adjective instead: *the treasurer is accountable to the board*.

Be answerable to someone, and be obliged to explain your conduct to them.

1. The trustee must *account to* the beneficiaries once a year.
2. Under the partnership agreement, each partner *accounts to* the others for outside income.
3. Agency heads *account to* Congress for how the appropriation was spent.
4. He liked the job because he *accounted to* nobody but the owner.
5. A general contractor *accounts to* the client, not to the subs.
6. Directors who ignore a conflict of interest may have to *account to* the corporation for their profits.

**See also:** [account for](#account-for) &middot; [answer for](#answer-for) &middot; [explain to](#explain-to) &middot; [belong to](#belong-to) &middot; [to](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#to)

### accrue to

**Pronunciation:** /əˈkru tu/ &middot; *uh-KROO too* &middot; weak /əˈkru tə/
**Русский:** накапливаться в пользу кого-то — доставаться (о выгоде, процентах); фин./юр.
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal; finance, economics, and law — an American meets it in a prospectus, an economics paper, or a contract clause about who gets the upside
**Complement:** noun phrase naming the **party who receives** the benefit — *to shareholders*, *to the estate*, *to whoever holds the patent*. **Never a gerund**, **never a to-infinitive**, **never a clause**. The subject is the benefit itself, never the recipient: *the savings accrue to the buyer*, ✗ *the buyer accrues to the savings*.
**Pattern:** [a benefit] *accrue to* [a party] &middot; [interest / vacation days] *accrue* — intransitive, no preposition &middot; [a gain] *accrue from* [a source] &middot; *accrue* [expenses] — transitive accounting sense, no preposition
**Contrast:** **Three prepositions, three roles.** *Accrue to* names who gains; *accrue from* names where the gain comes from (*the efficiencies accruing from the merger*); and bare *accrue* just means pile up over time (*interest accrues daily*). In legal drafting the near-synonym is *inure to*, as in *this warranty inures to the benefit of the buyer*, which is older and stiffer but means the same thing. Do not confuse *accrue* with *accrete* (grow by addition of matter) or with *ascribe*.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic, a slow process rather than an event, and it takes the progressive freely: *interest is accruing daily*.
**Transitivity:** **both** — **intransitive** in this pattern, where the benefit is the subject and the recipient takes *to* (*the savings accrue to the buyer*, ✗ *the buyer accrues to the savings*); **transitive** only in the accounting sense, *accrue expenses*.
**Passive:** **no** in the *accrue to* pattern — it has no object: ✗ *shareholders are accrued to*. The accounting transitive does passivize: *the expenses were accrued in the fourth quarter*.

Build up over time so that the benefit goes to a particular party.

1. Most of the tax savings *accrue to* households in the top bracket.
2. Any royalties *accrue to* the author's estate for seventy years.
3. The benefits of the new interchange will *accrue to* the warehouses first.
4. Interest *accrues to* the account holder from the date of deposit.
5. Whatever prestige came out of the study *accrued to* the lab director.
6. Rights that *accrue to* a tenant under this section cannot be waived by lease.

**See also:** [amount to](#amount-to) &middot; [account for](#account-for) &middot; [belong to](#belong-to) &middot; [result in, result from](#result-in-result-from) &middot; [to](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#to)

### accuse of

**Pronunciation:** /əˈkjuz əv/ &middot; *uh-KYOOZ uv* &middot; weak /əˈkjuz ə/
**Русский:** обвинять в — открыто заявлять, что кто-то поступил дурно
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*accused him of lying*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *accused him of to lie*) and **never a that-clause** (✗ *accused him that he lied*; for a clause you need a different verb, *alleged that* or *claimed that*). The person accused sits between the verb and *of* and can never be left out: ✗ *accused of theft the manager*.
**Pattern:** *accuse* [someone] *of* [noun phrase] &middot; *accuse* [someone] *of* [verb-ing] &middot; [someone] *be accused of* [noun phrase / verb-ing]
**Contrast:** ***accuse for* and *accuse about* are errors** — the preposition is fixed at *of*. Three verbs share this ground and none of them share a preposition: *accuse someone **of** something* (anyone can do it, publicly), *charge someone **with** something* (a prosecutor does it, formally), *blame someone **for** something* (assign fault, with no wrongdoing implied — you can blame the weather). *Suspect someone of* is the private, unspoken version of *accuse*.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic; a speech act performed at a moment.
**Transitivity:** **transitive** — the person accused is an obligatory direct object sitting between the verb and *of*: *accuse the contractor of padding the invoices*, and never ✗ *accused of theft the manager*.
**Passive:** **yes**, and it is extremely common — *she was accused of plagiarism by two separate reviewers*, *he was accused of leaking the memo*.

Say openly that someone has done something wrong.

1. They *accused* the contractor *of* padding the invoices.
2. She was *accused of* plagiarism by two separate reviewers.
3. Don't *accuse* me *of* something I didn't do.
4. The senator *accused* the agency *of* stonewalling the committee.
5. He *was accused of* leaking the memo, but nobody could prove it.
6. Both sides *accused* the other *of* negotiating in bad faith.

**See also:** [charge with](#charge-with-charge-for) &middot; [blame for](#blame-for-blame-on) &middot; [suspect of](#suspect-of) &middot; [admit to](#admit-to) &middot; [of](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#of)

### acquiesce in

**Pronunciation:** /ˌækwiˈɛs ɪn/ &middot; *ak-wee-ESS in* &middot; weak /ˌækwiˈɛs ən/
**Русский:** молча соглашаться с — не возражать, хотя и не одобряя; книжн.
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal and literary; court opinions and academic prose above all — *the union acquiesced in the change for six years* is the kind of sentence where an American actually meets it; in conversation the word sounds arch
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*acquiesced in being passed over*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *acquiesced in to be passed over*) and **never a that-clause**. The verb also stands alone with no complement (*she acquiesced*), and there is no direct object: ✗ *acquiesced the plan*.
**Pattern:** *acquiesce in* [a decision / an arrangement] &middot; *acquiesce in* [verb-ing] &middot; *acquiesce* — no complement &middot; *acquiescence in* [something] — the noun keeps the same preposition
**Variants:** *acquiesce to* — now at least as common as *in* in American writing, especially where the object is a demand or a request (*acquiesced to their demands*). Careful legal and academic writers keep *in* for accepting a state of affairs, and *to* for yielding to a specific demand; either is defensible in general prose.
**Contrast:** **Silence is the whole meaning.** To *acquiesce in* something is to let it stand without objecting — approval is not implied and often is pointedly absent, which is why *she acquiesced in the decision* hints that she disliked it. *Consent to* grants permission, *assent to* expresses agreement, *accede to* gives in after resisting, and *agree to* is the neutral everyday word. The legal weight matters too: acquiescence over time can forfeit a right you never formally waived.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic in form, though what it names is a pointed failure to object; it takes the progressive (*the city was acquiescing in the encroachment for years*).
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** — no direct object (✗ *acquiesced the plan*), and the complement is optional: *she acquiesced* is complete.
**Passive:** **restricted** — legal prose does write *the encroachment was acquiesced in for years*, but outside that register the prepositional passive is not idiomatic: ✗ *the decision was acquiesced in by the board*.

Accept something without protest, without necessarily approving of it.

1. The board *acquiesced in* the chairman's decision rather than force a vote.
2. For years the city *acquiesced in* the encroachment and lost the right to object.
3. He *acquiesced in* the arrangement because fighting it would have cost more than it was worth.
4. She would not *acquiesce in* being described as a junior author.
5. Neighboring states quietly *acquiesced in* the annexation.
6. The court held that the tenant had *acquiesced in* the landlord's use of the driveway.

**See also:** [accede to](#accede-to) &middot; [assent to](#assent-to) &middot; [consent to](#consent-to) &middot; [agree to](#agree-with-agree-to-agree-on) &middot; [in](../../06-prepositions/catalog/01-core-in-on-at.md#in)

### acquit of

**Pronunciation:** /əˈkwɪt əv/ &middot; *uh-KWIT uv* &middot; weak /əˈkwɪt ə/
**Русский:** оправдывать по (обвинению) — признавать невиновным; юр.
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal; legal — this is a verdict word, and an American meets it in trial coverage, jury instructions, and appellate opinions, almost never outside them
**Complement:** noun phrase naming the **charge or offense**, or a **gerund** naming it (*acquitted of killing his business partner*) — **never a to-infinitive** and **never a that-clause** (✗ *acquitted him that he was innocent*). The passive is the normal frame, because the jury is usually left unnamed: *was acquitted of all charges*. The prepositions ✗ *acquitted from* and ✗ *acquitted for* are errors, though a count number takes *on*: *acquitted on the first two counts*.
**Pattern:** [a jury] *acquit* [someone] *of* [a charge] &middot; [someone] *be acquitted of* [a charge] &middot; *be acquitted on* [count / all counts] &middot; *acquit oneself well* — an unrelated idiom, no preposition
**Contrast:** ***Acquit of* and [convict of](#convict-of) are the two verdicts and they share a preposition** — only the direction changes. Beside them, *absolve of* needs no court and often means moral clearing, and *clear of* is the neutral newspaper verb (*cleared of any wrongdoing*). Note what *acquit* does not claim: a verdict of not guilty says the state failed to prove its case, not that the defendant was innocent — which is why *acquitted of* and *exonerated* are not synonyms. Keep the reflexive idiom separate: *he acquitted himself well in the interview* means he performed creditably and has nothing to do with courts.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic; a verdict delivered at a point in time.
**Transitivity:** **transitive** — the defendant is an obligatory direct object and the charge takes *of*: *the jury acquitted him of both counts*. Keep the reflexive idiom apart: *he acquitted himself well*.
**Passive:** **yes**, and the passive is the normal frame, because the jury goes unnamed — *she was acquitted of manslaughter*, *two of the three defendants were acquitted of everything in the indictment*.

Find someone not guilty of a criminal charge.

1. The jury *acquitted* him *of* both counts of fraud after four hours.
2. She was *acquitted of* manslaughter but convicted of the weapons charge.
3. A defendant *acquitted of* a crime cannot be tried for it again.
4. The panel *acquitted* the officer *of* using excessive force.
5. Two of the three defendants were *acquitted of* everything in the indictment.
6. He was *acquitted of* stealing the equipment when the receipts turned up.

**See also:** [convict of](#convict-of) &middot; [absolve of](#absolve-of) &middot; [accuse of](#accuse-of) &middot; [charge with](#charge-with-charge-for) &middot; [suspect of](#suspect-of) &middot; [of](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#of)

### adapt to

**Pronunciation:** /əˈdæpt tu/ &middot; *uh-DAPT too* &middot; weak /əˈdæpt tə/
**Русский:** приспосабливать(ся) к — менять себя или вещь под новые условия
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*adapt to working nights*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *adapt to work nights*, which says something else entirely). A wh-clause is fine after *to whatever*: *adapt to whatever the market does*.
**Pattern:** *adapt to* [noun phrase] &middot; *adapt to* [verb-ing] &middot; *adapt* [something] *for* / *into* [a new medium] &middot; *be adapted to* [noun phrase]
**Contrast:** **The trap is the gerund.** *Adapt to work nights* reads as a purpose infinitive (*adapt in order to work nights*) and does not mean what a learner intends; the fixed preposition demands *adapt to working nights*. Keep *adapt* apart from *adopt* — you *adapt to* a climate but *adopt* a policy, a child, or an accent, and *adopt* takes no preposition at all. When the object is the thing being changed rather than the thing being fitted into, the preposition switches: *adapt the novel **for** the screen*, *adapt it **into** a musical*.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic, a change worked over time; the progressive is natural (*older workers are adapting faster than anyone predicted*).
**Transitivity:** **both** — intransitive when you fit yourself to conditions (*she adapted to the altitude*), transitive when something else is reworked (*adapt the curriculum to their needs*, *adapt the novel for the screen*).
**Passive:** **yes** in the transitive sense — *the curriculum was adapted to the needs of second-language students*. No passive of the intransitive: ✗ *the altitude was adapted to*.

Change yourself, or change something, so that it fits new conditions.

1. It took her a year to *adapt to* the altitude.
2. Older workers *adapted to* the new system faster than anyone predicted.
3. The species has *adapted to* living in brackish water.
4. Small firms can *adapt to* a downturn more quickly than large ones.
5. He never really *adapted to* being the boss.
6. The curriculum was *adapted to* the needs of second-language students.

**See also:** [react to](#react-to) &middot; [look forward to](#look-forward-to) &middot; [get used to](#get-used-to-get-accustomed-to) &middot; [cope with](#cope-with) &middot; [to](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#to)

### add to

**Pronunciation:** /ˈæd tu/ &middot; *AD too* &middot; weak /ˈæd tə/
**Русский:** добавлять к; усиливать — увеличивать количество или чувство
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase — a gerund is possible but rare (*it only added to feeling shut out*). **Never a to-infinitive** and never a that-clause. In the two-object frame the thing added comes first: *add sugar to the batter*, never ✗ *add to the batter sugar*.
**Pattern:** *add* [something] *to* [something] &middot; [something] *add to* [a quantity or a feeling] &middot; *add to* [the confusion / the cost / the pressure]
**Contrast:** *Add to* increases something that already exists; *add up* totals a column of figures; *add up to* announces the sum or the upshot (*it all adds up to a lousy quarter*); and *add up* on its own, in the negative, means make sense (*his story doesn't add up*). Note that the simple transitive *add* takes no preposition when a clause follows — *she added that the deadline had moved*, never ✗ *added about*.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic; with an abstract subject the increase-sense verges on stative (*every new form adds to the paperwork*), but the progressive stays available.
**Transitivity:** **both** — transitive with the thing added as direct object (*add the zest to the dry ingredients*, never ✗ *add to the batter sugar*); objectless with *to* alone when the subject is what does the increasing (*the delay added to the cost*).
**Passive:** **yes** in the transitive frame — *two more witnesses were added to the list*. The increase-sense passivizes badly: ✗ *the cost was added to by the delay*.

Put something in, or make an existing quantity or feeling greater.

1. *Add* the zest *to* the dry ingredients before the butter.
2. The delay only *added to* the cost of the project.
3. Two more witnesses were *added to* the list.
4. Her silence *added to* the awkwardness of the whole evening.
5. Every new form *adds to* the paperwork somebody has to file.
6. *Add* my name *to* the petition.

**See also:** [contribute to](#contribute-to) &middot; [lead to](18-phrasal-h-l.md#lead-to) &middot; [result in](#result-in-result-from) &middot; [in addition to](../../06-prepositions/catalog/11-complex-multiword.md#in-addition-to)

### adhere to

**Pronunciation:** /ədˈhɪr tu/ &middot; *ud-HEER too* &middot; weak /ədˈhɪr tə/
**Русский:** придерживаться чего-то; прилипать к — следовать правилу или плану; книжн.
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal; policy manuals, audits, style guides, and clinical writing (*adherence to the medication schedule*) — in conversation Americans say *stick to*
**Complement:** noun phrase naming a **standard, schedule, plan, principle, or surface** — *to the protocol*, *to the deadline*, *to the glass*. A gerund is grammatical but stiff (*adhered to using only the approved form*); **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *adhere to use the approved form*) and **never a that-clause**. There is no direct-object frame: ✗ *adhere the schedule*.
**Pattern:** *adhere to* [a rule / a standard / a schedule] &middot; [something] *adhere to* [a surface] &middot; *strictly adhere to* [a protocol] &middot; *adherence to* [something] — the noun keeps the preposition
**Contrast:** **One verb, two lives.** The physical sense is literal sticking — *the label won't adhere to a wet bottle* — and the figurative sense is following a rule over time. Against its neighbors: *comply with* meets a requirement someone else imposes and is the word regulators use; *abide by* accepts a decision already made; *conform to* matches a specification; *stick to* is the everyday spoken equivalent of the figurative sense and would sound out of place in an audit report. Note that *adhere* takes *to* in both senses and never ✗ *adhere with* or ✗ *adhere on*.
**Verb type:** **both** — action in the physical sense (*the paint won't adhere to galvanized metal*), and closer to **stative** in the figurative one, where it reports a standing practice: *she adheres to a strict rule about email*.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** with an obligatory *to* phrase in both senses — ✗ *adhere the schedule*, ✗ *adhere with the protocol*.
**Passive:** **restricted** — the figurative prepositional passive is standard in formal and technical writing (*the protocol was strictly adhered to*), but the physical sense has none: ✗ *the bottle was adhered to by the label*.

Stick to something — a rule, a plan, or a surface.

1. Labs that *adhere to* the protocol get results everyone can replicate.
2. The paint will not *adhere to* galvanized metal without a primer.
3. She *adheres to* a strict rule about not reading email after nine.
4. Few of the states *adhered to* the original timetable.
5. The style guide asks writers to *adhere to* American spellings throughout.
6. Patients who *adhere to* the regimen for a year do substantially better.

**See also:** [comply with](#comply-with) &middot; [abide by](#abide-by) &middot; [conform to, conform with](#conform-to-conform-with) &middot; [commit to](#commit-to) &middot; [to](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#to)

### admit to

**Pronunciation:** /ədˈmɪt tu/ &middot; *ud-MIT too* &middot; weak /ədˈmɪt tə/
**Русский:** признавать(ся) в — соглашаться с тем, о чём предпочёл бы промолчать
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*admit to taking the money*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *admit to take the money*). The preposition is optional: *admit taking the money* is equally good. It disappears entirely before a clause: *admit that he took the money*, never ✗ *admit to that he took it*.
**Pattern:** *admit to* [noun phrase] &middot; *admit to* [verb-ing] &middot; *admit* [verb-ing] &middot; *admit that* [clause] &middot; *be admitted to* [a place, a school, a hospital]
**Contrast:** **Two different verbs share the spelling.** *Admit to* a crime is confessing; *be admitted to* a hospital, a college, or the bar is being let in — which is why *She was admitted to Stanford* is congratulations and *She admitted to cheating* is not. Against *confess to*, *admit to* is milder and covers small things (*I admit to a weakness for cheap novels*); *confess* implies guilt worth confessing. And unlike *accuse of*, *admit* can shed its preposition altogether before an *-ing* form.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic in the confessing sense; the homonymous *be admitted to* (a school, a hospital) is a separate dynamic verb of letting in.
**Transitivity:** **both** — the preposition is optional before an *-ing* form or a noun (*admit to taking the money* = *admit taking the money*), it disappears before a clause (*admit that he took it*, ✗ *admit to that*), and the letting-in verb is plainly transitive (*the hospital admitted her*).
**Passive:** **yes** in the letting-in sense, which is where the passive lives — *she was admitted to Stanford*. In the confessing sense drop the preposition to passivize: *the error was finally admitted*, not ✗ *the burglary was admitted to*.

Concede that something is true, usually something you would rather not say.

1. He finally *admitted to* forging the signature.
2. She *admits to* a certain nervousness about the whole arrangement.
3. Nobody *admitted to* having seen the email.
4. The company *admitted to* underreporting emissions for six years.
5. I'll *admit to* being wrong about him.
6. Two of the three suspects *admitted to* the burglary.

**See also:** [confess to](#confess-to) &middot; [accuse of](#accuse-of) &middot; [suspect of](#suspect-of) &middot; [boast about](#boast-about) &middot; [look forward to](#look-forward-to)

### agree with, agree to, agree on

**Pronunciation:** /əˈɡri wɪθ/ &middot; *uh-GREE with* &middot; weak /əˈɡri wəθ/
**Русский:** соглашаться — with разделять мнение, to принять предложение, on договориться о чём-то
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** *with* takes a person, a group, or an opinion. *to* takes a **noun phrase** naming a proposal, a term, or a demand — and here is the trap: *agree **to do** something* is not this preposition at all but the infinitive marker, which is why *agree to leave* is correct while ✗ *agree to leaving* is not. *on* / *about* takes a noun phrase, a **gerund**, or a wh-clause (*agree on what to charge*). All three drop away before a clause: *agree that the price is fair*.
**Pattern:** *agree with* [person / opinion] &middot; *agree to* [proposal] &middot; *agree to* [verb — infinitive, not preposition] &middot; *agree on* / *about* [topic] &middot; *agree with* [someone] *on* / *about* [topic] &middot; *agree that* [clause]
**Contrast:** **This is the set that trips everyone.** *Agree with* takes a side; *agree to* accepts something offered; *agree on* settles something jointly. *I agree with the plan* says you think it is right; *I agree to the plan* says you consent to it, whatever you think of it; *we agreed on the plan* says we hammered it out together. Two more things: ✗ *I am agree* is not English — *agree* is a verb, not an adjective — and ✗ *agree me* is not either. *Agree with* has a second life meaning suit: *That shrimp didn't agree with me.*
**Verb type:** **both** — action in the consenting and settling senses (*they agreed to the terms*, *we agreed on a date*), **stative** in *agree with* meaning share an opinion and in the suit-sense: *that shrimp didn't agree with me*, ✗ *is not agreeing with me*.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** throughout — no direct object (✗ *agree me*), and no adjective either (✗ *I am agree*). Complements are prepositional, a to-infinitive (*agreed to testify*), or a that-clause.
**Passive:** **restricted** — the settling senses take a prepositional passive (*the terms were agreed to on Friday*, *a date was finally agreed on*); the opinion and suit senses have none: ✗ *Dana is agreed with by me*. British ✗ *the price was agreed* without a preposition is not American.

Share an opinion, consent to something, or settle something jointly — and English uses a different preposition for each.

| Pattern | What it means | Example |
|---|---|---|
| *agree **with*** [person, view] | share the opinion | *I agree with Dana.* |
| *agree **with*** [food, climate] | suit, sit well with | *Late coffee doesn't agree with me.* |
| *agree **to*** [proposal, terms] | consent, accept | *They agreed to our terms.* |
| *agree **to** do* [verb] | consent — **infinitive**, not preposition | *He agreed to testify.* |
| *agree **on** / **about*** [topic] | reach a joint decision | *We agreed on a date.* |
| *agree **that*** [clause] | state a shared belief — **no preposition** | *Everyone agrees that it's late.* |

1. I *agree with* you about the timeline, but not about the budget.
2. The union *agreed to* the terms on Friday morning.
3. We never *agreed on* a price, so the sale fell through.
4. She *agreed to* stay another six months.
5. The two economists *agree about* the diagnosis and disagree about the cure.
6. Something at that potluck did not *agree with* me.
7. Can we at least *agree on* what the problem is?

**See also:** [decide on](#decide-on-decide-against) &middot; [comply with](#comply-with) &middot; [object to](#object-to) &middot; [argue with](#argue-with-argue-about-argue-over) &middot; [with](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#with) &middot; [to](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#to)

### aim at

**Pronunciation:** /ˈeɪm æt/ &middot; *AYM at* &middot; weak /ˈeɪm ət/
**Русский:** целиться в; стремиться к — направлять оружие или усилие на цель
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*aimed at cutting costs*) — **never a to-infinitive** after *at* (✗ *aimed at cut costs*, ✗ *aimed at to cut costs*). *Aim* does take a bare infinitive directly, with no preposition: *we aim to finish by June*.
**Pattern:** *aim* [a weapon, a camera] *at* [target] &middot; [a policy] *be aimed at* [a group] &middot; *be aimed at* [verb-ing] &middot; *aim for* [a goal] &middot; *aim to* [verb]
**Contrast:** Three shapes, three meanings. *Aim at* points at a target, literal or figurative, and in the passive is the standard formula for describing what a policy is for: *a program aimed at reducing recidivism* — note the *-ing*, which is the single commonest error in this pattern. *Aim for* names a result you are trying to reach (*aim for Tuesday*). *Aim to do* is the plain infinitive of intention (*we aim to please*). Note also *take aim at*, which is now the standard newspaper idiom for attacking something publicly.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic in both the pointing and the purposing senses.
**Transitivity:** **both** — transitive when you point a thing (*she aimed the flashlight at the crawlspace*), intransitive in *aim for the end of the quarter* and *aim to please*.
**Passive:** **yes**, and the passive participle is the form most often met — *the new rules are aimed at offshore accounts*, *a program aimed at reducing recidivism* (note the *-ing*).

Point something at a target, or direct an effort toward a goal.

1. The new rules are *aimed at* offshore accounts.
2. She *aimed* the flashlight *at* the crawlspace.
3. The campaign is *aimed at* getting first-time voters registered.
4. That remark was *aimed at* me, and everyone in the room knew it.
5. We're *aiming for* the end of the quarter.
6. The grant program *aims at* closing the gap between rural and urban districts.

**See also:** [point at](#point-at-point-to) &middot; [look at](#look-at-look-for-look-after-look-into) &middot; [concentrate on](#concentrate-on) &middot; [prepare for](#prepare-for) &middot; [at](../../06-prepositions/catalog/01-core-in-on-at.md#at)

### allow for

**Pronunciation:** /əˈlaʊ fɔr/ &middot; *uh-LOW for* &middot; weak /əˈlaʊ fɚ/
**Русский:** учитывать, предусматривать — заранее оставлять запас на что-то
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*allow for having to reprint it*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *allow for to reprint it*). Do not confuse it with the bare transitive *allow*, which takes an object plus an infinitive and no preposition whatever: *allow him to leave*, never ✗ *allow to him to leave*.
**Pattern:** *allow for* [noun phrase] &middot; *allow for* [verb-ing] &middot; *allow* [someone] *to* [verb] &middot; *allow* [verb-ing] &middot; [a design] *allow for* [a possibility]
**Contrast:** *Allow for* means build in a margin for, or make provision for: *allow for traffic*, *the budget allows for two hires*. Bare *allow* means permit, and it is one of the verbs that takes an object plus a to-infinitive — ✗ *allow to him* and ✗ *allow that he leaves* are both errors. In the passive the two stay apart: *He was allowed to leave* (permitted) against *Time was allowed for questions* (set aside).
**Verb type:** **both** — action when a person makes provision (*allow for forty minutes of traffic*), **stative** when a design or a budget is the subject: *the schedule allows for one full day of testing*.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** with an obligatory *for* phrase in this pattern. Bare transitive *allow* is a different verb meaning permit, and it takes an object plus an infinitive: *allow him to leave*, ✗ *allow to him to leave*.
**Passive:** **yes** — *time was allowed for questions*, and the prepositional passive is fine in careful prose: *the cost of permits was never allowed for in the estimate*.

Take something into account in advance, or leave room for it.

1. *Allow for* about forty minutes of traffic on a Friday.
2. The estimate doesn't *allow for* the cost of permits.
3. The schedule *allows for* one full day of testing.
4. You have to *allow for* the fact that half the sample never responded.
5. The design *allows for* adding a second story later.
6. Even *allowing for* inflation, the increase is remarkable.

**See also:** [consist of](#consist-of) &middot; [provide for](#provide-with-provide-for) &middot; [prepare for](#prepare-for) &middot; [for](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#for)

### allude to

**Pronunciation:** /əˈlud tu/ &middot; *uh-LOOD too* &middot; weak /əˈlud tə/
**Русский:** намекать на — упоминать, не называя прямо; книжн.
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal and literary; book reviews, literary criticism, careful journalism, and academic prose — in conversation an American says *hint at* or *bring up*
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*alluded to having been fired*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *alluded to have been fired*) and **never a that-clause** (✗ *alluded to that he was leaving*; either drop the preposition and switch verbs, *hinted that he was leaving*, or nominalize, *alluded to his leaving*). There is no direct-object frame: ✗ *alluded the scandal*.
**Pattern:** *allude to* [a person / an event / a text] &middot; *allude to* [verb-ing] &middot; *an allusion to* [something] — the noun keeps the preposition
**Contrast:** ***Allude to* is indirect; [refer to](#refer-to) is direct.** If you name the thing, you refer to it; if you gesture at it without naming it, you allude to it — which is why *he alluded to her divorce* means he never said the word. A writer who quotes Milton *refers to* him; one who echoes a line without a footnote *alludes to* him. Two traps come with it: *elude* means escape (*the name eludes me*), and *an allusion* is a hint while *an illusion* is a false impression.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic; a speech act, and one performed indirectly.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** with an obligatory *to* phrase — there is no direct-object frame: ✗ *alluded the scandal*.
**Passive:** **restricted** — the reduced form is idiomatic (*the case alluded to in the opinion*), but a full passive clause is stiff: ✗ *her divorce was alluded to by the dean* reads better active.

Mention something indirectly, without naming it outright.

1. The dean *alluded to* budget trouble but never used the word layoffs.
2. Her acceptance speech *alluded to* the fight over the nomination.
3. The title *alludes to* a psalm the author's mother used to recite.
4. He *alluded to* having worked in the industry, though he gave no dates.
5. Nobody at the table *alluded to* what had happened the night before.
6. The opinion *alludes to* an earlier case without citing it.

**See also:** [refer to](#refer-to) &middot; [point at, point to](#point-at-point-to) &middot; [comment on](#comment-on) &middot; [talk about](#talk-to-talk-with-talk-about) &middot; [to](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#to)

### amount to

**Pronunciation:** /əˈmaʊnt tu/ &middot; *uh-MOWNT too* &middot; weak /əˈmaʊnt tə/
**Русский:** составлять (сумму); быть равнозначным — по сути сводиться к чему-то
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase (a total, a sum, a characterization) or **gerund** (*amounts to admitting the whole thing*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *amounts to admit the whole thing*) and **never a that-clause**. The verb has **no passive**: ✗ *was amounted to*. Its subject is the thing being totaled or characterized, never the person: *the charges amount to $4,300*.
**Pattern:** [figures] *amount to* [a total] &middot; [an action] *amount to* [a characterization] &middot; *amount to* [verb-ing] &middot; *not amount to much* &middot; *never amount to anything*
**Contrast:** **Two senses, one preposition.** The arithmetic sense adds up (*the repairs amounted to nine hundred dollars*); the evaluative sense says what something really is (*that amounts to a confession*), and it is the one that carries an edge. *Add up to* is the informal twin of both; *come to* is the spoken one at a cash register (*that comes to $12.40*); and *equal* takes no preposition at all (*two plus two equals four*, ✗ *equals to four*). The fixed phrase *never amount to anything* is the only common use with a human subject.
**Verb type:** **stative** — relational in both senses, arithmetic and evaluative, and it refuses the progressive: ✗ *the invoices are amounting to half the revenue*.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** with an obligatory *to* phrase — no direct object (✗ *amounts nine hundred dollars*), and the subject is the thing totaled, never the person, outside the fixed *never amount to anything*.
**Passive:** **no** — this is one of the stative verbs learners try to passivize: ✗ *nine hundred dollars was amounted to by the repairs*, ✗ *an admission is amounted to by refusing to answer*.

Add up to a total, or be equivalent to something in effect.

1. The unpaid invoices *amount to* almost half the quarter's revenue.
2. Refusing to answer *amounts to* an admission as far as the committee is concerned.
3. Her share of the estate *amounted to* a few thousand dollars and a piano.
4. What the policy *amounts to* is a pay cut for everyone under fifty.
5. Three years of complaints *amounted to* nothing.
6. The changes *amount to* rewriting the contract in the buyer's favor.

**See also:** [add to](#add-to) &middot; [account for](#account-for) &middot; [accrue to](#accrue-to) &middot; [result in, result from](#result-in-result-from) &middot; [to](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#to)

### answer for

**Pronunciation:** /ˈænsər fɔr/ &middot; *AN-sur for* &middot; weak /ˈænsər fər/
**Русский:** отвечать за — нести ответственность за что-то или ручаться за кого-то
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral, with a moral or legal edge — *answer for his crimes* is courtroom-adjacent, *a lot to answer for* is everyday
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*answer for letting the permit lapse*) — **never a to-infinitive** and **never a that-clause**. In the vouching sense the complement is a person or a quality: *I can answer for her*, *answer for his honesty*.
**Pattern:** *answer for* [something you did] &middot; *answer for* [verb-ing] &middot; *have a lot to answer for* &middot; *answer for* [someone] — vouch for them &middot; *answer to* [a person] — report to them
**Contrast:** **Three related patterns pull apart cleanly.** *Answer for* takes the deed you are accountable for; *answer to* takes the person you are accountable to (*she answers to the board*); and plain [answer](#answer) takes the question and no preposition at all — *answer the question*, ✗ *answer to the question*. Beside them, [account for](#account-for) is about explaining and carries no blame, while *answer for* implies you may have to pay. The vouching sense is a separate idiom: *I'll answer for him* means I will stand behind him, the same job *vouch for* does.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic; being called to account is an event (*he will answer for those decisions at the hearing*).
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** with an obligatory *for* phrase — keep it apart from plain transitive *answer*, which takes the question bare: *answer the question*, ✗ *answer to the question*.
**Passive:** **restricted** — *nobody was ever made to answer for the spill* is the idiomatic dodge; a direct prepositional passive (*the missing deposit was never answered for*) is grammatical but rare.

Take responsibility for something, or stand behind someone.

1. Somebody is going to have to *answer for* the missing deposit.
2. He will *answer for* those decisions at the hearing next month.
3. The airline has a lot to *answer for* after that weekend.
4. I can *answer for* her — she has worked for me for nine years.
5. She refused to *answer for* choices her predecessor had made.
6. Nobody was ever made to *answer for* the spill.

**See also:** [answer](#answer) &middot; [account for](#account-for) &middot; [account to](#account-to) &middot; [blame for, blame on](#blame-for-blame-on) &middot; [pay for](19-phrasal-m-r.md#pay-for) &middot; [for](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#for)

### apologize to, apologize for

**Pronunciation:** /əˈpɑləˌdʒaɪz tu/ &middot; *uh-POL-uh-jize too* &middot; weak /əˈpɑləˌdʒaɪz tə/
**Русский:** извиняться — to перед тем, кого обидел, for за то, что сделал
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** *to* takes a person or a group. *for* takes a noun phrase or a **gerund** (*apologize for being late*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *apologize for to be late*). The person and the offense need different prepositions and cannot share one: ✗ *apologize to being late*, ✗ *apologize for you*.
**Pattern:** *apologize to* [person] &middot; *apologize for* [noun phrase / verb-ing] &middot; *apologize to* [person] *for* [noun phrase / verb-ing] &middot; *apologize* (alone)
**Contrast:** ***Apologize someone* is an error** — the person always needs *to*, unlike *thank*, *forgive*, and *excuse*, which take the person as a direct object. Compare: *I thanked her* but *I apologized to her*; *forgive me for that* but *I apologize to you for that*. The noun follows the verb: *an apology to the family for the delay*. Note also that *apologize for* an act is not the same as the older, formal *apologize for* a position, which means defend it — that sense survives mostly in the noun *apologist*.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic; a speech act, complete at a moment.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** — it never takes a direct object (✗ *apologize someone*, unlike *thank her*, *forgive me*), both complements are prepositional, and both are optional: *he apologized* is complete.
**Passive:** **restricted** — the *for* frame passivizes in formal writing (*the four-hour delay was never apologized for*), and *I have never been apologized to like that* is real American speech but stays colloquial; neither is the default.

Say you are sorry — to the person wronged, for the thing you did.

1. He *apologized to* the entire staff for the way he'd handled it.
2. She *apologized for* missing the deadline and offered to work the weekend.
3. You owe him an apology; go *apologize to* him.
4. The airline *apologized for* the four-hour delay and refunded nothing.
5. I *apologize for* the confusion in my last message.
6. Nobody ever *apologized to* the family.

**See also:** [thank for](#thank-for) &middot; [forgive for](#forgive-for) &middot; [excuse for](#excuse-for) &middot; [blame for](#blame-for-blame-on)

### appeal to

**Pronunciation:** /əˈpil tu/ &middot; *uh-PEEL too* &middot; weak /əˈpil tə/
**Русский:** нравиться кому-то; взывать к кому-то; подавать апелляцию в (суд)
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral; the legal sense is formal
**Complement:** noun phrase naming a **person, group, court, or principle** — never a gerund after *to* (✗ *appeals to buying local*; the attraction sense takes a person: *buying local appeals to me*). A to-infinitive after the person is licensed by the person, not by the preposition: *appealed to donors **to** cover the gap*. The thing requested takes *for*: *appealed to the state for emergency funds*.
**Pattern:** [something] *appeal to* [someone] — be attractive &middot; *appeal to* [someone] *for* [something] &middot; *appeal to* [someone] *to* [verb] &middot; *appeal to* [a court] &middot; *appeal* [a ruling] — American transitive legal use, no preposition
**Contrast:** ***Appeal to* and *appeal against* divide by variety and by role.** American lawyers appeal a decision transitively — *she appealed the verdict* — and use *to* for the court they take it to: *appealed to the Ninth Circuit*. *Appeal against the verdict* is the British construction and reads as an import in American writing. Keep the everyday senses apart too: *the offer appeals to me* is about attraction and has no request in it, while *I appealed to him* is a plea. And *appeal to* a principle (*appealed to fairness*) is arguing from it, not asking it for anything.
**Verb type:** **both** — **stative** in the attraction sense, where the subject is the thing liked (*the offer appeals to me*, ✗ *is appealing to me* in that sense); action in the pleading and legal senses.
**Transitivity:** **both** — intransitive with *to* in the attraction, plea, and court senses; transitive in American legal usage, where you *appeal a verdict* with no preposition at all (British *appeal against the verdict* reads as an import).
**Passive:** **yes** in the transitive legal sense — *the ruling was appealed within thirty days*. **No** in the attraction sense: ✗ *I am appealed to by the color scheme*.

Be attractive to someone, ask them earnestly for something, or take a case to a higher court.

1. The color scheme *appeals to* almost nobody under forty.
2. Organizers *appealed to* the governor for National Guard support.
3. He *appealed to* the committee to reopen the application window.
4. The defense *appealed to* the state supreme court and lost again.
5. That argument *appeals to* people who have never had to make payroll.
6. She *appealed to* his sense of loyalty, which turned out to be a mistake.

**See also:** [apply for, apply to](#apply-for-apply-to) &middot; [ask for, ask about](#ask-for-ask-about) &middot; [object to](#object-to) &middot; [long for](#long-for) &middot; [to](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#to)

### apply for, apply to

**Pronunciation:** /əˈplaɪ fɔr/ &middot; *uh-PLY for* &middot; weak /əˈplaɪ fɚ/
**Русский:** подавать заявление на (for); относиться к, распространяться на (to)
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** *for* takes a noun phrase naming what you want (a job, a permit, asylum). *to* takes a noun phrase naming the body you approach, or the thing a rule covers. **Neither takes a gerund**: ✗ *apply for working there* — say *apply for the job*. *Apply* does take a to-infinitive of purpose (*she applied to study nursing*), which is the infinitive marker, not this preposition.
**Pattern:** *apply for* [a job, a grant, a visa] &middot; *apply to* [an institution] &middot; *apply to* [an institution] *for* [a thing] &middot; [a rule] *apply to* [people] &middot; *apply* [something] *to* [a surface] &middot; *apply* [oneself] *to* [work]
**Contrast:** *Apply **for*** names the prize; *apply **to*** names the door you knock on: *she applied to four schools for financial aid*. A third sense of *apply to* has no applicant in it at all — *the rule applies to everyone* means it covers them, and that is the sense in *does not apply*. Keep it away from *apply* meaning spread on (*apply the second coat*), which takes a direct object and *to*.
**Verb type:** **both** — action in the requesting senses (*she applied for the fellowship*), **stative** in the coverage sense, where a rule is the subject: *the overtime rule applies to salaried employees*, ✗ *is applying to them*.
**Transitivity:** **both** — intransitive in both prepositional senses (✗ *apply the fellowship*); transitive in *apply the second coat to the wall* and in *apply oneself to the work*.
**Passive:** **restricted** — *the permit was applied for in March* and *the second coat was applied* are both fine; the stative coverage sense has no passive: ✗ *contractors are not applied to by the rule*.

Make a formal request, or — in a separate sense — be relevant to something.

1. She *applied for* the fellowship twice before she got it.
2. He *applied to* six graduate programs and heard back from two.
3. You have to *apply for* a permit before any demolition starts.
4. The overtime rule *applies to* salaried employees as well.
5. *Apply to* the registrar's office, not to the department.
6. None of that *applies to* contractors.

**See also:** [ask for](#ask-for-ask-about) &middot; [qualify for](#qualify-for) &middot; [refer to](#refer-to) &middot; [consult with](#consult-with)

### approve of

**Pronunciation:** /əˈpruv əv/ &middot; *uh-PROOV uv* &middot; weak /əˈpruv ə/
**Русский:** одобрять — считать что-то правильным или приемлемым
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase, **gerund**, or possessive plus gerund (*approve of his leaving early*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *approve of to leave early*) and **never a that-clause** (✗ *approve of that he left*).
**Pattern:** *approve of* [noun phrase] &middot; *approve of* [verb-ing] &middot; *approve of* [someone] *-ing* &middot; *approve* [a budget, a plan, a drug] — no preposition
**Contrast:** **The preposition changes the verb completely.** *Approve* with a direct object and no preposition means authorize: *the board approved the budget*, *the FDA approved the drug*. *Approve **of*** means think well of, and carries no authority at all: *her father doesn't approve of him*. So *The committee approved the proposal* is a decision and *The committee approves of the proposal* is an opinion. *Disapprove* has only the *of* sense — there is no transitive ✗ *disapprove the budget* in careful American usage; use *reject*.
**Verb type:** **stative** — an opinion, not an act, and it resists the progressive: ✗ *I am approving of the marriage*. The bare transitive *approve* meaning authorize is a separate, dynamic verb.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** with an obligatory *of* phrase for the opinion sense; the authorizing verb is transitive and takes no preposition: *the board approved the budget*, *the FDA approved the drug*.
**Passive:** **restricted** — the authorizing verb passivizes freely (*the drug was approved by the FDA*), but the stative *of* sense resists: ✗ *the marriage was never approved of by her parents* is stilted where the active is not.

Think well of something, or find it acceptable.

1. Her parents never *approved of* the marriage.
2. I don't *approve of* airing this in a staff meeting.
3. He *approves of* the goal but not the method.
4. Nobody *approves of* being lectured at.
5. The neighbors did not *approve of* the color she painted the porch.
6. She *approves of* his taking the semester off.

**See also:** [disapprove of](#disapprove-of) &middot; [object to](#object-to) &middot; [agree with](#agree-with-agree-to-agree-on) &middot; [agree to](#agree-with-agree-to-agree-on)

### argue with, argue about, argue over

**Pronunciation:** /ˈɑrɡju wɪθ/ &middot; *AR-gyoo with* &middot; weak /ˈɑrɡju wəθ/
**Русский:** спорить — with с кем-то, about/over о чём-то; также доказывать мысль
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** *with* takes a person or a group. *about* and *over* take a noun phrase, a **gerund**, or a wh-clause (*argued about who would pay*) — **never a to-infinitive**. *Argue that* takes a full clause and no preposition (*she argued that the survey was flawed*), and *argue for* / *against* takes the position being urged.
**Pattern:** *argue with* [person] &middot; *argue about* / *over* [topic] &middot; *argue with* [person] *about* [topic] &middot; *argue for* / *against* [a position] &middot; *argue that* [clause]
**Contrast:** ***Argue to someone* is an error** in the quarreling sense — you argue *with* people. *About* and *over* are interchangeable for the topic, though *over* leans toward something contested and divisible (*argue over the check*, *over custody*) and *about* toward a subject (*argue about politics*). The formal sense is different again: *argue for a position* means make the case for it, and *argue that* is what appears in a brief or a paper, where nobody is quarreling with anybody.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic in both the quarreling and the case-making senses.
**Transitivity:** **both** — intransitive in the quarreling sense (✗ *argue the umpire*); it takes a that-clause in the formal sense (*she argued that the control group was too small*) and a direct object in legal use (*argue a case*).
**Passive:** **restricted** — *it was argued that the survey was flawed* is standard, and *the wording was argued over for three hours* is fine; the person cannot be promoted: ✗ *the umpire was argued with*.

Quarrel, or make a case.

1. They *argued about* money for most of the marriage.
2. Don't *argue with* the umpire; it never works.
3. The two sides *argued over* the wording for three hours.
4. She *argued that* the study's control group was too small.
5. He *argued with* his brother *about* the estate until neither would speak.
6. The paper *argues for* a much narrower reading of the statute.

**See also:** [agree with](#agree-with-agree-to-agree-on) &middot; [complain to](#complain-to-complain-about) &middot; [compete with](#compete-with-compete-against-compete-for) &middot; [object to](#object-to)

### arrive at, arrive in

**Pronunciation:** /əˈraɪv æt/ &middot; *uh-RIVE at* &middot; weak /əˈraɪv ət/
**Русский:** прибывать — at в точку (вокзал, дом), in в город или страну
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase only — no gerund, no clause. **The one thing *arrive* never takes is *to*** (✗ *arrive to the airport*). Before *home*, *here*, *there*, and *abroad* it takes **no preposition at all**: *arrive home*, never ✗ *arrive at home*.
**Pattern:** *arrive at* [a building, a point, an event, a small place] &middot; *arrive in* [a country, a city, a large area] &middot; *arrive* [home / here / there] &middot; *arrive at* [a decision, a conclusion, a figure]
**Contrast:** ***Arrive to* is the single most common preposition error made with this verb**, imported from Spanish *llegar a* and French *arriver à*. The choice between the two correct options is the same one *in* and *at* make everywhere: *in* for a space you end up inside (*arrive in Chicago, in Brazil*), *at* for a point on the map (*arrive at the hotel, at Gate 12, at the wedding*). Compare *reach*, which is transitive and takes no preposition at all — *reach the airport*, ✗ *reach to the airport* — and *get to*, which is the everyday spoken form of both.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic, and an achievement: it names the endpoint of a journey, not a stretch of one.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** always — never a direct object (✗ *arrive the airport*; that verb is *reach*), never ✗ *arrive to*, and no preposition at all before *home*, *here*, or *there*.
**Passive:** **no** in the motion sense — ✗ *Denver was arrived in on the last flight*. The figurative frame is the one exception: *a verdict was arrived at in under two hours* is idiomatic.

Come to the end of a journey.

1. We *arrived at* the trailhead a little after six.
2. She *arrived in* Denver on the last flight of the night.
3. The package finally *arrived at* the office.
4. They *arrived in* the country with two suitcases and no plan.
5. He *arrived* home to find the power out.
6. The jury *arrived at* a verdict in under two hours.

**See also:** [reach](#reach) &middot; [in](../../06-prepositions/catalog/01-core-in-on-at.md#in) &middot; [at](../../06-prepositions/catalog/01-core-in-on-at.md#at) &middot; [to](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#to)

### ascribe to

**Pronunciation:** /əˈskraɪb tu/ &middot; *uh-SKRYBE too* &middot; weak /əˈskraɪb tə/
**Русский:** приписывать кому-то — авторство, причину или свойство; книжн.
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal; scholarship, art history, criticism, and serious journalism — an American meets it in a museum label (*a panel long ascribed to Giotto*) or an academic paper
**Complement:** the thing ascribed is a **direct object**; *to* takes the **person, work, or cause** it is assigned to. A **gerund** is possible in the cause sense (*ascribed the delay to being understaffed*) but **never a to-infinitive** and **never a that-clause**. The object cannot be omitted: ✗ *ascribed to Giotto the panel* garbles the order, and ✗ *ascribed to carelessness* alone is incomplete.
**Pattern:** *ascribe* [a quality / a cause / a work] *to* [someone or something] &middot; *be ascribed to* [someone] &middot; *ascribe* [an outcome] *to* [verb-ing]
**Contrast:** ***Ascribe to* and [attribute to](#attribute-to) are near-twins.*** *Attribute* is the commoner word and does everything; *ascribe* leans toward assigning qualities, motives, and authorship, and sounds more bookish. Two neighbors sharpen the picture: *impute to* is the legal and moral version and is nearly always unflattering (*imputed bad faith to the seller*), and [credit with](#credit-with) reverses the frame and is always positive (*credited her with the idea*). Watch the spelling trap: *subscribe to* an idea means to hold it (*I don't subscribe to that theory*), which is not what *ascribe to* means at all.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic, a mental act of assignment (*historians ascribe the collapse to drought*); the participle shades toward stative in *powers ascribed to the office by custom*.
**Transitivity:** **transitive** — the thing ascribed is an obligatory direct object and the source takes *to*: ✗ *ascribed to carelessness* alone is incomplete, and ✗ *ascribed to Giotto the panel* garbles the order.
**Passive:** **yes**, and it is the commoner frame in art history and scholarship — *the sonnet was ascribed to Shakespeare for two centuries*.

Assign a quality, a cause, or an authorship to someone or something.

1. Historians *ascribe* the collapse *to* a mix of drought and bad debt.
2. The sonnet was *ascribed to* Shakespeare for two centuries.
3. She *ascribes* her recovery *to* stubbornness more than to medicine.
4. Don't *ascribe* motives *to* people whose reasoning you haven't heard.
5. The auditors *ascribed* the shortfall *to* being three staffers short all spring.
6. Powers *ascribed to* the office by custom are not powers granted by statute.

**See also:** [attribute to](#attribute-to) &middot; [credit with](#credit-with) &middot; [blame for, blame on](#blame-for-blame-on) &middot; [subscribe to](#subscribe-to) &middot; [to](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#to)

### ask for, ask about

**Pronunciation:** /ˈæsk fɔr/ &middot; *ASK for* &middot; weak /ˈæsk fɚ/
**Русский:** просить о чём-то (for); расспрашивать о чём-то (about)
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** *for* takes a noun phrase naming what you want. *about* takes a noun phrase, a **gerund**, or a wh-clause (*asked about getting a refund*, *asked about what it would cost*). **Neither takes a to-infinitive** — but *ask* itself does, in a separate frame with no preposition: *ask someone to do something*, *ask to leave*. The thing asked is a direct object, not a prepositional one: *ask a question*, *ask me something*.
**Pattern:** *ask for* [a thing] &middot; *ask* [someone] *for* [a thing] &middot; *ask about* [a topic] &middot; *ask* [someone] *about* [a topic] &middot; *ask* [someone] [a question] &middot; *ask* [someone] *to* [verb] &middot; *ask if* / *whether* [clause]
**Contrast:** ***Ask to me* and ✗ *ask a question to me* are errors** — the person is a direct object (*ask me a question*), which is why the two-preposition instinct from Romance languages misfires here.
