Within the set: *ask **for*** requests (*ask for a raise*), *ask **about*** inquires (*ask about the raise*), and *ask **after** someone* — a health inquiry — is the one member Americans mostly replace with *ask about*. Note that *ask for someone* means request to speak to them, so *There's a man at the door asking for you* is not about a favor. And *You asked for it* is a fixed idiom meaning you brought it on yourself.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic; a speech act in every frame.
**Transitivity:** **both**, and it reaches **ditransitive** — *ask me a question*, *ask her to wait* (no preposition at all: ✗ *ask a question to me*), against the prepositional *ask for a raise* and *ask about the refund policy*.
**Passive:** **yes** — *he was asked about the refund policy*, *they were asked to wait*. The *for* frame passivizes only stiffly: *more time was asked for* is grammatical but wooden.

Request something, or inquire about something.

| Pattern | What it means | Example |
|---|---|---|
| *ask **for*** [thing] | request it | *She asked for more time.* |
| *ask **for*** [person] | request to speak to them | *A reporter is asking for you.* |
| *ask **about*** [topic] | inquire about it | *He asked about the refund policy.* |
| *ask **after*** [person] | inquire about their health (formal, less common in the U.S.) | *She asked after your mother.* |
| *ask* [person] [question] | **no preposition** | *Ask me anything.* |
| *ask* [person] *to* [verb] | request an action — **infinitive** | *They asked us to wait.* |

1. She *asked for* a week's extension and got three days.
2. He *asked about* the health insurance before he asked about the salary.
3. Somebody at the front desk is *asking for* you.
4. I *asked about* transferring the deposit, and nobody knew.
5. Don't be afraid to *ask for* help.
6. They *asked about* how long the warranty runs.
7. *Ask* the pharmacist *about* the interaction before you take both.

**See also:** [apply for](#apply-for-apply-to) &middot; [search for](#search-for) &middot; [hope for](#hope-for) &middot; [hear about](#hear-about-hear-from-hear-of) &middot; [wish for](#wish-for)

### assent to

**Pronunciation:** /əˈsɛnt tu/ &middot; *uh-SENT too* &middot; weak /əˈsɛnt tə/
**Русский:** давать согласие на — формально одобрять; офиц./книжн.
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal; parliamentary, diplomatic, and philosophical writing — *the governor assented to the compromise*, *royal assent*, *assent to a proposition* — and stiff enough that in speech an American simply says *agreed to*
**Complement:** noun phrase naming a **proposal, request, terms, or a proposition**. A gerund is possible but rare (*assented to being interviewed*); **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *assented to be interviewed*, which means something else — that is a passive infinitive after *agreed*) and **never a that-clause**. The verb also stands alone: *she assented with a nod*. There is no direct object: ✗ *assented the plan*.
**Pattern:** *assent to* [a proposal / terms] &middot; *assent* — no complement &middot; *give one's assent to* [something] &middot; *assent to* [a proposition] — the philosophical sense, accept it as true
**Contrast:** **Five verbs of saying yes, and they are not interchangeable.** *Agree to* is the everyday word; *consent to* grants permission and is what medicine and law require (*consent to treatment*, *consent to the search*); *assent to* formally expresses approval, often of a document or a proposition; [accede to](#accede-to) is yielding after resistance; [acquiesce in](#acquiesce-in) is not objecting, with no approval implied. Do not let the spelling slip to *ascent*, which is a climb.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic; a formal act of approval, given at a moment (*the board assented without debate*).
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** — no direct object (✗ *assented the plan*), and the complement is optional: *she assented with a nod*.
**Passive:** **restricted** — legal boilerplate does write *the terms were assented to by both parties*, but in ordinary American prose the prepositional passive is stiff and *agreed to* replaces it.

Formally express agreement or approval.

1. The board *assented to* the revised bylaws without debate.
2. Both governments *assented to* the wording of the communiqué.
3. She *assented to* the interview only after her lawyer read the questions.
4. The bill became law when the president *assented to* it.
5. No serious scientist *assents to* that proposition anymore.
6. He *assented to* the change in venue but asked for a week's delay.

**See also:** [accede to](#accede-to) &middot; [consent to](#consent-to) &middot; [acquiesce in](#acquiesce-in) &middot; [agree to](#agree-with-agree-to-agree-on) &middot; [to](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#to)

### attest to

**Pronunciation:** /əˈtɛst tu/ &middot; *uh-TEST too* &middot; weak /əˈtɛst tə/
**Русский:** свидетельствовать о — подтверждать истинность или служить доказательством; юр.
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal; legal and documentary — a notary *attests to* a signature, a witness *attests to* what she saw, and academic prose uses *as the record attests*
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*attested to having seen him leave*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *attested to have seen him leave*). A **that-clause is fine only after the preposition is dropped**: *attested that the copy was genuine*, never ✗ *attested to that the copy was genuine*. Legal drafting also uses a bare transitive frame: *attest a will*, *attest the signature*.
**Pattern:** [a person] *attest to* [a fact] &middot; [a thing] *attest to* [a quality] &middot; *attest to* [verb-ing] &middot; *attest that* [clause] — no preposition &middot; *attest* [a document] — legal transitive
**Contrast:** ***Attest to* accepts an inanimate subject; *testify to* mostly does not.** *The lines outside attest to the show's popularity* is idiomatic; ✗ *the lines testify to* is strained, because testifying is something a person does under oath in a proceeding. Beside them, *swear to* is a personal oath (*I'd swear to it*), and *vouch for* backs a person's character rather than a fact. Note that the preposition disappears before a clause, which is where learners most often add it.
**Verb type:** **both** — action when a person testifies (*two coworkers attested to his whereabouts*), **stative** when a thing serves as evidence: *the wear on the steps attests to three centuries of foot traffic*, ✗ *is attesting to*.
**Transitivity:** **both** — intransitive with *to*; transitive in legal drafting (*attest a will*, *attest the signature*) and before a that-clause, where the preposition must go: ✗ *attested to that the copy was genuine*.
**Passive:** **restricted** — the legal transitive passivizes cleanly (*the will was attested by two witnesses*); the *to* frame does not travel well: ✗ *his whereabouts were attested to by two coworkers* is clumsy beside the active.

State or confirm that something is true, or serve as evidence of it.

1. Two coworkers *attested to* his whereabouts that afternoon.
2. The bank requires a notary to *attest to* every signature on the transfer.
3. The wear on the steps *attests to* three centuries of foot traffic.
4. She *attested to* having warned the supervisor twice.
5. Her colleagues *attest to* how hard she worked that year.
6. Anyone who has driven that stretch in February can *attest to* the potholes.

**See also:** [confess to](#confess-to) &middot; [account for](#account-for) &middot; [answer for](#answer-for) &middot; [refer to](#refer-to) &middot; [to](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#to)

### attribute to

**Pronunciation:** /əˈtrɪbjut tu/ &middot; *uh-TRIB-yoot too* &middot; weak /əˈtrɪbjət tə/
**Русский:** приписывать кому-то/чему-то — называть источник, причину или автора
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal; standard in science, journalism, and museum catalogs
**Complement:** the thing attributed is a **direct object**; *to* takes the **cause, person, or source** — a noun phrase or a **gerund** (*attributed the delay to being short-staffed*). **Never a to-infinitive** (✗ *attributed the delay to be short-staffed*) and **never a that-clause**. Both slots are obligatory in the active: ✗ *attributed to the weather* with no object is incomplete; the passive fills the gap instead — *the delay was attributed to the weather*.
**Pattern:** *attribute* [something] *to* [a cause / a person] &middot; *be attributed to* [someone] &middot; *attribute* [an outcome] *to* [verb-ing] &middot; *attributable to* — the adjective keeps the preposition
**Contrast:** **The three assignment verbs split by tone.** *Attribute to* is neutral and works for causes, quotes, and paintings alike; [blame on](#blame-for-blame-on) is the negative version (*blamed the delay on the weather*); [credit with](#credit-with) is the positive one and flips the frame, putting the person first (*credited the pilot with the landing*). [Ascribe to](#ascribe-to) is the bookish twin of *attribute to*, and *impute to* is its legal, usually unflattering cousin. Watch the stress: the verb is *uh-TRIB-yoot*, the noun *AT-ruh-byoot*.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic, a mental act of assigning a cause, a quote, or an authorship.
**Transitivity:** **transitive** — both slots are obligatory in the active: *attribute the fire to a failed extension cord*, and ✗ *attributed to the weather* with no object is incomplete.
**Passive:** **yes**, and the passive is where the pattern lives — *the quotation is usually attributed to Twain, who never said it*, *the panel was attributed to a follower of Rembrandt*.

Say that something was caused by, said by, or made by a particular source.

1. Investigators *attributed* the fire *to* a failed extension cord.
2. The quotation is usually *attributed to* Twain, who never said it.
3. She *attributes* her stamina *to* twenty years of swimming.
4. The panel was *attributed to* a follower of Rembrandt, not the master.
5. Analysts *attribute* the rebound *to* falling interest rates.
6. He *attributed* the mistake *to* being handed the file an hour before the meeting.

**See also:** [ascribe to](#ascribe-to) &middot; [credit with](#credit-with) &middot; [blame for, blame on](#blame-for-blame-on) &middot; [result in, result from](#result-in-result-from) &middot; [to](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#to)

### avail oneself of

**Pronunciation:** /əˈveɪl wʌnˈsɛlf əv/ &middot; *uh-VAYL wun-SELF uv* &middot; weak /əˈveɪl wənˈsɛlf ə/
**Русский:** воспользоваться чем-то — прибегнуть к предложенной услуге или праву; книжн.
**Type:** verb + reflexive object + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal; official letters, contracts, and court opinions — *tenants who avail themselves of this remedy*, *please avail yourself of the shuttle* — and pompous in ordinary speech, where Americans say *use* or *take advantage of*
**Complement:** a **reflexive pronoun matching the subject** is obligatory, then *of* plus a noun phrase naming an **opportunity, service, right, or remedy**. A gerund after *of* is grammatical but clumsy (*availed himself of being allowed to appeal*); **never a to-infinitive**. There is no plain transitive frame: ✗ *avail the opportunity*, ✗ *avail this service*.
**Pattern:** *avail* [oneself / yourself / themselves] *of* [an opportunity] &middot; *not avail oneself of* [a remedy] &middot; *to no avail* — a fixed noun phrase, unrelated frame
**Contrast:** **The reflexive is the whole difficulty.** American usage requires it: *avail yourself of the discount*, and ✗ *avail of the discount* — very common in Irish and Indian English — reads as an error in American writing. The everyday equivalent is *take advantage of*, which is neutral about opportunities (*take advantage of the sale*) but accusatory about people (*took advantage of her*); *avail oneself of* never carries that second reading. Note also the survival of the old intransitive in *to no avail*, meaning without success.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic; taking up an opportunity that is offered.
**Transitivity:** **transitive**, but only with a **reflexive** object matching the subject — *avail yourself of the discount*, and never ✗ *avail the opportunity* or ✗ *avail of the discount*, which is Irish and Indian English rather than American.
**Passive:** **no** — a reflexive object cannot be promoted: ✗ *themselves were availed of the tuition benefit*, ✗ *the shuttle was availed of by guests*. Use *take advantage of*, which passivizes.

Make use of an opportunity or a service that is offered to you.

1. Employees may *avail themselves of* the tuition benefit after one year.
2. Few defendants *avail themselves of* the right to a speedy trial.
3. Guests are welcome to *avail themselves of* the airport shuttle.
4. She never *availed herself of* the appeal the contract allowed her.
5. The statute lets small businesses *avail themselves of* an expedited filing.
6. I *availed myself of* his offer of a spare room for the week.

**See also:** [take care of](#take-care-of) &middot; [benefit from](#benefit-from) &middot; [qualify for](#qualify-for) &middot; [of](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#of)

## B

### believe in

**Pronunciation:** /bɪˈliv ɪn/ &middot; *buh-LEEV in* &middot; weak /bɪˈliv ən/
**Русский:** верить в — считать существующим или ценным
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*believe in taking the stairs*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *believe in to take the stairs*) and **never a that-clause** (✗ *believe in that it works*; drop the preposition and say *believe that it works*).
**Pattern:** *believe in* [a thing's existence] &middot; *believe in* [a person] &middot; *believe in* [verb-ing] &middot; *believe* [someone / something] — no preposition &middot; *believe that* [clause]
**Contrast:** ***I believe you* and *I believe in you* are two different sentences.** Bare *believe* plus an object means accept what was said as true; *believe **in*** means hold that something exists, or trust its value, or have confidence in a person. *I believe her* is about one statement; *I believe in her* is about her character. Likewise *Do you believe the report?* asks whether it is accurate, and *Do you believe in the report?* is barely English. Before a clause the preposition always goes: *believe that*, never ✗ *believe in that*.
**Verb type:** **stative** — a mental state, and it rejects the progressive: ✗ *I am believing in ghosts*, ✗ *he is believing in the program*.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** with an obligatory *in* phrase in this pattern; bare transitive *believe* (accept as true) is the other frame, and the preposition always goes before a clause: *believe that it works*, ✗ *believe in that it works*.
**Passive:** **no** for the *in* pattern — stative and prepositional both work against it: ✗ *ghosts are not believed in by her*. Only the bare transitive passivizes, and mostly impersonally: *it is widely believed that the numbers were revised*.

Hold that something exists, or trust its value or its worth.

1. She doesn't *believe in* ghosts, but she won't stay in that house alone.
2. I *believe in* paying people before the invoice is due.
3. He *believes in* the program even though the numbers are flat.
4. My grandfather *believed in* a cold shower every morning of his life.
5. *Believe in* yourself is easy advice and hard work.
6. Half the committee doesn't *believe in* the forecast at all.

**See also:** [rely on](#rely-on) &middot; [count on](#count-on) &middot; [depend on](#depend-on) &middot; [approve of](#approve-of) &middot; [in](../../06-prepositions/catalog/01-core-in-on-at.md#in)

### belong to

**Pronunciation:** /bɪˈlɔŋ tu/ &middot; *buh-LAWNG too* &middot; weak /bɪˈlɔŋ tə/
**Русский:** принадлежать кому-то; состоять в (организации); быть на своём месте
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase only — **no gerund, no infinitive, no clause**. *Belong* is a stative verb and does not take the progressive: ✗ *This is belonging to me*. When the sense is proper place rather than ownership, the preposition changes to *in*, *on*, *with*, or *under*, and can never be *to*: *these belong in the drawer*, ✗ *these belong to the drawer*.
**Pattern:** [something] *belong to* [an owner] &middot; [someone] *belong to* [a club, a party, a generation] &middot; [something] *belong in* / *on* / *with* [its proper place] &middot; *belong* (alone — feel at home)
**Contrast:** **Ownership takes *to*; location takes *in*, *on*, or *with*.** *That coat belongs to Marcus* names the owner; *That coat belongs in the closet* says where it goes. Membership also takes *to* (*she belongs to the bar association*), but note that Americans more often say *is a member of*. Bare *belong* with no complement means feel accepted — *I never belonged there* — and is one of the most common uses of the verb in ordinary speech.
**Verb type:** **stative** — pure relation, ownership or membership, with no progressive at all: ✗ *this is belonging to me*.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** — never a direct object (✗ *that truck belongs my father-in-law*); the owner takes *to*, the proper place takes *in*, *on*, or *with* (*these belong in the drawer*, ✗ *belong to the drawer*), and bare *belong* stands alone: *he never felt he belonged*.
**Passive:** **no** — this is the textbook case: objectless and stative together, so ✗ *my father-in-law is belonged to by that truck* and ✗ *three birding groups are belonged to by her*. Use *own* or *be a member of*, which do passivize.

Be owned by someone, be a member of something, or have a proper place.

1. That truck *belongs to* my father-in-law.
2. She *belongs to* three different birding groups.
3. The copyright *belongs to* the estate, not the publisher.
4. Those files *belong in* the archive, not on your desktop.
5. Which of these keys *belongs to* the shed?
6. He never felt he *belonged* at that school.

**See also:** [consist of](#consist-of) &middot; [share with](#share-with) &middot; [of](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#of) &middot; [to](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#to)

### benefit from

**Pronunciation:** /ˈbɛnəfɪt frʌm/ &middot; *BEN-uh-fit from* &middot; weak /ˈbɛnəfɪt frəm/
**Русский:** извлекать пользу из — получать выгоду от чего-то
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*benefit from having a second reader*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *benefit from to have a second reader*). Note the American spelling of the inflections: one *t* — *benefited*, *benefiting*.
**Pattern:** [someone] *benefit from* [something] &middot; *benefit from* [verb-ing] &middot; [something] *benefit* [someone] — no preposition &middot; *be of benefit to* [someone]
**Contrast:** **The verb runs both directions and the preposition tells you which.** *Farmers benefited from the subsidy* means they gained; *The subsidy benefited farmers* means it helped them — transitive, no preposition. Getting these backwards (✗ *the subsidy benefited from farmers*) reverses the sentence. *Benefit **by*** is older and now rare in American prose; *from* is the live form. The noun pattern is different again: *a benefit **to*** the community.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic in the sense of a process, and the progressive is natural: *coastal towns are benefiting from the tourism*.
**Transitivity:** **both**, and the two directions must not be crossed — intransitive with *from* when the subject gains (*farmers benefited from the subsidy*), transitive with no preposition when the subject helps (*the subsidy benefited farmers*, ✗ *the subsidy benefited from farmers*).
**Passive:** **restricted** — the transitive passive is grammatical but wooden (*farmers were benefited by the subsidy*; Americans write *benefited from*), and the *from* frame has none: ✗ *the subsidy was benefited from*.

Gain an advantage from something.

1. Small clinics *benefited from* the change more than hospitals did.
2. The manuscript would *benefit from* another round of cuts.
3. She *benefited from* having a mentor early in her career.
4. Who actually *benefits from* a rule like that?
5. Coastal towns *benefit from* the tourism and pay for it in traffic.
6. Everyone *benefits from* clearer instructions.

**See also:** [recover from](#recover-from) &middot; [suffer from](#suffer-from) &middot; [result from](#result-in-result-from) &middot; [take care of](#take-care-of)

### blame for, blame on

**Pronunciation:** /ˈbleɪm fɔr/ &middot; *BLAYM for* &middot; weak /ˈbleɪm fɚ/
**Русский:** винить — blame smb for smth = blame smth on smb, аргументы меняются местами
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** *for* takes a noun phrase or a **gerund** (*blamed her for losing the file*) — **never a to-infinitive**. *on* takes the person or thing held responsible. The two frames carry the same meaning with the arguments swapped, so each preposition goes with a fixed slot: ✗ *blame the delay for the weather* says the opposite of what you mean.
**Pattern:** *blame* [someone] *for* [something] &middot; *blame* [something] *on* [someone] &middot; [someone] *be to blame for* [something] &middot; *be blamed for* [something]
**Contrast:** **Same meaning, mirrored frames:** *They blamed the pilot for the crash* = *They blamed the crash on the pilot*. Keep *blame* apart from *accuse* and *charge*: *blame* assigns fault and needs no wrongdoing at all (you can blame the rain), while *accuse someone **of*** alleges misconduct and *charge someone **with*** files it formally. The adjective frame keeps *for*: *the contractor is to blame for the leak*, never ✗ *to blame of*.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic; assigning fault, with no wrongdoing implied (you can blame the rain).
**Transitivity:** **transitive** in both mirrored frames — *blame the intern for the policy* and *blame the delay on the printer*; the object is obligatory, and swapping the slots reverses the sentence: ✗ *blame the delay for the weather*.
**Passive:** **yes** in both — *she was blamed for everything that went wrong that summer*, *the outage was blamed on deferred maintenance*.

Say that someone or something is responsible for something bad.

1. Don't *blame* the intern *for* a policy she didn't write.
2. He *blamed* the missed deadline *on* the printer.
3. The report *blames* the outage *on* deferred maintenance.
4. She was *blamed for* everything that went wrong that summer.
5. Nobody is *to blame for* an honest mistake.
6. They *blamed* each other *for* the whole mess.

**See also:** [accuse of](#accuse-of) &middot; [charge with](#charge-with-charge-for) &middot; [punish for](#punish-for) &middot; [forgive for](#forgive-for) &middot; [apologize for](#apologize-to-apologize-for)

### boast about

**Pronunciation:** /ˈboʊst əˌbaʊt/ &middot; *BOHST uh-bowt* &middot; weak /ˈboʊst əbət/
**Русский:** хвастаться чем-то — говорить о своём с излишней гордостью
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** somewhat formal; *brag about* is the everyday word
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*boasted about winning*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *boasted about to win*). A clause takes no preposition: *boasted that he had won*.
**Pattern:** *boast about* / *of* [noun phrase / verb-ing] &middot; *boast that* [clause] &middot; [a place or thing] *boast* [a feature] — no preposition
**Contrast:** **Two senses split on the preposition.** With *about* or *of*, *boast* means brag, and it is unflattering. With a direct object and no preposition it means possess something worth showing off, and it is flattering — *the hotel boasts three restaurants* is advertising copy, not a criticism of the hotel. *Boast of* is a shade more formal than *boast about*; both are correct. In speech Americans overwhelmingly say *brag about*.
**Verb type:** **both** — action in the bragging sense (*he boasts about his commute*), **stative** in the possession sense, where a place or product is the subject: *the lodge boasts a wraparound porch*.
**Transitivity:** **both** — intransitive with *about* or *of*, and with a that-clause (*boasted that he had won*); transitive with no preposition in the possession sense.
**Passive:** **no** in either sense — the bragging frame is prepositional and awkward to promote (✗ *the award was never boasted about by her*), and the possession sense is stative, so it refuses outright: ✗ *a wraparound porch is boasted by the lodge*.

Talk with too much pride about something you have or have done.

1. He *boasts about* his commute the way other people boast about their kids.
2. She never *boasted about* the award; we found out from the newsletter.
3. They *boast of* a hundred percent placement rate, which is technically true.
4. *Boasting about* how little you sleep is not the flex you think it is.
5. The company *boasted about* the acquisition for a year and then wrote it off.
6. The lodge *boasts* a wraparound porch and no cell service at all.

**See also:** [complain about](#complain-to-complain-about) &middot; [admit to](#admit-to) &middot; [congratulate on](#congratulate-on) &middot; [talk about](#talk-to-talk-with-talk-about)

### borrow from

**Pronunciation:** /ˈbɑroʊ frʌm/ &middot; *BAR-oh from* &middot; weak /ˈbɑroʊ frəm/
**Русский:** занимать, брать взаймы у — брать на время с возвратом
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase only — **no gerund, no clause**. The thing borrowed is a direct object and the source takes *from*: *borrow a ladder from Ken*. ✗ *borrow of* is archaic and ✗ *borrow to someone* reverses the direction entirely.
**Pattern:** *borrow* [something] &middot; *borrow* [something] *from* [source] &middot; *borrow* [an idea, a word, a style] *from* [a source] &middot; *borrow against* [an asset]
**Contrast:** ***Borrow* and *lend* point in opposite directions and are constantly swapped.** You *borrow from* someone; they *lend to* you. ✗ *Can you borrow me twenty dollars?* is nonstandard in American English — the sentence you want is *Can you lend me twenty dollars?* or *Can I borrow twenty dollars from you?* Note that *borrow from* also covers influence (*the score borrows heavily from Copland*), and that *borrow against* a house or a policy means take a loan secured by it.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic; a taking with a return understood, literal or figurative (*the score borrows heavily from Copland*).
**Transitivity:** **transitive** — the thing borrowed is the direct object and the source takes *from*: *borrow a ladder from Ken*. The object can be left implicit only where money is understood (*borrowing from family instead of banks*), and the direction is fixed: ✗ *Can you borrow me twenty dollars?* (say *lend*).
**Passive:** **yes** — *the phrase was borrowed from an essay she'd read in college*, *the table was borrowed from the church next door*.

Take something temporarily, intending to give it back.

1. I *borrowed* a folding table *from* the church next door.
2. She *borrowed* the phrase *from* an essay she'd read in college.
3. Small businesses are *borrowing from* family instead of banks.
4. Can I *borrow* your charger?
5. The design *borrows from* Shaker furniture without copying it.
6. He *borrowed against* the equity in the house to cover tuition.

**See also:** [provide with](#provide-with-provide-for) &middot; [escape from](#escape-from) &middot; [learn from](#learn-about-learn-from) &middot; [from](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#from)

## C

### care about, care for

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkɛr əˌbaʊt/ &middot; *KAIR uh-bowt* &middot; weak /ˈkɛr əbət/
**Русский:** care about считать важным; care for ухаживать за; not care for не любить
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** *about* takes a noun phrase, a **gerund** (*cares about winning*), or a wh-clause (*cares about what people think*) — never a to-infinitive. *for* takes a noun phrase only. *Care* also takes a bare to-infinitive with no preposition (*care to join us?*), and a that-clause in the negative (*I don't care that it's raining*).
**Pattern:** *care about* [person / thing / issue] &middot; *care for* [someone — tend] &middot; *care for* [something — like, usually negative] &middot; *would you care for* [a thing] &middot; *care to* [verb] &middot; *take care of* [someone / something] &middot; *not care* (alone)
**Contrast:** **Four patterns, four meanings, and they are not interchangeable.** *Care **about*** = think it matters (*she cares about the outcome*). *Care **for*** = look after (*he cared for his mother for six years*) or, chiefly in the negative and in offers, like (*I don't care for oysters*; *Would you care for coffee?*). *Care **to** do* = be willing. *Take care **of*** = handle or look after, and is what Americans actually say for the tending sense. Note the idiom: *I couldn't care less* is the logical form, and the widespread American *I could care less* means exactly the same thing despite saying the opposite.
**Verb type:** **both** — **stative** in *care about* (✗ *I am caring about the deadline*) and in *care for* meaning like (*I don't care for jazz*); action in *care for* meaning tend: *she is caring for her father at home*.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** — no direct object (✗ *care the outcome*); complements are prepositional, a to-infinitive (*care to explain?*), or a negative that-clause (*I don't care that it's raining*).
**Passive:** **restricted** — the tending sense passivizes well (*patients cared for at home do better*, *she was cared for by her daughter*); the two stative senses do not: ✗ *the deadline is not cared about by anyone*, ✗ *jazz is not cared for by him*.

Think something matters, or look after someone — depending entirely on the preposition.

| Pattern | What it means | Example |
|---|---|---|
| *care **about*** [thing] | think it matters | *She cares about accuracy.* |
| *care **for*** [person] | tend, nurse, look after | *He cared for her after the surgery.* |
| *care **for*** [thing] | like — mostly in the negative | *I don't care for jazz.* |
| *Would you care **for*** [thing]? | polite offer | *Would you care for dessert?* |
| *care **to** do* | be willing — **infinitive** | *Care to explain?* |
| *take care **of*** [thing] | handle it, look after it | *I'll take care of the check.* |

1. Nobody on that committee *cares about* the deadline.
2. She *cared for* her father at home until the last month.
3. I don't *care for* his tone.
4. He *cares about* getting it right more than getting it done.
5. Would you *care for* another slice?
6. They *care about* what happens to the building after they sell it.
7. *Take care of* the paperwork and I'll handle the movers.

**See also:** [take care of](#take-care-of) &middot; [look after](#look-at-look-for-look-after-look-into) &middot; [worry about](#worry-about) &middot; [think about](#think-about-think-of)

### center on, center around

**Pronunciation:** /ˈsɛntər ɑn/ &middot; *SEN-tur on* &middot; weak /ˈsɛntər ən/
**Русский:** быть посвящённым чему-то, вращаться вокруг — иметь главной темой
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral; *center on* is the safe choice in edited prose, *center around* the one a copy editor may query
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*the argument centered on raising the cap*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *centered on raise the cap*) and **never a that-clause** (✗ *centered on that costs were rising*), though a **wh-clause is fine**: *the hearing centered on what the city knew*. The participial form is where most Americans meet it: *a novel centered on one summer*.
**Pattern:** [a discussion / a story] *center on* [a topic] &middot; *center around* [a topic] &middot; *be centered on* / *centered around* [something] &middot; *center* [something] *on* [something] — transitive, position it
**Contrast:** ***Center around* is the famous usage quarrel.*** The objection is geometric: a center is a point, and nothing can be around a point — so the purist demands *center on* or *revolve around*. The defense is that the phrase has been in respectable American prose since the 1860s and that *center* here means a cluster, not a dot. Practical advice: in a report, a brief, or a paper, write *center on* and lose nothing; in speech, *center around* passes unnoticed. Two neighbors do related work — *focus on* narrows to one thing, *revolve around* names what everything else moves in relation to.
**Verb type:** **stative** in the pattern that matters here — it reports what a thing is about, not something happening: *her research centers on migration*, and the progressive is rare. The positioning verb (*center the title*) is action.
**Transitivity:** **both** — intransitive with *on* or *around* in the subject-matter sense (✗ *centered that costs were rising*, though a wh-clause is fine); transitive in *center the image on the page* and *center your efforts on one district*.
**Passive:** **yes**, and the participle is the commonest form of all — *a quiet novel centered on one family and one street*, *the questions were centered on the timing of the layoffs*.

Have something as its main subject or its main point.

1. The debate *centered on* whether the district could afford both schools.
2. Her research *centers on* migration between the wars.
3. The whole weekend *centered around* the wedding rehearsal.
4. The complaint *centers on* two paragraphs in the disclosure.
5. Most of the questions *centered on* the timing of the layoffs.
6. It is a quiet novel *centered on* one family and one street.

**See also:** [concentrate on](#concentrate-on) &middot; [comment on](#comment-on) &middot; [depend on](#depend-on) &middot; [bear on](14-phrasal-b.md#bear-on) &middot; [on](../../06-prepositions/catalog/01-core-in-on-at.md#on)

### charge with, charge for

**Pronunciation:** /ˈtʃɑrdʒ wɪθ/ &middot; *CHARJ with* &middot; weak /ˈtʃɑrdʒ wəθ/
**Русский:** обвинять в, поручать (with); брать плату за (for)
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** *charge with* is legal or formal; *charge for* is everyday
**Complement:** *with* takes a noun phrase or a **gerund** (*charged with defrauding investors*) — **never a to-infinitive**. *for* takes a noun phrase or a gerund naming the service billed (*charged us for parking*). Neither takes a clause.
**Pattern:** *charge* [someone] *with* [an offense / verb-ing] &middot; *be charged with* [a duty] &middot; *charge* [someone] *for* [a service] &middot; *charge* [an amount] *for* [a thing] &middot; *charge* [something] *to* [an account] &middot; *charge at* [someone]
**Contrast:** **Three prepositions, three unrelated meanings.** *Charge **with*** files an accusation (*charged with arson*) or assigns a responsibility (*the panel is charged with reviewing appeals* — note the *-ing*). *Charge **for*** bills money. *Charge **to*** puts it on an account. And *charge **at*** is a bull. Against its neighbors: *accuse **of*** is what a person does, *charge **with*** is what a prosecutor does, *blame **for*** assigns fault with no proceeding at all. In American criminal usage the person is *charged with* the crime, never ✗ *charged of* or ✗ *charged for* it — *charged for* would mean billed.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic in all three prepositional senses: accusing, assigning a duty, and billing.
**Transitivity:** **transitive** — the person is a direct object in *charge her with arson* and *charge us for parking*, and the amount can fill a second slot (*they charge twelve dollars for delivery*); *how much do they charge for delivery?* leaves the payer unstated.
**Passive:** **yes**, and the passive is the standard legal frame — *he was charged with two counts of wire fraud*, *the committee is charged with recommending a candidate*. Note ✗ *charged of* and ✗ *charged for* a crime (that would mean billed).

Accuse formally, assign a duty, or bill money.

1. He was *charged with* two counts of wire fraud.
2. The clinic *charged* us *for* a visit we never made.
3. The committee is *charged with* recommending a candidate by June.
4. How much do they *charge for* delivery?
5. She *charged* the whole dinner *to* the department.
6. Prosecutors declined to *charge* anyone *with* a felony.

**See also:** [accuse of](#accuse-of) &middot; [blame for](#blame-for-blame-on) &middot; [pay for](19-phrasal-m-r.md#pay-for) &middot; [suspect of](#suspect-of) &middot; [with](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#with)

### cheat on

**Pronunciation:** /ˈtʃit ɑn/ &middot; *CHEET on* &middot; weak /ˈtʃit ən/
**Русский:** изменять (партнёру); списывать на (экзамене) — жульничать ради выгоды
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to informal
**Complement:** noun phrase only — no gerund, no clause. *On* takes either the test or the partner; *at* takes the game. Bare transitive *cheat* takes the victim (*cheat a customer*), and *cheat someone out of something* names what was taken.
**Pattern:** *cheat on* [a test, an exam, a tax return] &middot; *cheat on* [a spouse or partner] &middot; *cheat at* [a game] &middot; *cheat* [someone] &middot; *cheat* [someone] *out of* [something]
**Contrast:** **American English says *cheat on a test*; British English says *cheat in an exam*.** The preposition splits by the kind of activity: *on* for a test or a tax return, *at* for a game of cards or golf. With a person as object of *on*, the meaning is infidelity — *he cheated on her* — which is why *cheat on the exam* and *cheat on his wife* share a preposition and nothing else. Transitive *cheat* means defraud, and *cheat someone out of* names the loss (*cheated out of a pension*).
**Verb type:** action — dynamic in every sense, from a midterm to a tax return to a marriage.
**Transitivity:** **both** — intransitive with *on* (a test, a return, a partner) or *at* (a game); transitive when the victim is the object: *cheat a customer*, *cheat him out of six months' commission*.
**Passive:** **yes** — the transitive is straightforward (*he was cheated out of a pension*), and the infidelity sense has a fully idiomatic prepositional passive: *she found out she'd been cheated on for a year*. ✗ *The midterm was cheated on* is not idiomatic.

Break the rules to gain an advantage — on a test, at a game, or on a partner.

1. Two students were caught *cheating on* the midterm.
2. He *cheats at* cards and thinks nobody notices.
3. She found out he'd been *cheating on* her for a year.
4. Nearly a fifth of filers admit to *cheating on* their taxes.
5. The proctor rearranged the room so nobody could *cheat on* the final.
6. They *cheated* him *out of* six months' commission.

**See also:** [confess to](#confess-to) &middot; [accuse of](#accuse-of) &middot; [punish for](#punish-for) &middot; [suspect of](#suspect-of)

### check up on

**Pronunciation:** /ˈtʃɛk ˈʌp ɑn/ &middot; *chek UP on* &middot; weak /tʃɛk ʌp ən/
**Русский:** проверять, как дела у кого-то — наведываться, чтобы убедиться; разг.
**Type:** verb + adverb + preposition &middot; **Register:** informal to neutral; everyday American speech
**Complement:** noun phrase or pronoun naming a **person or a situation**, or a **wh-clause** (*check up on how she's settling in*) — **never a to-infinitive** and **never a that-clause**. The three words are frozen in order and the object always follows all of them: *check up on him*, never ✗ *check him up on*.
**Pattern:** *check up on* [someone] &middot; *check up on* [something] &middot; *check on* [someone] — the two-word variant, slightly more neutral &middot; *have a checkup* — the unrelated noun
**Contrast:** **The object test separates the two constructions.** In *check out a book*, *out* is a particle and the object can move — *check the book out*, *check it out* — but in *check up on the tenant* nothing can move: ✗ *check the tenant up on*, which is what marks *on* here as a fixed preposition. In meaning, *check up on* implies a little suspicion or supervision (*he's checking up on me*), while *check on* is neutral concern (*check on the roast*), and *check in on* is a friendly short visit (*check in on your grandmother*).
**Verb type:** action — dynamic; a visit or an inquiry, often repeated (*her manager keeps checking up on her*).
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** verb plus adverb plus preposition, frozen in that order — the object follows all three words and can never move: *check up on him*, ✗ *check him up on*.
**Passive:** **yes**, and the progressive passive is the everyday form — *she could tell she was being checked up on*. A full agentive passive (*the numbers were never checked up on until the audit*) is fine but less common.

Find out how someone or something is doing, often to make sure nothing is wrong.

1. Would you *check up on* the dog while we're at the game?
2. Her manager keeps *checking up on* her, which is why she is job-hunting.
3. The state occasionally *checks up on* licensed facilities without warning.
4. I called the pharmacy to *check up on* the refill.
5. He drives past the rental every few weeks to *check up on* the place.
6. Nobody *checked up on* the numbers until the audit.

**See also:** [look at, look for, look after, look into](#look-at-look-for-look-after-look-into) &middot; [cut down on](15-phrasal-c.md#cut-down-on) &middot; [ask about](#ask-for-ask-about) &middot; [on](../../06-prepositions/catalog/01-core-in-on-at.md#on)

### choose between

**Pronunciation:** /ˈtʃuz bɪˌtwin/ &middot; *CHOOZ bih-tween* &middot; weak /ˈtʃuz bɪtwən/
**Русский:** выбирать между — брать одно из противопоставленных вариантов
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** *between* takes two items joined by *and*, or a plural noun phrase (*choose between the two offers*) — the joiner must be *and*, never *or*: ✗ *choose between staying or going*. It also takes a **gerund** pair (*choose between staying and going*). *Choose* itself takes a direct object or a to-infinitive with no preposition at all: *choose a color*, *choose to stay*.
**Pattern:** *choose between* [A] *and* [B] &middot; *choose between* [plural noun phrase] &middot; *choose among* / *from* [a set of three or more] &middot; *choose* [something] &middot; *choose to* [verb]
**Contrast:** ***Between* is for options weighed one against another; *among* or *from* is for picking out of a pool.** *Choose between the two finalists* against *choose from a field of nine*. The old rule that *between* is only for two is too strict — *choose between the four candidates* is fine when they are being weighed against one another — but *choose among* remains the safer formal choice with a large set. The fixed error is ✗ *choose between X or Y*; the coordinator inside *between* is always *and*.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic; a decision made at a point.
**Transitivity:** **both** — transitive with a bare object or a to-infinitive (*choose a color*, *choose to stay*); intransitive with *between*, *among*, or *from*, where the joiner inside *between* is always *and*: ✗ *choose between staying or going*.
**Passive:** **yes** for the transitive — *a color was chosen that afternoon*. The prepositional frame has no passive: ✗ *the two offers were chosen between*.

Pick one of two or more options that are set against each other.

1. She had to *choose between* the fellowship *and* the job offer.
2. Nobody should have to *choose between* rent *and* insulin.
3. *Choose between* the two color schemes and we'll order the paper.
4. Voters are *choosing between* two very different visions of the city.
5. You can *choose from* a dozen plans, which is exactly the problem.
6. He *chose between* staying in Tulsa *and* starting over in Austin.

**See also:** [decide on](#decide-on-decide-against) &middot; [prefer to](#prefer-to) &middot; [compare to](#compare-to-compare-with) &middot; [vote for](#vote-for-vote-against) &middot; [between](../../06-prepositions/catalog/03-place-position.md#between)

### cite to

**Pronunciation:** /ˈsaɪt tu/ &middot; *SYTE too* &middot; weak /ˈsaɪt tə/
**Русский:** ссылаться на конкретное место источника — юр.; вне права предлог to не ставят
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal; **legal writing almost exclusively** — briefs, opinions, and law reviews (*counsel cited to Brown*, *cite to the record*). Outside law the *to* is not used, and an editor will strike it.
**Complement:** noun phrase naming an **authority, case, statute, page, or record** — **never a gerund**, **never a to-infinitive**, **never a clause**. The everyday construction is bare transitive: *cite three sources*, ✗ *cite to three sources* in ordinary prose.
**Pattern:** *cite to* [a case / a page / the record] &middot; *cite* [a source] — standard, no preposition &middot; *cite* [someone] *for* [an offense] — the ticket sense &middot; *cite* [someone] *as* [an authority]
**Contrast:** **Three constructions, three different jobs.** Bare *cite* takes the source as a direct object and is what every writer outside a courtroom uses. *Cite to* is a term of art in American legal writing, where it points the reader to a specific place in an authority. *Cite for* is enforcement — *cited for speeding*, *cited for a code violation* — and has nothing to do with quotation. Spelling traps come free with this one: *sight* is vision and *site* is a place.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic; an act of pointing a reader somewhere, or, in *cite for*, of writing a ticket.
**Transitivity:** **both** — bare transitive everywhere outside a courtroom (*cite three sources*, and ✗ *cite to three sources* in ordinary prose); intransitive with *to* in American legal writing (*cite to the record*).
**Passive:** **yes** for the transitive frames — *the article was cited eleven times*, *she was cited for speeding*. The legal *cite to* passivizes poorly: ✗ *the record was cited to by counsel*.

Point a reader to a source, especially to a particular page or authority.

1. The brief *cites to* three Ninth Circuit cases and one treatise.
2. Please *cite to* the page in the transcript, not to the exhibit.
3. The opinion *cites to* a statute that had already been repealed.
4. Local rules require counsel to *cite to* the record for every factual claim.
5. She *cited to* the deposition twice and never quoted it.
6. In a seminar paper you simply *cite* the article — ✗ *cite to* it sounds like a filing.

**See also:** [refer to](#refer-to) &middot; [allude to](#allude-to) &middot; [attribute to](#attribute-to) &middot; [to](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#to)

### collaborate with

**Pronunciation:** /kəˈlæbəreɪt wɪθ/ &middot; *kuh-LAB-uh-rayt with* &middot; weak /kəˈlæbəreɪt wəθ/
**Русский:** сотрудничать с — работать вместе над чем-то
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal; research, the arts, and business
**Complement:** noun phrase naming a **person or organization** — **never a gerund** (✗ *collaborated with writing the score*; the work takes *on*: *collaborated on the score*). **Never a to-infinitive** and **never a clause**. There is no direct-object frame: ✗ *collaborated the album*.
**Pattern:** *collaborate with* [someone] &middot; *collaborate with* [someone] *on* [a project] &middot; *collaborate in* [an effort] — formal &middot; *a collaboration with* [someone] — the noun keeps the preposition
**Contrast:** ***Collaborate with* and [cooperate with](#cooperate-with) are not synonyms.** Collaborating is making something together — a paper, a record, a design. Cooperating is not getting in the way: *the company cooperated with investigators* says it handed over documents, not that it helped write the report. And *collaborate* carries one sense cooperation does not: working with an occupying enemy, which is why *he collaborated with the regime* is an accusation. Note the two prepositions divide the roles — *with* a person, *on* a project — and cannot swap.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic; joint work carried on over time, and the progressive is natural (*our lab is collaborating with a hospital group*).
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** always — no direct object (✗ *collaborated the album*); the person takes *with*, the project takes *on*, and the two prepositions cannot swap.
**Passive:** **no** — objectless, so nothing can be promoted: ✗ *a younger songwriter was collaborated with*, ✗ *the paper was collaborated on by two economists*. Recast with *co-wrote* or *was written jointly*.

Work jointly with someone to produce something.

1. She *collaborated with* two other economists on the paper.
2. The studio wanted him to *collaborate with* a younger songwriter.
3. Our lab *collaborates with* a hospital group in Cleveland.
4. He refuses to *collaborate with* anyone he hasn't met in person.
5. The two agencies *collaborated with* each other for the first time in a decade.
6. Historians still argue about who *collaborated with* the occupiers and who had no choice.

**See also:** [cooperate with](#cooperate-with) &middot; [work with](#work-on-work-for-work-with) &middot; [consult with](#consult-with) &middot; [share with](#share-with) &middot; [with](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#with)

### comment on

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkɑmɛnt ɑn/ &middot; *KOM-ent on* &middot; weak /ˈkɑmɛnt ən/
**Русский:** комментировать что-то — высказываться по поводу чего-то
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase, **gerund**, or wh-clause (*commented on how quiet it was*) — **never a to-infinitive**. Before a full clause the preposition drops: *commented that the room was cold*, never ✗ *commented on that*.
**Pattern:** *comment on* [noun phrase / verb-ing / wh-clause] &middot; *comment that* [clause] &middot; *decline to comment on* [something] &middot; *no comment on* [something]
**Contrast:** *Comment **on*** is the standard American form; *comment **about*** is heard but is weaker in edited prose. The noun keeps *on* (*a comment on the draft*) and so does the fixed newsroom formula *declined to comment on the report*. Compare *remark on* (interchangeable, slightly more formal), *note* and *observe* (transitive, no preposition: *he noted the discrepancy*), and *criticize* (also transitive: *criticize the plan*, never ✗ *criticize on the plan*).
**Verb type:** action — dynamic; a speech act.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** with *on* — no direct object (✗ *comment the draft*), and the preposition disappears before a full clause: *commented that the room was cold*, ✗ *commented on that*.
**Passive:** **restricted** — *the length of the second act was widely commented on* is acceptable in writing, and impersonal *it was commented that…* occurs, but the active is far commoner in American prose.

Say something in reaction to a subject.

1. She declined to *comment on* the settlement.
2. Several reviewers *commented on* the length of the second act.
3. He *commented on* how little had changed in twenty years.
4. The mayor's office would not *comment on* the audit.
5. Feel free to *comment on* the draft in the margins.
6. Nobody *commented on* his new haircut, which disappointed him.

**See also:** [point to](#point-at-point-to) &middot; [complain about](#complain-to-complain-about) &middot; [talk about](#talk-to-talk-with-talk-about) &middot; [refer to](#refer-to) &middot; [on](../../06-prepositions/catalog/01-core-in-on-at.md#on)

### commit to

**Pronunciation:** /kəˈmɪt tu/ &middot; *kuh-MIT too* &middot; weak /kəˈmɪt tə/
**Русский:** обязываться, брать на себя обязательство — после to герундий
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*commit to hiring two more nurses*) — ***to* is a preposition here, not the infinitive marker**, so ✗ *commit to hire two more nurses* is an error. Never a that-clause after the preposition. The reflexive frame follows the same rule: *commit yourself to finishing it*. A separate transitive *commit* takes a bare object and no preposition at all: *commit a crime*, *commit funds*, *commit it to memory*.
**Pattern:** *commit to* [noun phrase] &middot; *commit to* [verb-ing] &middot; *commit* [oneself] *to* [noun phrase / verb-ing] &middot; *be committed to* [noun phrase / verb-ing] &middot; *commit* [a crime, an error, resources] — no preposition
**Contrast:** **Another member of the *look forward to* family, and the noun test settles it the same way:** you can say *commit to the plan*, so *to* is a preposition, so it has to be *commit to redrawing the plan*. Notice how far the transitive senses sit from this one — *commit a felony* and *commit a hundred thousand dollars* take no preposition, and *commit someone to* an institution is a third thing again. The participle is the form you meet most often, and the *-ing* after it is not optional: *a company committed to lowering emissions*, ✗ *committed to lower emissions*. Against its neighbors, *commit to* is firmer than *plan on* and softer than *promise to*, which takes the infinitive because *promise* has no preposition at all.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic; binding yourself at a moment. The participle *committed to* has drifted into an adjective: *a company committed to lowering emissions*.
**Transitivity:** **both** — intransitive with *to* (*commit to a date*), reflexive-transitive (*commit yourself to finishing it*), and plainly transitive in the unrelated senses: *commit a crime*, *commit funds*, *commit it to memory*.
**Passive:** **yes** in the transitive senses — *a felony was committed on the premises*, *a hundred thousand dollars has been committed to the project*. The *to* frame passivizes only as the adjectival participle, not as an event.

Bind yourself to a course of action.

1. The board *committed to* replacing every lead service line by 2032.
2. She won't *commit to* a date until the permits clear.
3. Both candidates *committed to* three debates.
4. He's *committed to* getting the certification this year.
5. Don't *commit to* anything before you've read the fine print.
6. The district has *committed to* smaller classes without saying how it will pay for them.

**See also:** [look forward to](#look-forward-to) &middot; [object to](#object-to) &middot; [dedicate to](#dedicate-to-devote-to) &middot; [plan on](#plan-on) &middot; [agree to](#agree-with-agree-to-agree-on) &middot; [to](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#to)

### communicate with

**Pronunciation:** /kəˈmjunəkeɪt wɪθ/ &middot; *kuh-MYOO-nuh-kayt with* &middot; weak /kəˈmjunəkeɪt wəθ/
**Русский:** общаться с; быть связанным с (о технике) — обмениваться информацией
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal; management and HR writing, technical documentation (*the sensor communicates with the hub*), and clinical notes
**Complement:** noun phrase naming a **person, group, or system** — **never a gerund** (✗ *communicate with telling them early*), **never a to-infinitive**, **never a clause**. The message itself is never the object of *with*: it is a direct object, and the audience takes *to*: *communicate the decision to staff*.
**Pattern:** *communicate with* [someone] &middot; *communicate* [something] *to* [someone] &middot; [two systems] *communicate with* [each other] &middot; *communication with* [someone] — the noun keeps the preposition
**Contrast:** ***With* is two-way; *to* is one-way.*** *She communicates with the field offices* describes an exchange; *she communicates the policy to the field offices* describes a transmission — and mixing them (✗ *communicate the policy with staff*) is the usual error. There is no bare transitive frame for the person: ✗ *communicate somebody*. In speech Americans mostly say *talk to* or *get in touch with* and reserve *communicate with* for the formal register, which is why *we are unable to communicate with the crew* sounds like an official statement, because it is one.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic; an exchange, human or mechanical (*the buoy communicates with a satellite twice an hour*).
**Transitivity:** **both** — intransitive with *with* for the other party (✗ *communicate somebody*), transitive with the message as object and the audience in *to*: *communicate the decision to staff*, and never ✗ *communicate the policy with staff*.
**Passive:** **yes** for the message frame — *the decision was communicated to staff on Friday*. The *with* frame is **restricted**: *the crew could not be communicated with for two days* is official-register only.

Exchange information with someone, or be connected to something.

1. The hospital could not *communicate with* the family for two days.
2. Sales and engineering barely *communicate with* each other.
3. The buoy *communicates with* a satellite twice an hour.
4. He *communicates with* his students almost entirely by email.
5. Air traffic control *communicated with* the aircraft until 3:14 a.m.
6. Two of the rooms *communicate with* the courtyard through French doors.

**See also:** [talk to, talk with, talk about](#talk-to-talk-with-talk-about) &middot; [consult with](#consult-with) &middot; [cooperate with](#cooperate-with) &middot; [share with](#share-with) &middot; [with](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#with)

### compare to, compare with

**Pronunciation:** /kəmˈpɛr tu/ &middot; *kum-PAIR too* &middot; weak /kəmˈpɛr tə/
**Русский:** сравнивать — with сопоставлять по признакам, to уподоблять одно другому
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral; the *to* / *with* distinction is formal-register only
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*compared moving to starting over*) — no to-infinitive, no clause. *Compare* also takes two objects joined by *and*: *compare the 2019 and 2024 figures*. The passive participle *compared to* / *compared with* works as a preposition in its own right (*compared with last year, sales are flat*).
**Pattern:** *compare* [A] *to* [B] &middot; *compare* [A] *with* [B] &middot; *compare* [A] *and* [B] &middot; [something] *compare to* / *with* [something — measure up] &middot; *compared to* / *with* [noun phrase] (sentence adverbial)
**Contrast:** **The textbook rule:** *compare **to*** likens two unlike things to make a point (*he compared the deficit to a leaky bucket*), while *compare **with*** sets two like things side by side to weigh them (*compare this quarter with last*). **In actual American usage the distinction is fading and *compared to* has taken over both jobs**, especially in speech; *compared with* survives in statistical and scientific writing, where the side-by-side sense matters. What is not optional: in the intransitive sense, *to* is standard — *nothing compares to a real vacation*.
**Verb type:** **both** — action in the transitive sense, a mental operation performed on two things; **stative** in the intransitive measure-up sense: *nothing compares to a real vacation*, ✗ *nothing is comparing to it*.
**Transitivity:** **both** — transitive with two slots (*compare this year's numbers with the same quarter in 2022*, or joined by *and*); intransitive in the measure-up sense, where *to* is standard.
**Passive:** **yes** for the transitive — *her early work was compared to Munro's, which she found irritating*. **No** for the measure-up sense: ✗ *a real vacation is compared to by nothing*.

Set two things side by side, or liken one to the other.

1. He *compared* the reorganization *to* rearranging deck chairs.
2. *Compare* this year's numbers *with* the same quarter in 2022.
3. Revenue rose four percent *compared with* the prior year.
4. Critics *compared* her early work *to* Munro's, which she found irritating.
5. Nothing *compares to* the first cold morning in October.
6. It's hard to *compare* a rural district *with* an urban one on test scores alone.

**See also:** [resemble](#resemble) &middot; [differ from](#differ-from) &middot; [prefer to](#prefer-to) &middot; [choose between](#choose-between) &middot; [compared to](../../06-prepositions/catalog/09-comparison-opposition.md#compared-to) &middot; [compared with](../../06-prepositions/catalog/09-comparison-opposition.md#compared-with)

### compensate for

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkɑmpənseɪt fɔr/ &middot; *KOM-pun-sayt for* &middot; weak /ˈkɑmpənseɪt fər/
**Русский:** компенсировать, возмещать — восполнять потерю или слабое место
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal; engineering, psychology, insurance, and payroll
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*compensate for arriving late*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *compensate for arrive late*) and **never a that-clause**. Two frames share the verb: *compensate for* [a loss] offsets it, and *compensate* [someone] *for* [something] pays them, with the person as a direct object.
**Pattern:** *compensate for* [a loss / a weakness] &middot; *compensate* [someone] *for* [something] &middot; *compensate for* [verb-ing] &middot; *overcompensate for* [something]
**Contrast:** **Two senses hang on the same preposition.** The offsetting sense — *thicker insulation compensates for the north exposure* — has no money in it at all; the paying sense always has a person as direct object — *compensate the homeowners for the damage*. The everyday equivalent of the first is *make up for*; the technical neighbors are *correct for* and *adjust for*, which remove a distortion from a measurement rather than balancing a deficiency. And *offset* does the same work with no preposition: *offset the loss*, ✗ *offset for the loss*.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic in both senses, offsetting a deficiency and paying for a loss.
**Transitivity:** **both** — intransitive with *for* when something offsets something else (*extra rehearsal compensated for the missing week*); transitive with the person paid as direct object (*compensate the homeowners for the damage*).
**Passive:** **yes** — the paying sense is idiomatic in the passive (*passengers were compensated for the canceled leg*), and technical writing passivizes the offsetting sense too: *temperature drift is compensated for automatically*.

Balance out a loss or a weakness, or pay someone for one.

1. Extra rehearsal *compensated for* the missing week.
2. The airline *compensated* passengers *for* the canceled leg with vouchers.
3. She *compensates for* a weak serve with a very good net game.
4. Nothing *compensates for* losing that much institutional memory at once.
5. The system *compensates for* temperature drift automatically.
6. He tried to *compensate for* being new by staying late every night.

**See also:** [pay for](19-phrasal-m-r.md#pay-for) &middot; [correct for](#correct-for) &middot; [substitute for](#substitute-for) &middot; [allow for](#allow-for) &middot; [for](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#for)

### compete with, compete against, compete for

**Pronunciation:** /kəmˈpit wɪθ/ &middot; *kum-PEET with* &middot; weak /kəmˈpit wəθ/
**Русский:** соперничать — with/against с кем-то, for за что-то
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** *with* and *against* take a rival. *for* takes the prize. *in* takes the event (*compete in the 400*). **No gerund and no clause** follows any of them: ✗ *compete for winning* — say *compete for the title*. *Compete* is intransitive and never takes a bare object: ✗ *compete the other firms*.
**Pattern:** *compete with* / *against* [a rival] &middot; *compete for* [a prize, a contract, attention] &middot; *compete in* [an event, a market] &middot; *compete with* [a rival] *for* [a prize]
**Contrast:** ***Compete with* and *compete against* are genuinely interchangeable** for the rival, with *against* a shade more adversarial and more common in sports. Keep the slots straight: the rival takes *with* or *against*, the prize takes *for*, the arena takes *in* — *three firms competing with each other for one contract in a shrinking market*. Note that *compete with* has a second, non-adversarial sense meaning match or rival in quality: *no supermarket tomato competes with one out of the garden*.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic; also a quieter comparative sense meaning match in quality (*nothing in a jar competes with fresh basil*), which is closer to stative.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** always — never a bare object: ✗ *compete the other firms*. The rival takes *with* or *against*, the prize takes *for*, the arena takes *in*.
**Passive:** **no** — there is no object to promote: ✗ *the big houses can't be competed with on advances*, ✗ *the airport contract is being competed for*. Recast in the active or use *rival*, which is transitive.

Try to win something that someone else also wants.

1. Small presses can't *compete with* the big houses on advances.
2. She *competed against* her own sister in the semifinal.
3. Four firms are *competing for* the airport contract.
4. He *competes in* three or four triathlons a year.
5. Streaming services are *competing for* the same twelve hours of anyone's week.
6. Nothing in a jar *competes with* fresh basil.

**See also:** [argue with](#argue-with-argue-about-argue-over) &middot; [differ from](#differ-from) &middot; [apply for](#apply-for-apply-to) &middot; [qualify for](#qualify-for) &middot; [against](../../06-prepositions/catalog/09-comparison-opposition.md#against)

### complain to, complain about

**Pronunciation:** /kəmˈpleɪn əˌbaʊt/ &middot; *kum-PLAYN uh-bowt* &middot; weak /kəmˈpleɪn əbət/
**Русский:** жаловаться — to кому-то, about на что-то
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** *to* takes the person or office hearing it. *about* takes a noun phrase, a **gerund** (*complained about having to reapply*), or a wh-clause. *of* takes a symptom, in medical and formal use (*complained of dizziness*). **None takes a to-infinitive.** A full clause drops the preposition: *complained that the room was cold*.
**Pattern:** *complain to* [person / office] &middot; *complain about* [noun phrase / verb-ing] &middot; *complain to* [person] *about* [thing] &middot; *complain of* [a symptom] &middot; *complain that* [clause]
**Contrast:** **The person and the grievance take different prepositions and cannot share one:** ✗ *complain to the noise*, ✗ *complain about the landlord* when you mean you told the landlord. *Complain **of*** is the narrow medical and legal use — *the patient complained of chest pain* — and sounds odd elsewhere. Compare *object to*, which registers formal opposition, and *criticize*, which is transitive and takes no preposition at all.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic; a speech act, and habitually repeated in the commonest uses (*everyone complains about the parking*).
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** — no direct object (✗ *complain the noise*); the hearer takes *to*, the grievance *about*, a symptom *of*, and a full clause takes nothing: *complained that the room was cold*.
**Passive:** **restricted** — *the elevator has been complained about for months* passes in writing; the hearer cannot be promoted comfortably: ✗ *the building manager was complained to*.

Say that something is wrong, unsatisfactory, or unfair.

1. She *complained to* the building manager *about* the elevator.
2. Everyone *complains about* the parking and nobody goes to the meetings.
3. He *complained of* headaches for a week before anyone made him see a doctor.
4. Two neighbors *complained to* the city.
5. They *complained about* having to submit the same form twice.
6. You can't *complain about* a system you never voted in.

**See also:** [object to](#object-to) &middot; [argue with](#argue-with-argue-about-argue-over) &middot; [boast about](#boast-about) &middot; [worry about](#worry-about) &middot; [apologize to](#apologize-to-apologize-for)

### comply with

**Pronunciation:** /kəmˈplaɪ wɪθ/ &middot; *kum-PLY with* &middot; weak /kəmˈplaɪ wəθ/
**Русский:** соблюдать, выполнять — делать то, чего требует правило или предписание; офиц.
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal — regulatory, legal, and corporate
**Complement:** noun phrase only, naming a rule, order, request, or standard. **No gerund** in normal use (✗ *comply with reporting quarterly* — write *comply with the quarterly reporting requirement*) and **no clause**. *Comply* is intransitive and never takes a bare object: ✗ *comply the order*.
**Pattern:** *comply with* [a rule, an order, a request, a standard] &middot; *fail to comply with* [something] &middot; *in compliance with* [something]
**Contrast:** ***Comply to* and ✗ *comply of* are errors** — the preposition is fixed at *with*. Its close neighbors each keep a different one: *conform **to*** a standard, *adhere **to*** a policy, *abide **by*** a rule, *obey* an order (transitive, no preposition), and *follow* instructions (also transitive). *Comply with* is heavier and more institutional than any of them; in ordinary speech an American says *follow the rules*.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic; doing what a rule requires, often as a continuing state of affairs (*the device complies with Part 15*).
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** — never a bare object (✗ *comply the order*), and ✗ *comply to*, ✗ *comply of* are errors; the verb stands alone only elliptically: *he refused to comply*.
**Passive:** **restricted** — the prepositional passive is standard in regulatory prose (*the reporting requirement was complied with in full*) but stiff anywhere else; ordinary speech says *the rules were followed*.

Do what a rule, an order, or a request requires.

1. The lab failed to *comply with* federal handling standards.
2. All vendors must *comply with* the new security requirements by October.
3. The company *complied with* the subpoena within a week.
4. Buildings put up before 1978 don't *comply with* the current code.
5. He was fired for refusing to *comply with* a direct order.
6. The device *complies with* Part 15 of the FCC rules.

**See also:** [subscribe to](#subscribe-to) &middot; [agree to](#agree-with-agree-to-agree-on) &middot; [object to](#object-to) &middot; [in compliance with](../../06-prepositions/catalog/11-complex-multiword.md#in-compliance-with) &middot; [in accordance with](../../06-prepositions/catalog/11-complex-multiword.md#in-accordance-with)

### concentrate on

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkɑnsənˌtreɪt ɑn/ &middot; *KON-sun-trayt on* &middot; weak /ˈkɑnsənˌtreɪt ən/
**Русский:** сосредоточиваться на — отдавать всё внимание одному делу
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase, **gerund** (*concentrate on finishing the draft*), or wh-clause — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *concentrate on finish the draft*, ✗ *concentrate on to finish it*).
**Pattern:** *concentrate on* [noun phrase / verb-ing / wh-clause] &middot; *concentrate* (alone) &middot; *be concentrated in* [a place — distribution] &middot; *concentrate* [efforts, resources] *on* [something]
**Contrast:** ***Concentrate in* is a different verb.** With *on*, it means direct your attention or effort — *concentrate on the road*. With *in*, and usually in the passive, it means be densely gathered somewhere: *most of the state's jobs are concentrated in three counties*. *Focus on* is exactly interchangeable with the *on* sense and is the more common word in American business speech; *center on* is the same again — and note that careful American usage prefers *center on*, since *center around* is widely criticized as illogical.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic; directing attention or effort, and freely progressive (*the district is concentrating on third-grade reading*).
**Transitivity:** **both** — intransitive with *on* or alone (*just concentrate*); transitive when effort or resources are the object: *concentrate its resources on two counties*.
**Passive:** **yes** — chiefly in the *in* sense of distribution, which is normally passive: *ninety percent of the growth is concentrated in the metro area*. The attention sense passivizes only with a thing as object: *the effort was concentrated on reading scores*.

Give your full attention or effort to one thing.

1. *Concentrate on* the first two chapters and leave the rest.
2. It's impossible to *concentrate on* anything with that leaf blower going.
3. The district is *concentrating on* third-grade reading scores.
4. She *concentrated on* getting the wiring right before touching the drywall.
5. Let's *concentrate on* what we can actually change this quarter.
6. Ninety percent of the growth is *concentrated in* the metro area.

**See also:** [work on](#work-on-work-for-work-with) &middot; [insist on](#insist-on) &middot; [think about](#think-about-think-of) &middot; [on](../../06-prepositions/catalog/01-core-in-on-at.md#on)

### confer on

**Pronunciation:** /kənˈfɜr ɑn/ &middot; *kun-FUR on* &middot; weak /kənˈfɜr ən/
**Русский:** присуждать, наделять кого-то (правом, званием, полномочием); юр./офиц.
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal; statutes, charters, and ceremonies — *the Act confers jurisdiction on the district courts*, *the university conferred an honorary degree on her*
**Complement:** the thing granted is a **direct object** — a right, a power, an honor, a benefit — and *on* takes the **recipient**. **Never a gerund**, **never a to-infinitive**, **never a clause**. Both slots are required: ✗ *conferred on the courts* with nothing granted is incomplete.
**Pattern:** *confer* [a right / a power / a degree] *on* [someone] &middot; *be conferred on* [someone] &middot; *confer with* [someone] — a different verb, meaning consult &middot; *confer about* [a matter]
**Variants:** *confer upon* — identical in meaning and the older form; standard in statutes, court opinions, and ceremonial citations, where *upon* still outnumbers *on*
**Contrast:** ***Confer on* and *confer with* are two different verbs wearing one spelling.*** *Confer on* is transitive and means grant from a position of authority; *confer with* takes no direct object and means consult — *may I confer with my client?* You cannot mix them: ✗ *conferred the degree with her*, ✗ *conferred on my client about the offer*. Nearby, *bestow on* is the ceremonial synonym, *grant to* is the everyday one (*granted the license to the applicant*), and *vest in* is the drafting term for placing a power somewhere permanently.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic; a grant made from a position of authority, often ceremonial. The homonymous *confer with* (consult) is a different verb.
**Transitivity:** **transitive** in this pattern — the right, power, or honor is an obligatory direct object and the recipient takes *on*: ✗ *conferred on the courts* with nothing granted is incomplete. *Confer with* takes no object at all.
**Passive:** **yes**, and the passive is the ceremonial norm — *an honorary doctorate was conferred on her in 1998*, *the medal is conferred upon fewer than a dozen people a year*.

Grant a right, a power, or an honor to someone.

1. The charter *confers* broad zoning authority *on* the county board.
2. An honorary doctorate was *conferred on* her in 1998.
3. Nothing in the lease *confers* a right of first refusal *on* the tenant.
4. The medal is *conferred upon* fewer than a dozen people a year.
5. Membership *confers* borrowing privileges *on* the whole household.
6. The statute *conferred* jurisdiction *on* a court that did not yet exist.

**See also:** [credit with](#credit-with) &middot; [provide with, provide for](#provide-with-provide-for) &middot; [attribute to](#attribute-to) &middot; [consult with](#consult-with) &middot; [on](../../06-prepositions/catalog/01-core-in-on-at.md#on)

### confess to

**Pronunciation:** /kənˈfɛs tu/ &middot; *kun-FES too* &middot; weak /kənˈfɛs tə/
**Русский:** признаваться в — открыто говорить о своей вине; после to герундий
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*confessed to taking the money*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *confessed to take the money*). The preposition is optional before a bare object: *confess a crime*, *confess his sins*. Before a clause it disappears: *confessed that he had lied*.
**Pattern:** *confess to* [a crime / verb-ing] &middot; *confess* [a sin, a crime] &middot; *confess that* [clause] &middot; *confess to* [a priest, the police] &middot; *confess to* [a small weakness]
**Contrast:** ***Confess to* has two objects it can take and you must read which one is meant:** *confess to the police* names the hearer, *confess to the burglary* names the deed. Against *admit to*, *confess* is heavier — it implies guilt and often a hearer — which is why *I admit to liking bad television* is light and *I confess to liking bad television* is arch. *Own up to* is the informal American equivalent, and it too takes a gerund: *own up to breaking it*.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic; a speech act, heavier than *admit* and usually implying guilt.
**Transitivity:** **both** — intransitive with *to*, which can take either the hearer (*confess to the police*) or the deed (*confess to the arson*); transitive with the preposition dropped (*confess a crime*, *confess his sins*), and a that-clause takes none.
**Passive:** **restricted** — drop the preposition and the transitive passivizes (*the crime was never confessed*); the prepositional frame does not travel: ✗ *the arson was confessed to after nine hours*.

Say openly that you did something wrong.

1. He *confessed to* the arson after nine hours of questioning.
2. She *confessed to* having read his email.
3. I'll *confess to* a certain fondness for the old logo.
4. Two of them *confessed to* the police and one never said a word.
5. He *confessed that* he had never actually read the report.
6. Nobody *confessed to* moving the thermostat.

**See also:** [admit to](#admit-to) &middot; [accuse of](#accuse-of) &middot; [look forward to](#look-forward-to) &middot; [apologize for](#apologize-to-apologize-for) &middot; [boast about](#boast-about) &middot; [suspect of](#suspect-of)

### conform to, conform with

**Pronunciation:** /kənˈfɔrm tu/ &middot; *kun-FORM too* &middot; weak /kənˈfɔrm tə/
**Русский:** соответствовать чему-то — отвечать стандарту, норме или образцу
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal and technical; building codes, specifications, standards, and social commentary
**Complement:** noun phrase naming a **standard, code, specification, type, or expectation**. A gerund is grammatical but rare (*conform to using the approved fasteners*); **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *conform to use the approved fasteners*) and **never a that-clause**. The verb also stands alone in the social sense, with no complement at all: *the pressure to conform*. There is no direct-object frame: ✗ *conform the code* (though *conform something to something* — bring it into line — exists in legal drafting).
**Pattern:** [a thing] *conform to* [a standard] &middot; [a statement] *conform with* [another] &middot; *conform* — no complement, social sense &middot; *conform* [a document] *to* [a requirement] — formal transitive
**Variants:** *conform with* — interchangeable in most contexts and slightly more formal; American usage prefers *to* for matching a standard (*conforms to code*) and leaves *with* for two things that agree with each other (*the summary conforms with the audited figures*)
