**Contrast:** **Things conform; people comply.** [Comply with](#comply-with) is what a person, a company, or an agency does about a requirement someone imposes — *the plant complies with the permit* — while *conform to* says a thing matches a specification: *the wiring conforms to code*. Beside them, [adhere to](#adhere-to) is following a rule steadily over time and [abide by](#abide-by) is accepting a decision already made. Note the third life of the word, in social criticism, where *conform* needs no preposition and always sounds faintly like a criticism.
**Verb type:** **both** — **stative** where a thing matches a specification (*the wiring conforms to code*, ✗ *is conforming to code*); action in the social sense, where a person is the subject: *he never much cared about conforming*.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** in normal use — no direct object (✗ *conform the code*) and the complement drops entirely in the social sense (*the pressure to conform*). Legal drafting keeps a transitive *conform a document to a statute*.
**Passive:** **no** for the stative matching sense — ✗ *current code is not conformed to by the basement outlets*. **Restricted** otherwise: the drafting transitive gives *the revised bylaws were conformed to the new state statute*.

Match a standard, a rule, or a type.

1. None of the outlets in the basement *conform to* current code.
2. The proposal must *conform to* the format described in the RFP.
3. Her account *conforms with* what the two other witnesses said.
4. Products that fail to *conform to* the standard cannot carry the label.
5. He never much cared about *conforming* to what the neighbors expected.
6. The revised bylaws were *conformed to* the new state statute.

**See also:** [comply with](#comply-with) &middot; [adhere to](#adhere-to) &middot; [abide by](#abide-by) &middot; [correspond to, correspond with](#correspond-to-correspond-with) &middot; [to](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#to)

### congratulate on

**Pronunciation:** /kənˈɡrætʃəˌleɪt ɑn/ &middot; *kun-GRACH-uh-layt on* &middot; weak /kənˈɡrætʃəˌleɪt ən/
**Русский:** поздравлять с — выражать радость по поводу чужого успеха
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*congratulated her on winning*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *congratulated her on to win*) and never a clause. The person is a direct object and never takes a preposition: ✗ *congratulate to her*.
**Pattern:** *congratulate* [someone] *on* [noun phrase / verb-ing] &middot; *congratulate* [someone] &middot; *congratulate* [oneself] *on* [something] &middot; *Congratulations on* [noun phrase / verb-ing]
**Contrast:** ***Congratulate for* is nonstandard** in careful American English, though it is common in speech; the fixed preposition is *on*, and the greeting keeps it too — *Congratulations on the baby*, ✗ *Congratulations for the baby*. British usage also allows *congratulate upon*, which sounds stiff in the U.S. Compare *thank someone **for*** something and *praise someone **for*** something, which take *for* and never *on* — three near-synonyms with three fixed and different prepositions.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic, a speech act performed in the saying; progressive is normal: *they're congratulating themselves a little early*.
**Transitivity:** **transitive** — the person is a direct object and never takes a preposition (✗ *congratulate to her*); *on* adds the achievement. The object is obligatory: ✗ *he congratulated on the promotion*.
**Passive:** **yes**, and the passive is very common: *she was congratulated on the promotion by everyone in the office*, *he was congratulated on a season nobody expected*.

Tell someone you are pleased about their good news or achievement.

1. I want to *congratulate* you *on* the promotion.
2. She *congratulated* the team *on* a season nobody expected.
3. He *congratulated* himself *on* having avoided the whole argument.
4. *Congratulations on* passing the bar.
5. The dean *congratulated* every graduate *on* stage, one at a time.
6. They *congratulated* her *on* the book and then asked when the next one was due.

**See also:** [thank for](#thank-for) &middot; [apologize to](#apologize-to-apologize-for) &middot; [boast about](#boast-about)

### consent to

**Pronunciation:** /kənˈsɛnt tu/ &middot; *kun-SENT too* &middot; weak /kənˈsɛnt tə/
**Русский:** давать согласие на — разрешать; мед. и юр. контексты
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal; medicine, law, and privacy notices — *consent to treatment*, *consent to the search*, *consent to the terms*
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*consented to being recorded*) — **never a that-clause** in modern American usage (✗ *consented to that we record it*). The one thing to keep straight: ***consent to be recorded*, with a bare infinitive, is also correct** — but there the *to* is the infinitive marker belonging to the verb *consent*, not this preposition, exactly as with *agree*. Both are good English and they mean the same thing; what is wrong is mixing them (✗ *consented to be questioning*).
**Pattern:** *consent to* [a procedure / a search / the terms] &middot; *consent to* [verb-ing] &middot; *consent to* [verb — infinitive, not preposition] &middot; *give consent to* [something] &middot; *consent* — no complement
**Contrast:** ***Consent to* grants permission; the neighbors do other things.*** *Agree to* is the everyday word and carries no notion of authority; [assent to](#assent-to) formally approves; [accede to](#accede-to) gives in after resisting; [acquiesce in](#acquiesce-in) merely fails to object. Legally the distinction matters: only the person with the right to permit can *consent*, which is why *informed consent* is a term of art and why ✗ *consent with* is not English. Note that *object to*, the opposite pattern, takes only the gerund — *objected to being recorded*, never ✗ *objected to be recorded* — so the two are not symmetrical.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic; a performative act of granting permission, not a state: *she is consenting to the search on camera*.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** with a fixed preposition — no direct object (✗ *consent the search*), and the verb can stand alone: *her father never consented*.
**Passive:** **restricted.** Legal prose does passivize it — *the search was consented to*, *a consented-to entry* — but outside that register it sounds odd: ✗ *another round of tests wouldn't be consented to*.

Give permission for something.

1. She would not *consent to* another round of tests.
2. The driver *consented to* a search of the trunk.
3. Both parties *consented to* the change of venue.
4. He *consented to* being photographed only from a distance.
5. Users must *consent to* the collection of location data.
6. Her father never *consented to* the marriage and never explained why.

**See also:** [agree to](#agree-with-agree-to-agree-on) &middot; [assent to](#assent-to) &middot; [accede to](#accede-to) &middot; [object to](#object-to) &middot; [to](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#to)

### consist in

**Pronunciation:** /kənˈsɪst ɪn/ &middot; *kun-SIST in* &middot; weak /kənˈsɪst ən/
**Русский:** заключаться в — составлять самую суть чего-то; книжн. (не путать с consist of)
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal and philosophical; essays, theology, and academic prose — *happiness consists in contentment* — and rare enough in speech that many Americans know only *consist of*
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*consists in knowing when to stop*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *consists in to know when to stop*) and **never a that-clause**. The verb takes no direct object, has **no passive** (✗ *is consisted in*) and **no progressive** (✗ *is consisting in*).
**Pattern:** [an abstraction] *consist in* [its essence] &middot; *consist in* [verb-ing] &middot; *the difficulty consists in* [something]
**Contrast:** ***Consist in* names the essence; [consist of](#consist-of) lists the parts.*** *The committee consists of nine members* is an inventory; *good management consists in hiring well and then leaving people alone* is a definition — swap them and you get nonsense: ✗ *the committee consists in nine members*. A useful test: *consist of* answers what is it made of, *consist in* answers what does it really amount to, and the second can almost always be replaced by *lies in*. Both refuse the passive and the progressive, which is the error learners make most often with either.
**Verb type:** **stative** — a relation of essence, never an activity; it rejects the progressive outright: ✗ *the difficulty is consisting in the paperwork*.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** — the verb takes no direct object at all; *in* plus its complement is obligatory: ✗ *her whole argument consists*.
**Passive:** **no.** The entry says so explicitly: ✗ *is consisted in*. Stative and objectless, there is nothing to promote — ✗ *one unproven assumption is consisted in by her argument*.

Have as its essential nature — be constituted by.

1. Her whole argument *consists in* one unproven assumption.
2. Real freedom, he wrote, *consists in* having nothing left to prove.
3. The difficulty *consists in* getting three agencies to sign the same document.
4. Virtue does not *consist in* never being tempted.
5. The novel's charm *consists in* how little it explains.
6. Most of the work *consists in* checking numbers somebody else typed.

**See also:** [consist of](#consist-of) &middot; [amount to](#amount-to) &middot; [result in, result from](#result-in-result-from) &middot; [in](../../06-prepositions/catalog/01-core-in-on-at.md#in)

### consist of

**Pronunciation:** /kənˈsɪst əv/ &middot; *kun-SIST uv* &middot; weak /kənˈsɪst ə/
**Русский:** состоять из — быть составленным из частей
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*consists of filling out three forms*) — **never a to-infinitive** and never a clause. *Consist* is intransitive: it has **no passive** (✗ *is consisted of*) and **no progressive** (✗ *is consisting of*).
**Pattern:** [a whole] *consist of* [its parts] &middot; [something] *consist in* [its essence] — formal &middot; *be composed of* / *be made up of* [parts]
**Contrast:** ***Is consisted of* is an error** — the verb is already active and cannot be passivized. *Consist **of*** lists the physical parts; the rarer *consist **in*** names where something's essence lies (*freedom consists in the absence of coercion*). The neighbors sort out like this: *consist of* and *be made up of* are the plain forms; *be composed of* is formal; and *comprise* means the same thing but takes a **direct object and no preposition** — *the collection comprises 400 prints* — so the very common *is comprised of* remains disputed in edited American prose. If you are unsure, *consists of* is always safe.
**Verb type:** **stative** — being made up of parts is a state, not an event, and the progressive is impossible: ✗ *the committee is consisting of five members*.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** — no direct object; *of* plus the parts is required: ✗ *the exam consists*. Use *comprise* if you want a true direct object: *the exam comprises forty questions*.
**Passive:** **no** — the classic learner error. ✗ *is consisted of* does not exist; the verb is already active in meaning. Say *be made up of* or *be composed of* when you want that shape.

Be made up of certain parts.

1. The committee *consists of* five members and two alternates.
2. Breakfast *consisted of* cold toast and an apology.
3. The exam *consists of* forty questions and one essay.
4. Her job largely *consists of* answering email she didn't ask for.
5. The archive *consists of* letters, photographs, and one reel of film.
6. True courage, he said, *consists in* doing it anyway.

**See also:** [lack](#lack) &middot; [belong to](#belong-to) &middot; [contribute to](#contribute-to) &middot; [separate from](#separate-from) &middot; [of](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#of)

### consult with

**Pronunciation:** /kənˈsʌlt wɪθ/ &middot; *kun-SULT with* &middot; weak /kənˈsʌlt wəθ/
**Русский:** советоваться с — обсуждать вопрос со специалистом
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** *consult with* is neutral American; bare *consult* is more formal
**Complement:** noun phrase only, naming a person or body. **No gerund, no clause.** The bare transitive is a different pattern: *consult a doctor*, *consult the manual* — a reference source is a direct object and never takes *with*: ✗ *consult with the dictionary*.
**Pattern:** *consult with* [a person, a colleague, a firm] &middot; *consult* [a doctor, a lawyer, a manual, a map] &middot; *consult* [someone] *about* / *on* [a matter] &middot; *consult for* [a client]
**Contrast:** ***Consult with* someone is standard, everyday American English** and is far more common in the U.S. than in Britain, where it can sound redundant next to plain *consult*. The useful distinction: you *consult* a source (it gives you information) and *consult **with*** a person (you talk it over together). A third pattern, *consult **for***, means work as a paid consultant: *she consults for two hospital systems*. The matter under discussion takes *about* or *on*.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic in both frames, talking something over (*let me consult with my partner*) and looking something up (*consult the appendix*); progressive fine: *she's consulting with two colleagues*.
**Transitivity:** **both** — intransitive with *with* before a person (*consulted with residents*), transitive before a source or a person consulted (*consult a tax attorney*, *consult the manual*), and *consult for* names a client.
**Passive:** **restricted.** The bare transitive passivizes cleanly — *a tax attorney should be consulted*, *residents were consulted about the bike lanes* — but the *with* frame is clumsy: ?*my partner was consulted with first*.

Talk something over with someone, or look something up in a source.

1. Let me *consult with* my partner before I commit to anything.
2. You should *consult* a tax attorney, not a message board.
3. The city *consulted with* residents *about* the bike lanes for eight months.
4. She *consults for* three startups and teaches one class.
5. *Consult* the appendix for the full list of exclusions.
6. The surgeon *consulted with* two colleagues before scheduling the operation.

**See also:** [talk with](#talk-to-talk-with-talk-about) &middot; [refer to](#refer-to) &middot; [apply to](#apply-for-apply-to) &middot; [agree with](#agree-with-agree-to-agree-on) &middot; [discuss](#discuss)

### contribute to

**Pronunciation:** /kənˈtrɪbjut tu/ &middot; *kun-TRIB-yoot too* &middot; weak /kənˈtrɪbjut tə/
**Русский:** вносить вклад в; способствовать чему-то — после to герундий
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*contributed to lowering the rate*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *contributed to lower the rate*, which changes the meaning to purpose). A wh-clause is possible after *to whatever*. Never a that-clause.
**Pattern:** *contribute to* [a fund, a cause, a result] &middot; *contribute* [something] *to* [something] &middot; *contribute to* [verb-ing] &middot; *contribute* [an article] *to* [a publication]
**Contrast:** **Watch the *to*.** In *contributed to lowering the rate* the *to* is the fixed preposition and the verb must be *-ing*; in *contributed money to lower the rate* the *to* is an infinitive of purpose and belongs to a different structure entirely. The two read almost alike and mean different things. Note also that *contribute* covers both giving and causing: *contribute to a pension* is generous, *contribute to a collapse* is not.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic in both senses, giving (*everyone contributed to the gift*) and causing (*deferred maintenance contributed to the failure*); progressive normal: *she's been contributing to that fund for years*.
**Transitivity:** **both** — intransitive with *to* alone (*three factors contributed to the drop*), transitive when the thing given is named: *he contributed a chapter to the anthology*.
**Passive:** **restricted.** Passive only where there is a real direct object: *a chapter was contributed to the anthology*. The causal frame refuses it: ✗ *the failure of the pump was contributed to by deferred maintenance*.

Give something toward a total, or help bring something about.

1. Everyone in the office *contributed to* the gift.
2. Deferred maintenance *contributed to* the failure of the pump.
3. She has *contributed to* the magazine for twenty years.
4. Three factors *contributed to* the drop in enrollment.
5. The tax credit *contributed to* lowering the uninsured rate.
6. He *contributed* a chapter *to* the anthology and never got paid.

**See also:** [add to](#add-to) &middot; [lead to](18-phrasal-h-l.md#lead-to) &middot; [result in](#result-in-result-from) &middot; [participate in](#participate-in) &middot; [invest in](#invest-in)

### converge on, converge to

**Pronunciation:** /kənˈvɜrdʒ ɑn/ &middot; *kun-VURJ on* &middot; weak /kənˈvɜrdʒ ən/
**Русский:** сходиться к одной точке — on съезжаться куда-то, to сходиться (мат.)
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral for *converge on*; *converge to* is technical, at home in mathematics and statistics
**Complement:** noun phrase naming a **place, target, or value** — **never a gerund**, **never a to-infinitive**, **never a clause**. There is no direct-object frame: ✗ *converged the plaza*. The subject is plural or collective, because convergence takes at least two things: ✗ *the reporter converged on city hall*.
**Pattern:** [people / vehicles] *converge on* [a place] &middot; [estimates / opinions] *converge on* [a figure] &middot; [a sequence] *converge to* [a limit] &middot; [two lines / two paths] *converge with* [each other]
**Contrast:** **The preposition marks the field you are writing in.** *Converge on* is the general word — crowds converge on a stadium, forecasters converge on a number. *Converge to* belongs to mathematics, where a series converges to a limit, and using it of a crowd sounds like a translation error. *Converge with* takes the other party rather than the destination (*the trail converges with the highway*). The opposite is *diverge from*, and note that *converge* has no passive: ✗ *the plaza was converged on* is at best jocular.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic, a process with duration; the progressive is normal: *three storm systems are converging on the valley*.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** — no direct-object frame at all (✗ *converged the plaza*), and the subject must be plural or collective: ✗ *the reporter converged on city hall*.
**Passive:** **no**, as the entry states: ✗ *the plaza was converged on*, ✗ *four percent has been converged on by the estimates*. Rewrite with *surround* or *agree on*.

Come together from different directions toward one point.

1. Reporters *converged on* the courthouse before the verdict was read.
2. Independent estimates have *converged on* about four percent.
3. Three storm systems *converged on* the valley in a single week.
4. The sequence *converges to* zero faster than the textbook example.
5. Both parties eventually *converged on* the same compromise figure.
6. The old logging road *converges with* the highway two miles north.

**See also:** [agree on](#agree-with-agree-to-agree-on) &middot; [arrive at, arrive in](#arrive-at-arrive-in) &middot; [amount to](#amount-to) &middot; [on](../../06-prepositions/catalog/01-core-in-on-at.md#on)

### convict of

**Pronunciation:** /kənˈvɪkt əv/ &middot; *kun-VIKT uv* &middot; weak /kənˈvɪkt ə/
**Русский:** признавать виновным в — выносить обвинительный приговор; юр.
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal; legal and news reporting
**Complement:** noun phrase naming the **crime**, or a **gerund** naming it (*convicted of lying to investigators*) — **never a to-infinitive** and **never a that-clause**. The passive is the normal frame: *was convicted of*. Counts and charges take *on* instead: *convicted on three counts*, *convicted on all charges*; ✗ *convicted for murder* and ✗ *convicted with murder* are errors, though *convicted for a crime he did not commit* passes as causal *for*.
**Pattern:** [a jury / a court] *convict* [someone] *of* [a crime] &middot; [someone] *be convicted of* [a crime] &middot; *be convicted on* [counts] &middot; *a convicted felon* &middot; *convict* [someone] *of* [verb-ing]
**Contrast:** ***Convict of* and [acquit of](#acquit-of) are the two verdicts and share one preposition.*** Upstream of both, *charge with* is what the prosecutor files and *indict for* is what a grand jury returns, so the preposition changes at every stage: *charged **with** fraud*, *indicted **for** fraud*, *convicted **of** fraud*, *sentenced **to** three years*. Watch the stress: the verb is *kun-VIKT*, the noun *KON-vikt*. And *convict* never takes a that-clause — for that you need *find*: *the jury found that he had lied*.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic; a court's act at a point in time: *the jury convicted her of the lesser charge*.
**Transitivity:** **transitive** — the person is the direct object and *of* names the crime; both slots are needed in the active: ✗ *the jury convicted of wire fraud*.
**Passive:** **yes** — and the passive is the normal frame in American reporting: *he was convicted of wire fraud and sentenced in March*, *two officers were convicted of falsifying the reports*.

Find someone guilty of a crime in a court of law.

1. He was *convicted of* wire fraud and sentenced in March.
2. The jury *convicted* her *of* the lesser charge only.
3. Anyone *convicted of* a felony loses the license automatically.
4. Two officers were *convicted of* falsifying the reports.
5. She was *convicted on* three counts and acquitted on the rest.
6. He was *convicted of* selling equipment that belonged to the county.

**See also:** [acquit of](#acquit-of) &middot; [accuse of](#accuse-of) &middot; [charge with, charge for](#charge-with-charge-for) &middot; [absolve of](#absolve-of) &middot; [punish for](#punish-for) &middot; [of](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#of)

### cooperate with

**Pronunciation:** /koʊˈɑpəreɪt wɪθ/ &middot; *koh-OP-uh-rayt with* &middot; weak /koʊˈɑpəreɪt wəθ/
**Русский:** сотрудничать с — содействовать, а не мешать (часто о следствии)
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral; extremely common in reporting on investigations
**Complement:** noun phrase naming a **person, agency, or organization** — **never a gerund** (✗ *cooperated with handing over the files*; the activity takes *on* or *in*: *cooperated on the study*, *cooperated in the investigation*). **Never a to-infinitive**, **never a clause**, and there is no direct object: ✗ *cooperated the inquiry*. The verb also stands alone: *the witness cooperated*.
**Pattern:** *cooperate with* [someone] &middot; *cooperate with* [someone] *on* / *in* [something] &middot; *cooperate* — no complement &middot; *cooperation with* [someone] — the noun keeps the preposition
**Contrast:** **Cooperating is not obstructing; [collaborating](#collaborate-with) is co-authoring.** *The company cooperated with regulators* means it answered the questions and produced the documents; *the company collaborated with regulators* would oddly suggest they wrote the rules together. In criminal practice *cooperate with* has hardened into a term of art — a cooperating witness trades information for leniency — and *refused to cooperate* is nearly a formula. Note that ✗ *cooperate to someone* and ✗ *cooperate someone* are both impossible.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic, something you do or refuse to do; progressive fine: *the airline is cooperating with investigators*.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** — no direct object (✗ *cooperated the inquiry*), and the verb stands alone happily: *the witness cooperated*.
**Passive:** **no.** The prepositional passive is unusable here: ✗ *federal investigators were cooperated with*, ✗ *the committee was refused to be cooperated with*.

Work together with someone, or help them rather than obstruct them.

1. The airline *cooperated with* federal investigators from the first day.
2. Two states *cooperated with* each other on the watershed plan.
3. He declined to *cooperate with* the committee and was subpoenaed.
4. Local police *cooperate with* the sheriff's office on anything over the county line.
5. Tenants who *cooperate with* the inspection get the repairs done first.
6. If the weather *cooperates with* us, we'll be on the water by six.

**See also:** [collaborate with](#collaborate-with) &middot; [work with](#work-on-work-for-work-with) &middot; [comply with](#comply-with) &middot; [consult with](#consult-with) &middot; [with](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#with)

### cope with

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkoʊp wɪθ/ &middot; *KOHP with* &middot; weak /ˈkoʊp wəθ/
**Русский:** справляться с — успешно преодолевать трудность
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*cope with being alone*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *cope with to be alone*) and never a clause. *Cope* is intransitive and cannot take a bare object: ✗ *cope the situation*. It does stand alone: *she's coping*.
**Pattern:** *cope with* [a difficulty] &middot; *cope with* [verb-ing] &middot; *cope* (alone) &middot; *manage to* [verb] — a different verb, different pattern
**Contrast:** ***Cope to* and ✗ *cope the problem* are both errors.** The preposition is fixed at *with*, and the verb never takes a direct object. Its neighbors split: *deal **with*** is the neutral everyday synonym, *handle* and *manage* are transitive with no preposition (*handle the complaints*, *manage the fallout*), and *put up with* means tolerate rather than manage. Bare *cope*, with nothing after it, has become the standard American shorthand for barely managing: *I'm coping.*
**Verb type:** action — dynamic; managing is something you do over time, and the progressive is the commonest form: *they're coping, but only just*.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** — *cope* never takes a bare object (✗ *cope the situation*) and *with* is fixed; the verb also stands alone: *she's coping*.
**Passive:** **no** in practice: ✗ *a heavy caseload is being coped with*, ✗ *that kind of traffic couldn't be coped with*. Switch to *handle* or *manage*, which are transitive: *that traffic couldn't be handled*.

Deal successfully with something difficult.

1. She's *coping with* a heavy caseload and a sick parent.
2. Small districts can't *cope with* an enrollment swing that size.
3. He *coped with* being passed over better than anyone expected.
4. The system wasn't built to *cope with* that kind of traffic.
5. How do you *cope with* the noise?
6. They're *coping*, but only just.

**See also:** [take care of](#take-care-of) &middot; [object to](#object-to) &middot; [adapt to](#adapt-to) &middot; [suffer from](#suffer-from)

### correct for

**Pronunciation:** /kəˈrɛkt fɔr/ &middot; *kuh-REKT for* &middot; weak /kəˈrɛkt fər/
**Русский:** вносить поправку на — устранять известное искажение; науч./техн.
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal and technical; statistics, laboratory science, economics — *corrected for inflation*, *corrected for altitude*
**Complement:** noun phrase naming a **distorting factor** — inflation, seasonality, temperature, sample bias. A gerund is rare but possible (*correcting for having oversampled retirees*); **never a to-infinitive** and **never a clause**. Keep it apart from the plain transitive: *correct the error* takes no preposition, and *correct someone on something* is a third pattern.
**Pattern:** *correct for* [a distortion] &middot; *be corrected for* [inflation / seasonality] &middot; *correct* [an error] — no preposition &middot; *correct* [someone] *on* [a point]
**Contrast:** **Three technical near-neighbors, and the difference is real.** *Correct for* and *adjust for* remove a known distortion from figures you already have — *adjust for* is the commoner word in finance. *Control for* is a design decision made in advance, holding a variable constant so it cannot vary; a study *controls for* age, and saying it *corrected for* age instead tells the reader the fix came afterward. And [account for](#account-for) merely explains a number rather than changing it.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic, an adjustment performed on figures: *the model corrects for seasonal swings*; progressive fine in describing a method.
**Transitivity:** **both** — transitive with no preposition in *correct the error*, and intransitive with the fixed *for* when a distortion is removed: *correct for inflation*. *Correct someone on something* is a third frame.
**Passive:** **yes**, and the prepositional passive is standard technical English: *blood values are corrected for altitude before they are compared*, *test scores corrected for school size tell a different story*.

Adjust a figure or a measurement to remove a known distortion.

1. Wages have barely moved once you *correct for* inflation.
2. The model *corrects for* seasonal swings in construction hiring.
3. Their instruments *correct for* the curvature of the lens automatically.
4. The survey did not *correct for* the fact that only landlines were called.
5. Test scores *corrected for* school size tell a different story.
6. Blood values are *corrected for* altitude before they are compared.

**See also:** [account for](#account-for) &middot; [allow for](#allow-for) &middot; [compensate for](#compensate-for) &middot; [for](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#for)

### correlate with

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkɔrəleɪt wɪθ/ &middot; *KOR-uh-layt with* &middot; weak /ˈkɔrəleɪt wəθ/
**Русский:** коррелировать с — изменяться согласованно с чем-то; науч.
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal and technical; statistics, medicine, and the social sciences, plus the business writing that borrows from them
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** naming the **other variable** (*correlates with having gone to preschool*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *correlates with to go to preschool*) and **never a clause**. There is also a transitive frame in which the researcher is the subject: *correlate the survey data with census figures*. The verb can stand alone with a plural subject: *the two measures correlate*.
**Pattern:** [one variable] *correlate with* [another] &middot; *correlate* [one set of data] *with* [another] &middot; [two things] *correlate* &middot; *be strongly / weakly / negatively correlated with* [something]
**Variants:** *correlate to* — heard in business and journalistic writing; *with* is the standard in scientific prose and the safer choice everywhere
**Contrast:** **The whole point of the word is that it does not claim causation.** *Ice cream sales correlate with drownings* asserts only that the two move together; *cause*, *lead to*, and *result in* assert direction, and swapping them in is the classic error. Two softer neighbors: *relate to* is vague about the relationship, and *vary with* describes co-movement without the statistical machinery (*the fee varies with the size of the lot*). Note the adjective frame is the commonest of all in papers: *strongly correlated with income*.
**Verb type:** **stative** in the relational sense — two variables simply move together, and the progressive is odd: ✗ *income is correlating with health outcomes*. **Action (dynamic)** in the researcher's frame: *she was correlating the readings with admissions*.
**Transitivity:** **both** — intransitive with *with* or with a plural subject alone (*the two measures correlate*), transitive when a researcher does the work: *correlate the sensor readings with hospital admissions*.
**Passive:** **restricted.** The researcher frame passivizes and the participle is everywhere in papers: *the readings were correlated with admissions*, *strongly correlated with income*. The relational sense does not: ✗ *health outcomes are correlated with by income*.

Move together with something in a measurable way.

1. Household income *correlates with* almost every health outcome in the study.
2. The two tests *correlate with* each other well enough to use either one.
3. Absenteeism *correlates with* the length of the commute.
4. Nothing in the data *correlates with* the change they expected to see.
5. The researchers *correlated* the sensor readings *with* hospital admissions.
6. Test scores *correlate with* having a parent who finished college.

**See also:** [correspond to, correspond with](#correspond-to-correspond-with) &middot; [compare to, compare with](#compare-to-compare-with) &middot; [result in, result from](#result-in-result-from) &middot; [depend on](#depend-on) &middot; [with](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#with)

### correspond to, correspond with

**Pronunciation:** /ˌkɔrəˈspɑnd tu/ &middot; *kor-uh-SPOND too* &middot; weak /ˌkɔrəˈspɑnd tə/
**Русский:** соответствовать чему-то (to); переписываться с кем-то (with)
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal; the letter-writing sense is formal and a little old-fashioned
**Complement:** noun phrase — **never a gerund** (✗ *corresponds to being late*; say *corresponds to the delay*), **never a to-infinitive**, **never a clause**. The verb has **no passive** (✗ *is corresponded to*) and no direct object.
**Pattern:** [one thing] *correspond to* [another] — match &middot; [a person] *correspond with* [another] — exchange letters &middot; [two things] *correspond* &middot; *the corresponding* [figure / period] — the participle, very common in reports
**Contrast:** **The two prepositions have drifted apart in American usage, and the split is worth keeping.** *Correspond to* means match or be equivalent: *the numbers on the invoice correspond to the packing list*, *the American term corresponds to the British one*. *Correspond with* means write letters back and forth: *they corresponded with each other for forty years*. Older and British writing also uses *with* for matching, so *the figures correspond with the audit* is not wrong — but in a document where both senses could be read, *to* for matching keeps the sentence clear. Compare [conform to](#conform-to-conform-with), which measures a thing against a standard rather than pairing two things off.
**Verb type:** **stative** in the matching sense — a static equivalence, no progressive: ✗ *each color is corresponding to a tract*. **Action (dynamic)** in the letter-writing sense: *they were corresponding with each other for forty years*.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** in both senses — no direct object at all (✗ *correspond the invoice the packing list*); the preposition is obligatory except with a plural subject: *the two figures correspond*.
**Passive:** **no**, as the entry states: ✗ *is corresponded to*, ✗ *a census tract is corresponded to by each color*. The participle *the corresponding period* is an adjective, not a passive.

Match something, or exchange letters with someone.

1. Each color on the map *corresponds to* a census tract.
2. The two economists *corresponded with* each other for a decade before meeting.
3. The serial number does not *correspond to* anything in our records.
4. Roughly, a bachelor's degree there *corresponds to* an associate's here.
5. She *corresponded with* the family in Odessa until the mail stopped.
6. Revenue for the quarter fell short of the *corresponding* period last year.

**See also:** [conform to, conform with](#conform-to-conform-with) &middot; [correlate with](#correlate-with) &middot; [compare to, compare with](#compare-to-compare-with) &middot; [communicate with](#communicate-with) &middot; [to](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#to)

### count on

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkaʊnt ɑn/ &middot; *KOWNT on* &middot; weak /ˈkaʊnt ən/
**Русский:** рассчитывать на — быть уверенным, что не подведёт
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to informal
**Complement:** noun phrase, pronoun, or **gerund** (*counting on getting the grant*) — **never a to-infinitive after the preposition itself** (✗ *count on to get the grant*). A to-infinitive is correct after a **person**: *count on her to remember*. A wh-clause is fine: *don't count on what he tells you*. *Count on* also takes a that-clause in speech (*count on it that he'll be late*), but the smoother form is *count on his being late*.
**Pattern:** *count on* [someone / something] &middot; *count on* [someone] *to* [verb] &middot; *count on* [verb-ing] &middot; *you can count on it*
**Contrast:** **Same family as *depend on* and *rely on*, with a difference of register:** *count on* is the warm, spoken one, *rely on* the neutral one, *depend on* the one that also means be determined by. *Count on* almost never carries that second sense — *That counts on the weather* is not English; you want *That depends on the weather*. Note the unrelated phrasal verbs *count in* (*count me in*), *count out* (exclude), and *count up* (total), none of which are this pattern.
**Verb type:** **stative** in meaning — trust is a state, not an act — but unlike most statives it takes the progressive freely: *I'm counting on you*, *we were counting on a bigger turnout*.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** with a fixed preposition — no bare object (✗ *count her*), though *count on someone to do something* adds an infinitive after the person. Bare *count* (total, matter) is a different verb.
**Passive:** **yes** — the prepositional passive is idiomatic: *she can be counted on to have read every page*, *that bus line is counted on by half the neighborhood*.

Trust that someone or something will not let you down.

1. You can *count on* her to have read every page.
2. Don't *count on* the mail getting there by Friday.
3. Half the neighborhood *counts on* that bus line.
4. I'm *counting on* you.
5. We were *counting on* a bigger turnout than forty people.
6. *Count on* it — he'll bring up the parking again.

**See also:** [depend on](#depend-on) &middot; [rely on](#rely-on) &middot; [believe in](#believe-in) &middot; [wait for](#wait-for-wait-on)

### cover with

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkʌvɚ wɪθ/ &middot; *KUV-er with* &middot; weak /ˈkʌvɚ wəθ/
**Русский:** покрывать чем-то — накладывать слой поверх
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase only — no gerund, no clause. The passive takes either preposition: *covered with snow* and *covered in snow* are both standard, with *in* a shade more thorough. *Cover* itself is transitive: *cover the pot*, ✗ *cover with the pot* if the pot is the lid.
**Pattern:** *cover* [something] *with* [something] &middot; *be covered with* / *in* [something] &middot; *cover* [a topic, a distance, a shift] — no preposition &middot; *be covered by* [insurance, a policy, a warranty]
**Contrast:** ***Covered with* and *covered in* are genuinely interchangeable**; *in* suggests more complete coverage (*covered in mud* beats *covered with mud* for effect). *Covered **by*** is a different thing entirely — it names an insurer, a policy, or a reporter: *the surgery is covered by insurance*, *the trial was covered by three networks*. And bare *cover* has a raft of transitive senses that take no preposition at all: *cover the material*, *cover forty miles*, *cover for a colleague* (that last one being *cover for*, meaning substitute or make excuses).
**Verb type:** action — dynamic; the resulting *be covered with / in* is a **state** rather than an event: *his desk is permanently covered in sticky notes*.
**Transitivity:** **transitive** — *cover* needs a direct object (✗ *she covered with a rug*), and *with* names the layer. Bare transitive *cover the material / forty miles / a shift* takes no preposition.
**Passive:** **yes**, and the passive is the commonest form: *by morning the deck was covered with ice*. Keep *covered by* apart — it names an insurer or a reporter: *the procedure isn't covered by our plan*.

Put a layer over something.

1. *Cover* the dough *with* a damp towel and let it rest.
2. By morning the deck was *covered with* ice.
3. His desk is permanently *covered in* sticky notes.
4. She *covered* the stain *with* a rug and hoped nobody looked.
5. The whole field was *covered with* frost at sunrise.
6. The procedure isn't *covered by* our plan.

**See also:** [consist of](#consist-of) &middot; [provide with](#provide-with-provide-for) &middot; [dress in](#dress-in) &middot; [protect from](#protect-from-protect-against) &middot; [with](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#with)

### credit with

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkrɛdɪt wɪθ/ &middot; *KRED-it with* &middot; weak /ˈkrɛdɪt wəθ/
**Русский:** приписывать кому-то заслугу — признавать чей-то вклад
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral; standard in journalism, citations, and banking
**Complement:** the **person or thing honored is a direct object**, and *with* takes a noun phrase or a **gerund** (*credited her with saving the account*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *credited her with to save the account*) and **never a clause**. Both slots are obligatory in the active: ✗ *credited with the discovery* alone needs a passive subject — *she was credited with the discovery*.
**Pattern:** *credit* [someone] *with* [an achievement] &middot; *credit* [someone] *with* [verb-ing] &middot; *be credited with* [something] &middot; *credit* [an achievement] *to* [someone] &middot; *credit* [an account] *with* [an amount]
**Contrast:** ***Credit with* and [blame for](#blame-for-blame-on) are mirror images**, one for things that went right and one for things that went wrong, and they take different prepositions: *credited her **with** the turnaround*, *blamed her **for** the collapse*. [Attribute to](#attribute-to) and [ascribe to](#ascribe-to) say the same thing with the arguments reversed — *attributed the turnaround to her* — so the achievement leads and the person follows. American speech also uses *credit someone for something*, which careful writers reserve for the effort or the reason (*credit him for trying*) while keeping *with* for the accomplishment itself. The banking sense runs both ways: *credit the account with $50* or *credit $50 to the account*.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic; an act of ascribing recognition, performed by reviewers, historians, or a bank: *historians now credit her with having written most of the report*.
**Transitivity:** **transitive** — the person honored is the direct object and *with* takes the achievement; both slots are obligatory in the active: ✗ *reviewers credited with rescuing the second act*.
**Passive:** **yes**, and the passive is the default frame: *she is credited with discovering two of the three isotopes*, *the account was credited with the disputed charge*.

Say that someone deserves recognition for something.

1. Reviewers *credited* the director *with* rescuing the second act.
2. She is *credited with* discovering two of the three isotopes.
3. Nobody *credits* the mayor *with* the drop in crime except the mayor.
4. He *credits* his sister *with* talking him out of dropping the case.
5. The bank *credited* the account *with* the disputed charge the next morning.
6. Historians now *credit* her *with* having written most of the report.

**See also:** [attribute to](#attribute-to) &middot; [ascribe to](#ascribe-to) &middot; [blame for, blame on](#blame-for-blame-on) &middot; [thank for](#thank-for) &middot; [with](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#with)

## D

### deal with, deal in

**Pronunciation:** /ˈdil wɪθ/ &middot; *DEEL with* &middot; weak /ˈdil wəθ/
**Русский:** иметь дело с, улаживать, быть о чём-то (with); торговать чем-то (in)
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase, **gerund** (*deal with getting the permits*), or a wh-clause (*deal with whatever the inspector finds*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *deal with to get the permits*) and **never a that-clause** (✗ *deal with that he quit*; use *deal with the fact that he quit*). *Deal in* takes a noun phrase naming goods or a kind of thing, and nothing else. The preposition is fixed in both: the object never moves, so ✗ *deal it with* is not English.
**Pattern:** *deal with* [a problem, a person, a subject] &middot; *deal with* [verb-ing] &middot; *deal in* [antiques, futures, rumor] &middot; *deal* [someone] [cards, a hand] — no preposition &middot; *deal* [a blow] *to* [someone]
**Contrast:** ***Deal with* handles something; *deal in* trades in something.** A manager *deals with* complaints; a gallery *deals in* prints. The two are never interchangeable: ✗ *the gallery deals with prints* says it is coping with them. Note also that *deal with* covers two jobs English usually splits — take action about a problem (*deal with the leak*) and be about a subject (*the chapter deals with tariffs*) — and that the transitive *deal* takes no preposition at all: *deal the cards*, *deal a blow*.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic in every sense: handling a problem, trading in goods, or a chapter being about a subject (that last one shading **stative**: *the second chapter deals with the savings-and-loans*).
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** with both fixed prepositions — the object never moves (✗ *deal it with*) and there is no direct-object frame here. Bare transitive *deal* belongs to cards and blows: *deal the cards*, *deal a blow to*.
**Passive:** **restricted.** *Deal with* passivizes well: *the flooding was dealt with before the drywall went up*, *he'll be dealt with on Monday*. *Deal in* does not: ✗ *rare coins are dealt in by his uncle*.

Handle a problem or a person, be about a subject — or, with *in*, trade in a kind of goods.

1. Somebody has to *deal with* the flooding in the basement before the drywall goes up.
2. I'll *deal with* him on Monday when everyone has cooled off.
3. The second chapter *deals with* the collapse of the savings-and-loans.
4. She *dealt with* being laid off better than anyone expected.
5. His uncle *deals in* rare coins out of a shop on Grand Avenue.
6. That firm *deals in* municipal bonds and nothing else.

**See also:** [cope with](#cope-with) &middot; [put up with](19-phrasal-m-r.md#put-up-with) &middot; [take care of](#take-care-of) &middot; [dispose of](#dispose-of) &middot; [with](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#with)

### decide on, decide against

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈsaɪd ɑn/ &middot; *dih-SIDE on* &middot; weak /dɪˈsaɪd ən/
**Русский:** останавливаться на чём-то (on); решать отказаться от чего-то (against)
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** *on* and *against* take a noun phrase, a **gerund** (*decided against moving*), or a wh-clause (*decided on where to eat*) — **never a to-infinitive after the preposition** (✗ *decided on to move*). *Decide* also takes a bare to-infinitive with no preposition (*decided to move*) and a that-clause (*decided that it wasn't worth it*).
**Pattern:** *decide on* [a choice] &middot; *decide against* [a course / verb-ing] &middot; *decide to* [verb] &middot; *decide not to* [verb] &middot; *decide that* [clause] &middot; *decide between* [A] *and* [B] &middot; *decide* [a case, a question]
**Contrast:** ***Decide to leave* and *decide on leaving* are both correct and mean the same thing** — the first is the infinitive frame, the second the prepositional one — but you cannot mix them: ✗ *decide on to leave*, ✗ *decide leaving*. *Decide **against*** is the negative of *decide on*, not of *decide to*: the negative of *decided to sell* is *decided not to sell*, and *decided against selling* is its prepositional twin. Note also transitive *decide*, which takes no preposition and belongs to courts and referees: *the judge decided the case*.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic, a punctual act of settling something: *they finally decided on the yellow one*; progressive fine while it is still open: *we're still deciding on a date*.
**Transitivity:** **both** — intransitive with *on* / *against* / *between* (*decided against raising dues*), transitive in the courtroom sense: *the judge decided the case*.
**Passive:** **restricted.** The transitive sense passivizes cleanly (*the case was decided in March*); the prepositional one is heavy but possible: ?*the yellow one was finally decided on*, ✗ *raising dues was decided against by the board*.

Settle on one option after weighing them.

1. They finally *decided on* the yellow one.
2. She *decided against* law school after two weeks of shadowing.
3. We haven't *decided on* where to hold it.
4. The board *decided against* raising dues this year.
5. He *decided on* the cheaper contractor and regretted it by March.
6. After all that, they *decided* to stay put.

**See also:** [choose between](#choose-between) &middot; [agree on](#agree-with-agree-to-agree-on) &middot; [insist on](#insist-on) &middot; [plan on](#plan-on) &middot; [vote for](#vote-for-vote-against)

### dedicate to, devote to

**Pronunciation:** /ˈdɛdəˌkeɪt tu/ &middot; *DED-uh-kayt too* &middot; weak /ˈdɛdəˌkeɪt tə/
**Русский:** посвящать чему-то или кому-то — отдавать целиком; после to герундий
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral; *dedicate* is the more ceremonial of the two
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*devoted the summer to rebuilding the porch*) — ***to* is a preposition here**, so a verb after it must be *-ing*: ✗ *dedicated to preserve the wetland*, ✗ *devoted himself to restore it*. Never a clause. In the active, both verbs need a direct object before the *to* — the thing given over, or a reflexive: *dedicate the fund to scholarships*, *devote yourself to the work*. In the participial forms that object drops away, but the *to* and its gerund do not: *dedicated to ending hunger*, *devoted to running the shop*.
**Pattern:** *dedicate* [something] *to* [noun phrase / verb-ing] &middot; *devote* [time, money, oneself] *to* [noun phrase / verb-ing] &middot; *be dedicated to* / *be devoted to* [noun phrase / verb-ing] &middot; *dedicate* [a book, a building] *to* [a person]
**Contrast:** ***Dedicated to preserving* and ✗ *dedicated to preserve* differ by a suffix and by a whole grammar.** This is the *look forward to* trap wearing formal clothes, and it shows up constantly in mission statements, where *an organization dedicated to ending hunger* is right and ✗ *dedicated to end hunger* is not. The two verbs part company in one place: *dedicate* alone carries the ceremonial sense of naming something in someone's honor — *dedicate the library to her memory*, *the album is dedicated to his father* — which *devote* never does. In the other direction *devote* is the ordinary word for spending time (*devoted three weeks to the appendix*), where *dedicate* sounds grand. Note that the adjective *devoted* can stand alone meaning loyal (*a devoted friend*), and that ✗ *dedicate on* and ✗ *devote for* are errors.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic, an act of giving something over; the participial *be dedicated to* / *be devoted to* is a **state**: *two floors are devoted to storage*.
**Transitivity:** **transitive** — both verbs need a direct object before *to* in the active, often a reflexive: *dedicate the fund to scholarships*, *they devoted themselves to raising the money*. ✗ *the foundation dedicates to restoring habitat*.
**Passive:** **yes**, and the passive is where these verbs live: *the wing was dedicated to the founder's wife in 1961*, *the foundation is dedicated to restoring salmon habitat*.

Give something over entirely to a purpose or a person.

1. The foundation is *dedicated to* restoring salmon habitat.
2. She *devoted* most of her thirties *to* getting the clinic open.
3. He *dedicated* the book *to* the teacher who failed him twice.
4. Two floors are *devoted to* storage that nobody has opened in years.
5. They *devoted themselves to* raising the money before the roof gave out.
6. The wing was *dedicated to* the founder's wife in 1961.

**See also:** [commit to](#commit-to) &middot; [look forward to](#look-forward-to) &middot; [object to](#object-to) &middot; [contribute to](#contribute-to) &middot; [specialize in](#specialize-in) &middot; [to](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#to)

### deduce from

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈdus frʌm/ &middot; *dih-DOOSS from* &middot; weak /dɪˈdus frəm/
**Русский:** выводить, заключать из — доходить рассуждением от известного
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral, leaning formal — logic, detective stories, and academic prose
**Complement:** *from* takes a noun phrase naming the evidence, or a **gerund** (*deduced from watching the tape*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *deduced from to watch the tape*). What is deduced is the **direct object**, and it may be a noun phrase or a that-clause: *deduced that the lock had been picked*. The preposition disappears in front of that clause: ✗ *deduced from that the lock had been picked*.
**Pattern:** *deduce* [something] *from* [evidence] &middot; *deduce that* [clause] *from* [evidence] &middot; [something] *can be deduced from* [evidence] &middot; *deduce* [something] — no preposition, when the evidence is understood
**Contrast:** ***Deduce from* and *infer from* share a preposition and split the work.** *Deduce* reasons down from a rule or a premise and claims certainty; *infer* reads a likely conclusion off the evidence and claims only probability. *Derive from* is about origin rather than reasoning (*the word derives from Latin*), and *extrapolate from* projects a known trend past the data you have. All four take *from* and none of them takes *of*: ✗ *deduce of the evidence*.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic; an act of reasoning carried out at a moment: *from the mud on the boots he deduced that she had come through the orchard*.
**Transitivity:** **transitive** — what is deduced is the direct object or a that-clause (*deduced that the lock had been picked*); *from* names the evidence and may be dropped when it is understood.
**Passive:** **yes**, and the modal passive is the natural frame: *nothing about his motive can be deduced from the emails alone*, *the rule was deduced from eight sample sentences*.

Work something out by reasoning from what you already know.

1. From the mud on the boots he *deduced* that she had come through the orchard.
2. You can *deduce* the missing figure *from* the two subtotals.
3. Nothing about his motive can be *deduced from* the emails alone.
4. She *deduced from* the silence on the call that the offer had already been made.
5. The students were asked to *deduce* the rule *from* eight sample sentences.
6. He *deduced from* watching two rehearsals that the show would run long.

**See also:** [infer from](#infer-from) &middot; [derive from](#derive-from) &middot; [extrapolate from](#extrapolate-from) &middot; [follow from](#follow-from) &middot; [from](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#from)

### default on

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈfɔlt ɑn/ &middot; *dih-FAWLT on* &middot; weak /dɪˈfɔlt ən/
**Русский:** не платить по (кредиту), нарушать обязательство; фин./юр.
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral — finance, lending, and law
**Complement:** noun phrase naming an obligation: a loan, a mortgage, a payment, a bond, a lease, an agreement. **A gerund is unidiomatic** (✗ *defaulted on paying the rent* — say *defaulted on the rent*), and **no clause is possible** (✗ *defaulted on that he owed us*). The verb is also fine with **no object at all** (*the borrower defaulted in March*), and there it is not a dependent preposition, just an intransitive verb.
**Pattern:** *default on* [a loan, a payment, an obligation] &middot; *default* — intransitive &middot; *default to* [a setting, a value] — a different verb &middot; *be in default on* [a loan] &middot; *default judgment* — the noun
**Contrast:** ***Default on* is failing to pay; *default to* is falling back on a preset.** *The county defaulted on its bonds* is a financial disaster; *the form defaults to Eastern time* is a software convenience, and the two never mix: ✗ *defaulted to its bonds*. The legal fixed phrase is [in default of](../../06-prepositions/catalog/11-complex-multiword.md#in-default-of) (*in default of payment*), which is a complex preposition rather than this verb. Compare *renege **on*** a promise, which is the same frame applied to a commitment nobody signed.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic, an event with a date: *he defaulted on a student loan in 2011*.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** — no direct object (✗ *defaulted the loan*), and the verb is fine with nothing after it: *nobody expected a AAA borrower to default*.
**Passive:** **restricted.** Financial writing allows the prepositional passive — *loans defaulted on during the crisis*, *the note was defaulted on in March* — but the ordinary phrasing is *be in default on*: ✗ *their mortgages were defaulted on by two homeowners*.

Fail to make a payment or meet an obligation you are bound to.

1. Two homeowners on the block *defaulted on* their mortgages that spring.
2. If the company *defaults on* the note, the collateral goes to the bank.
3. He *defaulted on* a student loan in 2011 and spent a decade fixing his credit.
4. The country has *defaulted on* its external debt three times in forty years.
5. Tenants who *default on* rent get thirty days' notice under the ordinance.
6. Nobody expected a AAA borrower to *default*.

**See also:** [foreclose on](#foreclose-on) &middot; [dispose of](#dispose-of) &middot; [pay for](19-phrasal-m-r.md#pay-for) &middot; [in default of](../../06-prepositions/catalog/11-complex-multiword.md#in-default-of) &middot; [on](../../06-prepositions/catalog/01-core-in-on-at.md#on)

### defer to

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈfɚ tu/ &middot; *dih-FUR too* &middot; weak /dɪˈfɚ tə/
**Русский:** уступать чьему-то мнению или власти — полагаться на чужое суждение
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal — courts, committees, and polite disagreement
**Complement:** noun phrase naming a person, a body, or somebody's judgment or expertise. **A gerund is grammatical but rare** (*deferred to having the committee decide*), and a **to-infinitive is impossible** (✗ *defer to decide* — that is the infinitive of a different verb, meaning postpone). No clause: ✗ *defer to that she knows more*.
**Pattern:** *defer to* [a person, a court, an authority] &middot; *defer to* [someone's judgment, expertise, wishes] &middot; *defer* [a decision, payment, maintenance] — postpone, no preposition &middot; *defer* [something] *to* [a later date, the next meeting]
**Contrast:** **One spelling, two verbs.** *Defer to the board* is yielding to somebody else's authority; *defer the vote* is putting it off, and that second verb takes a direct object, not *to*. Both can appear with *to* and mean different things: *we deferred to the chair* (yielded) versus *we deferred the item to June* (postponed). In appellate writing *defer to* is a term of art — *the court defers to the agency's reading* — and *deference to* is its noun. Do not confuse it with *differ from*.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic in the sense of yielding on a given occasion (*the panel deferred to the trial judge*), and habitual-**stative** in *she rarely defers to anyone on structural engineering*.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** with a fixed *to* — no direct object here (✗ *defer her judgment* in this sense). The homonym meaning postpone is transitive: *defer the vote*, *defer maintenance*.
**Passive:** **yes** — the prepositional passive is idiomatic and the entry uses it: *younger associates learn which partners expect to be deferred to*, *the agency's reading was deferred to*.

Yield to somebody else's judgment or authority instead of your own.

1. On anything involving the tax code I *defer to* her.
2. The panel *deferred to* the trial judge's findings of fact.
3. Younger associates learn quickly which partners expect to be *deferred to*.
4. He *deferred to* his brother's wishes about the funeral arrangements.
5. Reviewing courts *defer to* the agency where the statute is genuinely ambiguous.
6. She rarely *defers to* anyone on questions of structural engineering.

**See also:** [refer to](#refer-to) &middot; [comply with](#comply-with) &middot; [go along with](17-phrasal-g.md#go-along-with) &middot; [rely on](#rely-on) &middot; [to](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#to)

### depart from

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈpɑrt frʌm/ &middot; *dih-PART from* &middot; weak /dɪˈpɑrt frəm/
**Русский:** отправляться из; отступать от (правила, традиции, текста)
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal — travel announcements for the literal sense, policy and legal prose for the figurative one
**Complement:** noun phrase naming a place, or a practice, text, precedent, or norm. **A gerund is possible but stiff** (*departed from following the script*); **no to-infinitive** (✗ *depart from to follow the script*) and no clause. *Depart* is also intransitive with no object at all (*the train departs at six*), and there nothing is a dependent preposition.
**Pattern:** *depart from* [a city, a gate, a station] &middot; *depart from* [precedent, the text, standard practice] &middot; *depart for* [a destination] &middot; *depart* — intransitive &middot; *a departure from* [the norm] — the noun keeps *from*
**Contrast:** ***From* is where you leave; *for* is where you are headed.** *Departed from Denver* and *departed for Denver* are opposite trips. In the figurative sense the neighbors divide finely: *depart from* a practice is a deliberate break, *deviate from* a plan is a step off a required path, *diverge from* a route is a gradual separation, and *differ from* is not motion at all but a static comparison. Note that in ordinary American speech nobody *departs from* a house — that is *leave* — and the verb belongs to airports, timetables, and briefs.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic in both the travel sense (*the 6:12 departs from Track 9*) and the figurative one (*the opinion departs from settled precedent*).
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** — no object at all (✗ *depart the practice* in this sense), and *depart* stands alone with a time: *the train departs at six*. Note *depart for* is the opposite trip.
**Passive:** **restricted.** Legal and technical prose allows it — *thirty years of precedent were departed from in two paragraphs* — but the travel sense refuses: ✗ *Track 9 was departed from*.

Leave a place, or break with an established practice or text.

1. The 6:12 to Chicago *departs from* Track 9.
2. The opinion *departs from* thirty years of settled precedent in two paragraphs.
3. He never *departs from* the recipe, which is why the bread is always the same.
4. The translation *departs from* the original in ways the preface never admits.
5. Delegates *departed from* the capital before the vote was certified.
6. Any *departure from* the protocol has to be logged.

**See also:** [deviate from](#deviate-from) &middot; [diverge from](#diverge-from) &middot; [differ from](#differ-from) &middot; [derogate from](#derogate-from) &middot; [from](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#from)

### depend on

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈpɛnd ɑn/ &middot; *dih-PEND on* &middot; weak /dɪˈpɛnd ən/
**Русский:** зависеть от; полагаться на — определяться чем-то или доверять кому-то
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase, pronoun, or **gerund** (*depend on winning*) &middot; also a wh-clause (*it depends on what she says*) — **never a to-infinitive** and **never a that-clause**. A to-infinitive is correct only after a person: *depend on him to be late*.
**Pattern:** [subject] *depend on* [noun phrase] &middot; *depend on* [someone] *to* [verb] &middot; *depend on* [verb-ing] &middot; *it depends on* [wh-clause]
**Variants:** *depend upon* — identical in meaning, more formal and more common in writing
**Contrast:** ***Depend of* and *depend from* are errors** — the preposition is fixed. Bare *depend* with no preposition works only in the elliptical *It depends.* Note the two senses: *depend on someone* (rely on) versus *depend on something* (be determined by), which is why *That depends on the weather* is not about trust. Against its neighbors, *rely on* is the neutral synonym of the first sense only, and *count on* the warm spoken one; neither carries the be-determined-by sense.
**Verb type:** **stative** in both senses — trusting and being determined by. The rely sense takes the progressive (*she has three kids depending on her*); the be-determined-by sense does not: ✗ *how long it takes is depending on traffic*.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** with a fixed preposition — no bare object (✗ *depend him*), though *depend on someone to do something* adds an infinitive after the person. Bare *depend* survives only in *It depends.*
**Passive:** **restricted** by sense. The rely sense passivizes: *he can be depended on to be five minutes late*. The be-determined-by sense cannot: ✗ *traffic is depended on by how long it takes*.

Rely on, or be determined by.

1. The whole schedule *depends on* whether the permit clears.
2. She has three kids *depending on* her.
3. You can *depend on* him to be five minutes late.
4. How long it takes *depends on* traffic.
5. Funding for next year *depends upon* the audit.
6. Everything *depends on* getting the roof done before November.

**See also:** [rely on](#rely-on) &middot; [count on](#count-on) &middot; [believe in](#believe-in) &middot; [on](../../06-prepositions/catalog/01-core-in-on-at.md#on) &middot; [depending on](../../06-prepositions/catalog/12-marginal-participial.md#depending-on)

### deprive of

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈpraɪv əv/ &middot; *dih-PRIVE uv* &middot; weak /dɪˈpraɪv ə/
**Русский:** лишать чего-то — отнимать или не давать иметь
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal to neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*deprived of seeing his kids*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *deprived of to see them*) and never a clause. The person deprived is a direct object: *deprive someone of something*, never ✗ *deprive of someone something*.
**Pattern:** *deprive* [someone] *of* [something] &middot; *be deprived of* [something] &middot; *sleep-deprived*, *oxygen-deprived* (compounds)
**Contrast:** ***Deprive from* is an error.** Compare the family: *deprive someone **of*** takes something away that they had or needed; *rob someone **of*** does the same thing with more violence or unfairness; *strip someone **of*** a title or a rank is formal and public. All three keep *of*. Contrast *prevent someone **from** doing*, which blocks an action rather than removing a thing — a different preposition because a different structure. The past participle has become a productive compound: *sleep-deprived*, *deprived neighborhoods*.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic; an act or event of taking something away: *the blackout deprived half the county of power*.
**Transitivity:** **transitive** — the person deprived is the direct object and *of* names what is taken; the order is fixed: ✗ *deprive of someone something*.
**Passive:** **yes**, and the passive is the commonest form: *he was deprived of counsel for the first thirty-six hours*, *deprived of sunlight, the seedlings went pale*.

Take something away from someone, or keep them from having it.

1. The blackout *deprived* half the county *of* power for three days.
2. He was *deprived of* counsel for the first thirty-six hours.
3. A newborn *deprives* both parents *of* sleep for about a year.
4. The redistricting *deprived* the neighborhood *of* a representative it had had since 1974.
5. *Deprived of* sunlight, the seedlings went pale and leggy.
6. She refused to *deprive* herself *of* one small pleasure a day.

**See also:** [rob of](#rob-of) &middot; [prevent from](#prevent-from) &middot; [protect from](#protect-from-protect-against) &middot; [save from](#save-from) &middot; [of](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#of)

### derive from

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈraɪv frʌm/ &middot; *dih-RIVE from* &middot; weak /dɪˈraɪv frəm/
**Русский:** происходить от; извлекать из — брать начало в источнике
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral, leaning formal — etymology, finance, and academic prose
**Complement:** *from* takes a noun phrase naming a source, or a **gerund** (*derives from being the only supplier in the county*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *derives from to be the only supplier*) and never a clause. Two frames share the verb: transitive *derive* [pleasure, income, authority] *from* [a source], and intransitive *derive from*, where the subject is the thing that came out of the source.
**Pattern:** *derive* [something] *from* [a source] &middot; [something] *derive from* [an origin] &middot; *be derived from* [a source] &middot; *a derivative of* [something] — the noun takes *of*
**Contrast:** ***Derive from* is about where something came from; *deduce from* is about how you reasoned.** A word *derives from* Latin, but a conclusion is *deduced from* the evidence, and swapping them is a real error in student writing. Against the neighbors: *stem from* points at a cause (*the delay stems from a supply problem*), *result from* names the outcome of an event, and *follow from* is strictly logical. Note the noun switches prepositions — *a derivative **of** morphine*, never ✗ *a derivative from morphine*.
**Verb type:** **stative** in the origin sense — no progressive: ✗ *the word is deriving from Old French*. **Action (dynamic)** in the transitive sense of extracting: *she derives satisfaction from labeling things*.
**Transitivity:** **both** — intransitive when the subject is the thing that came out of the source (*most of the revenue derives from one tax*), transitive when someone takes something from a source: *derive income from a trust*.
**Passive:** **restricted.** Only the transitive frame passivizes, and it does so constantly: *the drug is derived from a fungus*. The intransitive origin sense has no passive: ✗ *Old French is derived from by the word*.

Come from a source, or take something from a source.

1. Most of the county's revenue *derives from* a single property tax.
2. The word *derives from* an Old French verb meaning to trap.
3. She *derives* an unusual amount of satisfaction *from* labeling things.
4. The board's authority *derives from* a 1974 ordinance nobody has read since.
5. Their advantage *derives from* being the only supplier within four hundred miles.
6. The drug is *derived from* a fungus that grows on rye.

**See also:** [stem from](#stem-from) &middot; [result in, result from](#result-in-result-from) &middot; [deduce from](#deduce-from) &middot; [follow from](#follow-from) &middot; [from](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#from)

### derogate from

**Pronunciation:** /ˈdɛrəɡeɪt frʌm/ &middot; *DAIR-uh-gayt from* &middot; weak /ˈdɛrəɡeɪt frəm/
**Русский:** умалять, ущемлять (право, норму) — юр., оговорка в договорах
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal and legal — contract boilerplate, treaties, and judicial opinions. An American meets it in one place above all: the clause reading *nothing in this Agreement shall derogate from the rights of either party*. It is not a word anyone says out loud.
**Complement:** noun phrase naming a right, an authority, a standard, or a reputation. **No gerund, no infinitive, no clause** in real use: ✗ *derogate from being paid*. The verb has no everyday intransitive life; the fixed legal phrase [in derogation of](../../06-prepositions/catalog/11-complex-multiword.md#in-derogation-of) covers the same ground as a complex preposition.
**Pattern:** *derogate from* [a right, an obligation, an authority] &middot; *nothing herein shall derogate from* [something] &middot; *derogate from* [someone's reputation, achievement] — literary &middot; *in derogation of* [a right] — the fixed phrase
**Contrast:** **Do not reach for it when you mean *denigrate*.** *Derogate from* takes something away from a right or a standard; *denigrate* attacks a person's character, and it is transitive: *denigrated his work*, never ✗ *denigrated from his work*. The plain-English equivalents are *detract from* and *take away from*, and either is better anywhere outside a contract. The adjective *derogatory* belongs to the *denigrate* side of the family, which is exactly why the verb gets misused.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic in form but abstract in effect, a drafting term for cutting into a right; the progressive is rare outside participles: *a clause derogating from the warranty*.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** with a fixed *from* — no direct object (✗ *derogate the tenant's remedies*), and no everyday objectless use; the noun phrase *in derogation of* covers the same ground.
**Passive:** **no** in real use: ✗ *the tenant's statutory remedies shall not be derogated from* is not how drafters write it — they keep the active *nothing shall derogate from*. For a passive, switch to *be diminished* or *be waived*.

Take away from a right, a rule, or a standard — chiefly in legal drafting.

1. Nothing in this section shall *derogate from* the tenant's statutory remedies.
2. The amendment does not *derogate from* the treaty's core protections.
3. Counsel argued that the waiver *derogated from* rights the legislature made non-waivable.
4. No local ordinance may *derogate from* the state minimum.
5. None of this *derogates from* the achievement of the people who built it.
6. The court refused to read the clause as *derogating from* the warranty.

**See also:** [depart from](#depart-from) &middot; [desist from](#desist-from) &middot; [divest of](#divest-of) &middot; [in derogation of](../../06-prepositions/catalog/11-complex-multiword.md#in-derogation-of) &middot; [from](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#from)

### desist from

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈzɪst frʌm/ &middot; *dih-ZIST from* &middot; weak /dɪˈzɪst frəm/
**Русский:** прекращать что-то делать — обычно по требованию; юр. (cease and desist)
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal and legal. In American life it is nearly a one-phrase word: the *cease and desist* letter, and the injunction ordering a party to *cease and desist from* something. Using it conversationally sounds like a joke about lawyers.
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*desist from contacting her*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *desist from to contact her*) and never a clause. The verb also stands alone with no object (*the picketing did not desist*), where nothing follows it at all.
**Pattern:** *desist from* [conduct] &middot; *desist from* [verb-ing] &middot; *cease and desist from* [verb-ing] &middot; *desist* — intransitive
**Contrast:** ***Refrain from* is the neutral version and *stop* is what people actually say.** All three take the same complement — *refrain from smoking*, *desist from smoking* — but *desist* implies an order already given, usually in writing, while *refrain* is a request or a personal choice. Its formal cousin *abstain from* is about deliberately going without something (food, alcohol, a vote). None of them takes an infinitive.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic; the ceasing of an activity, usually after an order: *the letter demanded that he desist from using the logo*.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** — no direct object (✗ *desist the excavation*), and the verb stands alone: *the picketing did not desist*.
**Passive:** **no.** The prepositional passive does not occur: ✗ *the practice was desisted from*, ✗ *the witnesses were desisted from contacting*. The order itself goes passive instead: *he was ordered to cease and desist*.

Stop doing something, usually because you have been told to.

1. The letter demanded that he *desist from* using the company's logo.
2. The court ordered the contractor to cease and *desist from* further excavation.
3. She was told to *desist from* contacting the witnesses directly.
4. The agency asked two clinics to *desist from* advertising the treatment as a cure.
5. Neither side would *desist from* the filings long enough to talk.
6. They *desisted from* the practice only after the fine doubled.

**See also:** [refrain from](#refrain-from) &middot; [prevent from](#prevent-from) &middot; [discourage from](#discourage-from) &middot; [derogate from](#derogate-from) &middot; [from](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#from)

### deviate from

**Pronunciation:** /ˈdiviˌeɪt frʌm/ &middot; *DEE-vee-ayt from* &middot; weak /ˈdiviˌeɪt frəm/
**Русский:** отклоняться от — отступать от курса, правила или стандарта
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal — protocols, procedures, statistics
**Complement:** noun phrase naming a plan, a script, a protocol, a norm, or a value. **A gerund is possible but uncommon** (*deviated from following the checklist*); **no to-infinitive** (✗ *deviate from to follow the checklist*) and no clause. The verb is otherwise intransitive: *the readings deviated sharply* takes no object at all.
**Pattern:** *deviate from* [a plan, the script, the mean] &middot; *deviate* — intransitive &middot; *a deviation from* [the norm] — the noun keeps *from* &middot; *standard deviation* — the fixed statistical term
**Contrast:** ***Deviate from* implies there was a rule to leave.** You *deviate from* a protocol, a flight path, or an agreed script — something prescribed. *Depart from* is the same move made deliberately and often defensibly (*depart from precedent*); *diverge from* is a gradual parting of two things that started together; *differ from* is not movement at all, just unlikeness. And the noun of *deviate* is *deviation*: *a deviation **from** the norm*, never ✗ *a deviation of the norm*.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic, a step off a required path; progressive fine: *the readings are deviating from the mean again*.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** — no direct object (✗ *deviate the checklist*), and the verb works alone with an adverb: *the readings deviated sharply*.
**Passive:** **restricted.** Clinical and technical writing allows it (*the protocol was deviated from in two cases*), but ordinary prose does not: ✗ *the checklist must not be deviated from*, ✗ *the filed route was deviated from by the pilot*.

Leave a course, a rule, or a standard you were expected to follow.

1. Do not *deviate from* the checklist, even when you are sure of the next step.
2. The pilot *deviated from* the filed route to get around the weather.
3. This year's numbers *deviate from* the ten-year average by about four percent.
4. He gave the same talk for a decade and never once *deviated from* it.
5. Any lab that *deviates from* the published method has to say so in the writeup.
6. The remodel *deviated from* the approved drawings in three places.

**See also:** [depart from](#depart-from) &middot; [diverge from](#diverge-from) &middot; [differ from](#differ-from) &middot; [derogate from](#derogate-from) &middot; [from](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#from)

### devolve on, devolve upon

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈvɑlv ɑn/ &middot; *dih-VAHLV on* &middot; weak /dɪˈvɑlv ən/
**Русский:** переходить к кому-то по наследству или по закону; юр./книжн.
