**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal and legal — wills, constitutions, and successions; an American meets it in an estate document or a line about who takes over when an office falls vacant
**Complement:** noun phrase naming the person or body that receives the duty, estate, or power. **No gerund, no infinitive, no clause**: ✗ *the duty devolved on running the office*. The subject is the thing handed down — a duty, a title, an estate, an authority — not the person taking it.
**Variants:** ***Devolve upon*** is the same pattern in a more formal key, and older drafting prefers it. ***Devolve to*** appears where the point is transfer downward to a smaller unit — *powers devolved to the states*, *devolved government*. Keep all three apart from ***devolve into***, which is the everyday word meaning degenerate.
**Pattern:** [a duty, an estate, a title] *devolve on* / *upon* [someone] &middot; [powers] *be devolved to* [a body] &middot; *devolve into* [chaos, a shouting match] — a different sense &middot; *devolution of* [power] — the noun
**Contrast:** ***Devolve on* is passive inheritance; *delegate to* is a deliberate handoff.** A responsibility *devolves on* whoever is left standing; a manager *delegates* it *to* somebody chosen. And *devolve into* is not this word at all — *the meeting devolved into a shouting match* means it deteriorated, and swapping the prepositions produces nonsense: ✗ *the chairmanship devolved into the vice chair*. In plain American English the whole first sense is usually *fall to*: *the job fell to me*.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic, an event of passing down by succession or default: *on his death the estate devolved on his sister*. Keep it apart from *devolve into*, meaning degenerate.
**Transitivity:** **both** by frame — intransitive with *on* / *upon*, where the duty or estate is the subject (✗ *his sister devolved on the estate*); transitive in *devolve powers to a body*, where the power is an object.
**Passive:** **restricted.** Only the *devolve X to Y* frame passivizes, and there it is standard: *substantial powers were devolved to the regional assemblies*. The *on* frame refuses: ✗ *his sister was devolved on*, ✗ *the ranking member was devolved upon*.

Pass down to somebody by default, succession, or operation of law.

1. On his death the estate *devolved on* his sister.
2. If the chair resigns, the duties *devolve upon* the ranking member.
3. Responsibility for the audit *devolved on* the one accountant who had not quit.
4. Under the 1998 act, substantial powers were *devolved to* the regional assemblies.
5. The title *devolves upon* the eldest surviving child, whatever the will says.
6. Everything the founder used to handle personally has *devolved on* a committee.

**See also:** [impose on, impose upon](#impose-on-impose-upon) &middot; [preside over](#preside-over) &middot; [inhere in](#inhere-in) &middot; [belong to](#belong-to) &middot; [upon](../../06-prepositions/catalog/01-core-in-on-at.md#upon)

### die of, die from

**Pronunciation:** /ˈdaɪ əv/ &middot; *DYE uv* &middot; weak /ˈdaɪ ə/
**Русский:** умирать от — предлог называет причину смерти
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*died from falling*) — never a to-infinitive, never a clause. *Die* also takes *for* (a cause), *in* (an event or place), and *by* in a small set of fixed phrases (*die by suicide*, *die by the sword*).
**Pattern:** *die of* [a disease, a condition] &middot; *die from* [an injury, a cause, complications] &middot; *die for* [a cause] &middot; *die in* [a crash, a fire, a war] &middot; *be dying to* [verb] — idiom, infinitive
**Contrast:** ***Die of* and *die from* overlap heavily in American English** and reversing them will not confuse anyone. The tendency, not a rule: *of* goes with diseases and internal conditions (*died of pancreatic cancer*, *died of a heart attack*), *from* with external causes, injuries, and chains of consequence (*died from his injuries*, *died from complications of surgery*). *Die by* is restricted — *by suicide*, *by fire*, *by his own hand* — and *died by suicide* is now the preferred American phrasing over the older *committed suicide*. Note the hyperboles, which take *of*: *dying of thirst*, *died of embarrassment*.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic; an event, not a state, and the progressive is normal both literally (*he was dying from the injuries*) and in hyperbole: *I'm dying of thirst*.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** — no object of any kind; the cause is always in a prepositional phrase (*of*, *from*, *in*, *by*, *for*). ✗ *he died emphysema*.
**Passive:** **no** — intransitive, so there is no passive at all: ✗ *emphysema was died of*, ✗ *smoke inhalation was died from by two firefighters*. For an agent, switch verbs: *two firefighters were killed by smoke inhalation*.

Stop living — and the preposition names the cause.

1. Her grandfather *died of* emphysema at eighty-one.
2. Two firefighters *died from* smoke inhalation.
3. He nearly *died of* embarrassment when the microphone stayed on.
4. Thousands *died from* the complications rather than the infection itself.
5. She *died in* a crash on the interstate.
6. I'm *dying of* thirst — is there anything cold in there?

**See also:** [suffer from](#suffer-from) &middot; [recover from](#recover-from) &middot; [result from](#result-in-result-from) &middot; [save from](#save-from)

### differ from

**Pronunciation:** /ˈdɪfɚ frʌm/ &middot; *DIF-er from* &middot; weak /ˈdɪfɚ frəm/
**Русский:** отличаться от — быть непохожим; differ with расходиться во мнениях
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal to neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase only — **no gerund, no clause**. *Differ* is intransitive and never takes an object: ✗ *differ the other model*. In the disagree sense it takes *with*: *I differ with her on that*. The related adjective takes its own preposition — see the contrast.
**Pattern:** [A] *differ from* [B] &middot; [A] *differ from* [B] *in* [respect] &middot; *differ with* [someone] *on* / *about* [something] &middot; *differ* (plural subject, alone): *opinions differ*
**Variants:** *different from* — the safe standard everywhere &middot; *different than* — established American, and the natural form before a clause &middot; *different to* — British; avoid in American writing
**Contrast:** **The adjective is where the trouble is.** *Different **from*** is standard everywhere and always safe. *Different **than*** is fully established in American English, and is the natural choice before a clause (*a different city than I remembered*), where *from* forces an awkward rewrite. *Different **to*** is British and sounds wrong to American ears. The verb itself allows only *from* for unlikeness — ✗ *differs than*, ✗ *differs to* — and *with* only when people disagree: *the two economists differ with each other about the cause*.
**Verb type:** **stative** — unlikeness is a static fact, not an event, and the progressive is out: ✗ *this year's model is differing from last year's*.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** — never takes an object (✗ *differ the other model*); with a plural subject the preposition can drop: *opinions differ*. Disagreement takes *with*: *I differ with him about the timing*.
**Passive:** **no.** Stative and objectless, it has no passive: ✗ *last year's model is differed from*, ✗ *mine is differed from by her reading of the statute*.

Be unlike something else — or, with *with*, disagree.

1. This year's model *differs from* last year's in almost every respect.
2. American practice *differs from* the European standard here.
3. The two accounts *differ from* each other on every material point.
4. Her reading of the statute *differs from* mine *in* one important way.
5. I *differ with* him about how much of this the public needs to see.
6. On the question of timing, reasonable people *differ*.

**See also:** [compare with](#compare-to-compare-with) &middot; [separate from](#separate-from) &middot; [compete with](#compete-with-compete-against-compete-for) &middot; [different from](../../06-prepositions/catalog/09-comparison-opposition.md#different-from) &middot; [from](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#from)

### disapprove of

**Pronunciation:** /ˌdɪsəˈpruv əv/ &middot; *diss-uh-PROOV uv* &middot; weak /ˌdɪsəˈpruv ə/
**Русский:** не одобрять — считать неправильным или неприемлемым
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral, slightly formal
**Complement:** noun phrase, **gerund**, or possessive plus gerund (*disapproved of her going*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *disapprove of to go*) and **never a that-clause**.
**Pattern:** *disapprove of* [noun phrase] &middot; *disapprove of* [verb-ing] &middot; *disapprove of* [someone] *-ing* &middot; *disapprove* (alone) &middot; *a disapproving look*
**Contrast:** **Unlike *approve*, *disapprove* has no ordinary transitive use.** *The board approved the budget* is authorization; ✗ *The board disapproved the budget* is not the mirror image American editors want — write *rejected* or *voted down*. So *disapprove* survives almost entirely in the *of* frame, where it means think badly of. Note the register: it is a mild, slightly prim word, which is why *she disapproves of him* is funnier than *she dislikes him*.
**Verb type:** **stative** — a settled attitude rather than an act; the progressive is marginal (?*she is disapproving of the arrangement*), though the participle is common as an adjective: *a disapproving look*.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** with a fixed *of* — and, unlike *approve*, it has no ordinary transitive use: ✗ *the board disapproved the budget* (write *rejected*). The verb can stand alone: *she disapproves, loudly*.
**Passive:** **restricted.** The prepositional passive is grammatical but stiff — ?*his taking the money was disapproved of* — and the participle survives mainly as a modifier: *a disapproved-of arrangement*. ✗ *he is disapproved of by my mother* is best rewritten.

Think that something is wrong or unacceptable.

1. My mother *disapproved of* the whole arrangement and said so once, at length.
2. He *disapproves of* eating at your desk.
3. The faculty *disapproved of* the change but voted for it anyway.
4. She *disapproved of* his taking the money.
5. Nobody actually *disapproves of* a shorter meeting.
6. They *disapprove of* almost everything the new coach does.

**See also:** [approve of](#approve-of) &middot; [object to](#object-to) &middot; [complain about](#complain-to-complain-about) &middot; [comment on](#comment-on)

### discourage from

**Pronunciation:** /dɪsˈkɜrɪdʒ frʌm/ &middot; *diss-KUR-ij from* &middot; weak /dɪsˈkɜrɪdʒ frəm/
**Русский:** отговаривать от — убеждать не делать чего-то; после from герундий
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal
**Complement:** **gerund** or noun phrase (*discouraged him from applying*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *discouraged him to apply*). This is the classic asymmetry: *encourage* takes an infinitive and *discourage* takes *from* plus *-ing*.
**Pattern:** *discourage* [someone] *from* [verb-ing] &middot; *discourage* [an activity] &middot; *be discouraged from* [verb-ing] &middot; *encourage* [someone] *to* [verb] — the mirror pattern
**Contrast:** **The pair does not mirror.** *Encourage her **to** apply* / *discourage her **from** applying* — same idea, opposite structures, and ✗ *discourage her to apply* is one of the most persistent advanced errors in English. The same shape governs *prevent from*, *stop from*, *keep from*, *ban from*, *prohibit from*, and *bar from*: all of them take *from* plus *-ing* while their positive counterparts take an infinitive. Note also that *discourage* has a plain transitive use meaning dishearten (*the results discouraged her*), which takes no preposition.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic; an act of persuading someone away from something: *steep fees discourage small filers from appealing*. The plain transitive sense (dishearten) is dynamic too: *the results discouraged her*.
**Transitivity:** **transitive** — the person is the direct object and *from* plus *-ing* follows; the object is obligatory: ✗ *the policy discourages from driving downtown*.
**Passive:** **yes**, and the passive is very common: *she was discouraged from applying by an advisor who barely knew her*, *filers are discouraged from appealing at all*.

Try to persuade someone not to do something.

1. The counselor *discouraged* him *from* taking five APs at once.
2. Steep fees *discourage* small filers *from* appealing at all.
3. She was *discouraged from* applying by an advisor who barely knew her.
4. Nothing *discourages* a reader *from* finishing a book like a bad first paragraph.
5. The policy is designed to *discourage* people *from* driving downtown.
6. Don't let one rejection *discourage* you *from* sending it out again.

**See also:** [prevent from](#prevent-from) &middot; [punish for](#punish-for) &middot; [allow for](#allow-for) &middot; [protect from](#protect-from-protect-against)

### dispose of

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈspoʊz ʌv/ &middot; *dih-SPOHZ uv* &middot; weak /dɪˈspoʊz əv/
**Русский:** избавляться от; распродавать; окончательно решать (дело); книжн. к get rid of
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal — waste rules, estate documents, and court dockets
**Complement:** noun phrase naming what is being got rid of, sold, or settled: *dispose of the packaging*, *dispose of the assets*, *dispose of the motion*. **A gerund is rare and stiff** (*dispose of having to store it*), and there is **no infinitive and no clause**: ✗ *dispose of to sell the house*. The preposition is fixed and the object never moves: ✗ *dispose the trash of*.
**Pattern:** *dispose of* [waste, property, a body, an argument, a case] &middot; *be disposed of* [properly, at auction] &middot; *dispose* [someone] *to* [something] — a different verb &middot; *be disposed to* [verb] — an adjective with an **infinitive** &middot; *at* [someone's] *disposal* — the noun
**Contrast:** ***Dispose of* is the formal twin of *get rid of***, and it does three jobs: throw away (*dispose of the solvent*), sell off (*dispose of the property*), and settle for good (*the court disposed of the appeal in a footnote*). Keep it clear of *be disposed to*, which is an adjective meaning inclined and takes an **infinitive**: *disposed to agree*, never ✗ *disposed to agreeing*. And the noun is *disposal* — *the disposal **of** hazardous waste*, *the money at your disposal*.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic in all three senses: throwing away, selling off, and settling a matter for good (*the panel disposed of the objection in two sentences*).
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** with a fixed *of* — nothing may come between verb and preposition (✗ *dispose the trash of*), and the *of* is not optional: ✗ *dispose the old servers*. Keep it clear of the adjective *be disposed to* + infinitive.
**Passive:** **yes** — the prepositional passive is standard: *paint and motor oil must be disposed of at the transfer station*, *the appeal was disposed of in a footnote*.

Get rid of something, sell it off, or settle a matter finally.

1. There is a fee for *disposing of* paint and motor oil.
2. The estate had to *dispose of* the house before the taxes came due.
3. The panel *disposed of* the objection in two sentences.
4. Nobody could say how the company *disposed of* the old servers.
5. She *disposed of* her shares the week before the merger closed.
6. He *disposed of* the last three questions without looking at his notes.

**See also:** [get rid of](#get-rid-of) &middot; [divest of](#divest-of) &middot; [deprive of](#deprive-of) &middot; [take care of](#take-care-of) &middot; [of](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#of)

### diverge from

**Pronunciation:** /daɪˈvɚdʒ frʌm/ &middot; *dye-VURJ from* &middot; weak /dɪˈvɚdʒ frəm/
**Русский:** расходиться с — начать отличаться от того, чему прежде соответствовал
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal — mathematics, data, narrative, and commentary
**Complement:** noun phrase naming a path, a text, a norm, or another account. **A gerund is unidiomatic** (✗ *diverged from following the trail*), and there is **no infinitive and no clause**. The verb is very often intransitive with a plural subject and no preposition at all: *the two roads diverge*, *their estimates diverge sharply*.
**Pattern:** *diverge from* [a route, an account, the consensus] &middot; [two things] *diverge* — intransitive &middot; *diverge on* / *over* [a question] — disagree about &middot; *a divergence from* [the mean] &middot; *divergent* [views]
**Contrast:** **Four verbs, one preposition, four pictures.** *Diverge from* is a gradual parting of two things that started together; *deviate from* is a step off a path you were required to keep to; *depart from* is a deliberate break with a practice or a text; *differ from* is not motion at all, only unlikeness. So *the trail diverges from the creek* (they separate), *the driver deviated from the route* (he broke a rule), *the opinion departs from precedent* (a considered break), *my copy differs from yours* (a static fact). Its opposite is *converge*, which takes *on* or *toward* rather than *from*.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic where two things gradually part (*the bike path diverges from the highway*), **stative** where it reports a fixed unlikeness: *her account diverges from his in every detail*.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** — no object (✗ *diverge the route*), and with a plural subject the preposition drops entirely: *where the sources diverge, the footnote says so*.
**Passive:** **no.** There is nothing to promote: ✗ *the highway is diverged from by the bike path*, ✗ *the model's forecast was diverged from*.

Move apart, or come to differ, from something you started out matching.

1. The bike path *diverges from* the highway just past the reservoir.
2. Her account *diverges from* his in every detail that matters.
3. After 1994 the two economies *diverged from* one another for good.
4. This year's figures *diverge from* the model's forecast by a wide margin.
5. The screenplay *diverges from* the novel about halfway through.
6. Where the sources *diverge*, the footnote says so.

**See also:** [deviate from](#deviate-from) &middot; [depart from](#depart-from) &middot; [differ from](#differ-from) &middot; [derive from](#derive-from) &middot; [from](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#from)

### divest of

**Pronunciation:** /daɪˈvɛst ʌv/ &middot; *dye-VEST uv* &middot; weak /dɪˈvɛst əv/
**Русский:** лишать(ся) чего-то, продавать (активы, доли); фин./юр.
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal — securities filings, trust documents, and university endowment fights; the literary sense (*divested of illusions*) belongs to essays rather than speech
**Complement:** a person, company, or fund is the object, and *of* takes a noun phrase naming what is given up: holdings, powers, a title, illusions. **A gerund is rare** (*divested itself of having to run the plant*), and there is **no infinitive and no clause**. The reflexive frame is extremely common: *divest itself of the subsidiary*.
**Variants:** ***Divest from*** — *the trustees voted to divest from fossil fuels* — has become standard American usage in the divestment-campaign sense, where the object of the verb disappears and *from* names the sector being exited. Older editors still prefer *divest itself of*, but *divest from* is now the ordinary way the argument is phrased.
**Pattern:** *divest* [someone / a fund] *of* [holdings, power, a title] &middot; *divest oneself of* [something] &middot; *be divested of* [something] &middot; *divest from* [a sector] &middot; *divestiture* / *divestment of* [assets] — the nouns keep *of*
**Contrast:** ***Divest of* removes something formally; *deprive of* takes it away against your interest.** A board *divests itself of* a money-losing division on purpose; a storm *deprives* a town *of* power. *Rob of* and *strip of* are the hostile members of the same family, and *get rid of* is the plain-English one. Note that the *of* is not optional in the transitive frame: ✗ *divested the subsidiary* means something quite different — that you sold the subsidiary itself.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic, a deliberate and usually formal act: *the company divested itself of three regional papers in a single quarter*.
**Transitivity:** **transitive** — the person, fund, or company is the direct object (very often a reflexive) and *of* names what is given up; the *of* phrase is not optional: ✗ *divested the subsidiary* means something else. *Divest from* a sector drops the object.
**Passive:** **yes**: *the board was divested of any say in the appointment*, *once divested of its patents, the firm was worth very little*.

Give up a holding, a power, or an illusion — usually deliberately and formally.

1. The company *divested itself of* three regional papers in a single quarter.
2. Cabinet nominees are expected to *divest* themselves *of* conflicting holdings.
3. The statute *divested* the board *of* any say in the appointment.
4. Two decades on the road had *divested* him *of* every romantic idea about touring.
5. The endowment finally voted to *divest from* coal and tar sands.
6. Once *divested of* its patents, the firm was worth very little.

**See also:** [deprive of](#deprive-of) &middot; [rob of](#rob-of) &middot; [dispose of](#dispose-of) &middot; [get rid of](#get-rid-of) &middot; [of](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#of)

### draw on, draw upon

**Pronunciation:** /ˈdrɔ ɑn/ &middot; *DRAW on* &middot; weak /ˈdrɔ ən/
**Русский:** черпать из — использовать запас денег, опыта или материала как источник
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal — scholarship, journalism, and banking (*draw on a line of credit*)
**Complement:** noun phrase naming a resource — savings, experience, sources, a reserve — or a **gerund** (*drew on having run a kitchen for ten years*). **No to-infinitive** (✗ *draw on to have run a kitchen*) and no clause. The preposition is fixed, not a movable particle: ✗ *draw my savings on*, ✗ *draw it on*.
**Variants:** ***Draw upon*** is the same pattern one register higher, common in academic prose and prefaces; the meaning is identical.
**Pattern:** *draw on* / *upon* [a resource, an account, experience] &middot; *draw* [something] *from* [a source] &middot; *draw down* [a balance] &middot; *draw on* — intransitive, of time (*as evening drew on*)
**Contrast:** **Do not confuse the fixed *draw on* with the separable phrasal verbs.** *Draw up a contract*, *draw out a meeting*, and *draw in a crowd* all allow the object to move — *draw the contract up* — which is what makes their second words particles rather than prepositions; those belong with the phrasal verbs, not here. This *draw on* never separates. Note the sister frame *draw* [something] *from* [a source] — *drew her material from letters* — where the resource follows *from* and what you took becomes the object. And *as evening drew on* is a third, intransitive verb meaning approach.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic; using up a store of money, material, or experience: *they had to draw on savings for the second year of treatment*. The unrelated intransitive *as evening drew on* means approach.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** with a fixed preposition — the object never moves (✗ *draw my savings on*, ✗ *draw it on*), which is what separates it from the separable *draw up* / *draw out*. The sister frame *draw something from a source* is transitive.
**Passive:** **yes** — the prepositional passive is natural in reports: *the credit line had already been drawn on twice*, *three unpublished diaries are drawn on throughout*.

Use a store of money, experience, or material as a source.

1. The essay *draws on* three unpublished diaries.
2. They had to *draw on* savings for the second year of treatment.
3. She *draws on* twenty years in emergency medicine every time she teaches.
4. The company *drew on* its credit line to make payroll in February.
5. His novels *draw upon* a childhood he describes only obliquely.
6. The report *draws on* interviews with forty-one former employees.

**See also:** [rely on](#rely-on) &middot; [depend on](#depend-on) &middot; [count on](#count-on) &middot; [derive from](#derive-from) &middot; [on](../../06-prepositions/catalog/01-core-in-on-at.md#on)

### dream about, dream of

**Pronunciation:** /ˈdrim əˌbaʊt/ &middot; *DREEM uh-bowt* &middot; weak /ˈdrim əbət/
**Русский:** видеть во сне (about); мечтать о (of)
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*dreamed of playing at Carnegie Hall*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *dream of to play there*). A clause takes no preposition: *dreamed that the house was on fire*. American English prefers *dreamed*; *dreamt* is chiefly British.
**Pattern:** *dream about* [what happened in a dream] &middot; *dream of* [an ambition] &middot; *dream of* / *about* [verb-ing] &middot; *dream that* [clause] &middot; *wouldn't dream of* [verb-ing] &middot; *dream up* [an idea]
**Contrast:** ***Dream about* reports what your sleeping brain did; *dream of* names what your waking self wants.** *I dreamed about my old apartment* is a night's event; *I dream of a corner office* is an ambition. The two overlap freely for daydreaming (*dreaming about* / *of retirement*), but the fixed idiom takes only *of*: *I wouldn't dream of asking* means I would never — note the gerund, since ✗ *wouldn't dream of to ask* is impossible.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic in both senses; the progressive is normal for sleeping (*half the class is dreaming about the exam*) and for wishing: *she's been dreaming of opening a bakery for years*.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** with the fixed prepositions — no bare object here (✗ *dream the old apartment*); a clause takes no preposition (*dreamed that every tooth fell out*), and *dream up an idea* is a separate transitive phrasal verb.
**Passive:** **no.** Neither frame passivizes: ✗ *that house was dreamed about for years*, ✗ *a bakery is dreamed of by her*. Only the attributive participle survives: *the long dreamed-of bakery*.

See something while asleep, or long for something while awake.

1. I *dreamed about* that house for years after we sold it.
2. She *dreams of* opening a bakery somewhere with a real winter.
3. He *dreamed that* every tooth in his head fell out at once.
4. They *dreamed of* going back and never did.
5. I wouldn't *dream of* charging you for that.
6. Half the class *dreams about* the exam the night before it.

**See also:** [hope for](#hope-for) &middot; [long for](#long-for) &middot; [wish for](#wish-for) &middot; [think about](#think-about-think-of) &middot; [plan on](#plan-on)

### dress in

**Pronunciation:** /ˈdrɛs ɪn/ &middot; *DRESS in* &middot; weak /ˈdrɛs ən/
**Русский:** одеваться во что-то — носить одежду определённого вида
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase naming clothing or a color — **no gerund, no clause**. *As* takes a character or role (*dressed as a pirate*), and *up as* is the costume version. *Dress* on its own is intransitive (*dress warmly*) or transitive with a person (*dress the baby*), and takes no preposition either way.
**Pattern:** *be dressed in* [clothing / a color] &middot; *dress in* [a style] &middot; *dress as* / *up as* [a character] &middot; *dress* [someone] &middot; *dress for* [an occasion, the weather] &middot; *dress up* / *down*
**Contrast:** ***Dressed in* is about the clothes; *dressed as* is about who you are pretending to be.** *Dressed in black* describes fabric; *dressed as a nun* describes a costume. *Dress **for*** names the occasion or condition you are dressing to suit (*dress for the weather*, *dress for the job you want*). Note that *dressed with* is wrong for clothing but right for salad — *dressed with a little vinaigrette* — and that *dress down* means both wear casual clothes and reprimand someone.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic when someone does the dressing (*he dresses in the same three shirts*, *dress the baby*); the far commoner *be dressed in* is a resultant **state**, close to an adjective: *everyone was dressed in black*.
**Transitivity:** **both** — intransitive with a manner or a *for* phrase (*dress warmly*, *dress for the weather*), transitive with a person or a reflexive (*dress the baby*, *she dressed herself*). *In* takes the clothing, never a bare object: ✗ *dress black*.
**Passive:** **yes**, though most examples are stative rather than eventive: *she came to the reading dressed in head-to-toe corduroy* describes a state, while *the child was dressed by her father* is a true action passive.

Wear, or put on, a particular kind of clothing.

1. She came to the reading *dressed in* head-to-toe corduroy.
2. Everyone at the funeral was *dressed in* black except the deceased's brother.
3. He *dresses in* the same three shirts on rotation.
4. The kids came *dressed as* characters from books nobody had read.
5. *Dress for* the weather; it's supposed to drop twenty degrees by four.
6. The waiters were *dressed in* white jackets that had clearly seen better decades.

**See also:** [cover with](#cover-with) &middot; [provide with](#provide-with-provide-for) &middot; [in](../../06-prepositions/catalog/01-core-in-on-at.md#in) &middot; [as](../../06-prepositions/catalog/10-topic-reference-role.md#as)

## E–G

### elaborate on

**Pronunciation:** /ɪˈlæbəreɪt ɑn/ &middot; *ih-LAB-uh-rayt on* &middot; weak /ɪˈlæbəreɪt ən/
**Русский:** развивать мысль о — рассказывать подробнее о уже сказанном
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral, common in interviews, seminars, and reporting
**Complement:** noun phrase (*elaborate on the second point*), **gerund** (*elaborate on being passed over*), or a wh-clause (*elaborate on what went wrong*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *elaborate on to explain the delay*) and never a that-clause: ✗ *elaborate on that the delay was ours*. The verb also stands alone with no object (*Could you elaborate?*), and there it takes no preposition at all.
**Variants:** ***Elaborate upon*** is the formal-register twin, at home in an academic paper or a brief; the meaning is identical, and *on* is what an American says.
**Pattern:** *elaborate on* / *upon* [a point, a claim, a topic] &middot; *elaborate on* [verb-ing] &middot; *elaborate* — intransitive &middot; *elaborate* [a theory, a design] — transitive, meaning develop in detail
**Contrast:** ***Elaborate on* and *expand on* are interchangeable**; *comment on* is a step lighter, meaning simply to say something about a subject rather than to say more about it. Watch two things. First, transitive *elaborate* exists and means something else — *elaborate a theory* is to work it out in detail, not to say more about it. Second, *explain* takes no preposition before the thing explained (*explain the rule*, ✗ *explain on the rule*); the *to* in *explain to me* points at the listener, not the topic. And the adjective *elaborate* is stressed differently: /ɪˈlæbərət/.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic, an act of saying more; progressive fine: *he's elaborating on a point nobody questioned*. The transitive homonym (*elaborate a theory*, work it out in detail) is dynamic too.
**Transitivity:** **both** — intransitive with *on* / *upon* and also with nothing at all (*Could you elaborate?*); transitive in the develop-in-detail sense: *elaborate a design*.
**Passive:** **restricted.** The prepositional passive is acceptable in formal prose — *the second recommendation was elaborated on at length* — and the transitive sense passivizes freely: *a theory elaborated over three decades*.

Say more about something you have already mentioned.

1. Would you *elaborate on* the second recommendation?
2. She declined to *elaborate on* the reasons for her resignation.
3. The memo *elaborates on* a policy that had been one sentence long.
4. He *elaborated on* being the only engineer in a building full of salespeople.
5. The witness was asked to *elaborate on* what he had seen from the porch.
6. I'd rather not *elaborate* in front of the whole committee.

**See also:** [comment on](#comment-on) &middot; [explain to](#explain-to) &middot; [concentrate on](#concentrate-on) &middot; [refer to](#refer-to) &middot; [on](../../06-prepositions/catalog/01-core-in-on-at.md#on)

### encroach on, encroach upon

**Pronunciation:** /ɪnˈkroʊtʃ ɑn/ &middot; *in-KROHCH on* &middot; weak /ɪnˈkroʊtʃ ən/
**Русский:** посягать на, вторгаться в — понемногу захватывать чужое
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal-leaning — property law, zoning disputes, and civil-liberties commentary. An American most often meets it in a survey report (*the fence encroaches on the neighbor's lot*) or an editorial about government power.
**Complement:** noun phrase naming territory, a right, a domain, or time. **A gerund is possible but uncommon** (*encroaching on having any evening to yourself*); **no to-infinitive** (✗ *encroach on to take his land*) and no clause. The subject is usually a thing — a fence, a program, a deadline — rather than a person acting deliberately.
**Variants:** ***Encroach upon*** is the same pattern in a higher register and is common in judicial writing. The noun keeps the preposition: *an encroachment on* / *upon* [something].
**Pattern:** *encroach on* / *upon* [land, rights, authority, time] &middot; *an encroachment on* [a right] &middot; *encroach* — intransitive, of vegetation or development
**Contrast:** ***Encroach on* is gradual advance across a line; *infringe on* is violation of a right.** A hedge *encroaches on* a driveway inch by inch, but a knockoff *infringes on* a trademark the moment it ships. *Impinge on* is the physics-flavored version (one thing pressing against another), *intrude on* is social (*intrude on a private conversation*), and *interfere with* is the most general. All four resist the direct object: ✗ *encroach the property line*.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic and gradual, which is why the progressive suits it: *sagebrush is encroaching upon what used to be grassland*. The subject is typically a thing, not a deliberate agent.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** — no direct object (✗ *encroach the property line*), like *infringe on*, *impinge on*, and *intrude on*.
**Passive:** **restricted.** The participle is the usable form — *rights constantly encroached upon*, *land encroached on by development* — while a full clause sounds labored: ?*the setback is being encroached on by the new deck*.

Advance gradually onto somebody else's land, rights, or time.

1. The new deck *encroaches on* the setback by almost two feet.
2. He argued that the ordinance *encroaches on* rights the state constitution protects.
3. Work has begun to *encroach on* the one evening a week she keeps for herself.
4. Sagebrush is *encroaching upon* what used to be grassland.
5. The agency's new rule *encroaches on* territory Congress reserved to the states.
6. Each department resented any attempt to *encroach on* its budget.

**See also:** [infringe on, infringe upon](#infringe-on-infringe-upon) &middot; [impose on, impose upon](#impose-on-impose-upon) &middot; [interfere with, interfere in](#interfere-with-interfere-in) &middot; [on](../../06-prepositions/catalog/01-core-in-on-at.md#on)

### escape from

**Pronunciation:** /ɪˈskeɪp frʌm/ &middot; *ih-SKAYP from* &middot; weak /ɪˈskeɪp frəm/
**Русский:** убегать из, спасаться от — вырываться из заточения или опасности
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*escaped from having to testify*) — never a to-infinitive, never a clause. But the verb also has a **transitive** use with no preposition at all: *escape injury*, *escape prosecution*, *escape notice* — and there the *from* would be wrong.
**Pattern:** *escape from* [a place, a captor, a situation] &middot; *escape* [a fate, notice, detection] — no preposition &middot; *escape to* [somewhere] &middot; [something] *escape* [someone] — elude the memory
**Contrast:** ***Escape from* a place, but *escape* a consequence.** You *escape from* prison, a burning car, or a bad marriage — a thing you were physically or literally inside. You *escape* injury, blame, punishment, or someone's notice — no preposition, because you were never inside those. Both are correct; using *from* with the second group (✗ *escaped from injury*) is the error. Note the idiom *it escapes me*, meaning I can't recall it, which is the transitive sense turned around.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic; an event of getting away: *two inmates escaped from the county facility*. The idiom *the name escapes me* is **stative**.
**Transitivity:** **both** — intransitive with *from* before a place or captor, transitive with no preposition before a consequence: *escape serious injury*, *escape notice*; ✗ *escaped from injury*.
**Passive:** **no** in either frame, despite the transitivity: ✗ *serious injury was escaped*, ✗ *the audit's notice had been escaped by the detail*, ✗ *the county facility was escaped from*. Rewrite as *avoided* or *went unnoticed*.

Get away from confinement, danger, or something unpleasant.

1. Two inmates *escaped from* the county facility on a Sunday morning.
2. She *escaped from* the meeting by inventing a dentist appointment.
3. Everyone in the front car *escaped* serious injury.
4. He spent his twenties trying to *escape from* the town he grew up in.
5. The detail had *escaped* everyone's notice until the audit.
6. The name *escapes* me, but I'd know the face.

**See also:** [recover from](#recover-from) &middot; [prevent from](#prevent-from) &middot; [hide from](#hide-from) &middot; [rescue from](#rescue-from) &middot; [save from](#save-from)

### excuse for

**Pronunciation:** /ɪkˈskjuz fɔr/ &middot; *ik-SKYOOZ for* &middot; weak /ɪkˈskjuz fɚ/
**Русский:** прощать за (небольшую провинность) — а excuse from освобождать от обязанности
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** *for* takes a noun phrase or a **gerund** (*excuse me for interrupting*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *excuse me for to interrupt*). *from* takes a duty or requirement. The person excused is a direct object and takes no preposition: ✗ *excuse to me*.
**Pattern:** *excuse* [someone] *for* [something / verb-ing] &middot; *excuse* [someone] *from* [a duty] &middot; *excuse* [someone] &middot; *excuse* [behavior] &middot; *Excuse me* &middot; *there's no excuse for* [something]
**Contrast:** ***Excuse for* forgives; *excuse from* releases.** *The dean excused her for the late filing* means it was forgiven; *The dean excused her from the requirement* means she doesn't have to do it. Both are standard, and mixing them (✗ *excused from the late filing*) says something else. Note that *excuse* takes the person directly — like *forgive* and *thank*, unlike *apologize*, which needs *to*. The noun keeps *for*: *no excuse for that*.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic, a performative act of forgiving or releasing: *the judge excused two jurors from service*.
**Transitivity:** **transitive** — the person is the direct object with no preposition (✗ *excuse to me*), and *for* or *from* follows. *Excuse* also takes a bare thing as object: *you'll have to excuse the mess*.
**Passive:** **yes**, and the passive is the usual frame for the *from* sense: *she was excused from gym for the rest of the semester*, *he was excused for the late filing*.

Forgive a small offense, or release someone from an obligation.

1. *Excuse* me *for* asking, but is any of this actually funded?
2. The judge *excused* two jurors *from* service.
3. You'll have to *excuse* the mess.
4. Nothing *excuses* the way he spoke to her.
5. She was *excused from* gym for the rest of the semester.
6. There is no *excuse for* sending it out unproofed.

**See also:** [forgive for](#forgive-for) &middot; [apologize for](#apologize-to-apologize-for) &middot; [blame for](#blame-for-blame-on) &middot; [punish for](#punish-for) &middot; [thank for](#thank-for)

### explain to

**Pronunciation:** /ɪkˈspleɪn tu/ &middot; *ik-SPLAYN too* &middot; weak /ɪkˈspleɪn tə/
**Русский:** объяснять кому-то — только explain smth to smb, не ✗ explain me smth
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** *to* takes the person hearing it, and the person **always** needs it: ✗ *explain me the rules*. The thing explained is a direct object, a that-clause, or a wh-clause: *explain the rules*, *explain that the rules changed*, *explain how it works*. A gerund is possible as the object (*explain being late*) but a to-infinitive is not.
**Pattern:** *explain* [something] *to* [someone] &middot; *explain to* [someone] *that* / *how* / *why* [clause] &middot; *explain* [something] &middot; *explain* [oneself]
**Contrast:** ***Explain me something* is one of the most persistent errors in English**, because *explain* looks like *tell* and behaves like *say*. *Tell*, *give*, *show*, *send*, and *offer* take the person as a bare indirect object — *tell me the rules* — while *explain*, *say*, *describe*, *suggest*, *mention*, *announce*, *report*, and *introduce* all require *to*: *explain the rules to me*, *say it to me*, *describe it to me*. When the explanation is long, put the person first: *explain to the class why the second method fails*.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic; progressive is normal: *try explaining that to a nine-year-old*.
**Transitivity:** **transitive, but never ditransitive** — the thing explained is the direct object and the hearer must take *to*: *explain the rules to me*, ✗ *explain me the rules*. The object may be a that- or wh-clause: *explained to the board why the pilot ran over budget*.
**Passive:** **yes** — the thing explained promotes: *the billing cycle was explained to me twice*. The hearer never does: ✗ *I was explained the billing cycle*, one of the most persistent errors in English.

Make something clear to someone.

1. Could you *explain* the billing cycle *to* me one more time?
2. She *explained to* the board why the pilot had run over budget.
3. He never *explained* himself and never had to.
4. Try *explaining* that *to* a nine-year-old.
5. The letter *explains to* applicants exactly what documentation is required.
6. Nobody could *explain* the discrepancy *to* the auditors.

**See also:** [introduce to](#introduce-to) &middot; [refer to](#refer-to) &middot; [comment on](#comment-on) &middot; [apologize to](#apologize-to-apologize-for)

### extrapolate from

**Pronunciation:** /ɪkˈstræpəleɪt frʌm/ &middot; *ik-STRAP-uh-layt from* &middot; weak /ɪkˈstræpəleɪt frəm/
**Русский:** экстраполировать из — переносить известную закономерность за пределы данных
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral-technical — statistics, forecasting, lab work, and business planning
**Complement:** *from* takes a noun phrase naming the data or the case you are reasoning out of, or a **gerund** (*extrapolating from having seen two quarters of it*). **No to-infinitive** (✗ *extrapolate from to see two quarters*) and no clause. What is projected may appear as a direct object before *from*: *extrapolate a trend from the sample*.
**Pattern:** *extrapolate from* [data, a sample, one case] &middot; *extrapolate* [a trend, a value] *from* [data] &middot; *extrapolate* [a curve] *to* [a future point] &middot; *extrapolation from* [the data] — the noun keeps *from*
**Contrast:** ***Extrapolate from* projects past the edge of your data; *interpolate* fills in between points you already have.** Against the reasoning verbs: *deduce from* claims certainty from a premise, *infer from* claims probability from evidence, and *extrapolate from* claims a continuation of a pattern — which is why it so often carries a warning: *you can't extrapolate from a single quarter*. Note the second preposition in the technical frame: you extrapolate *from* the known values *to* the unknown one.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic, an act of projection: *forecasters extrapolate from the last five days of ice loss*; progressive and participle both normal.
**Transitivity:** **both** — intransitive with *from* alone (*it's a mistake to extrapolate from one warm winter*), transitive when the projected thing is named: *extrapolate a trend from the sample*.
**Passive:** **yes** in the transitive frame: *the growth curve was extrapolated from eighteen months of billing data*. The prepositional passive is weaker: ?*a single quarter should not be extrapolated from*.

Project a known pattern beyond the evidence you actually have.

1. It's a mistake to *extrapolate from* one unusually warm winter.
2. The team *extrapolated* the growth curve *from* eighteen months of billing data.
3. Nothing in the sample lets you *extrapolate from* the city to the state as a whole.
4. She *extrapolated from* two cases and got the policy badly wrong.
5. Forecasters *extrapolate from* the last five days of ice loss, which is why the numbers move so much.
6. *Extrapolating from* the pilot program, the district projected a shortfall of $4 million.

**See also:** [infer from](#infer-from) &middot; [deduce from](#deduce-from) &middot; [derive from](#derive-from) &middot; [follow from](#follow-from) &middot; [from](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#from)

### face up to, back out of, drop out of, walk out on

**Pronunciation:** /ˈfeɪs ʌp tu/ &middot; *FAYSS up too* &middot; weak /ˈfeɪs əp tə/
**Русский:** признать и принять (face up to); отступиться — back out of, drop out of, walk out on
**Type:** verb + particle + preposition (phrasal-prepositional) &middot; **Register:** informal to neutral — ordinary spoken American English
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** after the final preposition — *face up to being broke*, *backed out of buying the house*, *dropped out of teaching altogether*, *walked out on a twenty-year marriage*. **Never a to-infinitive** (✗ *face up to be broke*, ✗ *back out of to buy it*) and never a clause. Each of the four takes its own kind of object: *face up to* a fact or a failing, *back out of* an agreement, *drop out of* a school or a process, *walk out on* a person or a commitment.
**Pattern:** *face up to* [a fact, a mistake, a debt] &middot; *back out of* [a deal, a wedding, a promise] &middot; *drop out of* [school, a race, the workforce] &middot; *walk out on* [a spouse, a job, a negotiation] &middot; [any of them] + [verb-ing]
**Contrast:** **These are three-part verbs, and the giveaway is that the object cannot move.** With a genuine separable particle you have a choice — *turn the light on* or *turn on the light*, *throw the mail out* or *throw out the mail* — and those belong to the phrasal-verb discussion rather than to this file, because the second word is a particle, not a preposition. Here the whole string is welded together: ✗ *face it up to*, ✗ *back the deal out of*, ✗ *drop school out of*, ✗ *walk his wife out on*. The final word behaves exactly like the dependent prepositions in the rest of this file, which is why a gerund follows it and an infinitive never does. Note the two-part relatives that mean something else: *face the music* (no preposition), *back out* with no object (*he backed out at the last minute*), *drop out* alone (*she dropped out in March*), and *walk out* alone (*the union walked out*).
**Verb type:** action — dynamic, all four; each names a deliberate move, and the progressive is normal: *he's backing out of the deal*, *she's dropping out of the program*.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** three-part verbs — the object follows the final preposition and can never move: ✗ *face it up to*, ✗ *back the deal out of*, ✗ *walk his wife out on*. Each takes its own object type: a fact, an agreement, a school, a person.
**Passive:** **restricted.** *Back out of* and *walk out on* passivize in speech (*the deal was backed out of two days before closing*, *nobody likes being walked out on*); *face up to* and *drop out of* resist: ✗ *the debt was faced up to*, ✗ *the program was dropped out of*.

Confront something unpleasant, or withdraw from a commitment — four fixed three-word verbs on the same frame.

1. At some point he is going to have to *face up to* what the surgery costs.
2. She never *faced up to* being the reason the partnership dissolved.
3. The buyer *backed out of* the deal two days before closing.
4. Half the cohort *dropped out of* the program in the first semester.
5. He *walked out on* the negotiation and did not come back.
6. Nobody *walks out on* a pension that size without a plan.
7. They *backed out of* renovating and sold the place as it stood.

**See also:** [put up with](19-phrasal-m-r.md#put-up-with) &middot; [get away with](17-phrasal-g.md#get-away-with) &middot; [keep up with, catch up with, catch up on](#keep-up-with-catch-up-with-catch-up-on) &middot; [live up to](#live-up-to) &middot; [look up to, look down on](#look-up-to-look-down-on)

### follow from

**Pronunciation:** /ˈfɑloʊ frʌm/ &middot; *FAH-loh from* &middot; weak /ˈfɑloʊ frəm/
**Русский:** следовать из — быть логическим следствием
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal — logic, mathematics, and argument
**Complement:** *from* takes a noun phrase naming a premise, an assumption, or an earlier result, or a **gerund** (*follows from assuming the rate is constant*). **No to-infinitive** and no clause after the preposition. The subject is the conclusion, and the commonest frame puts it in a dummy *it*: *it follows from the first two lemmas that the series converges*.
**Pattern:** [a conclusion] *follow from* [a premise] &middot; *it follows from* [X] *that* [clause] &middot; *it follows that* [clause] &middot; *follow* [a rule, a recipe, a car] — transitive, no preposition
**Contrast:** ***Follow from* is logical consequence, not sequence.** *B follows A* means B comes next; *B follows from A* means B is entailed by A, and the *from* is doing all the work. Keep it apart from transitive *follow* (*follow the instructions*, ✗ *follow to the instructions*) and from *follow up on* (*follow up on the complaint*), which means chase a matter further. The British *follow on from* — *following on from last week's memo* — is not American usage; write *following* or *further to*. Against the neighbors: *result from* names a real-world outcome, *stem from* names a cause, and *follow from* names an entailment.
**Verb type:** **stative** — logical entailment, not an event; the progressive is out: ✗ *the conclusion is following from the premises*.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** — no object (✗ *follow the premises* in this sense, which is a different verb), and the commonest frame is a dummy subject: *it follows from the first two lemmas that the series converges*.
**Passive:** **no.** Stative and objectless: ✗ *the premises are followed from by the conclusion*, ✗ *that one definition is followed from*. Transitive *follow* (*the instructions were followed*) is a different verb.

Be a logical consequence of something already established.

1. Nothing in the report *follows from* the data they actually collected.
2. It *follows from* the first assumption that the payments cannot both be optional.
3. The conclusion *follows from* the premises only if the sample was random.
4. Two corollaries *follow from* the theorem, and neither is obvious.
5. Does it *follow from* his silence that he agreed? It does not.
6. Everything else in the argument *follows from* that one definition.

**See also:** [result in, result from](#result-in-result-from) &middot; [stem from](#stem-from) &middot; [deduce from](#deduce-from) &middot; [infer from](#infer-from) &middot; [from](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#from)

### foreclose on

**Pronunciation:** /fɔrˈkloʊz ɑn/ &middot; *for-KLOHZ on* &middot; weak /fɚˈkloʊz ən/
**Русский:** обращать взыскание на (заложенное имущество); исключать (возможность)
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral — banking, real estate, and the courts
**Complement:** noun phrase naming the property, the loan, or the borrower: *foreclose on the house*, *on the mortgage*, *on the family*. **No gerund** (✗ *foreclose on missing three payments*), no infinitive, and no clause. The verb also appears with no object at all (*the bank moved to foreclose*).
**Pattern:** *foreclose on* [a property, a mortgage, a borrower] &middot; *foreclose* — intransitive &middot; *foreclose* [an option, a possibility, an argument] — transitive, no preposition &middot; *foreclosure on* [a home] — the noun keeps *on*
**Contrast:** **The financial sense takes *on*; the abstract sense usually takes nothing.** A lender *forecloses on* a house, but a ruling *forecloses* an argument — direct object, no preposition — and *the statute forecloses that reading* is the standard legal phrasing. (*Foreclose on the possibility* does occur, and nobody will misunderstand it, but the bare transitive is cleaner.) Note the sequence in ordinary use: a borrower *defaults on* the loan, the bank *forecloses on* the property, and the house is then *disposed of* at auction.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic, a legal process with a date: *the bank foreclosed on the property after eleven months of missed payments*; progressive normal: *the credit union is foreclosing on them*.
**Transitivity:** **both** — intransitive with *on* in the financial sense, and with no object at all (*the bank moved to foreclose*); transitive with no preposition in the abstract sense: *the decision forecloses any argument that the deadline was advisory*.
**Passive:** **yes** in both frames, and the entry uses it: *three houses on that block were foreclosed on in the same year*, *the argument is foreclosed by the decision*.

Take back mortgaged property after the borrower stops paying — or, transitively, rule something out.

1. The bank *foreclosed on* the property after eleven months of missed payments.
2. Three houses on that block were *foreclosed on* in the same year.
3. They refinanced twice to keep the credit union from *foreclosing on* them.
4. Nobody wants to *foreclose on* a family with two kids in the local school.
5. The decision *forecloses* any argument that the deadline was advisory.
6. Filing late does not *foreclose* the claim entirely.

**See also:** [default on](#default-on) &middot; [dispose of](#dispose-of) &middot; [petition for](#petition-for) &middot; [sue for](#sue-for) &middot; [on](../../06-prepositions/catalog/01-core-in-on-at.md#on)

### forgive for

**Pronunciation:** /fɚˈɡɪv fɔr/ &middot; *fer-GIV for* &middot; weak /fɚˈɡɪv fɚ/
**Русский:** прощать за — перестать держать обиду (человек без предлога: forgive me for)
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*forgive her for missing it*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *forgive her for to miss it*). The person forgiven is a direct object: ✗ *forgive to her*. An older two-object frame survives in fixed religious language: *forgive us our trespasses*.
**Pattern:** *forgive* [someone] *for* [something / verb-ing] &middot; *forgive* [someone] &middot; *forgive* [a debt, a loan] &middot; *forgive* [someone] [something] — formal, fixed &middot; *You could be forgiven for* [verb-ing]
**Contrast:** **Like *excuse* and *thank*, *forgive* takes the person directly and the offense with *for*** — ✗ *forgive to someone* and ✗ *forgive about something* are both wrong. Note the specialized financial sense: *forgive a loan* means cancel the obligation, and takes no preposition at all. And note the useful hedge *you could be forgiven for thinking* — always with a gerund — which is how American writers concede a reasonable misreading.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic as an act (*I forgive you*), and the resulting *be forgiven* is a state. Progressive is rare but possible: *she's slowly forgiving him for it*.
**Transitivity:** **transitive** — the person is the direct object with no preposition (✗ *forgive to her*) and the offense takes *for*. A frozen **ditransitive** survives in religious language: *forgive us our trespasses*. The financial sense takes a thing: *forgive a loan*.
**Passive:** **yes**, and one passive is a fixed hedge: *you could be forgiven for thinking the building was abandoned*. Also *he was forgiven for the first two lapses and fired for the third*.

Stop holding something against someone.

1. She never quite *forgave* him *for* missing the funeral.
2. *Forgive* me *for* asking, but how much did that cost?
3. He was *forgiven for* the first two lapses and fired for the third.
4. You could be *forgiven for* thinking the building was abandoned.
5. The lender agreed to *forgive* half the balance.
6. I *forgive* you, but I'm not forgetting it.

**See also:** [excuse for](#excuse-for) &middot; [apologize to](#apologize-to-apologize-for) &middot; [blame for](#blame-for-blame-on) &middot; [punish for](#punish-for) &middot; [thank for](#thank-for)

### get along with

**Pronunciation:** /ɡɛt əˈlɔŋ wɪθ/ &middot; *get uh-LAWNG with* &middot; weak /ɡɪt əˈlɔŋ wəθ/
**Русский:** ладить с — уживаться с человеком; разг.
**Type:** verb + particle + preposition (phrasal-prepositional) &middot; **Register:** informal to neutral — the ordinary American way to talk about relationships at work and at home
**Complement:** noun phrase naming a person or group — *get along with your sister*, *with the new manager*, *with everybody*. **No gerund** (✗ *get along with working nights*) and no clause; if you want a manner, use an adverb instead: *they get along fine*. The string is fixed and the object never moves: ✗ *get her along with*.
**Pattern:** *get along with* [a person, a group] &middot; *get along* — intransitive (*they get along*) &middot; *get along without* [something] — manage &middot; *get along* — of age or time (*getting along in years*)
**Contrast:** ***Get along with* is about the relationship; *go along with* is about agreeing.** You can *get along with* a colleague you never *go along with*. The British *get on with someone* means the same as *get along with*, but in American English *get on with it* means only stop stalling. And note the third member of the family, *get along without*, which means manage in the absence of something: *we got along without a car for a year*.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic enough to take the progressive (*they're getting along better lately*), but it reports an ongoing relationship rather than an event, so it is close to **stative** in the habitual present: *she gets along with everyone on the crew*.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** — a fixed string with no direct-object slot (✗ *get her along with*); it stands alone with a plural subject: *the two of them have never gotten along*.
**Passive:** **no.** The idiom cannot be passivized: ✗ *the foreman isn't gotten along with*, ✗ *the schedulers are gotten along with by new hires*.

Have an easy enough relationship with somebody.

1. She *gets along with* everyone on the crew except the foreman.
2. The two of them have never *gotten along*, and the trip did not help.
3. He *gets along with* his ex better now than when they were married.
4. New hires who *get along with* the schedulers get the shifts they want.
5. Do you think you could *get along with* someone that particular for six months?
6. They *got along without* air conditioning for thirty summers.

**See also:** [go along with](17-phrasal-g.md#go-along-with) &middot; [hang out with](#hang-out-with) &middot; [put up with](19-phrasal-m-r.md#put-up-with) &middot; [deal with, deal in](#deal-with-deal-in) &middot; [with](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#with)

### get rid of

**Pronunciation:** /ɡɛt ˈrɪd ʌv/ &middot; *get RID uv* &middot; weak /ɡɪt ˈrɪd əv/
**Русский:** избавляться от — убирать, выбрасывать, освобождаться от чего-то
**Type:** verb + adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** informal to neutral — the everyday American twin of *dispose of*
**Complement:** noun phrase naming a thing, a person, or a problem. **No gerund** (✗ *get rid of smoking* — English says *quit smoking*), no infinitive, no clause. Nothing may be inserted into the string: ✗ *get it rid of*, ✗ *get rid the couch of*; the object always follows *of*.
**Pattern:** *get rid of* [something, someone] &middot; *be rid of* [something] &middot; *rid* [a place] *of* [something] — transitive, formal &middot; *good riddance* — the fixed noun
**Contrast:** ***Get rid of* is the plain version of *dispose of***, and it covers throwing out, selling, firing, and curing: *get rid of the couch*, *of the car*, *of the manager*, *of a cough*. The formal relatives are *rid X of Y* (*rid the building of asbestos*) and *be rid of* (*glad to be rid of him*). Note that *rid* here is not a movable particle but an adjective-like word inside a fixed frame, which is why the object cannot climb over it. For a habit, English switches verbs: *quit smoking*, *give up smoking*, never ✗ *get rid of smoking*.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic; a fixed idiom for removing, discarding, selling, firing, or curing: *the new director got rid of three vice presidents in a month*. *Rid* here is adjective-like inside the frame, so *get* is close to **linking** — compare the stative *be rid of*.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** as a frame — the object always follows *of* and nothing may be inserted: ✗ *get it rid of*, ✗ *get rid the couch of*. The formal relative *rid X of Y* is genuinely transitive: *rid the building of asbestos*.
**Passive:** **no** in American English: ✗ *the couch was finally gotten rid of* is clumsy at best. Use *dispose of*, which passivizes cleanly (*the servers were disposed of*), or the stative *be rid of*: *they were glad to be rid of it*.

Remove or discard something, or free yourself of it.

1. We finally *got rid of* the couch that had been in the garage since 2009.
2. He can't *get rid of* this cough.
3. The new director *got rid of* three vice presidents in a month.
4. If you want to *get rid of* the smell, you have to pull up the pad underneath.
5. I'd *get rid of* the second paragraph entirely.
6. They were glad to be *rid of* the whole arrangement.

**See also:** [dispose of](#dispose-of) &middot; [divest of](#divest-of) &middot; [deprive of](#deprive-of) &middot; [deal with, deal in](#deal-with-deal-in) &middot; [of](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#of)

### get used to, get accustomed to

**Pronunciation:** /ˈɡɛt ˈjust tu/ &middot; *get YOOST too* &middot; weak /ˈɡɛt ˈjust tə/
**Русский:** привыкать к — после to герундий: get used to working, не ✗ to work
**Type:** verb + adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** *get used to* is everyday; *get accustomed to* is formal
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*got used to driving on the left*) — ***to* is a preposition**, so ✗ *get used to drive on the left* is an error, and so is ✗ *I'm getting used to work nights*. Never a clause. Note the pronunciation and spelling of *used* here: /just/ with an *s*, not /juzd/, and the *d* is silent in *used to*, which is why learners write ✗ *use to*.
**Pattern:** *get used to* [noun phrase / verb-ing] &middot; *be used to* [noun phrase / verb-ing] &middot; *get accustomed to* [noun phrase / verb-ing] &middot; *grow accustomed to* [verb-ing] &middot; *used to* [verb — a different pattern entirely]
**Contrast:** **Two different *used to*s share a spelling and nothing else, and this is the distinction to nail down first.** *I used to smoke* is a past habit — *to* is the infinitive marker, the verb is bare, and there is no *be* or *get* in front of it. *I'm used to the smoke* is tolerating a smoky room and *I'm used to smoking* means the habit no longer bothers me — in both of those *to* is a preposition, so the verb has to be *-ing*. The noun test separates them instantly: *I'm used to the noise* works, so that *to* is a preposition; ✗ *I used to the noise* does not, so that one is not. *Get* marks the process and *be* marks the finished state: *it took a month to get used to it, and now I'm used to it*. *Accustomed* is the formal twin with identical grammar, and the adjective forms are catalogued in the [companion adjective file](../../06-prepositions/catalog/15-dependent-adjectives.md#used-to).
**Verb type:** **linking (copular)** — *get* joins the subject to the adjective *used* / *accustomed*, which then takes its own preposition. *Get* marks the process (dynamic, progressive normal: *he's getting accustomed to the software*); *be* marks the finished state: *now I'm used to it*.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** — copular, so there is no object; *to* is a preposition and takes a noun phrase or *-ing*: ✗ *get used to drive on the left*, ✗ *I'm getting used to work nights*.
**Passive:** **no** — a copular frame has nothing to promote: ✗ *the cold was gotten used to*, ✗ *being on call is gotten used to by nobody*.

Grow familiar with something so that it stops bothering you.

1. It took her most of a winter to *get used to* the cold.
2. You never really *get used to* being on call.
3. Don't *get used to* the corner office; the lease is up in March.
4. He's *getting accustomed to* the new scheduling software, slowly.
5. I'm still not *used to* seeing his name on the door.
6. The horses have *gotten used to* the trains going by.

**See also:** [look forward to](#look-forward-to) &middot; [get around to](17-phrasal-g.md#get-around-to) &middot; [adapt to](#adapt-to) &middot; [cope with](#cope-with) &middot; [object to](#object-to) &middot; [accustomed to](../../06-prepositions/catalog/15-dependent-adjectives.md#accustomed-to)

## H–I

### hang out with

**Pronunciation:** /ˈhæŋ aʊt wɪθ/ &middot; *HANG out with* &middot; weak /ˈhæŋ aʊt wəθ/
**Русский:** проводить время с, тусоваться с — разг.
**Type:** verb + particle + preposition (phrasal-prepositional) &middot; **Register:** informal — conversational American English, out of place in a report or a cover letter
**Complement:** noun phrase naming a person or group. **No gerund** (✗ *hang out with playing cards*) and no clause; what you do together goes in a separate phrase: *hang out with them playing cards*. The string is fixed: ✗ *hang him out with*.
**Pattern:** *hang out with* [people] &middot; *hang out* — intransitive (*we just hung out*) &middot; *hang out at* / *in* [a place] &middot; *hang around with* [people] — the same idea, slightly more idle
**Contrast:** **Two verbs, one spelling.** *Hang out the laundry* is a separable particle verb — you can say *hang the laundry out* — and it belongs with the phrasal verbs; *hang out with* somebody is fixed and cannot be split. The neutral-register equivalents are *spend time with* and *see* (*I saw a lot of her that year*), and *socialize with* is the word a résumé would use. In American English *hang out with* implies unstructured time; *meet with* implies an appointment.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic, and the progressive is its natural habitat: *they're hanging out at the taqueria*. Implies unstructured time, unlike *meet with*.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** — a fixed string that cannot be split (✗ *hang him out with*); it also stands alone: *we just hung out*. The separable *hang out the laundry* is a different verb.
**Passive:** **no.** The idiom refuses the passive: ✗ *the interns aren't hung out with*, ✗ *the dog would rather be hung out with*.

Spend unstructured time with somebody.

1. He *hangs out with* the same four people he met in high school.
2. Nobody *hangs out with* the interns because they leave at five.
3. She *hung out with* her cousins all summer at the lake.
4. They *hang out* at the taqueria on Fourth most nights.
5. I don't *hang out with* anyone from work anymore.
6. My daughter would rather *hang out with* the dog than with the family.

**See also:** [get along with](#get-along-with) &middot; [go along with](17-phrasal-g.md#go-along-with) &middot; [talk to, talk with, talk about](#talk-to-talk-with-talk-about) &middot; [share with](#share-with) &middot; [with](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#with)

### hear about, hear from, hear of

**Pronunciation:** /ˈhɪr əˌbaʊt/ &middot; *HEER uh-bowt* &middot; weak /ˈhɪr əbət/
**Русский:** слышать — from получить весть от кого-то, about узнать новости о, of знать о существовании
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** *about* takes a noun phrase, a **gerund**, or a wh-clause. *from* takes a person or organization only. *of* takes a noun phrase or a gerund and is largely restricted to existence and to the negative. **None takes a to-infinitive.** Bare *hear* is transitive (*hear the alarm*) and also takes *someone do* or *someone doing*: *I heard her leave*, *I heard her singing*. A clause takes no preposition: *I heard that they sold it*.
**Pattern:** *hear about* [news, details] &middot; *hear from* [a person — receive word] &middot; *hear of* [existence] &middot; *never hear of* [something] &middot; *won't hear of* [verb-ing] &middot; *hear* [something] &middot; *hear that* [clause] &middot; *hear* [someone] *do* / *doing*
**Contrast:** **Three prepositions, three jobs, and one of the highest-value distinctions in the language.** *Hear **from** Marcus* means Marcus contacted you. *Hear **about** Marcus* means somebody told you news of him. *Hear **of** Marcus* means you know he exists — which is why *I've never heard of her* is a statement about your ignorance and *I've never heard from her* is a complaint. The fixed idiom *won't hear of it* means flatly refuse, and takes a gerund: *she wouldn't hear of our paying*. Note the business-letter formula *I look forward to hearing from you* — *from*, and the gerund.
**Verb type:** Perception *hear* is **stative** and resists the progressive (✗ *I am hearing a door slam*); the three prepositional frames are closer to **dynamic** events of getting news, and journalism does allow *we're hearing from readers that…*
**Transitivity:** **both** — bare *hear* is transitive (*hear the alarm*, *hear that she's retiring*, *hear her leave*), while *hear about* / *from* / *of* are intransitive with a fixed preposition: ✗ *hear the layoffs about*.
**Passive:** **restricted.** Bare transitive *hear* passivizes (*the alarm was heard three blocks away*), and *hear of* / *from* survive in fixed passives: *she was never heard from again*, *a town nobody had ever heard of*. ✗ *the layoffs were heard about* is not idiomatic.

Receive news, learn that something exists, or get word from someone.

| Pattern | What it means | Example |
|---|---|---|
| *hear **from*** [person] | receive a call, letter, or message from them | *I heard from Dad this morning.* |
| *hear **about*** [thing] | get the news or the details | *Did you hear about the layoffs?* |
| *hear **of*** [thing] | know it exists | *I've never heard of that town.* |
| *won't hear **of*** [verb-ing] | flatly refuse to allow | *He wouldn't hear of us leaving.* |
| *hear* [thing] | perceive with your ears — **no preposition** | *I heard a door slam.* |
| *hear **that*** [clause] | learn the fact — **no preposition** | *I hear that she's retiring.* |

1. Did you *hear about* the fire on Kingsbury?
2. I haven't *heard from* my brother since Thanksgiving.
3. I've never *heard of* the band, but the tickets were free.
4. We *heard about* the merger from a reporter before anyone told the staff.
5. You'll *hear from* our attorney.
6. She wouldn't *hear of* our paying for the room.
7. Let me know as soon as you *hear from* them.

**See also:** [learn about](#learn-about-learn-from) &middot; [listen to](#listen-to) &middot; [ask about](#ask-for-ask-about) &middot; [talk about](#talk-to-talk-with-talk-about)

### hide from

**Pronunciation:** /ˈhaɪd frʌm/ &middot; *HIDE from* &middot; weak /ˈhaɪd frəm/
**Русский:** прятать(ся) от — держать себя или вещь вне поля зрения
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase only — **no gerund, no clause** (✗ *hide from being seen*; say *hide so nobody sees you*). The verb works both intransitively (*hide from the police*) and transitively with two arguments (*hide the letter from his wife*). Note the irregular forms: *hide, hid, hidden*.
**Pattern:** *hide from* [someone] &middot; *hide* [something] *from* [someone] &middot; *hide* [somewhere] &middot; *be hidden from* [view, sight] &middot; *hide out* &middot; *nothing to hide*
**Contrast:** ***Hide from* is the direction of concealment and cannot be reversed:** *He hid the diagnosis from his kids* is the only order that works — ✗ *hid his kids from the diagnosis* says something else entirely. Compare *keep something from someone*, which is the everyday synonym and takes the same shape, and *conceal something from someone*, the formal one. Note *hidden from view*, a fixed phrase, and the idiomatic *I have nothing to hide*, which takes no preposition at all.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic; progressive normal in both frames: *two of them were hiding from the storm*, *he was hiding the diagnosis from his kids*. Irregular: *hide, hid, hidden*.
**Transitivity:** **both** — intransitive when you hide yourself (*the cat hides from anyone who isn't me*), transitive with two arguments when you hide a thing: *he hid the diagnosis from his kids*. The order cannot reverse: ✗ *hid his kids from the diagnosis*.
**Passive:** **yes** in the transitive frame, and it is very common: *the entrance is hidden from the road by a hedge*. The intransitive frame has no passive of its own: ✗ *the subpoena was hidden from* meaning you hid from it.

Keep yourself or something out of sight.

1. The cat *hides from* anyone who isn't me.
2. He *hid* the diagnosis *from* his kids for almost a year.
3. You can't *hide from* a subpoena forever.
4. The entrance is *hidden from* the road by a hedge.
5. She *hid* the good chocolate *from* her roommates.
6. Two of them *hid from* the storm under a picnic shelter.

**See also:** [escape from](#escape-from) &middot; [protect from](#protect-from-protect-against) &middot; [prevent from](#prevent-from) &middot; [from](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#from)

### hinge on

**Pronunciation:** /ˈhɪndʒ ɑn/ &middot; *HINJ on* &middot; weak /ˈhɪndʒ ən/
**Русский:** целиком зависеть от — определяться одним решающим обстоятельством
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral — journalism, argument, and courtroom summary
**Complement:** noun phrase, **gerund** (*hinges on getting the votes*), or a wh-clause (*hinges on whether the Senate acts*, *on how the judge reads the statute*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *hinges on to get the votes*) and never a bare that-clause: ✗ *hinges on that the Senate acts*. The subject is abstract — a case, an outcome, a decision, the whole thing — because people do not hinge on anything: ✗ *she hinges on her lawyer*.
**Variants:** ***Hinge upon*** is the same pattern one register up, at home in a brief or an editorial.
**Pattern:** [an outcome] *hinge on* / *upon* [a fact, a condition] &middot; *hinge on* [verb-ing] &middot; *hinge on whether* / *how* / *what* [clause] &middot; *everything hinges on* [X] — the commonest frame
**Contrast:** ***Hinge on* is narrower than *depend on* in two ways.** Its subject must be a thing, not a person — *the case hinges on the timeline*, but *she depends on her sister* — and it names a single pivot rather than a general reliance, which is why *everything hinges on one witness* sounds right and ✗ *everything hinges on my family* does not. *Turn on* is its close synonym in legal writing (*the appeal turns on a question of standing*), and *rest on* is the static version: a claim *rests on* an assumption without pivoting on it.
**Verb type:** **stative** — a relation of dependence, not an event; the progressive is out: ✗ *the case is hinging on the timeline*.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** with a fixed preposition — no object (✗ *hinge the outcome*), and the subject must be abstract: ✗ *she hinges on her lawyer*.
**Passive:** **no.** Stative and objectless: ✗ *what the camera recorded is hinged on by the case*, ✗ *the permit is hinged on*. Use *turn on* or *rest on* if you want the same idea; they refuse the passive too.

Depend entirely on one deciding fact or condition.

1. The whole case *hinges on* what the security camera recorded.
2. Their forecast *hinges on* interest rates holding through the spring.
3. Everything *hinges on* getting the permit before the season closes.
4. The appeal *hinges on* whether the notice was timely.
5. His argument *hinges upon* a definition the statute never gives.
6. Nothing much *hinges on* which of the two spellings you use.

**See also:** [depend on](#depend-on) &middot; [rely on](#rely-on) &middot; [count on](#count-on) &middot; [result in, result from](#result-in-result-from) &middot; [on](../../06-prepositions/catalog/01-core-in-on-at.md#on)

### hope for

**Pronunciation:** /ˈhoʊp fɔr/ &middot; *HOHP for* &middot; weak /ˈhoʊp fɚ/
**Русский:** надеяться на — хотеть, чтобы что-то случилось, и считать это возможным
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** *for* takes a noun phrase only — **no gerund and no to-infinitive after the preposition** (✗ *hope for to win*, ✗ *hope for winning*). To put a verb after *hope*, drop the preposition and use the infinitive: *hope to win*. A clause also takes no preposition: *hope that it clears up* — and note that the present tense is normal there, not *will*: *I hope it clears up tomorrow*.
**Pattern:** *hope for* [a noun phrase] &middot; *hope to* [verb] &middot; *hope that* [clause] &middot; *I hope so* / *I hope not* &middot; *hoping for* [the best] &middot; *hope against hope*
**Contrast:** ***Hope for winning* is not English.** The preposition takes a noun and only a noun — *hope for rain*, *hope for a quick decision* — and the moment you want a verb you must switch frames to *hope to*. This is the opposite of *look forward to*, where the preposition demands the *-ing* form. The fixed pair *hope for the best* and *hope against hope* are frozen. Note also that after *hope* the future is usually expressed with a present tense: *I hope the flight lands on time*.
**Verb type:** **stative** in meaning — a mental attitude rather than an act — but it takes the progressive freely: *they're hoping for a settlement before the trial date*.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** with *for* before a noun phrase, and **transitive** before a clause or an infinitive, where the preposition disappears: *hope that it clears up*, *hope to finish the draft*; ✗ *hope for to win*, ✗ *hope for winning*.
**Passive:** **restricted.** Impersonal formal writing allows it (*a quick decision is hoped for*) and the participle is fixed (*the hoped-for settlement*), but an agent makes it fail: ✗ *a shorter meeting was hoped for by everyone*. Use *it is hoped that…* instead.

Want something to happen and think it might.

1. They're *hoping for* a settlement before the trial date.
2. We *hoped for* rain all August and got two inches in September.
3. She *hopes to* finish the draft before the semester starts.
4. Everyone was *hoping for* a shorter meeting.
5. *Hope for* the best, plan for the other thing.
6. I *hope* the connection holds.

**See also:** [wish for](#wish-for) &middot; [long for](#long-for) &middot; [look forward to](#look-forward-to) &middot; [dream of](#dream-about-dream-of) &middot; [plan on](#plan-on)
