### impose on, impose upon

**Pronunciation:** /ɪmˈpoʊz ɑn/ &middot; *im-POHZ on* &middot; weak /ɪmˈpoʊz ən/
**Русский:** налагать (налог, правило) на кого-то; злоупотреблять чьей-то любезностью
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal — taxes, sanctions, deadlines, and polite apology
**Complement:** in the transitive frame the thing imposed is the direct object and *on* takes the person or body that has to bear it: *impose a curfew on the neighborhood*. In the intransitive frame *on* takes a person and the verb means take unfair advantage of them: *I don't want to impose on you*. **A gerund after *on* is rare**, and there is **no infinitive and no clause**: ✗ *impose on to stay another night*.
**Variants:** ***Impose upon*** is the formal twin, standard in legal and academic prose (*the duty imposed upon the trustee*) and slightly old-fashioned in the apology sense (*I fear we imposed upon your hospitality*).
**Pattern:** *impose* [a tax, a rule, a deadline, a fine] *on* / *upon* [someone] &middot; *impose on* / *upon* [someone] — trespass on their good nature &middot; *be imposed on* [someone] &middot; *impose oneself on* [a group] &middot; *an imposition on* [someone] — the noun keeps *on*
**Contrast:** **The two senses share a preposition and nothing else.** *The state imposed a fee on filers* is authority; *I hate to impose* is etiquette, and in that second sense the verb has no object of its own at all. Against the neighbors: *inflict on* is worse (*inflict a speech on the guests*), *foist on* is sneakier (*foist the bill on his brother*), and *encroach on* is gradual rather than imposed at once. Note that with people the passive is fine — *conditions were imposed on the defendant* — but the thing imposed can never take *to*: ✗ *imposed a fine to the company*.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic in both senses: an authority levies something (*Congress imposed new requirements*), or a guest trespasses on good will (*I don't want to impose*). Progressive is fine: *they're imposing a curfew*.
**Transitivity:** **both** — transitive in the authority sense, where the thing imposed is the direct object and *on* takes the bearer (*impose a fee on filers*); **intransitive** in the etiquette sense, where the verb has no object of its own at all (*I hate to impose on you*).
**Passive:** **yes** in both — *conditions were imposed on the defendant*; and the person passivizes in the etiquette sense too: *she was imposed upon by every relative she had*.

Place a burden, rule, or cost on somebody — or take advantage of their good nature.

1. Congress *imposed* new reporting requirements *on* small lenders.
2. The court *imposed* a six-month deadline *on* both parties.
3. I don't want to *impose on* you two any longer than one night.
4. Sanctions were *imposed on* the bank within a week of the ruling.
5. She *imposes* her taste in music *on* everyone in the car.
6. The tariff *imposed upon* imported steel raised the cost of the whole project.

**See also:** [encroach on, encroach upon](#encroach-on-encroach-upon) &middot; [impute to](#impute-to) &middot; [devolve on, devolve upon](#devolve-on-devolve-upon) &middot; [charge with, charge for](#charge-with-charge-for) &middot; [on](../../06-prepositions/catalog/01-core-in-on-at.md#on)

### impute to

**Pronunciation:** /ɪmˈpjut tu/ &middot; *im-PYOOT too* &middot; weak /ɪmˈpjut tə/
**Русский:** вменять кому-то (мотив, вину, стоимость) — приписывать незаявленное; юр./книжн.
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal — law, accounting, and theology. An American meets it as a participle long before he meets the verb: *imputed income* on a tax return, *imputed knowledge* in an agency case, *imputed negligence* in a jury instruction.
**Complement:** the thing imputed is the **direct object** — a motive, a statement, a value, a fault — and *to* takes the person or entity it is charged to. **A gerund after *to* is rare** (*imputed to having signed the form*), and there is **no infinitive** (✗ *impute the delay to be careless*) and no clause. The object cannot be dropped: ✗ *he imputed to the agency*.
**Pattern:** *impute* [a motive, a fault, a value] *to* [someone] &middot; *be imputed to* [someone] &middot; *imputed income* / *knowledge* / *negligence* — the participle &middot; *imputation of* [bad faith] — the noun takes *of*
**Contrast:** ***Impute to* assigns something discreditable or artificial; *attribute to* is neutral.** You *attribute* a quotation *to* Twain and *impute* a motive *to* an opponent — and *ascribe to* sits between them, more literary than either. *Credit with* is the flattering member of the family (*credited her with the idea*), and *blame on* the blunt one. Note that the law uses *impute* for something nobody actually did or knew: *the agent's knowledge is imputed to the principal* means the principal is treated as knowing it.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic; a mental-cum-declarative act of charging something to somebody, and the participle (*imputed income*, *imputed knowledge*) is where Americans meet it first.
**Transitivity:** **transitive** — the thing imputed is the direct object and *to* takes the party charged; the object cannot be dropped: ✗ *he imputed to the agency*.
**Passive:** **yes**, and the passive is the commoner form — *the agent's knowledge is imputed to the principal*, *no bad faith was imputed to the trustee*.

Charge somebody with a motive, a fault, or a value they did not actually declare.

1. Counsel *imputed* bad faith *to* the other side without producing a document.
2. The knowledge of the broker is *imputed to* the firm that hired him.
3. The IRS *imputes* interest *to* below-market family loans.
4. Do not *impute to* carelessness what a simple software bug explains.
5. Critics *imputed* motives *to* her that the record does not support.
6. Negligence was *imputed to* the parent company through the subsidiary.

**See also:** [blame for, blame on](#blame-for-blame-on) &middot; [accuse of](#accuse-of) &middot; [impose on, impose upon](#impose-on-impose-upon) &middot; [refer to](#refer-to) &middot; [to](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#to)

### indemnify against

**Pronunciation:** /ɪnˈdɛmnəfaɪ əˈɡɛnst/ &middot; *in-DEM-nuh-fye uh-GENST* &middot; weak /ɪnˈdɛmnəfaɪ əɡənst/
**Русский:** гарантировать возмещение убытков от — договорное обязательство; юр.
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** legal — contract drafting and insurance. An American meets it in exactly one place: the indemnification clause of a lease, a vendor agreement, or a settlement, usually in the fixed triple *indemnify, defend, and hold harmless*.
**Complement:** the party protected is the direct object, and *against* takes a noun phrase naming a loss, a claim, a liability, or a suit: *indemnify the landlord against claims arising from the tenant's use*. **A gerund is rare**, and there is **no infinitive and no clause**: ✗ *indemnify the seller against to be sued*. The protected party cannot be dropped: ✗ *indemnify against all claims* with nobody named is drafting malpractice, not grammar.
**Variants:** ***Indemnify from*** is equally standard in American clauses (*indemnify and hold harmless from any liability*), and ***indemnify for*** points at a loss already suffered rather than one that might happen: *indemnified the owner for the cost of repairs*. Practice varies firm to firm; all three are read the same way.
**Pattern:** *indemnify* [a party] *against* / *from* [claims, losses, liability] &middot; *indemnify* [a party] *for* [an amount already lost] &middot; *indemnify, defend, and hold* [a party] *harmless against* [X] &middot; *indemnity against* [loss] — the noun keeps the preposition
**Contrast:** ***Indemnify against* is a promise between parties; *insure against* is a policy you buy.** A subcontractor *indemnifies* the general contractor *against* third-party claims, while the general contractor separately *insures against* fire. Both differ from *compensate for*, which is payment after the fact and takes *for*, and from *protect from*, which is the everyday word. Note that the object is the person protected, never the risk: ✗ *indemnify the losses against the tenant*.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic and performative: signing the clause is the act. Ordinarily non-progressive in drafting (*Tenant shall indemnify Landlord*), not because it is stative but because contracts use *shall*.
**Transitivity:** **transitive** — the party protected is the direct object and *against* names the loss; the object is obligatory: ✗ *indemnify against all claims* with nobody named.
**Passive:** **yes** — *the landlord is indemnified against claims arising from the tenant's use*, and the participial *indemnified and held harmless* is standard boilerplate.

Promise in a contract to cover somebody else's losses if a specified risk lands.

1. The vendor agrees to *indemnify* the university *against* any third-party infringement claim.
2. Directors are routinely *indemnified against* liability for good-faith decisions.
3. The clause *indemnifies* the landlord *against* everything short of his own negligence.
4. We would not sign until the contractor agreed to *indemnify* us *from* mechanics' liens.
5. Nothing in the policy *indemnifies* the company *against* fines imposed by a regulator.
6. She was *indemnified for* the legal fees but not for the lost year of income.

**See also:** [insure against](#insure-against) &middot; [protect from, protect against](#protect-from-protect-against) &middot; [relieve of](#relieve-of) &middot; [liable for, liable to](../../06-prepositions/catalog/15-dependent-adjectives.md#liable-for-liable-to) &middot; [against](../../06-prepositions/catalog/09-comparison-opposition.md#against)

### indict for

**Pronunciation:** /ɪnˈdaɪt fɔr/ &middot; *in-DITE for* &middot; weak /ɪnˈdaɪt fɚ/ &mdash; the *c* is silent
**Русский:** предъявлять обвинение в — через большое жюри; юр., США
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** legal and news — grand juries, U.S. Attorneys, and the front page
**Complement:** the person is the direct object and *for* takes a noun phrase naming the offense, or a **gerund** naming the conduct: *indicted for lying to investigators*. **Never a to-infinitive** (✗ *indicted for to lie*) and never a clause: ✗ *indicted for that he lied*. The frame *indict on* takes counts or charges instead — *indicted on three counts of wire fraud* — and is at least as common in reporting.
**Pattern:** *indict* [someone] *for* [an offense, verb-ing] &middot; *indict* [someone] *on* [counts, charges] &middot; *be indicted for* / *on* [something] &middot; *return an indictment against* [someone] &middot; *an indictment of* [a policy] — the figurative noun takes *of*
**Contrast:** **Four verbs run the same case and none of them shares a preposition.** A prosecutor *charges* someone ***with*** a crime, a grand jury *indicts* him ***for*** it (or ***on*** counts of it), a jury *convicts* him ***of*** it, and a judge *sentences* him ***to*** something. *Accuse of* is the everyday word for the same act, and *arraign on* is the step in between. Note the figurative noun, which flips the preposition entirely: *the report is an indictment **of** the whole program*, never ✗ *an indictment for*.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic; a single official act by a grand jury, punctual rather than durative.
**Transitivity:** **transitive** — the person is the direct object and *for* (or *on*, with counts) names the offense; the object is obligatory: ✗ *the grand jury indicted for wire fraud*.
**Passive:** **yes**, and it is the normal news form — *he was indicted for lying to investigators*, *she was indicted on three counts of wire fraud*.

Formally charge somebody with a crime by grand jury.

1. A federal grand jury *indicted* him *for* mail fraud in November.
2. Two officers were *indicted for* falsifying the incident report.
3. She was *indicted on* seven counts, four of them dismissed before trial.
4. The prosecutor declined to *indict* anyone *for* the leak.
5. Being *indicted for* a crime is not the same as being convicted of one.
6. The grand jury refused to *indict*, and the case ended there.

**See also:** [charge with, charge for](#charge-with-charge-for) &middot; [accuse of](#accuse-of) &middot; [punish for](#punish-for) &middot; [suspect of](#suspect-of) &middot; [for](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#for)

### infer from

**Pronunciation:** /ɪnˈfɚ frʌm/ &middot; *in-FUR from* &middot; weak /ɪnˈfɚ frəm/
**Русский:** делать вывод из — заключать по имеющимся данным
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal — argument, science, and careful writing
**Complement:** *from* takes a noun phrase naming the evidence, or a **gerund** (*inferred from watching the two of them*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *inferred from to watch them*). What is inferred is the direct object and may be a that-clause: *inferred that the deal was off*. The preposition never introduces the conclusion: ✗ *infer from that the deal was off* is a common slip for *infer from her tone that the deal was off*.
**Pattern:** *infer* [something] *from* [evidence] &middot; *infer from* [evidence] *that* [clause] &middot; [something] *may be inferred from* [evidence] &middot; *an inference from* [the record] — the noun keeps *from*
**Contrast:** **The usage everyone argues about: *imply* and *infer* are opposite ends of one act.** The speaker *implies*; the listener *infers*. ✗ *Are you inferring that I lied?* means the opposite of what the speaker intends — say *implying*. Within the reasoning family, *deduce from* claims certainty, *infer from* claims likelihood, and *extrapolate from* claims a pattern will continue. All three take *from*; none takes *of* or *out of*: ✗ *infer of his tone*.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic; a mental act you perform, and it takes the progressive: *what exactly are you inferring from that?*
**Transitivity:** **transitive** — what is inferred is the direct object or a that-clause (*inferred that the deal was off*) and *from* names the evidence; the conclusion cannot hide inside the preposition: ✗ *infer from that the deal was off*.
**Passive:** **yes** — *nothing can be inferred from a sample that small*, *it was inferred from her tone that the deal was off*.

Draw a probable conclusion from evidence.

1. From the tone of the email she *inferred* that the offer had been withdrawn.
2. You can *infer* a great deal *from* what a company does not disclose.
3. Nothing about intent can be *inferred from* a single missed payment.
4. The jury was told not to *infer* guilt *from* the defendant's silence.
5. He *inferred from* watching two shifts that the schedule was written by seniority.
6. Readers are left to *infer* the ending *from* three lines of dialogue.

**See also:** [deduce from](#deduce-from) &middot; [extrapolate from](#extrapolate-from) &middot; [derive from](#derive-from) &middot; [follow from](#follow-from) &middot; [from](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#from)

### infringe on, infringe upon

**Pronunciation:** /ɪnˈfrɪndʒ ɑn/ &middot; *in-FRINJ on* &middot; weak /ɪnˈfrɪndʒ ən/
**Русский:** нарушать, посягать на — на право, свободу или охраняемую собственность
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** legal to neutral — constitutional argument, intellectual property, and op-eds
**Complement:** noun phrase naming a right, a freedom, or a protected interest. **A gerund is unusual** (*infringing on being able to speak freely* is possible but clumsy), and there is **no infinitive and no clause**: ✗ *infringe on to speak freely*.
**Variants:** ***Infringe upon*** is the formal twin and is common in judicial opinions. The important variant is the **bare transitive**: in intellectual-property law you *infringe a patent*, *infringe a copyright*, *infringe the mark* — with no preposition at all, and that is the professional usage.
**Pattern:** *infringe on* / *upon* [a right, a freedom, someone's autonomy] &middot; *infringe* [a patent, a copyright, a trademark] — no preposition &middot; *an infringement of* [copyright] &middot; *an infringement on* [rights]
**Contrast:** ***Rights are infringed on; patents are infringed.*** *The ordinance infringes on the right to assemble* and *the knockoff infringes the design patent* are both standard, and swapping them marks a writer as an outsider to the field. Against the neighbors: *encroach on* is gradual and often physical, *violate* is blunt and transitive (*violate the statute*), *impinge on* is the most abstract, and *interfere with* is the broadest of all. The noun splits too — *infringement **of** copyright* but *an infringement **on** civil liberties*.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic; an ordinance or a product encroaches on a right or a patent. Often durative and progressive: *the rule is infringing on speech*.
**Transitivity:** **both** — **intransitive** with the preposition when the object is a right (*infringes on the right to assemble*), **transitive** with no preposition in the IP sense (*the knockoff infringes the design patent*).
**Passive:** **yes** in both frames — *the patent was infringed*, and the prepositional passive is idiomatic too: *rights that are being infringed on every day*.

Intrude on a right, a freedom, or protected property.

1. The plaintiffs say the curfew *infringes on* rights the state constitution guarantees.
2. Requiring a permit for a two-person picket *infringes upon* speech, the court held.
3. He felt the new metrics *infringed on* how he ran his own department.
4. The company was found to have *infringed* three of the four patents.
5. Nothing in the lease *infringes on* the tenant's right to quiet enjoyment.
6. Streaming the whole film *infringes* the copyright whether or not you charge for it.

**See also:** [encroach on, encroach upon](#encroach-on-encroach-upon) &middot; [interfere with, interfere in](#interfere-with-interfere-in) &middot; [impose on, impose upon](#impose-on-impose-upon) &middot; [comply with](#comply-with) &middot; [on](../../06-prepositions/catalog/01-core-in-on-at.md#on)

### inhere in

**Pronunciation:** /ɪnˈhɪr ɪn/ &middot; *in-HEER in* &middot; weak /ɪnˈhɪr ən/
**Русский:** быть неотъемлемо присущим чему-то — книжн./филос.
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal and technical — philosophy, jurisprudence, and constitutional opinions (*the power inheres in the office*). It is rare enough in speech that most Americans know only its adjective, *inherent in*.
**Complement:** noun phrase naming the thing the quality belongs to — an office, a relationship, a system, a role. **No gerund, no infinitive, no clause**, and **no passive**: ✗ *is inhered in*. The subject is the quality, right, or power, never the possessor: ✗ *the court inheres in that power*.
**Pattern:** [a right, a power, a quality] *inhere in* [an office, a thing, a relationship] &middot; *inherent in* [something] — the far commoner adjective &middot; *inhere* — no other frame; the verb needs *in*
**Contrast:** **Prefer the adjective.** *The risk inherent in the procedure* is ordinary English; *the risk inheres in the procedure* is a philosopher's sentence. Against the neighbors: *consist in* names what something essentially is (*happiness consists in useful work*), *reside in* is where a power lives by design (*sovereignty resides in the people*), and *vest in* is the legal act of putting it there (*the estate vests in the heir*). *Inhere in* claims the quality could not be separated from the thing at all.
**Verb type:** **stative** — a quality belongs to a thing; nothing happens. It rejects the progressive: ✗ *the power is inhering in the office*. Prefer the adjective, *inherent in*.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** — the subject is the quality or power and *in* takes the thing it belongs to; there is no object and no other frame: ✗ *the court inheres that power*.
**Passive:** **no** — the verb is intransitive and stative, and the entry marks the prepositional passive out as impossible: ✗ *the office is inhered in*.

Belong to something as an inseparable part of what it is.

1. Certain powers *inhere in* the office and do not depend on the statute.
2. The dignity that *inheres in* the individual is not conferred by government.
3. Whatever value the collection has *inheres in* keeping it together.
4. Risk *inheres in* any procedure performed under general anesthesia.
5. The authority to punish contempt *inheres in* the court itself.
6. He argued that the flaw *inheres in* the design rather than the execution.

**See also:** [devolve on, devolve upon](#devolve-on-devolve-upon) &middot; [belong to](#belong-to) &middot; [consist of](#consist-of) &middot; [inherent in](../../06-prepositions/catalog/15-dependent-adjectives.md#inherent-in) &middot; [in](../../06-prepositions/catalog/01-core-in-on-at.md#in)

### insist on

**Pronunciation:** /ɪnˈsɪst ɑn/ &middot; *in-SIST on* &middot; weak /ɪnˈsɪst ən/
**Русский:** настаивать на — твёрдо требовать или упорно утверждать; после on герундий
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase, **gerund** (*insisted on paying*), or possessive plus gerund (*insisted on my paying*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *insist to pay*, ✗ *insist on to pay*). Before a clause the preposition drops, and American English uses the **bare subjunctive**: *insisted that she be told*, not *that she is told*.
**Pattern:** *insist on* [noun phrase] &middot; *insist on* [verb-ing] &middot; *insist on* [someone's] *-ing* &middot; *insist that* [subject] [bare verb] &middot; *insist* (alone)
**Contrast:** ***Insist to do* is a fixed error** — the verb has no infinitive frame at all. The two live patterns are *insist **on** doing* and *insist **that** X do*, and the second one keeps the American mandative subjunctive: *He insisted that the door be locked*, *She insists that he take the earlier flight* — bare verb, no *-s*, no *should*. (British English often inserts *should*.) Note the second sense, maintain in the face of doubt: *she insisted that she'd sent it*, where the clause is a plain indicative, not a subjunctive.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic; a speech act you repeat, and it takes the progressive freely: *she's insisting on paying*.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** — no direct object ever; the demand comes through *on* (*insisted on paying*) or through a that-clause with the bare subjunctive (*insisted that she be told*).
**Passive:** **restricted** — there is no object to promote, and the prepositional passive is stiff formal-record English at best: *a written waiver was insisted on*, but ✗ *I was insisted on to pay*.

Demand something firmly, or maintain something in the face of doubt.

1. He *insisted on* paying for everyone, which nobody had asked him to do.
2. The landlord *insisted on* a second deposit.
3. She *insisted that* the contract be reviewed before anyone signed.
4. They *insisted on* our staying for dinner.
5. He *insisted* he had mailed it, and the postmark said otherwise.
6. The union *insisted on* binding arbitration.

**See also:** [object to](#object-to) &middot; [agree to](#agree-with-agree-to-agree-on) &middot; [decide on](#decide-on-decide-against) &middot; [comply with](#comply-with) &middot; [on](../../06-prepositions/catalog/01-core-in-on-at.md#on)

### insure against

**Pronunciation:** /ɪnˈʃʊr əˈɡɛnst/ &middot; *in-SHOOR uh-GENST* &middot; weak /ɪnˈʃʊr əɡənst/
**Русский:** страховать от — покупать полис на случай риска (не путать с ensure, assure)
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral — insurance, banking, and risk talk
**Complement:** *against* takes a noun phrase naming a risk or peril — fire, flood, theft, loss, liability — or a **gerund** (*insured against being sued by a former client*). **No to-infinitive** (✗ *insure against to be sued*) and no clause. The party or property covered is the direct object: *insure the building against flood*.
**Pattern:** *insure* [property, a person] *against* [a risk] &middot; *be insured against* [a risk] &middot; *insure* [something] *for* [an amount] &middot; *insure* [something] *with* [a carrier] &middot; *insurance against* [loss] — the noun keeps *against*
**Contrast:** **Three verbs Americans mix daily.** *Insure* is about policies, *ensure* is about making certain (*ensure that the door locks*), and *assure* is about telling a person something confidently (*assured her it was fine*) — so ✗ *insure that the door locks* is the error, and it is everywhere. Within the risk family: *insure against* buys coverage, *indemnify against* is a contractual promise between two parties with no policy involved, and *hedge against* offsets a risk with an opposite position (*hedge against inflation*). Note the amount takes *for*, not *against*: *insured for $400,000 against fire*.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic; buying or writing coverage. Keep it clear of stative *ensure* (make certain) and *assure* (tell someone).
**Transitivity:** **transitive** — the property or party covered is the direct object and *against* names the peril: *insure the building against flood*.
**Passive:** **yes**, and the passive is the everyday form — *the building is insured against flood*, *we're insured for $400,000 against fire*.

Buy or provide coverage that pays out if a particular risk occurs.

1. The house is *insured against* fire but not against flood.
2. Small clinics can rarely afford to *insure against* a judgment that size.
3. The policy *insures* the fleet *against* theft and collision.
4. Growers *insure against* a late frost the way other people buy lottery tickets in reverse.
5. She was *insured against* being sued for work done before the merger.
6. Nothing *insures against* bad luck entirely.

**See also:** [indemnify against](#indemnify-against) &middot; [protect from, protect against](#protect-from-protect-against) &middot; [provide with, provide for](#provide-with-provide-for) &middot; [against](../../06-prepositions/catalog/09-comparison-opposition.md#against)

### interfere with, interfere in

**Pronunciation:** /ˌɪntɚˈfɪr wɪθ/ &middot; *in-ter-FEER with* &middot; weak /ˌɪntɚˈfɪr wəθ/
**Русский:** мешать чему-то (with); вмешиваться во что-то не своё (in)
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*interferes with getting any real work done*) — **never a to-infinitive**. *Interfere* is intransitive and never takes a bare object: ✗ *interfere the signal* — say *interfere with the signal*.
**Pattern:** *interfere with* [a process, a signal, a plan] &middot; *interfere in* [an affair, a dispute, a country] &middot; *interfere* (alone)
**Contrast:** ***With* disrupts; *in* meddles.** *The construction interferes with the Wi-Fi* is about disruption of a thing; *Don't interfere in their marriage* is about intruding into a situation that isn't yours. In American political writing, *interfere in* is the standard collocation for meddling in another country's affairs or an election, while *interfere with* covers evidence, a witness, a signal, or a schedule. Legal usage keeps both: *tortious interference **with** contract*, *interference **in** the proceeding*.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic; a disruption or an intrusion that goes on over time: *the construction is interfering with the Wi-Fi*.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** — the verb never takes a bare object: ✗ *interfere the signal*. Everything comes through *with* (disrupt a thing) or *in* (meddle in a situation).
**Passive:** **restricted** — the *with* frame passivizes in legal and news prose (*the witness had been interfered with*, *the evidence was interfered with*); the *in* frame does not: ✗ *the election was interfered in*.

Disrupt something, or get involved where you have no business.

1. The new tower *interferes with* reception all along the ridge.
2. She was told, more than once, not to *interfere in* her sister's divorce.
3. Nothing should *interfere with* the vote count.
4. The medication can *interfere with* several common blood thinners.
5. Foreign governments *interfered in* the election in at least three documented ways.
6. Long meetings *interfere with* getting anything actually finished.

**See also:** [prevent from](#prevent-from) &middot; [cope with](#cope-with) &middot; [comply with](#comply-with) &middot; [involve in](#involve-in) &middot; [participate in](#participate-in)

### introduce to

**Pronunciation:** /ˌɪntrəˈdus tu/ &middot; *in-truh-DOOSS too* &middot; weak /ˌɪntrəˈdus tə/
**Русский:** представлять кого-то кому-то; знакомить с чем-то — предлог to обязателен
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase only — **no gerund, no clause**. The person being introduced is a direct object and the person met takes *to*: *introduce her to my parents*. ✗ *introduce her with my parents* is an error carried over from other languages. *Into* is a separate pattern, used for putting something new inside a system.
**Pattern:** *introduce* [someone] *to* [someone] &middot; *introduce* [someone] *to* [a subject, a food, a habit] &middot; *introduce* [something] *into* [a system, a market] &middot; *introduce* [a bill, a topic] &middot; *introduce* [oneself]
**Contrast:** ***Introduce with* is wrong.** The two live prepositions are *to* and *into*, and they do different work: *introduce her to sailing* opens a person's acquaintance with something, while *introduce a species into an ecosystem* physically inserts it. Note that *introduce* belongs to the *explain*/*describe*/*mention* group — the person always needs *to*, never a bare indirect object: ✗ *introduce me your friend*.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic; a single act of making an acquaintance or inserting something new into a system.
**Transitivity:** **transitive** — the person or thing introduced is the direct object and *to* takes the person or activity met; the object is obligatory: ✗ *he introduced to my parents*. Never a bare indirect object: ✗ *introduce me your friend*.
**Passive:** **yes** — *she was introduced to my parents at the rehearsal dinner*, *the species was introduced into the lake in 1954*.

Make one person known to another, or bring something in for the first time.

1. Let me *introduce* you *to* my colleague from the Denver office.
2. His grandmother *introduced* him *to* opera when he was six.
3. The bill was *introduced* in March and died in committee.
4. Zebra mussels were *introduced into* the Great Lakes by ballast water.
5. She *introduced* herself *to* everyone in the row before the plane took off.
6. Nobody *introduced* the new hire *to* anyone for a week.

**See also:** [explain to](#explain-to) &middot; [refer to](#refer-to) &middot; [invite to](#invite-to)

### inure to

**Pronunciation:** /ɪˈnjʊr tu/ &middot; *ih-NYOOR too* &middot; weak /ɪˈnjʊr tə/
**Русский:** доставаться в пользу кого-то; (в пассиве) быть закалённым к чему-то; юр./книжн.
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal and legal, in two separate lives. In a trust, a policy, or a contract you meet the fixed phrase *inure to the benefit of*; in essays and obituaries you meet the participle *inured to* meaning hardened by exposure. Neither is conversational.
**Complement:** in the legal sense, *to* takes a noun phrase naming the party who gets the benefit — almost always inside *to the benefit of* [someone]. In the participial sense, *be inured to* takes a noun phrase or a **gerund**: *inured to being ignored*, *inured to the cold*. **Never a to-infinitive** in either sense: ✗ *inured to be ignored*, and that trap is the reason the entry is here.
**Variants:** The spelling ***enure*** survives in older and British legal drafting and means exactly the same thing; American filings use *inure*.
**Pattern:** [a benefit, a right] *inure to the benefit of* [a party] &middot; [something] *inure to* [someone's advantage] &middot; *be* / *become inured to* [hardship, verb-ing] &middot; *this Agreement shall inure to the benefit of and be binding upon the parties* — the boilerplate
**Contrast:** **Two senses, one spelling, and neither is the everyday word.** *The proceeds inure to the beneficiary* means they accrue to her — the plain verbs are *go to* and *accrue to*. *He is inured to criticism* means he has been hardened to it — the plain phrases are *used to* and *hardened to*. The second is where the gerund rule bites: like *get used to* and *accustomed to*, *inured to* is followed by *-ing*, never by an infinitive. Do not confuse either with *in your interest* or with *ensure*.
**Verb type:** **stative** in the legal sense — benefits simply accrue (*the proceeds inure to the beneficiary*), no progressive: ✗ *the proceeds are inuring to her*. The other life is participial *inured to*, a resultant state of hardening.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** in the legal sense — the subject is what accrues and *to* takes the party; the hardening sense lives almost entirely in *be/get inured to* plus a noun or **gerund** (*inured to being ignored*).
**Passive:** **restricted** — the accrual sense is intransitive and has no passive at all (✗ *the beneficiary is inured to*); the hardening sense exists chiefly as a passive participle, *he is inured to criticism*, the active (*the work inured him to it*) being rare.

Accrue to somebody's benefit — or, in the passive, be hardened to something by long exposure.

1. This warranty shall *inure to* the benefit of any subsequent owner.
2. Any savings from the settlement *inure to* the members of the class.
3. The rights granted here *inure to* the benefit of the licensee's successors.
4. Twelve years on the night desk had *inured* her *to* bad news at three in the morning.
5. He is thoroughly *inured to* being told the idea will never work.
6. Nurses in that unit become *inured to* the alarms within a month.

**See also:** [get used to, get accustomed to](#get-used-to-get-accustomed-to) &middot; [adapt to](#adapt-to) &middot; [belong to](#belong-to) &middot; [accustomed to](../../06-prepositions/catalog/15-dependent-adjectives.md#accustomed-to) &middot; [to](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#to)

### invest in

**Pronunciation:** /ɪnˈvɛst ɪn/ &middot; *in-VEST in* &middot; weak /ɪnˈvɛst ən/
**Русский:** вкладывать(ся) во что-то — деньги, время или силы в расчёте на отдачу
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*invest in retraining workers*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *invest in to retrain them*). A formal, unrelated pattern takes *with*: *invest someone with authority*.
**Pattern:** *invest in* [a company, a fund, a thing worth having] &middot; *invest* [an amount] *in* [something] &middot; *invest in* [verb-ing] &middot; *invest* [someone] *with* [authority] — formal &middot; *be invested in* [an outcome]
**Contrast:** ***Invest on* and ✗ *invest to* are errors** — the preposition is fixed at *in*. Two senses live in the same frame: the financial one (*invest in bonds*) and the everyday one meaning buy something worth the money (*invest in a decent mattress*). The passive *be invested in* has drifted to mean emotionally committed — *she's heavily invested in the outcome* — and is now more common in that sense than the literal one. Keep it apart from formal *invest someone with* a power or a title, which is the ceremonial sense behind *investiture*.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic; putting money, time, or trust into something: *she's investing in retraining the crew*. The participle *invested in* has drifted into a stative sense, emotionally committed.
**Transitivity:** **both** — **intransitive** with *in* (*invest in bonds*, *invest in a decent mattress*); **transitive** in the ceremonial frame *invest someone with authority*, and with an amount as object (*invested $40,000*).
**Passive:** **yes** — *the endowment is invested in municipal bonds*, *she's heavily invested in the outcome*, *he was invested with full authority*.

Put money, time, or effort into something you expect to pay off.

1. They *invested in* index funds and left it alone for thirty years.
2. The district is *investing in* early literacy instead of testing.
3. Honestly, *invest in* a good chair before you invest in a standing desk.
4. She *invested* four years *in* a degree she never used and doesn't regret.
5. The state has *invested* heavily *in* broadband for rural counties.
6. He's too *invested in* being right to hear the argument.

**See also:** [contribute to](#contribute-to) &middot; [pay for](19-phrasal-m-r.md#pay-for) &middot; [benefit from](#benefit-from) &middot; [specialize in](#specialize-in) &middot; [in](../../06-prepositions/catalog/01-core-in-on-at.md#in)

### invite to

**Pronunciation:** /ɪnˈvaɪt tu/ &middot; *in-VITE too* &middot; weak /ɪnˈvaɪt tə/
**Русский:** приглашать на, приглашать к — звать прийти или сделать что-то
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** *to* takes a noun phrase naming an event or a place — **no gerund after the preposition** (✗ *invited us to having dinner*). *Invite someone **to** do something* is a separate frame in which *to* is the **infinitive marker**, not the preposition: *invited us to speak*. Both are correct; what is impossible is mixing them.
**Pattern:** *invite* [someone] *to* [an event / a place] &middot; *invite* [someone] *to* [verb] &middot; *invite* [someone] *for* [dinner, drinks] &middot; *invite* [someone] *in* / *over* / *along* / *back* &middot; *be invited to* [something]
**Contrast:** **One preposition, two structures, and the difference is what follows it.** *Invite her to the wedding* (preposition + noun) against *invite her to speak at the wedding* (infinitive). Note the American directional particles that need no *to* at all: *invite them over*, *invite him in*, *invite her along*. *Invite for* names the occasion in a looser way (*invited us for coffee*), and *invite* also has a figurative transitive use meaning provoke: *that kind of memo invites trouble*.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic; a single act. A figurative transitive sense means provoke: *that kind of memo invites trouble*.
**Transitivity:** **transitive** — the guest is the direct object and *to* takes the event; the object is obligatory: ✗ *she invited to the wedding*.
**Passive:** **yes** — *we were invited to the wedding*, and the infinitive frame passivizes too: *they were invited to speak*.

Ask someone to come, or ask them to do something.

1. They *invited* half the department *to* the housewarming.
2. She was *invited to* speak at the conference in Portland.
3. We *invited* them *over* for the game and they stayed until midnight.
4. He wasn't *invited to* the wedding and has mentioned it four times.
5. The library *invites* the public *to* comment on the renovation plan.
6. *Invite* your neighbors *for* dinner once and you'll know within an hour.

**See also:** [introduce to](#introduce-to) &middot; [ask for](#ask-for-ask-about) &middot; [apply to](#apply-for-apply-to) &middot; [belong to](#belong-to) &middot; [to](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#to)

### involve in

**Pronunciation:** /ɪnˈvɑlv ɪn/ &middot; *in-VOLV in* &middot; weak /ɪnˈvɑlv ən/
**Русский:** вовлекать(ся) во что-то — участвовать в чём-то или включать как часть
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*involved in raising the money*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *involved in to raise the money*). But note the other frame: transitive *involve* meaning entail takes a **gerund with no preposition at all** — *the job involves traveling*, and ✗ *the job involves to travel* is a common and serious error.
**Pattern:** *involve* [someone] *in* [something] &middot; *be involved in* [something / verb-ing] &middot; [a job, a process] *involve* [verb-ing] — no preposition &middot; *get involved with* [a person, an organization]
**Contrast:** **Two patterns that look alike and are not.** *She's involved in the campaign* (prepositional, about participation) against *The campaign involves knocking on doors* (transitive, about what it entails). And *involved **with*** is different again: *involved in* a project means working on it, while *involved with* a person means romantically entangled, and *involved with* an organization means affiliated. *He got involved with her* and *He got involved in her case* are not remotely the same sentence.
**Verb type:** **both** — dynamic in the participation sense (*they got her involved in the campaign*), and **stative** in the transitive entail sense, which rejects the progressive: ✗ *the job is involving a lot of travel*.
**Transitivity:** **transitive** in both — *involve her in the planning*; and the entail sense takes an object or a bare gerund with no preposition: *the job involves traveling*, ✗ *the job involves to travel*.
**Passive:** **yes** in the participation sense, where the passive is the usual form — *she was involved in the campaign*, *he got involved with the union*. The entail sense resists a *by*-phrase: ✗ *a lot of travel is involved by the job*.

Take part in something, or have something as a necessary part.

1. Three agencies were *involved in* the response.
2. She's been *involved in* local politics since she was nineteen.
3. The renovation *involves* moving a load-bearing wall.
4. Nobody wanted to get *involved in* a dispute between neighbors.
5. He was *involved in* raising almost half the endowment.
6. The role *involves* a fair amount of weekend work.

**See also:** [participate in](#participate-in) &middot; [interfere in](#interfere-with-interfere-in) &middot; [contribute to](#contribute-to) &middot; [concentrate on](#concentrate-on) &middot; [work on](#work-on-work-for-work-with)

## K–M

### keep up with, catch up with, catch up on

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkip ʌp wɪθ/ &middot; *KEEP up with* &middot; weak /ˈkip əp wəθ/
**Русский:** не отставать от; догонять кого-то; наверстывать накопившееся
**Type:** verb + particle + preposition (phrasal-prepositional) &middot; **Register:** informal to neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** — *keep up with paying the invoices*, *catch up on grading*. **Never a to-infinitive** (✗ *keep up with to pay them*) and never a clause. The three words are welded together and the object always follows the preposition: ✗ *keep the news up with*, ✗ *catch my email up on*.
**Pattern:** *keep up with* [the news, a person, inflation, the payments] &middot; *catch up with* [someone ahead of you; also, meet socially] &middot; *catch up to* [someone] — the physical sense, American variant &middot; *catch up on* [sleep, email, the backlog] &middot; *keep up* — intransitive (*I couldn't keep up*)
**Contrast:** **One family, three jobs, and the preposition does the sorting.** *Keep up with* means stay level with something moving — the reading, the rent, a faster walker. *Catch up with* means close a gap that has already opened, and it has a second, friendlier life meaning meet for coffee (*let's catch up*) and a third, grimmer one meaning consequences arrive (*the drinking caught up with him*). *Catch up on* takes the backlog itself as its object — sleep, laundry, three weeks of email — and never a person: ✗ *catch up on my sister*. Note that *keep up* alone is intransitive, and *keep something up* (*keep the pressure up*) is a separable particle verb that belongs with the phrasal verbs, not here.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic; all three are durative and take the progressive: *she's catching up on three weeks of email*, *he isn't keeping up with the rent*.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** — phrasal-prepositional, so the verb has no direct object and nothing may be inserted: ✗ *keep the news up with*, ✗ *catch my email up on*.
**Passive:** **restricted** — three-word units passivize badly. *The payments couldn't be kept up with* is possible and clumsy; ✗ *the backlog has been caught up on* is not idiomatic. Keep the active.

Stay level with something moving, close a gap, or work through a backlog.

1. I can't *keep up with* the reading in that seminar.
2. Wages have not *kept up with* rent in this county since 2015.
3. She walked ahead and we *caught up with* her at the light.
4. Let's *catch up* next week when the quarter closes.
5. He spent Sunday *catching up on* email and laundry.
6. The paperwork he ignored in March finally *caught up with* him in July.
7. Nobody *keeps up with* paying two mortgages for long.

**See also:** [face up to, back out of, drop out of, walk out on](#face-up-to-back-out-of-drop-out-of-walk-out-on) &middot; [live up to](#live-up-to) &middot; [put up with](19-phrasal-m-r.md#put-up-with) &middot; [cope with](#cope-with) &middot; [with](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#with)

### laugh at

**Pronunciation:** /ˈlæf æt/ &middot; *LAF at* &middot; weak /ˈlæf ət/
**Русский:** смеяться над — at насмешка над кем-то, laugh with смеяться вместе с ним
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*laughed at being called sir*) — never a to-infinitive, never a clause. *Laugh* is intransitive: the thing amusing you always needs a preposition. ✗ *laugh the joke*.
**Pattern:** *laugh at* [someone / something] &middot; *laugh about* [something, usually later] &middot; *laugh with* [someone] &middot; *laugh off* [criticism] &middot; *be laughed at*
**Contrast:** ***Laugh at* mocks; *laugh about* shares the joke; *laugh with* includes the person.** *They laughed at him* is cruel and *They laughed with him* is not — the same event described from two sides, and the preposition is the whole difference. *Laugh **about*** is what you do afterward, when the thing has become funny (*we laugh about it now*). Note that *laugh at* can also mean dismiss as absurd: *the bank laughed at his projections*. The phrasal *laugh off* means brush aside.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic; a reaction you perform, freely progressive: *they were laughing at him*.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** — the thing amusing you always needs the preposition: ✗ *laugh the joke*. Bare *laugh* stands alone (*everybody laughed*).
**Passive:** **yes** — the prepositional passive is fully idiomatic: *he was laughed at by the whole room*, *her projections were laughed at*.

Show amusement at someone or something.

1. Nobody *laughed at* the joke and he told it twice more anyway.
2. They *laughed at* her accent for a year and then hired her.
3. We'll *laugh about* this eventually, but not this week.
4. He *laughed at* the very idea of finishing by Friday.
5. She *laughed at* being mistaken for the intern.
6. The whole table was *laughing with* him, not at him.

**See also:** [smile at](#smile-at) &middot; [stare at](#stare-at) &middot; [boast about](#boast-about) &middot; [complain about](#complain-to-complain-about) &middot; [at](../../06-prepositions/catalog/01-core-in-on-at.md#at)

### learn about, learn from

**Pronunciation:** /ˈlɜrn əˌbaʊt/ &middot; *LURN uh-bowt* &middot; weak /ˈlɜrn əbət/
**Русский:** узнавать о чём-то (about); учиться у кого-то или на чём-то (from)
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** *about* takes a noun phrase, a **gerund**, or a wh-clause. *from* takes a person, an experience, or a source. **Neither takes a to-infinitive** — but *learn* itself does, with no preposition: *learn to swim*. Bare *learn* is also transitive (*learn the routine*) and takes a clause (*learned that the office had moved*). The past is *learned* in American English; *learnt* is British.
**Pattern:** *learn about* [a subject] &middot; *learn from* [a person / an experience] &middot; *learn to* [verb] &middot; *learn* [something] &middot; *learn that* [clause] &middot; *learn of* [something] — formal, means hear about
**Contrast:** ***Learn about* is the subject; *learn from* is the source; *learn to* is the skill.** All three can appear in one sentence: *she learned about soil chemistry from her father and learned to test it herself*. The formal *learn **of*** means come to hear (*we learned of his death from the paper*) and sounds stiff in casual speech. Note the direction trap: *learn* is what the student does and *teach* is what the instructor does — ✗ *she learned me how to drive* is nonstandard for *taught me*.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic; a process, not a state, and it takes the progressive: *she's learning about soil chemistry*. The past is *learned* in American English.
**Transitivity:** **both** — **intransitive** with *about* and *from*; **transitive** bare (*learn the routine*), plus a to-infinitive (*learn to swim*) and a that-clause (*learned that the office had moved*).
**Passive:** **restricted** — the bare transitive passivizes (*the routine has to be learned by Friday*, *lessons were learned*), the prepositional frames do not: ✗ *the war was learned about*, ✗ *his father was learned from*.

Acquire knowledge or a skill.

1. He spent a semester *learning about* watershed management.
2. She *learned* everything she knows about wiring *from* her mother.
3. You *learn from* a bad manager exactly what not to do.
4. They *learned about* the closure from a sign on the door.
5. It's never too late to *learn to* swim.
6. We only *learned of* the change the week it took effect.

**See also:** [hear about](#hear-about-hear-from-hear-of) &middot; [explain to](#explain-to) &middot; [think about](#think-about-think-of) &middot; [refer to](#refer-to) &middot; [consult with](#consult-with)

### listen to

**Pronunciation:** /ˈlɪsən tu/ &middot; *LISS-un too* &middot; weak /ˈlɪsən tə/
**Русский:** слушать — предлог to обязателен: listen to music, не ✗ listen music (ср. hear)
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase, or a person plus a bare infinitive or *-ing* (*listen to her sing*, *listen to the rain falling*). A **gerund** alone is fine (*listen to arguing all day*), but **never a to-infinitive** and never a that-clause. *Listen* is intransitive and the object always needs *to*: ✗ *listen music*, ✗ *listen me*.
**Pattern:** *listen to* [something / someone] &middot; *listen to* [someone] *do* / *doing* &middot; *listen for* [a sound you're waiting for] &middot; *listen* (alone) &middot; *listen up* &middot; *Listen.*
**Contrast:** ***Listen music* is one of the most common errors learners make**, because *hear* is transitive and *listen* is not. *Hear* is the passive faculty and *listen to* is the deliberate act: *I heard the alarm* (it reached me) against *I listened to the whole thing* (I chose to). *Listen **for*** is different again — it means wait alertly for a particular sound: *listen for the buzzer*. And *listen to someone* often means heed them: *nobody listened to the engineer*.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic and deliberate, unlike the passive faculty *hear*: *I listened to the whole thing* against *I heard the alarm*.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** — the object always needs *to*: ✗ *listen music*, ✗ *listen me*. Bare *listen* stands alone as a command (*Listen.*).
**Passive:** **restricted** — natural with a person as object (*the engineer wasn't listened to*, *nobody was listened to*), stiff with a thing: ✗ *the album was listened to twice*.

Pay deliberate attention to a sound.

1. She *listens to* the same three albums on every long drive.
2. Nobody *listened to* the safety officer until after the accident.
3. *Listen to* him tell it and you'd think he built the thing himself.
4. I *listened to* about forty minutes of hold music.
5. *Listen for* the click; that's how you know it's seated.
6. You're not *listening to* me.

**See also:** [hear about](#hear-about-hear-from-hear-of) &middot; [hear of](#hear-about-hear-from-hear-of) &middot; [look at](#look-at-look-for-look-after-look-into) &middot; [stare at](#stare-at) &middot; [concentrate on](#concentrate-on)

### live up to

**Pronunciation:** /ˈlɪv ʌp tu/ &middot; *LIV up too* &middot; weak /ˈlɪv əp tə/
**Русский:** оправдывать (ожидания, репутацию) — соответствовать заданной планке
**Type:** verb + particle + preposition (phrasal-prepositional) &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase naming a standard, a promise, a reputation, or the hype. **A gerund is possible but rare** (*never lived up to being called a prodigy*), and there is **no to-infinitive** (✗ *live up to be a prodigy*) and no clause. Nothing may be inserted: ✗ *live it up to*, which is a different idiom entirely.
**Pattern:** *live up to* [expectations, a promise, a name, the billing] &middot; *fail to live up to* [something] &middot; *live up to* [verb-ing] — rare &middot; *live it up* — an unrelated idiom (enjoy yourself)
**Contrast:** **The word order matters more here than almost anywhere else in the file.** *Live up to it* means meet the standard; *live it up* means spend the weekend enjoying yourself. Against the neighbors: *live up to* a promise is meeting a standard someone set, *comply with* a rule is obeying an instruction, and *keep up with* something is staying level with a moving target. *Live with* is the resigned one — *live with the consequences* — and *live off* or *live on* names what supports you.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic; meeting a standard over time, and progressive: *the sequel isn't living up to the hype*.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** — phrasal-prepositional, no direct object, and nothing may be inserted: ✗ *live it up to*, which is a different idiom entirely.
**Passive:** **restricted** — *a promise that was never lived up to* is possible in writing but heavy; American speech keeps the active: *he never lived up to it*.

Meet the standard that a promise, a reputation, or an expectation set.

1. The sequel never *lives up to* the first film.
2. She has spent a decade *living up to* a nickname she got at nineteen.
3. The district failed to *live up to* the settlement it signed in 2019.
4. Few restaurants *live up to* that kind of advance press.
5. He worries about *living up to* what his father built.
6. The new hire *lived up to* every word in her references.

**See also:** [keep up with, catch up with, catch up on](#keep-up-with-catch-up-with-catch-up-on) &middot; [make up for](19-phrasal-m-r.md#make-up-for) &middot; [comply with](#comply-with) &middot; [look up to, look down on](#look-up-to-look-down-on) &middot; [to](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#to)

### long for

**Pronunciation:** /ˈlɔŋ fɔr/ &middot; *LAWNG for* &middot; weak /ˈlɔŋ fɚ/
**Русский:** тосковать по, страстно желать — обычно о недостижимом; книжн.
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** literary or heightened
**Complement:** *for* takes a noun phrase — a **gerund** is possible but unusual (*longed for being left alone*). To put an action after *long*, drop the preposition and use the infinitive: *long to go home*, which is the more natural form by far. Never a clause.
**Pattern:** *long for* [a noun phrase] &middot; *long to* [verb] &middot; *be longing for* [something] &middot; *a longing for* [something]
**Contrast:** **Register is the whole story here.** *Long for* is heightened, close to poetry, and an American in ordinary conversation says *miss*, *want*, or *can't wait for*. Its neighbors line up by intensity: *wish for* (mild), *hope for* (expectant), *long for* and *yearn for* (aching), *pine for* (aching and slightly comic). Note that with a verb, *long **to** go* is preferred over *long for going* — the opposite of *look forward to*, where the gerund is compulsory.
**Verb type:** **stative** — a sustained emotional state rather than an act, though unlike most statives it takes the progressive freely in literary use: *she was longing for home*.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** — everything comes through *for*; to put an action after it, drop the preposition and use the infinitive: *long to go home*.
**Passive:** **no** — the verb is intransitive and the prepositional passive is not English: ✗ *home was longed for by the whole crew*. Only the attributive participle survives: *the longed-for reunion*.

Want something badly, usually something absent or out of reach.

1. By February everyone in the office *longs for* daylight.
2. He *longed for* the noise of a city and moved back within a year.
3. She *longed to* see the house one more time.
4. They *long for* a version of the town that never really existed.
5. You could hear him *longing for* an argument.
6. After a week of rain we were all *longing for* one clear morning.

**See also:** [hope for](#hope-for) &middot; [wish for](#wish-for) &middot; [dream of](#dream-about-dream-of) &middot; [wait for](#wait-for-wait-on) &middot; [care about](#care-about-care-for)

### look at, look for, look after, look into

**Pronunciation:** /ˈlʊk æt/ &middot; *LOOK at* &middot; weak /ˈlʊk ət/
**Русский:** смотреть на; искать; присматривать за; разбираться в — предлог решает всё
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** All four take a noun phrase. *look at* and *look into* also take a **gerund** or a wh-clause (*look at raising the fee*, *look into what went wrong*). *look for* takes a noun phrase only — ✗ *look for getting a refund*. **None of them takes a to-infinitive**, and none takes a that-clause. Bare *look* takes an adjective, not an adverb: *look tired*, ✗ *look tiredly*.
**Pattern:** *look at* [something — direct your eyes, or examine] &middot; *look for* [something — try to find] &middot; *look after* [someone — take care of] &middot; *look into* [something — investigate] &middot; *look over* [something — review] &middot; *look through* [something — scan or search]
**Contrast:** **This is the family that makes *look* the most treacherous verb in the dictionary — six prepositions, six unrelated meanings, and no way to guess any of them.** *Look at the report* is reading it; *look for the report* is hunting for it; *look after the report* is nonsense unless the report is a child; *look into the report* is investigating what it says; *look over the report* is reviewing it quickly. Three more sit in their own entries: *look forward to* (anticipate), *look up to* (admire), *look down on* (despise). And *look up* a word is a seventh thing again. When in doubt, name the meaning in plain words rather than guessing a particle.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic in all four senses — reading, hunting, tending, investigating. Bare *look* also has a **linking** use taking an adjective: *look tired*, ✗ *look tiredly*.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** — every one of the four requires its preposition; the verb takes no bare object: ✗ *look the report*.
**Passive:** **yes** — this family gives English its textbook prepositional passives: *the complaint is being looked into*, *the kids were well looked after*, *the file was never looked at*.

Direct your eyes, search, tend, or investigate — the preposition decides which.

| Pattern | What it means | Example |
|---|---|---|
| *look **at*** | direct your eyes; examine | *Look at this photo.* |
| *look **for*** | try to find | *I'm looking for my keys.* |
| *look **after*** | take care of | *She looks after her nephew.* |
| *look **into*** | investigate | *We'll look into the complaint.* |
| *look **over*** | review quickly | *Look over the draft tonight.* |
| *look **through*** | scan or rummage | *He looked through the files.* |
| *look **forward to** + -ing* | anticipate with pleasure | *I look forward to hearing from you.* |
| *look **up to*** | admire | *He looks up to his sister.* |
| *look **down on*** | consider inferior | *They look down on outsiders.* |
| *look **up*** [a word] | find in a reference | *Look it up.* |

1. *Look at* the third column and tell me if that number makes sense.
2. I've been *looking for* my other glove since Tuesday.
3. Her neighbor *looks after* the dog whenever she travels.
4. The state is *looking into* how the permits were issued.
5. We're *looking at* raising the fee about eight percent.
6. He *looked through* the whole drawer and found nothing.
7. Can you *look over* the contract before I sign it?

**See also:** [look forward to](#look-forward-to) &middot; [look up to](#look-up-to-look-down-on) &middot; [search for](#search-for) &middot; [care for](#care-about-care-for) &middot; [take care of](#take-care-of) &middot; [concentrate on](#concentrate-on) &middot; [stare at](#stare-at)

### look forward to

**Pronunciation:** /ˈlʊk ˈfɔrwɚd tu/ &middot; *look FOR-werd too* &middot; weak /ˈlʊk ˈfɔrwɚd tə/
**Русский:** с нетерпением ждать — после to герундий: looking forward to hearing from you
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral; the standard closing of American business correspondence
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** — ***to* here is a preposition, not the infinitive marker**, so the verb after it must be *-ing*: *look forward to hearing from you*, never ✗ *look forward to hear from you*. A wh-clause is possible (*look forward to whatever comes next*). Never a to-infinitive, never a that-clause.
**Pattern:** *look forward to* [noun phrase] &middot; *look forward to* [verb-ing] &middot; *be looking forward to* [something] &middot; *I look forward to hearing from you* (fixed closing)
**Contrast:** **This is the single most-cited gerund trap in English**, and the reason is that the *to* looks exactly like the *to* of *want to go*. The test that settles it: substitute a plain noun. *I look forward to the meeting* works, so *to* is a preposition, so a verb after it must be *-ing*. The same test exposes the rest of the family — *object to*, *be used to*, *get used to*, *be accustomed to*, *resort to*, *confess to*, *admit to*, *commit to*, *devote yourself to*, *be opposed to*, all of them *-ing*. Register note: *I look forward to* is the formal closing and *I'm looking forward to* is the warmer one; both are correct.
**Verb type:** **stative** — an attitude of anticipation, not an act; it is unusual among statives in taking the progressive freely, and both forms are standard: *I look forward to hearing from you*, *I'm looking forward to it*.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** — no direct object; *to* is a **preposition**, so a verb after it must be *-ing*: ✗ *look forward to hear from you*.
**Passive:** **restricted** — grammatically available and almost never used; *your reply is looked forward to* is dead commercialese, and ✗ *the meeting is looked forward to by everyone* is not written.

Anticipate something with pleasure.

1. I *look forward to* hearing from you.
2. She's *looking forward to* three weeks with no email.
3. We *look forward to* working with your team on this.
4. He *looks forward to* the county fair the way children look forward to Christmas.
5. I'm *looking forward to* seeing the finished kitchen.
6. Nobody *looks forward to* open enrollment.

**See also:** [object to](#object-to) &middot; [resort to](#resort-to) &middot; [commit to](#commit-to) &middot; [get used to](#get-used-to-get-accustomed-to) &middot; [get around to](17-phrasal-g.md#get-around-to) &middot; [hope for](#hope-for) &middot; [long for](#long-for) &middot; [look at](#look-at-look-for-look-after-look-into) &middot; [accustomed to](../../06-prepositions/catalog/15-dependent-adjectives.md#accustomed-to) &middot; [to](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#to)

### look up to, look down on

**Pronunciation:** /ˈlʊk ˈʌp tu/ &middot; *look UP too* &middot; weak /ˈlʊk ˈʌp tə/
**Русский:** смотреть снизу вверх, уважать (up to); смотреть свысока, презирать (down on)
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or pronoun naming a person or group — **no gerund, no clause, no infinitive**. Both are fixed three-word units and neither can be split: ✗ *look him up to*, ✗ *look down them on*. The object always follows the whole phrase.
**Pattern:** *look up to* [someone] &middot; *look down on* [someone] &middot; *be looked up to* &middot; *be looked down on*
**Contrast:** **A matched pair of opposites, and neither one is literal.** *Look up to* means admire; *look down on* means consider beneath you. Do not confuse either with *look up* a word (consult a reference), which is a separate phrasal verb and **is** separable: *look it up*. The British *look down one's nose at* is the same idea with more scorn. Note that both of these passivize freely, which is unusual for prepositional verbs: *he was looked up to by everyone in the shop*.
**Verb type:** **stative** — standing attitudes of admiration and contempt, not events; the progressive is odd: ✗ *she is looking up to her brother these days*.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** — both are fixed three-word units taking a person through the preposition, and neither can be split: ✗ *look him up to*, ✗ *look down them on*.
**Passive:** **yes** — unusually free for prepositional verbs, as the entry notes: *he was looked up to by everyone in the shop*, *she was looked down on for years*.

Admire someone, or consider someone inferior.

1. Every apprentice in that shop *looked up to* her.
2. He *looks down on* anyone who didn't go to a four-year school.
3. Kids *look up to* coaches more than coaches realize.
4. The town has always been *looked down on* by the county seat.
5. She *looked up to* her older brother until she was about fourteen.
6. Nobody likes being *looked down on* by a twenty-four-year-old consultant.

**See also:** [look at](#look-at-look-for-look-after-look-into) &middot; [look forward to](#look-forward-to) &middot; [think of](#think-about-think-of) &middot; [approve of](#approve-of) &middot; [disapprove of](#disapprove-of)

### major in, minor in

**Pronunciation:** /ˈmeɪdʒɚ ɪn/ &middot; *MAY-jer in* &middot; weak /ˈmeɪdʒɚ ən/
**Русский:** специализироваться на (основной профиль); брать вторым профилем; амер. вуз
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral — standard American college vocabulary; British speakers say *read* [a subject] or *do a degree in*
**Complement:** noun phrase naming a field of study. **No gerund** (✗ *major in studying biology*), no infinitive, no clause. The verb has no other object: ✗ *she majored biology*, ✗ *she majored to biology*.
**Pattern:** *major in* [a subject] &middot; *minor in* [a subject] &middot; *double major in* [X] *and* [Y] &middot; *major in* [X] *with a minor in* [Y] &middot; *her major is* [a subject] &middot; *a major in* [a subject] — the noun keeps *in*
**Contrast:** ***Major in* is what a student does; *specialize in* is what a professional does.** You *major in* economics in college and *specialize in* bankruptcy work afterward. The preposition never changes: ✗ *major on chemistry* is not American English, and ✗ *graduate in chemistry* is not either — Americans *graduate **with** a degree **in** chemistry* or simply *graduate from* a school. Note that the same *in* stays on the noun: *a major in chemistry*, *a minor in Spanish*.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic and durative; a course of study you pursue: *she's majoring in biology*.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** — the field always comes through *in*, and the verb has no other object: ✗ *she majored biology*, ✗ *she majored to biology*.
**Passive:** **no** — intransitive, and the prepositional passive does not exist: ✗ *biology was majored in by half her class*.

Choose a field as the main, or the secondary, focus of a college degree.

1. She *majored in* mechanical engineering and *minored in* Portuguese.
2. Half the people I know who *majored in* philosophy ended up in software.
3. He *double majored in* history and studio art, which took five years.
4. You don't have to *major in* the subject to take the seminar.
5. Nobody *majors in* that anymore; the department closed in 2018.
6. A *minor in* statistics is worth more on the transcript than most electives.

**See also:** [specialize in](#specialize-in) &middot; [participate in](#participate-in) &middot; [succeed in](#succeed-in) &middot; [apply for, apply to](#apply-for-apply-to) &middot; [in](../../06-prepositions/catalog/01-core-in-on-at.md#in)

### militate against

**Pronunciation:** /ˈmɪləteɪt əˈɡɛnst/ &middot; *MIL-uh-tayt uh-GENST* &middot; weak /ˈmɪləteɪt əɡənst/
**Русский:** говорить против, свидетельствовать не в пользу — книжн.
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal — academic writing, judicial opinions, and policy analysis. An American meets it in a law review or a peer-reviewed paper, almost never in speech.
**Complement:** noun phrase naming a conclusion, a finding, an outcome, or a course of action, or a **gerund** (*militates against raising rates*). **No to-infinitive** (✗ *militates against to raise rates*) and no clause. The subject is a fact or a consideration, never a person: ✗ *the senator militates against the bill* — a person *argues against* it.
**Pattern:** [a fact, a factor] *militate against* [a conclusion, a course] &middot; *militate against* [verb-ing] &middot; *militate in favor of* [something] — the much rarer positive
**Contrast:** ***Militate* is not *mitigate*, and ✗ *mitigate against* is the single commonest error in this corner of the language.** *Mitigate* is transitive and means lessen: *mitigate the damage*, *mitigating circumstances*. *Militate against* means count as a reason against something. The two are unrelated words, and an editor will catch the swap every time. If the sentence would work with *argues against* or *weighs against*, *militate* is the word you want; if it would work with *reduces*, you want *mitigate*.
**Verb type:** **stative** — a fact or consideration counts as a reason against something; nothing is done, and the subject is never a person: ✗ *the senator militates against the bill*. Not the transitive *mitigate*: ✗ *mitigate against*.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** — the conclusion opposed always comes through *against*; there is no bare object: ✗ *militates the conclusion*.
**Passive:** **no** — intransitive and stative; ✗ *raising rates was militated against*. (Transitive *mitigate*, the word people mean by mistake, passivizes fine: *the damage was mitigated*.)

Be a consideration that weighs against a conclusion or a course of action.

1. Everything in the record *militates against* a finding of bad faith.
2. The cost of relocation *militates against* moving the plant at all.
3. Two factors *militate against* raising rates before the fall.
4. His inexperience *militates against* giving him the district by himself.
5. Nothing in the survey *militates against* the committee's reading of the data.
6. The short timeline *militates against* a full environmental review.

**See also:** [insure against](#insure-against) &middot; [protect from, protect against](#protect-from-protect-against) &middot; [warn about, warn against](#warn-about-warn-against) &middot; [decide on, decide against](#decide-on-decide-against) &middot; [against](../../06-prepositions/catalog/09-comparison-opposition.md#against)

### move for

**Pronunciation:** /ˈmuv fɔr/ &middot; *MOOV for* &middot; weak /ˈmuv fɚ/
**Русский:** ходатайствовать о — вносить формальное предложение в суде или на собрании; юр.
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal — courtroom and parliamentary procedure. An American meets it on a docket (*defendant moves for summary judgment*), in a transcript, or at a meeting run under *Robert's Rules*.
**Complement:** *for* takes a noun phrase naming the relief or action requested: *summary judgment*, *a mistrial*, *a continuance*, *dismissal*, *adjournment*. **No gerund** (✗ *move for dismissing the case*) and no clause after *for*. The verb's other two frames use no preposition at all: *move to* [verb] is a **to-infinitive** (*moved to dismiss*), and *move that* [clause] takes a subjunctive (*I move that the meeting be adjourned*).
**Pattern:** *move for* [relief] &middot; *move to* [verb] — infinitive, not a preposition &middot; *move that* [clause] — subjunctive &middot; *a motion for* [relief] &middot; *a motion to* [verb] — the nouns split the same way
**Contrast:** **Three frames, one verb, and the preposition is only in the first.** *Moved for summary judgment*, *moved to dismiss*, and *moved that the case be reassigned* are all correct and all mean ask formally; ✗ *moved for to dismiss* and ✗ *moved for that the case be reassigned* are not. Against the neighbors: you *move for* relief inside a case that already exists, you *petition for* something to start a proceeding, and you *apply for* a permit or a job. And do not confuse any of this with everyday *move to Denver*, where *to* marks direction.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic and performative: saying it does it, in court or under *Robert's Rules*. Non-progressive in use — *defendant moves for summary judgment*.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** in this frame — the relief comes through *for*; the verb's other two frames take a to-infinitive (*moved to dismiss*) or a subjunctive clause (*moved that the meeting be adjourned*), never a bare object: ✗ *moved summary judgment*.
**Passive:** **no** — ✗ *summary judgment was moved for* is not idiomatic; the record instead passivizes the ruling: *the motion was granted*, *summary judgment was denied*.

Ask a court or a meeting formally for a ruling or an action.

1. The defense *moved for* a directed verdict at the close of the state's case.
2. Counsel *moved for* a continuance, citing the witness's surgery.
3. Both parties *moved for* summary judgment, and the judge denied both motions.
4. I *move for* adjournment.
5. The plaintiff *moved to* strike two paragraphs of the answer.
6. She *moved that* the vote be taken by roll call.

**See also:** [petition for](#petition-for) &middot; [apply for, apply to](#apply-for-apply-to) &middot; [ask for, ask about](#ask-for-ask-about) &middot; [comply with](#comply-with) &middot; [for](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#for)

## O–P

### object to

**Pronunciation:** /əbˈdʒɛkt tu/ &middot; *ub-JEKT too* &middot; weak /əbˈdʒɛkt tə/
**Русский:** возражать против — после to герундий: object to being questioned
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal
**Complement:** noun phrase, **gerund**, or possessive plus gerund (*objected to my being there*) — ***to* is a preposition here, so a to-infinitive is impossible**: ✗ *object to be questioned*, ✗ *object to answer*. Before a clause the preposition drops: *objected that the sample was too small*.
**Pattern:** *object to* [noun phrase] &middot; *object to* [verb-ing] &middot; *object to* [someone's] *-ing* &middot; *object that* [clause] &middot; *object* (alone) &middot; *I object.* &middot; *objection to* [something]
**Contrast:** **Same trap as *look forward to*, and the same test defuses it:** you can say *object to the delay*, so *to* is a preposition, so it must be *object to being delayed*. Note the difference from [*oppose*](#oppose), which is transitive and takes no preposition at all — *oppose the merger*, ✗ *oppose to the merger* — although the adjective goes back to a preposition, *opposed to the merger*. In register, *object to* is stronger and more formal than *mind* (*I don't mind waiting*) and weaker than *refuse*.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic; a speech act in the courtroom or the meeting (*counsel objected*), and by extension a standing position (*she objects to being called ma'am*).
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** — no direct object, unlike transitive *oppose*: *oppose the merger*, ✗ *oppose to the merger*, against *object to the merger*. Before a clause the preposition drops: *objected that the sample was too small*.
**Passive:** **restricted** — fine in formal minutes and briefs (*the proposal was objected to by two members*, *the exhibit was objected to*), unnatural in speech.

State that you are against something.

1. Two members *objected to* holding the vote without a quorum.
2. She *objects to* being called by her first name in court.
3. Nobody *objected to* the change until the invoices arrived.
4. He *objected to* my parking in front of his house, which is a public street.
5. The defense *objected* and was overruled.
6. I don't *object to* the fee; I object to finding out about it afterward.

**See also:** [look forward to](#look-forward-to) &middot; [oppose](#oppose) &middot; [complain about](#complain-to-complain-about) &middot; [approve of](#approve-of) &middot; [disapprove of](#disapprove-of) &middot; [agree to](#agree-with-agree-to-agree-on) &middot; [insist on](#insist-on)

### occur to

**Pronunciation:** /əˈkɜr tu/ &middot; *uh-KUR too* &middot; weak /əˈkɜr tə/
**Русский:** приходить в голову кому-то — it never occurred to me; ср. occur происходить
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** *to* takes a person only. The subject is nearly always the dummy *it*, followed by a **that-clause** or a **to-infinitive**: *it occurred to me that she was right*, *it never occurred to him to ask*. A noun phrase subject also works: *the idea occurred to her in the shower*. There is no gerund pattern here at all.
**Pattern:** *it occur to* [someone] *that* [clause] &middot; *it occur to* [someone] *to* [verb] &middot; [an idea] *occur to* [someone] &middot; *it never occurred to me* &middot; *occur* (alone — happen)
**Contrast:** **Two verbs share one spelling.** Plain *occur* means happen and takes no preposition: *the outage occurred at 3 a.m.*, ✗ *occurred to 3 a.m.* *Occur **to*** means come into someone's mind, and always names a person. American speakers use the negative far more than the positive: *It never occurred to me* is the everyday form. Note the spelling of the inflections — *occurred*, *occurring*, with two *r*'s.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic but punctual, an idea arriving; it still rejects the progressive: ✗ *it is occurring to me that she was right*. Note the spellings *occurred*, *occurring*.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** — the subject is the idea (usually dummy *it* plus a that-clause or infinitive) and *to* takes the person: *it never occurred to him to ask*.
**Passive:** **no** — intransitive, and the person cannot become a subject: ✗ *I was occurred to*, ✗ *he was never occurred to*.

Come into someone's mind.

1. It never *occurred to* me that he might say no.
2. Did it *occur to* anyone to check whether the room was booked?
3. The solution *occurred to* her on the drive home.
4. It *occurs to* me that we've been arguing about two different documents.
5. It hadn't *occurred to* the committee to ask the tenants.
6. The thought must have *occurred to* him at some point.

**See also:** [remind of](#remind-of-remind-about) &middot; [think of](#think-about-think-of) &middot; [explain to](#explain-to)

### offset against, set off against

**Pronunciation:** /ˌɔfˈsɛt əˈɡɛnst/ &middot; *awf-SET uh-GENST* &middot; weak /ˌɔfˈsɛt əɡənst/ &mdash; the **verb** stresses the second syllable, the noun the first (*an ˈoffset*)
**Русский:** зачитывать одно против другого — засчитывать в погашение; фин./юр.
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal — accounting, tax, and contract drafting
**Complement:** the amount applied is the direct object and *against* takes the amount it is applied to: *offset the loss against this year's gains*. **No gerund** (✗ *offset against having paid twice*), no infinitive, no clause. Both slots must name quantities of the same kind — money against money, hours against hours: ✗ *offset the delay against the budget*.
