**Variants:** ***Set off against*** is the same operation in the language of contracts and litigation, and its noun is a ***setoff*** (or *set-off*): *the buyer asserted a setoff against the unpaid balance*. Note also the commoner everyday frame ***offset by***, where the counterweight follows a passive: *the gains were offset by higher fuel costs*.
**Pattern:** *offset* [an amount] *against* [another amount] &middot; *set off* [a claim] *against* [a debt] &middot; [something] *be offset by* [something else] &middot; *a setoff against* [a balance] &middot; *deduct* [an amount] *from* [a total] — the neighboring frame with *from*
**Contrast:** ***Offset against* subtracts one figure from another of the same kind; *deduct from* removes a figure from a total.** You *offset* capital losses *against* capital gains, but you *deduct* mileage *from* taxable income. *Offset by* is the passive counterweight frame and needs no arithmetic at all (*the raise was offset by the new premium*), and *make up for* is the plain-English version of the whole idea. In British and legal drafting *set off against* dominates; American filings use both, and *offset* is the safer everyday choice.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic; an arithmetical operation performed on two quantities of the same kind.
**Transitivity:** **transitive** — the amount applied is the direct object and *against* names what it is applied to: *offset the loss against this year's gains*; the object is obligatory: ✗ *offset against the gains*.
**Passive:** **yes** — *the loss can be offset against next year's gains*, and the counterweight frame is passive by nature: *the raise was offset by the new premium*.

Apply one amount against another so that the two cancel.

1. Capital losses may be *offset against* capital gains in the same tax year.
2. The contractor sought to *set off* the cost of repairs *against* the final invoice.
3. Any advance is *offset against* the royalties the book eventually earns.
4. Higher volume was *offset by* a thinner margin on every unit.
5. The tenant *set off* three months of unmade repairs *against* the rent she owed.
6. Overtime in December was *offset against* the hours he had banked in the fall.

**See also:** [make up for](19-phrasal-m-r.md#make-up-for) &middot; [compensate for](#compensate-for) &middot; [charge with, charge for](#charge-with-charge-for) &middot; [pay for](19-phrasal-m-r.md#pay-for) &middot; [against](../../06-prepositions/catalog/09-comparison-opposition.md#against)

### participate in

**Pronunciation:** /pɑrˈtɪsəˌpeɪt ɪn/ &middot; *par-TISS-uh-payt in* &middot; weak /pɑrˈtɪsəˌpeɪt ən/
**Русский:** участвовать в — принимать деятельное участие
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal to neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*participated in drafting the report*) — **never a to-infinitive**. *Participate* is intransitive and cannot take a bare object: ✗ *participate the study*.
**Pattern:** *participate in* [an activity, a program, a study] &middot; *participate in* [verb-ing] &middot; *participate* (alone) &middot; *take part in* [something] — the plainer synonym
**Contrast:** ***Participate to* and ✗ *participate at* are errors**; the preposition is fixed at *in*. *Take part in* means exactly the same thing in plainer English and is what Americans say out loud. Keep it apart from *attend*, which is transitive and takes no preposition at all — *attend the meeting*, ✗ *attend to the meeting* — so *she attended the workshop* (was present) and *she participated in the workshop* (did the exercises) are genuinely different claims.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic; doing the exercises, not merely being present — compare transitive *attend*, which claims only presence.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** — the verb cannot take a bare object: ✗ *participate the study*. Everything comes through *in*.
**Passive:** **no** — intransitive, and the prepositional passive is unidiomatic: ✗ *the study was participated in by forty volunteers*. Say *forty volunteers took part*.

Take an active part in something.

1. Four hundred families *participated in* the pilot program.
2. He *participated in* drafting the original charter.
3. Students who *participate in* the survey get a gift card.
4. Only two states declined to *participate in* the settlement.
5. You can attend without *participating in* the breakout sessions.
6. She *participated in* every rehearsal and missed the performance.

**See also:** [involve in](#involve-in) &middot; [attend](#attend) &middot; [contribute to](#contribute-to) &middot; [compete with](#compete-with-compete-against-compete-for) &middot; [interfere in](#interfere-with-interfere-in)

### petition for

**Pronunciation:** /pəˈtɪʃən fɔr/ &middot; *puh-TISH-un for* &middot; weak /pəˈtɪʃən fɚ/
**Русский:** подавать ходатайство о — официально просить суд или орган; юр.
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal — courts, agencies, legislatures, and civic organizing. Americans meet the frame in the First Amendment itself: the right *to petition the Government for a redress of grievances*.
**Complement:** *for* takes a noun phrase naming what is asked for — *review*, *custody*, *bankruptcy*, *clemency*, *a variance*, *a recount*. **No gerund** (✗ *petition for getting the fee waived*) and no clause. The body petitioned is a direct object placed before *for*: *petition the board for a hearing*. A **to-infinitive** frame exists and is not this preposition: *petitioned the council to rezone the parcel*.
**Pattern:** *petition for* [relief, a status, a hearing] &middot; *petition* [a body] *for* [something] &middot; *petition* [a body] *to* [verb] — infinitive &middot; *file a petition for* [something] &middot; *petition in bankruptcy*
**Contrast:** **Three verbs, three settings.** You *apply for* a permit, a job, or a loan — routine, on a form. You *petition for* something that requires a body to exercise judgment, and the word implies a formal filing or a signed request. You *move for* relief inside a case already pending. *Sue for* is different again: it names the remedy you are demanding in litigation (*sue for damages*). Note that the noun keeps the preposition — *a petition for review*, *a petition for certiorari* — and that with a person the verb goes straight to the object: *petition the mayor*, not ✗ *petition to the mayor*.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic and formal: a filing or a signed request, a single act.
**Transitivity:** **both** — **intransitive** with *for* alone (*petitioned for review*), **transitive** when the body petitioned is named, which takes no preposition: *petition the board for a hearing*, ✗ *petition to the mayor*.
**Passive:** **restricted** — the person-object promotes (*the board was petitioned for a hearing*), the *for*-object does not: ✗ *review was petitioned for*.

Ask a court, an agency, or an official body formally for something.

1. The company *petitioned for* Chapter 11 protection in March.
2. Six hundred residents *petitioned for* a special election.
3. She *petitioned the court for* sole custody after the second incident.
4. Counsel *petitioned for* review, and the Supreme Court declined to hear it.
5. The neighbors *petitioned* the zoning board *for* a variance and lost twice.
6. He *petitioned for* clemency every year for a decade.

**See also:** [move for](#move-for) &middot; [apply for, apply to](#apply-for-apply-to) &middot; [ask for, ask about](#ask-for-ask-about) &middot; [foreclose on](#foreclose-on) &middot; [for](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#for)

### plan on

**Pronunciation:** /ˈplæn ɑn/ &middot; *PLAN on* &middot; weak /ˈplæn ən/
**Русский:** рассчитывать, планировать — после on герундий: plan on staying; разг.
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral American; more common in the U.S. than in Britain
**Complement:** **gerund** or noun phrase (*plan on staying*, *plan on rain*) — **never a to-infinitive after the preposition** (✗ *plan on to stay*). The infinitive belongs to the other frame: *plan to stay*, with no preposition. Both are correct and mean the same thing.
**Pattern:** *plan on* [verb-ing] &middot; *plan on* [noun phrase] &middot; *plan to* [verb] &middot; *plan for* [a contingency] &middot; *plan* [an event] &middot; *plan on* [someone] *-ing*
**Contrast:** ***Plan on doing* is characteristically American**; British English leans harder on *plan to do*. Both are standard in the U.S., with *plan on* slightly more casual and slightly more provisional — *I plan to retire in June* sounds settled, *I'm planning on retiring in June* sounds like it could move. *Plan **for*** is different: it means prepare for a possibility (*plan for a bad winter*), not intend. And *plan on* someone doing something means count on it: *don't plan on him being early*.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic; forming an intention, freely progressive and slightly provisional: *I'm planning on retiring in June*.
**Transitivity:** **both** — **intransitive** with *on* plus a gerund or noun (*plan on staying*, *plan on rain*); **transitive** bare (*plan the trip*), and with a to-infinitive and no preposition (*plan to stay*).
**Passive:** **restricted** — the bare transitive passivizes (*the route was planned by a committee*), the prepositional frame does not: ✗ *staying an extra night was planned on*.

Intend to do something.

1. We're *planning on* driving up Thursday night.
2. Don't *plan on* getting a seat if you show up at seven.
3. She *plans on* finishing the certificate in the spring.
4. *Plan on* about two hours with traffic.
5. They *planned for* a shortfall and got a surplus instead.
6. I hadn't *planned on* any of this.

**See also:** [decide on](#decide-on-decide-against) &middot; [commit to](#commit-to) &middot; [prepare for](#prepare-for) &middot; [hope for](#hope-for) &middot; [count on](#count-on) &middot; [depend on](#depend-on)

### point at, point to

**Pronunciation:** /ˈpɔɪnt æt/ &middot; *POYNT at* &middot; weak /ˈpɔɪnt ət/
**Русский:** показывать пальцем на (at); указывать на, свидетельствовать о (to)
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase only — **no gerund, no clause**. *Point out* is a separate, separable phrasal verb that does take a that-clause: *point out that the numbers don't match*, and *point something out to someone*.
**Pattern:** *point at* [a person, a thing] &middot; *point to* [a direction, a piece of evidence] &middot; *point* [something] *at* [something] &middot; *point out* [something] &middot; *point out to* [someone] *that* [clause] &middot; *point toward* [a conclusion]
**Contrast:** ***At* is the physical gesture; *to* is the argument.** *She pointed at the map* is a finger; *the data point to a slowdown* is an inference. Where a person is the target, *point at* is faintly rude — American children are told not to point at people — while *point to someone* as a model is a compliment. *Point **out*** is a third thing entirely: it means draw attention to a fact, takes an object or a clause, and is separable (*point it out*). ✗ *point on* is not English in any of these senses.
**Verb type:** **both** — dynamic in the gesture sense (*she was pointing at the map*); **stative** in the evidential sense, which resists the progressive: ✗ *the data are pointing to a slowdown* is far weaker than *the data point to a slowdown*.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** in both — ✗ *point the map*. The verb is transitive only when what you aim is the object: *point a finger at him*, *point the camera at the door*.
**Passive:** **restricted** — the gesture sense passivizes with a person (*nobody likes being pointed at*); the evidential sense does not: ✗ *a slowdown is pointed to by the data*.

Indicate something with a finger, or indicate it with evidence.

1. He *pointed at* the crack in the foundation and said nothing.
2. Every indicator *points to* a soft landing, which is what they said last time too.
3. Don't *point at* people.
4. She *pointed* the flashlight *at* the ceiling to see the water stain.
5. The report *points to* deferred maintenance as the underlying cause.
6. I should *point out* that none of this was in the original scope.

**See also:** [aim at](#aim-at) &middot; [refer to](#refer-to) &middot; [lead to](18-phrasal-h-l.md#lead-to) &middot; [stare at](#stare-at) &middot; [comment on](#comment-on)

### prefer to

**Pronunciation:** /prɪˈfɜr tu/ &middot; *prih-FUR too* &middot; weak /prɪˈfɜr tə/
**Русский:** предпочитать что-то чему-то — здесь to значит чем, а не к
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal
**Complement:** In the comparison frame, ***to* is a preposition** and both sides must be the same kind of thing — two noun phrases (*prefer tea to coffee*) or **two gerunds** (*prefer walking to driving*). ✗ *prefer to walk to drive* is impossible. In the other frame, *prefer* takes a bare **to-infinitive** with no comparison (*I'd prefer to walk*), and *would prefer* takes *that* plus a subjunctive (*I'd prefer that he not come*).
**Pattern:** *prefer* [A] *to* [B] &middot; *prefer* [verb-ing] *to* [verb-ing] &middot; *prefer to* [verb] &middot; *prefer* [verb-ing] &middot; *would prefer* [someone] *to* [verb] &middot; *would prefer that* [clause]
**Contrast:** **The trap is that *prefer* uses *to* for two completely different jobs.** *I prefer coffee **to** tea* — preposition, comparison. *I prefer **to** drink coffee* — infinitive marker, no comparison. You cannot run both at once, which is why ✗ *I prefer to walk to drive* collapses; say *I prefer walking to driving*, or switch to *I'd rather walk than drive*, which is the form Americans actually use. Note that *than* is impossible with *prefer* in careful writing (✗ *prefer coffee than tea*), even though it is common in speech.
**Verb type:** **stative** — a standing preference, not an act, and it rejects the progressive: ✗ *I am preferring tea to coffee*.
**Transitivity:** **transitive** — *prefer tea to coffee*, *prefer walking to driving*; both sides of the comparison must match, so ✗ *prefer to walk to drive* collapses. The object is obligatory: ✗ *she prefers to coffee*.
**Passive:** **restricted** — the agentless passive is standard in impersonal writing (*the metric version is preferred to the older one*), but the stative verb refuses a *by*-phrase: ✗ *tea is preferred to coffee by her*.

Like one thing better than another.

1. She *prefers* mornings *to* evenings for anything requiring a brain.
2. I *prefer* driving *to* flying for anything under six hours.
3. Most of the staff *prefer* the older system.
4. He'd *prefer to* handle it himself, which is the whole problem.
5. They *prefer* email *to* phone calls, and they are correct.
6. I'd *prefer that* we settle this before the meeting.

**See also:** [choose between](#choose-between) &middot; [compare to](#compare-to-compare-with) &middot; [decide on](#decide-on-decide-against) &middot; [long for](#long-for) &middot; [in preference to](../../06-prepositions/catalog/09-comparison-opposition.md#in-preference-to)

### prepare for

**Pronunciation:** /prɪˈpɛr fɔr/ &middot; *prih-PAIR for* &middot; weak /prɪˈpɛr fɚ/
**Русский:** готовиться к — заранее приводить себя в готовность
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** *for* takes a noun phrase or a **gerund** (*prepare for having to move*) — **never a to-infinitive after the preposition** (✗ *prepare for to move*). *Prepare* also takes a bare to-infinitive with no preposition: *prepare to move*, *be prepared to negotiate* — and that frame means readiness to act, not readiness for an event.
**Pattern:** *prepare for* [an event] &middot; *prepare for* [verb-ing] &middot; *prepare* [someone] *for* [something] &middot; *prepare to* [verb] &middot; *be prepared for* [something] &middot; *be prepared to* [verb]
**Contrast:** ***Prepared for* and *prepared to* are not the same claim.** *We're prepared for a strike* means we have contingencies; *We're prepared to strike* means we're willing to do it. Reversing them in a negotiation would be expensive. Compare *get ready for*, the plain synonym, and *provide for*, which means support financially. Note also transitive *prepare*: you *prepare a meal*, *prepare a brief*, *prepare the ground* — no preposition.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic and durative: *they've been preparing for a strike all month*.
**Transitivity:** **both** — **intransitive** with *for* (*prepare for a strike*) or a bare to-infinitive (*prepared to negotiate*); **transitive** with no preposition (*prepare a meal*, *prepare a brief*).
**Passive:** **yes** in the transitive frame — *the brief was prepared overnight*. The prepositional frame is **restricted**: *everything had been prepared for* is possible, ✗ *a strike was prepared for by management* is not.

Get ready for something in advance.

1. The whole county spent a week *preparing for* the storm.
2. She's been *preparing for* the boards since March.
3. Nothing *prepares* you *for* how loud a newborn is.
4. *Prepare for* having to explain this twice.
5. They were *prepared for* a long negotiation and got a handshake in twenty minutes.
6. He's *prepared to* walk away if the number doesn't move.

**See also:** [plan on](#plan-on) &middot; [provide for](#provide-with-provide-for) &middot; [allow for](#allow-for) &middot; [protect against](#protect-from-protect-against) &middot; [wait for](#wait-for-wait-on)

### preside over

**Pronunciation:** /prɪˈzaɪd ˈoʊvɚ/ &middot; *prih-ZIDE OH-ver* &middot; weak /prɪˈzaɪd əvɚ/
**Русский:** председательствовать на, возглавлять — руководить заседанием, органом, эпохой
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal — courts, boards, ceremonies, and, with a twist of irony, institutional decline
**Complement:** noun phrase naming a proceeding, a body, an era, or a process: *preside over the trial*, *over the committee*, *over the collapse of the company*. **No gerund** (✗ *preside over running the meeting*), no infinitive, no clause. The verb needs the preposition — ✗ *preside the meeting* is not English, though *chair the meeting* is.
**Variants:** ***Preside at*** is used for a single ceremony or sitting — *preside at the wedding*, *at the hearing* — while *preside over* takes the whole proceeding, body, or period. Both are standard; *over* is far commoner.
**Pattern:** *preside over* [a trial, a meeting, an agency, an era] &middot; *preside at* [a ceremony, a hearing] &middot; *preside* — intransitive (*Judge Alvarez presided*) &middot; *presiding judge* / *presiding officer* &middot; *the president of* [an organization] — the noun takes *of*
**Contrast:** ***Preside over* always takes a preposition; its synonyms usually do not.** *Chair the committee*, *run the meeting*, and *oversee the project* are all transitive, and ✗ *preside the committee* is the error that follows from them. The ironic use is worth knowing because it is everywhere in American commentary: *he presided over the loss of half the endowment* attributes no action to the subject at all, only the office he held while it happened.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic and durative; holding the chair while something runs its course, and progressive: *Judge Ellis is presiding over the trial*.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** — the verb needs its preposition: ✗ *preside the meeting*, unlike transitive *chair*, *run*, and *oversee*.
**Passive:** **yes** — one of the fully natural prepositional passives: *the trial was presided over by Judge Ellis*, *a hearing presided over by the deputy commissioner*.

Be the person in charge of a proceeding, a body, or a period.

1. Judge Alvarez *presided over* the trial for eleven weeks.
2. She *presided over* the commission during its most productive stretch.
3. He *presided over* a merger that cost four hundred people their jobs.
4. The vice president *presides over* the Senate and votes only to break a tie.
5. A retired bishop *presided at* the funeral.
6. Whoever *presides over* that department inherits a decade of deferred maintenance.

**See also:** [prevail over](#prevail-over) &middot; [devolve on, devolve upon](#devolve-on-devolve-upon) &middot; [participate in](#participate-in) &middot; [belong to](#belong-to) &middot; [over](../../06-prepositions/catalog/04-movement-direction.md#over)

### prevail over

**Pronunciation:** /prɪˈveɪl ˈoʊvɚ/ &middot; *prih-VAYL OH-ver* &middot; weak /prɪˈveɪl əvɚ/
**Русский:** одержать верх над — победить соперника, притязание или препятствие
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal — law, sports writing, and scripture-flavored prose
**Complement:** noun phrase naming an opponent, a rival claim, or an obstacle. **No gerund** (✗ *prevailed over being outspent*), no infinitive, no clause. The verb also stands alone (*the plaintiff prevailed*) and takes other prepositions in other senses, listed below.
**Variants:** ***Prevail against*** is the older, more literary form and survives in fixed phrases (*the gates of hell shall not prevail against it*); in legal writing *prevail over* a competing provision is the standard. ***Prevail in*** names the arena rather than the loser: *prevailed in the arbitration*.
**Pattern:** *prevail over* / *against* [an opponent, a competing claim] &middot; *prevail in* [a suit, an election] &middot; *prevail* — intransitive (*the plaintiff prevailed*) &middot; *prevail on* / *upon* [someone] *to* [verb] — persuade, a different pattern &middot; *the prevailing wage, the prevailing wind* — be widespread
**Contrast:** **Two patterns, and confusing them changes the meaning completely.** *Prevail over someone* is defeating them; *prevail on someone to do something* is talking them into it — and that second frame takes a person and then a **to-infinitive**: *prevailed on her to stay another year*. A third, intransitive sense means be widespread or hold sway (*the practice still prevails in rural counties*, *the prevailing view*). In drafting, *this section shall prevail over any conflicting provision* is the standard way to say which clause wins.
**Verb type:** **both** — dynamic in the defeat sense, an achievement (*the plaintiff prevailed over three co-defendants*); **stative** in the be-widespread sense, which rejects the progressive: ✗ *the practice is prevailing in rural counties*, *the prevailing view*.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** in every sense — the opponent comes through *over*, the person you talk around through *on* plus a to-infinitive (*prevailed on her to stay*); ✗ *prevailed the defendant*.
**Passive:** **no** for *prevail over* — intransitive: ✗ *the defendant was prevailed over*. But *prevail on* passivizes and is idiomatic: *she was prevailed upon to stay another year*.

Win out over an opponent, a rival claim, or an obstacle.

1. The plaintiffs *prevailed over* three motions to dismiss before reaching trial.
2. Where the two provisions conflict, the later one *prevails over* the earlier.
3. Nebraska *prevailed over* a heavily favored opponent in double overtime.
4. Common sense finally *prevailed over* the committee's fondness for process.
5. She *prevailed in* the arbitration but spent more than she recovered.
6. His sister *prevailed upon* him to stay through the holidays.

**See also:** [preside over](#preside-over) &middot; [compete with, compete against, compete for](#compete-with-compete-against-compete-for) &middot; [militate against](#militate-against) &middot; [insist on](#insist-on) &middot; [over](../../06-prepositions/catalog/04-movement-direction.md#over)

### prevent from

**Pronunciation:** /prɪˈvɛnt frʌm/ &middot; *prih-VENT from* &middot; weak /prɪˈvɛnt frəm/
**Русский:** не давать сделать, предотвращать — после from герундий: prevent him from leaving
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **gerund** or noun phrase — ***never a to-infinitive*** (✗ *prevent him to leave*, one of the highest-frequency advanced errors in English). The only correct verb form after *from* is *-ing*: *prevent him from leaving*. *Prevent* also takes a plain object with no preposition: *prevent an accident*.
**Pattern:** *prevent* [someone / something] *from* [verb-ing] &middot; *prevent* [something] &middot; *be prevented from* [verb-ing] &middot; *prevent* [someone] [verb-ing] — British; add *from* in American English
**Contrast:** ***Prevent someone to do something* does not exist.** The pattern is *from* plus *-ing*, and it is shared by a whole family: *stop from*, *keep from*, *discourage from*, *prohibit from*, *ban from*, *bar from*, *deter from*, *hinder from*, *restrain from*. Their positive counterparts take infinitives — *allow to*, *permit to*, *enable to*, *encourage to* — which is exactly why the error is so persistent. British English also permits *prevent him leaving* with no preposition; American editing puts the *from* back in.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic; stopping something before it happens.
**Transitivity:** **transitive** — the person or thing stopped is the direct object and *from* takes a **gerund**: *prevent him from leaving*, ✗ *prevent him to leave*. Bare transitive is also fine: *prevent an accident*.
**Passive:** **yes** — *he was prevented from boarding*, *the leak was prevented*. The passive keeps the same *from* plus *-ing*: ✗ *he was prevented to board*.

Stop something from happening, or stop someone from acting.

1. A bad hinge *prevented* the door *from* closing all the way.
2. Nothing *prevents* you *from* filing an amended return.
3. The guardrail *prevented* the car *from* going into the ravine.
4. She was *prevented from* testifying by a scheduling order.
5. Two inches of insulation *prevents* most of the heat loss.
6. What's *preventing* us *from* starting today?

**See also:** [discourage from](#discourage-from) &middot; [protect from](#protect-from-protect-against) &middot; [allow for](#allow-for) &middot; [interfere with](#interfere-with-interfere-in)

### protect from, protect against

**Pronunciation:** /prəˈtɛkt frʌm/ &middot; *pruh-TEKT from* &middot; weak /prəˈtɛkt frəm/
**Русский:** защищать от — оберегать от опасности или вреда
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*protect against having to pay twice*) — never a to-infinitive. Both prepositions take the same complements and are largely interchangeable; see the contrast for the tendency that separates them.
**Pattern:** *protect* [someone] *from* [harm] &middot; *protect* [someone] *against* [a risk] &middot; *be protected from* / *against* [something] &middot; *protect* [something] — no preposition &middot; *protection from* / *against* [something]
**Contrast:** ***From* leans toward harm already present; *against* leans toward risk anticipated.** You *protect a child from the sun* (it's shining now) and *protect a portfolio against inflation* (it might come). The overlap is enormous and no American will misunderstand either. One place *against* wins outright is inoculation and insurance: *vaccinated against measles*, *insured against flood*, *hedge against a downturn* — ✗ *vaccinated from measles* is wrong. *Guard against* takes only *against*, and *shield from* takes only *from*.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic and often durative: *the coating is protecting the deck from UV*.
**Transitivity:** **transitive** — the person or thing protected is the direct object; the object is obligatory, reflexive if need be: ✗ *protect from the sun* with nobody named. Both prepositions take the same complements.
**Passive:** **yes**, and the passive is the everyday form — *the portfolio is protected against inflation*, *she was protected from the worst of it*, *vaccinated against measles*.

Keep someone or something safe.

1. A good hat *protects* you *from* more sun than sunscreen does.
2. The statute *protects* whistleblowers *from* retaliation.
3. The vaccine *protects against* four strains.
4. Nothing *protects* a small business *against* a bad landlord.
5. The seawall *protects* the district *from* ordinary tides and nothing more.
6. She *protected* her sources *from* everyone, including her editor.

**See also:** [prevent from](#prevent-from) &middot; [save from](#save-from) &middot; [hide from](#hide-from) &middot; [deprive of](#deprive-of) &middot; [against](../../06-prepositions/catalog/09-comparison-opposition.md#against)

### provide with, provide for

**Pronunciation:** /prəˈvaɪd wɪθ/ &middot; *pruh-VIDE with* &middot; weak /prəˈvaɪd wəθ/
**Русский:** обеспечивать — provide smb with smth = provide smth for smb; provide for также содержать
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal to neutral
**Complement:** *with* takes the thing supplied and the recipient is the direct object: *provide students with laptops*. *for* takes the beneficiary, or the contingency: *provide for a family*, *the contract provides for arbitration*. A **gerund** works after *for* (*provides for extending the deadline*); ✗ a to-infinitive does not.
**Pattern:** *provide* [someone] *with* [something] &middot; *provide* [something] *for* [someone] &middot; *provide for* [a family] &middot; [a document] *provide for* [something] &middot; *provide that* [clause] — legal &middot; *provide* [something]
**Contrast:** **Two frames say the same thing with the arguments swapped:** *provide the crew **with** meals* = *provide meals **for** the crew*. Mixing them (✗ *provide the crew meals* in formal writing, ✗ *provide with meals to the crew*) is the error. Then *provide for* on its own means support financially (*he provided for four children on a machinist's wage*), and in a contract it means stipulate (*the lease provides for a five percent escalator*). The legal *provide that* takes a full clause and no preposition.
**Verb type:** **both** — dynamic in the supply and support senses (*the district is providing students with laptops*, *he provided for four children*); **stative** in the drafting sense, which rejects the progressive: ✗ *the lease is providing for a five percent escalator*.
**Transitivity:** **both** — **transitive** in *provide students with laptops* and *provide meals*; **intransitive** with the preposition in *provide for a family* and *the contract provides for arbitration*. Not ditransitive in careful writing: ✗ *provide the crew meals*.
**Passive:** **yes** — *students were provided with laptops*, *meals were provided for the crew*, and the drafting sense gives a clean prepositional passive: *arbitration is provided for in section 12*.

Supply something, support someone, or stipulate something.

1. The district *provides* every ninth grader *with* a laptop.
2. They *provided* coffee and donuts *for* the volunteers.
3. He *provided for* his mother for the last eleven years of her life.
4. The bylaws *provide for* removing a director by two-thirds vote.
5. The grant *provides* the lab *with* three years of funding.
6. Nothing in the policy *provides for* a refund.

**See also:** [cover with](#cover-with) &middot; [pay for](19-phrasal-m-r.md#pay-for) &middot; [prepare for](#prepare-for) &middot; [allow for](#allow-for) &middot; [with](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#with)

### punish for

**Pronunciation:** /ˈpʌnɪʃ fɔr/ &middot; *PUN-ish for* &middot; weak /ˈpʌnɪʃ fɚ/
**Русский:** наказывать за — налагать взыскание за содеянное
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*punished for lying*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *punished for to lie*) and never a clause. The person punished is a direct object: ✗ *punish to him*. The penalty itself takes *with* or *by*: *punishable by a fine*.
**Pattern:** *punish* [someone] *for* [something / verb-ing] &middot; *be punished for* [something] &middot; *punish* [someone] *with* [a penalty] &middot; *punishable by* [a sentence] &middot; *punishment for* [an offense]
**Contrast:** **The offense takes *for*; the penalty takes *by* or *with*.** *He was punished **for** the leak* names what he did; *punishable **by** five years* names what happens to him. ✗ *punish someone of* and ✗ *punish someone about* are errors. The noun keeps the same split: *punishment **for** the offense*, *a sentence **of** five years*. Compare *blame for* (assign fault, no penalty), *charge with* (formal accusation), and *fine someone for* (a specific monetary penalty, same shape as *punish*).
**Verb type:** action — dynamic; imposing a penalty, a single act.
**Transitivity:** **transitive** — the person punished is the direct object, the offense takes *for*, and the penalty takes *by* or *with*: ✗ *punish to him*, ✗ *punish someone of the leak*.
**Passive:** **yes**, and it is the commoner form — *he was punished for the leak*, *the offense is punishable by five years*.

Impose a penalty on someone for something they did.

1. Two students were *punished for* something a third one did.
2. He was *punished for* taking the complaint over his manager's head.
3. The rule *punishes* small filers *for* an honest mistake.
4. Nobody should be *punished for* asking a question.
5. Tampering is *punishable by* a fine of up to ten thousand dollars.
6. She *punished* him *for* the lie rather than the thing he lied about.

**See also:** [blame for](#blame-for-blame-on) &middot; [charge with](#charge-with-charge-for) &middot; [accuse of](#accuse-of) &middot; [forgive for](#forgive-for) &middot; [excuse for](#excuse-for)

## Q–R

### qualify for

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkwɑləˌfaɪ fɔr/ &middot; *KWOL-uh-fye for* &middot; weak /ˈkwɑləˌfaɪ fɚ/
**Русский:** иметь право на, подходить под условия — соответствовать требованиям
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** *for* takes a noun phrase — **no gerund** in ordinary use (✗ *qualify for getting aid*; say *qualify for aid*). To put a verb after *qualify*, use the infinitive with no preposition: *qualify to vote*, *qualified to practice in three states*. *As* takes a category: *qualifies as a hardship*.
**Pattern:** *qualify for* [a benefit, a program, a round] &middot; *qualify to* [verb] &middot; *qualify as* [a category] &middot; *be qualified for* [a job] &middot; *qualify* [a statement] — hedge it
**Contrast:** ***Qualify for* is eligibility; *qualify to* is capacity; *qualify as* is classification.** *She qualifies for the subsidy* (income), *she's qualified to teach chemistry* (credential), *that qualifies as an emergency* (definition). All three are live and none takes the others' preposition. A fourth, transitive sense means hedge or limit: *he qualified the claim in a footnote* — no preposition at all, and the opposite of endorsing it.
**Verb type:** **both** — **stative** in the eligibility sense, which resists the progressive: ✗ *she is qualifying for the subsidy*; dynamic in the athletic sense (*he qualified for the finals*) and in the transitive hedge sense (*he qualified the claim in a footnote*).
**Transitivity:** **both** — **intransitive** with *for* and *as* (*qualifies as a hardship*) and with a bare to-infinitive (*qualified to practice in three states*); **transitive** in the hedge sense: *qualify the statement*.
**Passive:** **no** in the stative eligibility sense — ✗ *the subsidy is qualified for by most applicants*. **Yes** in the hedge sense: *the claim was carefully qualified in a footnote*.

Meet the requirements for something.

1. A family of four *qualifies for* assistance under about sixty thousand.
2. Two of the four teams *qualified for* the regional round.
3. He isn't *qualified for* the position and everyone in the room knew it.
4. She's *qualified to* practice in Ohio and Michigan.
5. Does a burst pipe *qualify as* an emergency repair?
6. Very few of the applications actually *qualified for* review.

**See also:** [apply for](#apply-for-apply-to) &middot; [compete for](#compete-with-compete-against-compete-for) &middot; [prepare for](#prepare-for) &middot; [vote for](#vote-for-vote-against) &middot; [consist of](#consist-of)

### react to

**Pronunciation:** /riˈækt tu/ &middot; *ree-AKT too* &middot; weak /riˈækt tə/
**Русский:** реагировать на — вести себя определённым образом в ответ; после to герундий
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*reacted to being asked*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *react to be asked*) and never a clause. *React* is intransitive: ✗ *react the news*. In chemistry the preposition is *with*: *sodium reacts with water*.
**Pattern:** *react to* [something] &middot; *react to* [verb-ing] &middot; [a substance] *react with* [another] &middot; *react against* [a trend, an upbringing] &middot; *a reaction to* [something]
**Contrast:** ***React on* is an error** in the ordinary sense; the preposition is *to*. Three related shapes: *react **to*** responds to a stimulus, *react **with*** is the chemical sense, and *react **against*** means push back at something you were formed by (*a generation reacting against the earnestness of its parents*). Note the medical use — *react to* a drug, and the noun *an allergic reaction to*, both with *to* — and compare *respond to*, which is interchangeable but implies more deliberation.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic and involuntary, more immediate than considered *respond to*; freely progressive: *the market is reacting to the announcement*.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** — the stimulus always needs a preposition: ✗ *react the news*. *to* for a stimulus, *with* for chemistry, *against* for a generation pushing back.
**Passive:** **no** — intransitive, and the prepositional passive is not English here: ✗ *the announcement was reacted to badly*. Say *the announcement went over badly*.

Behave in a particular way because of something that happened.

1. Markets *reacted to* the announcement before anyone had read it.
2. She *reacted to* the news better than I expected.
3. About one patient in a thousand *reacts to* the dye.
4. He *reacted to* being corrected in front of the class exactly as you'd guess.
5. The whole neighborhood *reacted against* the rezoning.
6. Chlorine *reacts with* ammonia to produce something you do not want in your bathroom.

**See also:** [adapt to](#adapt-to) &middot; [object to](#object-to) &middot; [refer to](#refer-to) &middot; [occur to](#occur-to)

### reconcile with

**Pronunciation:** /ˈrɛkənˌsaɪl wɪθ/ &middot; *REK-un-syle with* &middot; weak /ˈrɛkənˌsaɪl wəθ/
**Русский:** мирить(ся) с; согласовывать с — а reconcile oneself to смиряться с неизбежным
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral — the *reconcile X with Y* frame is standard in accounting and in argument
**Complement:** *with* takes a noun phrase — a person, a set of figures, a claim — or a **gerund** (*reconcile that with claiming to be neutral*); **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *reconcile it with to claim neutrality*). The separate reflexive pattern *reconcile oneself **to*** also takes a noun phrase or a **gerund** (*reconciled herself to losing the house*) and never an infinitive (✗ *reconciled himself to lose it*).
**Pattern:** *reconcile* [X] *with* [Y] &middot; *be reconciled with* [person] &middot; *reconcile with* [person] &middot; *reconcile oneself to* [noun phrase / verb-ing] &middot; *be reconciled to* [noun phrase / verb-ing]
**Contrast:** ***With* joins two things that ought to match; *to* resigns you to one you cannot change.** *She reconciled with her father* is a repaired relationship; *she reconciled herself to her father's decision* is acceptance without repair, and the second cannot use *with*. In accounting both prepositions are live: *reconcile the statement with the ledger* is the general idiom, *reconcile the statement to the ledger* the bookkeeper's. Note that the intransitive *the couple reconciled* needs no preposition at all.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic in every sense: repairing a relationship, squaring two sets of figures, or coming to accept something (*she's slowly reconciling herself to it*).
**Transitivity:** **both** — **transitive** in *reconcile the statement with the ledger* and in the reflexive *reconcile oneself to*; **intransitive** in *the couple reconciled* and *she reconciled with her father*.
**Passive:** **yes** in the transitive frame — *the two accounts can't be reconciled with each other*, *the figures were reconciled to the ledger*. No passive of the intransitive: ✗ *her father was reconciled with*.

Make two things agree, make peace with someone, or accept what cannot be changed.

1. The two brothers *reconciled with* each other after eleven years.
2. I can't *reconcile* his account *with* what the timestamps show.
3. Accounting *reconciles* the daily receipts *with* the deposit slips every Monday.
4. She eventually *reconciled herself to* living on half the income.
5. How do you *reconcile* a hiring freeze *with* opening two new offices?
6. They were *reconciled* about a month before he died.

**See also:** [agree with](#agree-with-agree-to-agree-on) &middot; [comply with](#comply-with) &middot; [compare with](#compare-to-compare-with) &middot; [differ from](#differ-from) &middot; [with](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#with)

### recover from

**Pronunciation:** /rɪˈkʌvɚ frʌm/ &middot; *rih-KUV-er from* &middot; weak /rɪˈkʌvɚ frəm/
**Русский:** оправляться после (болезни, беды); а recover без предлога возвращать себе
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*recovering from having the flu*) — never a to-infinitive, never a clause. The **transitive** verb is a different pattern with no preposition: *recover the data*, *recover damages* — and there *from* names the source instead (*recover the money from the insurer*).
**Pattern:** *recover from* [an illness, a setback] &middot; *recover from* [verb-ing] &middot; *recover* [something lost] &middot; *recover* [something] *from* [a source] &middot; *make a recovery from* [something]
**Contrast:** **Intransitive *recover from* is about getting better; transitive *recover* is about getting something back.** *She recovered from the surgery* and *They recovered the stolen equipment* share no structure at all. Watch the ambiguity in *recover from*: *the firm recovered from the loss* means it bounced back, while *the firm recovered from the insurer* means it collected. Compare *get over*, the everyday spoken synonym of the first sense, and *heal from*, which is newer and common in American therapeutic usage.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic; a process in the intransitive sense (*she's still recovering from the surgery*) and a single act of retrieval in the transitive one.
**Transitivity:** **both** — **intransitive** with *from* naming the illness or setback (*recover from the loss*); **transitive** with no preposition in the get-something-back sense (*recover the data*, *recover damages*), where *from* names the source instead.
**Passive:** **yes** in the transitive sense — *the stolen equipment was recovered*, *the fees were recovered from the insurer*. **No** in the intransitive: ✗ *the surgery was recovered from*.

Get better after illness or difficulty, or get something back.

1. He's still *recovering from* the knee replacement.
2. The town never really *recovered from* the plant closing.
3. It took her a semester to *recover from* a bad first year.
4. Divers *recovered* the black box *from* about ninety feet of water.
5. Nobody *recovers from* that kind of press in one news cycle.
6. *Recovering from* being publicly wrong is a skill worth having.

**See also:** [suffer from](#suffer-from) &middot; [benefit from](#benefit-from) &middot; [escape from](#escape-from) &middot; [die of](#die-of-die-from) &middot; [cope with](#cope-with)

### recuse from

**Pronunciation:** /rɪˈkjuz frʌm/ &middot; *rih-KYOOZ frum* &middot; weak /rɪˈkjuz frəm/
**Русский:** брать самоотвод в — устраняться из-за конфликта интересов; юр.
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** legal — an American meets this word in a judge's order, an ethics memo, or a news story about a justice stepping aside; in conversation you would say *step aside* or *sit this one out*
**Complement:** the verb normally takes a **reflexive object** first — *recuse himself from* — and *from* then takes a noun phrase or a **gerund** (*recused herself from hearing the appeal*); **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *recused himself from to hear it*). American journalism now also drops the reflexive (*the judge recused from the case*) and even the object (*the justice recused*), but a brief will keep *himself*.
**Pattern:** *recuse* [oneself] *from* [a case, a vote, a decision] &middot; *recuse* [oneself] *from* [verb-ing] &middot; *recuse from* [noun phrase] &middot; *be recused from* [a panel]
**Contrast:** **Four verbs, four distances.** *Recuse from* steps out of one matter because of a conflict and keeps the job; *resign from* leaves the job; *abstain from* keeps the seat and declines to vote; *withdraw from* says nothing about why. A judge who *recuses herself from the case* is still on the bench the next morning. The outsider's version is *disqualify*: a party *moves to disqualify* the judge, the judge *recuses herself*. ✗ *Recuse of* and ✗ *recuse out of* are errors.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic and performative: the judge's stepping aside is done by saying so.
**Transitivity:** **transitive with a reflexive object** in careful writing — *recuse himself from the case*; American journalism now also runs it **intransitively** (*the judge recused from the case*, *the justice recused*), but a brief keeps *himself*.
**Passive:** **no** in edited legal prose — a reflexive object gives nothing to promote: ✗ *he was recused from the case*, which the press nonetheless prints. Use *was disqualified* when you need a passive.

Step out of a case or a decision because you have a conflict of interest.

1. The judge *recused herself from* the case because her nephew worked for the defendant.
2. Two board members *recused themselves from* voting on the contract.
3. He should have *recused himself from* the review of his own department.
4. The attorney general *recused from* the investigation last spring.
5. She *recuses herself from* anything involving her former firm.
6. If the conflict is disclosed early, a member can *recuse* and the meeting goes on.

**See also:** [refrain from](#refrain-from) &middot; [retire from](#retire-from) &middot; [separate from](#separate-from) &middot; [testify to](#testify-to) &middot; [from](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#from)

### refer to

**Pronunciation:** /rɪˈfɜr tu/ &middot; *rih-FUR too* &middot; weak /rɪˈfɜr tə/
**Русский:** ссылаться на; обращаться к (источнику); направлять к (специалисту); обозначать
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*referred to being underpaid*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *refer to be underpaid*). In the send-someone-onward frame the person is a direct object: *refer the patient to a specialist*. Note the doubled *r* in *referred*, *referring*, *referral*.
**Pattern:** *refer to* [something — mention] &middot; *refer to* [a source — consult] &middot; *refer* [someone] *to* [someone else] &middot; [a word] *refer to* [a thing — denote] &middot; *refer to* [something] *as* [a name]
**Contrast:** **Four senses, one preposition, and they are told apart by what surrounds it.** *He referred to the memo* (mentioned it), *refer to the appendix* (consult it), *her doctor referred her to a cardiologist* (sent her onward), *"quorum" refers to the minimum number* (denotes it). The compound *referred to as* is the standard formula for naming: *the area referred to as the Loop*. ✗ *refer about* is not English. Compare *mention*, which is transitive and takes no preposition — ✗ *mention about it* is a common error.
**Verb type:** **both** — dynamic in the mention, consult, and send-onward senses (*she's referring to the appendix*, *her doctor referred her to a cardiologist*); **stative** in the denote sense, which rejects the progressive: ✗ *'quorum' is referring to the minimum number*.
**Transitivity:** **both** — **intransitive** with *to* in the mention, consult, and denote senses; **transitive** in the send-onward frame: *refer the patient to a specialist*.
**Passive:** **yes** — *the case was referred to the appeals panel*, and the prepositional passive is a fixed naming formula: *the area referred to as the Loop*. The denote sense stays awkward with a *by*-phrase: ✗ *the minimum number is referred to by 'quorum'*.

Mention something, consult a source, or send someone onward.

1. She never *referred to* the incident again.
2. *Refer to* the chart on page nine for the full schedule.
3. The clinic *referred* him *to* a specialist in Cleveland.
4. The term *refers to* any structure built before the 1968 code.
5. Everyone at the plant *refers to* it *as* the annex, though it isn't attached to anything.
6. The footnote *refers to* a study that has since been retracted.

**See also:** [point to](#point-at-point-to) &middot; [comment on](#comment-on) &middot; [consult with](#consult-with) &middot; [introduce to](#introduce-to) &middot; [subscribe to](#subscribe-to)

### refrain from

**Pronunciation:** /rɪˈfreɪn frʌm/ &middot; *rih-FRAYN frum* &middot; weak /rɪˈfreɪn frəm/
**Русский:** воздерживаться от — сознательно не делать; после from герундий
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal — the language of signs, announcements, and policies (*please refrain from smoking*); in conversation Americans say *don't* or *lay off*
**Complement:** **gerund**, or a noun phrase naming an activity or substance (*refrain from comment*, *refrain from alcohol*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *refrain from to smoke*, ✗ *refrain to comment*) and never a that-clause. The one who refrains is always the subject: you cannot ✗ *refrain him from talking* — that verb is *prevent from* or *keep from*.
**Pattern:** *refrain from* [verb-ing] &middot; *refrain from* [noun phrase] &middot; *please refrain from* [verb-ing]
**Contrast:** **Four ways of not doing something.** *Refrain from* is a deliberate, usually temporary holding back; *abstain from* is a standing policy — from alcohol, from meat, from a vote; *desist from* is what a cease-and-desist letter demands; *stop* takes a bare gerund and no preposition (*stop talking*, ✗ *stop from talking*). And unlike *prevent from* and *discourage from*, *refrain* never takes an object of its own. ✗ *Refrain of* is an error.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic; a deliberate, usually temporary holding back, more occasional than policy-like *abstain from*.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** — the one who refrains is always the subject and the verb never takes an object of its own: ✗ *refrain him from talking* — that verb is *prevent from* or *keep from*.
**Passive:** **no** — intransitive, and the prepositional passive is not used: ✗ *comment was refrained from by the office*. Say *the office declined to comment*.

Deliberately hold back from doing something.

1. Please *refrain from* using flash photography in the gallery.
2. The judge asked both attorneys to *refrain from* interrupting each other.
3. He *refrained from* saying what everyone in the room was thinking.
4. Employees should *refrain from* discussing the settlement publicly.
5. She *refrained from* alcohol for the whole year of her residency.
6. I *refrained from* pointing out that the same thing happened last spring.

**See also:** [prevent from](#prevent-from) &middot; [discourage from](#discourage-from) &middot; [recuse from](#recuse-from) &middot; [object to](#object-to) &middot; [from](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#from)

### relate to

**Pronunciation:** /rɪˈleɪt tu/ &middot; *rih-LAYT too* &middot; weak /rɪˈleɪt tə/
**Русский:** касаться чего-то; понимать, сочувствовать кому-то; излагать что-то кому-то
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral; the empathy sense (*I can relate to that*) is informal and thoroughly American
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*I can relate to being the new person*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *relate to be the new person*). A wh-clause is fine (*everything relating to what happened that night*). In the transitive *relate X to Y* frame both slots are noun phrases.
**Pattern:** [something] *relate to* [something] &middot; *relate to* [someone / an experience] &middot; *relate to* [verb-ing] &middot; *relate* [X] *to* [Y] &middot; *be related to* [someone] &middot; *relating to* [topic]
**Contrast:** **Three verbs wear one spelling.** *This relates to the second complaint* means it concerns it; *I can relate to her* means I identify with her — informal, and the object may be a person, a feeling, or a whole situation; *he related the story to us* means he told it. Family is a fourth thing again and needs the passive participle: *she's related to the governor*, never ✗ *she relates to the governor*. The participle *relating to* is a stock connector in contracts and subpoenas, interchangeable with *concerning* and *in relation to*.
**Verb type:** **both** — **stative** in the concern sense (*this relates to the second complaint*) and the identify-with sense (*I can relate to her*), both odd in the progressive; dynamic in the narrate sense: *he related the story to us*.
**Transitivity:** **both** — **intransitive** with *to* in the concern and identify senses; **transitive** in *relate the story to us* and in *relate X to Y*. Kinship needs the participle: *she's related to the governor*, ✗ *she relates to the governor*.
**Passive:** **restricted** — the narrate sense passivizes (*the story was related to us by her brother*) and the kinship participle is fixed (*she's related to the governor*), but the stative concern sense does not: ✗ *the complaint is related to by this clause*.

Concern something, identify with someone, or connect one thing to another.

1. Nothing in the memo *relates to* the layoffs.
2. I can *relate to* wanting to quit halfway through a shift.
3. He has trouble *relating to* people he didn't grow up with.
4. The professor *related* the data *to* an earlier study out of Michigan.
5. All documents *relating to* the merger have to be preserved.
6. She's *related to* half the families in the county.

**See also:** [refer to](#refer-to) &middot; [respond to](#respond-to) &middot; [belong to](#belong-to) &middot; [in relation to](../../06-prepositions/catalog/11-complex-multiword.md#in-relation-to) &middot; [to](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#to)

### relieve of

**Pronunciation:** /rɪˈliv əv/ &middot; *rih-LEEV uv* &middot; weak /rɪˈliv ə/
**Русский:** освобождать от (обязанности, ноши) — снимать бремя с человека
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal, and usually a euphemism — the wording of a personnel notice or a military order (*relieved of command*); its joking use (*relieved me of forty dollars*) is a joke about the euphemism
**Complement:** the person always sits between the verb and *of* — *relieve someone of something* — and *of* takes a noun phrase or a **gerund** (*relieved of having to file the report*); **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *relieved of to file the report*). There is no ✗ *relieve of* with no person named, and the passive (*was relieved of his duties*) is far commoner than the active.
**Pattern:** *relieve* [someone] *of* [duties / a burden / a possession] &middot; [someone] *be relieved of* [noun phrase / verb-ing]
**Contrast:** **Do not confuse the verb with the feeling.** *He was relieved of his post* means he was removed from it; *he was relieved at the news*, *relieved by the verdict*, *relieved to hear it* means he felt relief — a different word, an adjective, with its own prepositions. Only the first is this pattern. Among neighbors, *relieve someone of* takes a burden away, *free someone from* removes a constraint, *absolve someone of* clears the blame, and *fire* says plainly what *relieved of his duties* is written to avoid.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic; taking a burden or a post away from somebody. Do not confuse it with the adjective of feeling in *relieved at the news*.
**Transitivity:** **transitive** — the person always sits between verb and preposition: *relieve someone of something*; the object is obligatory: ✗ *relieve of the reporting requirement*.
**Passive:** **yes**, and the passive is far commoner than the active — *he was relieved of his duties*, *she was relieved of having to file the report*.

Take a duty, a burden, or a possession away from someone.

1. The colonel was *relieved of* command within a week.
2. A settlement would *relieve* the company *of* any further obligation.
3. She was *relieved of* the paperwork so she could spend the hour with patients.
4. The new system *relieves* the front desk *of* having to re-key every order.
5. Two administrators were quietly *relieved of* their responsibilities in June.
6. Somebody in the crowd *relieved* him *of* his wallet.

**See also:** [deprive of](#deprive-of) &middot; [rob of](#rob-of) &middot; [rescue from](#rescue-from) &middot; [retire from](#retire-from) &middot; [of](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#of)

### rely on

**Pronunciation:** /rɪˈlaɪ ɑn/ &middot; *rih-LIE on* &middot; weak /rɪˈlaɪ ən/
**Русский:** полагаться на — доверять, что сделает нужное
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal
**Complement:** noun phrase, pronoun, or **gerund** (*relying on getting reimbursed*) — **never a to-infinitive after the preposition** (✗ *rely on to get reimbursed*). A to-infinitive is correct after a **person**: *rely on her to check it*. A wh-clause is fine. *Rely* never appears without its preposition: ✗ *you can rely me*.
**Pattern:** *rely on* [someone / something] &middot; *rely on* [someone] *to* [verb] &middot; *rely on* [verb-ing] &middot; *rely on* [someone's] *-ing* &middot; *reliance on* [something]
**Variants:** *rely upon* — identical in meaning, more formal, common in legal and academic writing
**Contrast:** ***Rely to*, ✗ *rely in*, and ✗ *rely of* are all errors.** Within the family, *rely on* is the neutral written form, *count on* the warm spoken one, and *depend on* the one that also carries the be-determined-by sense that neither of the others has. *Rely upon* is the formal variant, standard in legal and academic prose. Note the noun: *reliance on* — and the legal phrase *detrimental reliance*, which is this same preposition frozen.
**Verb type:** **stative** — a standing state of dependence rather than an act, though it takes the progressive for a temporary arrangement: *we're relying on you this week*.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** — the verb never appears without its preposition: ✗ *you can rely me*. A to-infinitive is possible after a person: *rely on her to check it*.
**Passive:** **restricted** — the prepositional passive is standard in legal and academic prose (*the affidavit was relied on by the court*, *the cases relied upon*), stiff in speech: ✗ *I'm relied on by everybody*.

Trust something or someone to do what is needed.

1. The whole valley *relies on* one two-lane bridge.
2. You can *rely on* her to catch anything I miss.
3. They *relied on* volunteer labor for the first three seasons.
4. Don't *rely on* the automatic backup; check it once a month.
5. The finding *relies on* a sample of forty-one people.
6. Rural clinics *rely upon* federal reimbursement for most of their revenue.

**See also:** [depend on](#depend-on) &middot; [count on](#count-on) &middot; [believe in](#believe-in) &middot; [on](../../06-prepositions/catalog/01-core-in-on-at.md#on)

### remind of, remind about

**Pronunciation:** /rɪˈmaɪnd əv/ &middot; *rih-MIND uv* &middot; weak /rɪˈmaɪnd ə/
**Русский:** напоминать — of о сходстве или о факте, about о деле, о котором забыли
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** *of* takes a noun phrase or a **gerund** and covers both resemblance and recollection. *about* takes a noun phrase or a gerund and covers upcoming obligations. The person reminded is always a direct object: ✗ *remind to me*. *Remind* also takes a **to-infinitive** after the person (*remind me to call*) and a that-clause (*remind him that it's Tuesday*) — neither with a preposition.
**Pattern:** *remind* [someone] *of* [something / someone] &middot; *remind* [someone] *about* [an obligation] &middot; *remind* [someone] *to* [verb] &middot; *remind* [someone] *that* [clause] &middot; *That reminds me.*
**Contrast:** **Four patterns, and *of* versus *about* is the one that carries meaning.** *You remind me **of** my father* is resemblance — it says you are like him. *Remind me **about** the deposit* is a prompt about a task. And *remind me **to** make the deposit* is a prompt to act, using the infinitive rather than any preposition. Mixing them produces sentences that are grammatical and wrong: ✗ *remind me of making the deposit* says the act of depositing calls something else to mind.
**Verb type:** **both** — dynamic in the prompting sense (*remind me about the deposit*, *remind me to call*); **stative** in the resemblance sense, where nothing happens: *you remind me of my father*.
**Transitivity:** **transitive** — the person reminded is always a direct object: ✗ *remind to me*. The verb also takes a to-infinitive after the person (*remind me to call*) and a that-clause (*remind him that it's Tuesday*), neither with a preposition.
**Passive:** **yes** in the prompting sense — *staff were reminded of the deadline*, *she was reminded to sign both copies*. The stative resemblance sense resists: ✗ *my father is reminded of by you*.

Cause someone to remember something, or make you think of something else.

1. This street *reminds* me *of* the town my mother grew up in.
2. *Remind* me *about* the parking pass before we leave.
3. She *reminded* him *to* bring the second set of keys.
4. Your handwriting *reminds* me *of* my grandmother's.
5. *Remind* the committee *that* the deadline is a statutory one.
6. Nothing *reminds* you *of* how old a house is like a January heating bill.

**See also:** [forget about](16-phrasal-d-f.md#forget-about) &middot; [occur to](#occur-to) &middot; [think of](#think-about-think-of) &middot; [warn about](#warn-about-warn-against) &middot; [explain to](#explain-to)

### rescue from

**Pronunciation:** /ˈrɛskju frʌm/ &middot; *RESS-kyoo from* &middot; weak /ˈrɛskju frəm/
**Русский:** спасать из, спасать от — вытаскивать из опасности или беды
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*rescued from having to speak*) — never a to-infinitive, never a clause. The person or thing rescued is a direct object and the danger takes *from*: ✗ *rescue to someone*.
**Pattern:** *rescue* [someone] *from* [danger] &middot; *be rescued from* [something] &middot; *rescue* [someone] &middot; *a rescue from* [something] &middot; *rescue* [an animal] — as a noun, an adopted animal
**Contrast:** ***Rescue* takes only *from*.** Its neighbors keep their own: *save someone **from*** (interchangeable, broader), *free someone **from*** or ***of***, *deliver someone **from*** (biblical or formal), *bail someone **out of*** trouble (informal, and note the particle). In American usage *rescue* has also become a noun for an adopted shelter animal — *she's a rescue* — with no preposition at all.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic; a single act of pulling someone out of danger.
**Transitivity:** **transitive** — the person or thing rescued is the direct object and *from* names the danger: ✗ *rescue to someone*, ✗ *rescue from the fire* with nobody named.
**Passive:** **yes**, and it is the usual news form — *the crew was rescued from the water by a Coast Guard helicopter*, *she was rescued from having to speak*.

Get someone out of danger or difficulty.

1. The crew *rescued* four people *from* the roof of a submerged car.
2. A neighbor *rescued* the dog *from* a storm drain.
3. She *rescued* the whole presentation *from* a dead projector and a lost cable.
4. Two hikers were *rescued from* the ridge after dark.
5. He was *rescued from* having to give a toast by a well-timed power outage.
6. The fund *rescued* the theater *from* closing.

**See also:** [save from](#save-from) &middot; [protect from](#protect-from-protect-against) &middot; [escape from](#escape-from) &middot; [recover from](#recover-from)

### reside in

**Pronunciation:** /rɪˈzaɪd ɪn/ &middot; *rih-ZYDE in* &middot; weak /rɪˈzaɪd ən/
**Русский:** проживать где-то (юр.); быть заключённым в — о свойстве или власти
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal — a lease, a tax form, a voter-registration rule, a court opinion, or a civics sentence about where power lies; in speech Americans say *live*
**Complement:** noun phrase naming a place, a body, or an abstraction (*the difficulty resides in the wording*). A **gerund** is possible in the abstract sense but stilted (*its value resides in being reproducible*) — prefer a noun. **Never a to-infinitive** (✗ *resides in to be reproducible*) and never a that-clause.
**Pattern:** *reside in* [a city, a state, a district] &middot; *reside at* [a street address] &middot; *reside with* [a person, or a body that holds authority] &middot; [a quality / a problem] *reside in* [something]
**Contrast:** **The three prepositions divide by scale and by kind.** You *reside in* a county, *reside at* 400 East Sixth Street, and *reside with* your grandmother — or, for authority, *with* the body that holds it (*final approval resides with the board*), which is the same frame as *rest with*. Against plain *live*: *reside* is what the form asks (*Where do you currently reside?*) and *live* is what people answer. The abstract sense — *the problem resides in the design* — is the formal twin of *lie in*, and is standard in academic prose.
**Verb type:** **stative** — where a person lives or where a power belongs by design; it rejects the progressive: ✗ *sovereignty is residing in the people*, ✗ *the difficulty is residing in the wording*.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** — the place, body, or abstraction always comes through *in* (or *at* for a street address, *with* for authority): ✗ *she resides the county*.
**Passive:** **no** — intransitive and stative, so there is nothing to promote: ✗ *the county is resided in by 40,000 people*, ✗ *the wording is resided in*.

Have your legal home somewhere — or, of a quality or a power, be located in something.

1. Both parties *resided in* Cook County at the time of filing.
2. Sovereignty *resides in* the people, not in the office.
3. She *resides at* 400 East Sixth Street.
4. Custody *resides with* the mother until the hearing.
5. The real difficulty *resides in* the definition of "employee."
6. Anyone *residing in* the district for thirty days may register to vote.

**See also:** [rest with](#rest-with) &middot; [belong to](#belong-to) &middot; [vest in](#vest-in) &middot; [consist of](#consist-of) &middot; [in](../../06-prepositions/catalog/01-core-in-on-at.md#in)

### resort to

**Pronunciation:** /rɪˈzɔrt tu/ &middot; *rih-ZORT too* &middot; weak /rɪˈzɔrt tə/
**Русский:** прибегать к — обращаться к нежелательному средству; после to герундий
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** — ***to* is a preposition here**, so a verb after it must be *-ing*: *resorted to lying*, never ✗ *resorted to lie*. Never a clause. *Resort* has no other verb frame at all: ✗ *he resorted the lie*.
**Pattern:** *resort to* [something] &middot; *resort to* [verb-ing] &middot; *as a last resort* &middot; *without resorting to* [verb-ing]
**Contrast:** **Another member of the *look forward to* family, and the same test settles it:** you can say *resort to violence*, so *to* is a preposition, so it is *resort to shouting*, not ✗ *resort to shout*. The word carries a built-in judgment — whatever you resort to is a fallback you would rather not have used, which is why *resort to force* and *resort to bribery* sound natural and ✗ *resorted to a sensible compromise* sounds odd. Compare the noun *last resort*, and the unrelated homonym *resort* meaning a vacation property.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic; falling back on something you would rather not have used, which is why *resort to force* works and ✗ *resorted to a sensible compromise* sounds odd.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** — the verb has no other frame at all: ✗ *he resorted the lie*. *to* is a **preposition**, so a verb after it is *-ing*: *resorted to lying*, ✗ *resorted to lie*.
**Passive:** **restricted** — available in formal and legal prose (*force was resorted to only after negotiations failed*), unnatural in speech: ✗ *lying was resorted to by him*.

Turn to something undesirable because nothing better is available.

1. They *resorted to* hauling water by hand for most of August.
2. Nobody wants to *resort to* layoffs, which is what everyone says before layoffs.
3. He *resorted to* checking the spreadsheet line by line.
4. The department *resorted to* a hiring freeze rather than cut programs.
5. You can usually settle it without *resorting to* lawyers.
6. *As a last resort*, we can push the launch to October.

**See also:** [look forward to](#look-forward-to) &middot; [object to](#object-to) &middot; [get around to](17-phrasal-g.md#get-around-to) &middot; [refer to](#refer-to) &middot; [cope with](#cope-with) &middot; [to](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#to)

### respond to

**Pronunciation:** /rɪˈspɑnd tu/ &middot; *rih-SPOND too* &middot; weak /rɪˈspɑnd tə/
**Русский:** отвечать на, реагировать на — на вопрос, письмо или ситуацию
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral, a step more formal than *answer*
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*responded to being passed over by quitting*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *respond to be asked*). A clause attaches to bare *respond* with no preposition at all: *he responded that the file was closed*. The medical and technical sense takes a plain noun: *the tumor responded to radiation*.
**Pattern:** *respond to* [a question, a letter, a crisis] &middot; *respond to* [verb-ing] &middot; *respond with* [an action, a counteroffer] &middot; *respond by* [verb-ing] &middot; *respond that* [clause]
**Contrast:** ***Answer* takes no preposition and *respond* requires one** — *answer the question* but *respond **to** the question*; ✗ *answer to the question* is an error, and *answer to* with a person means something else entirely (be accountable to). *Reply to* is the near-twin but is limited to messages, while *respond to* also covers situations, treatments, and stimuli (*responded to the medication*, *responded to the alarm*). *React to* is involuntary and immediate; *respond to* implies a considered move.
**Verb type:** action — dynamic, and more considered than involuntary *react to*: *she's responding to every comment herself*. The medical sense is dynamic too: *the tumor responded to radiation*.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** — the verb requires its preposition, unlike transitive *answer*: *answer the question* but *respond **to** the question*. A clause attaches to bare *respond*: *he responded that the file was closed*.
**Passive:** **restricted** — *the letter was never responded to* occurs and is clumsy; the natural passive belongs to the transitive twin, *the letter was never answered*.

Say or do something in answer to a question, a message, or a situation.

1. Nobody *responded to* my email for four days.
2. The department *responded to* the complaint with a two-page denial.
3. Her blood pressure *responded to* the lower dose almost immediately.
4. Three engines *responded to* the alarm at two in the morning.
5. He *responded to* being left off the invitation by showing up anyway.
6. The company has yet to *respond to* questions about the recall.

**See also:** [react to](#react-to) &middot; [relate to](#relate-to) &middot; [refer to](#refer-to) &middot; [answer](#answer) &middot; [response to](../../06-prepositions/catalog/16-dependent-nouns.md#response-to) &middot; [to](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#to)

### rest on

**Pronunciation:** /ˈrɛst ɑn/ &middot; *REST on* &middot; weak /ˈrɛst ən/
**Русский:** опираться на — держаться на чём-то физически или логически
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral; *rest upon* is formal and literary
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*the whole case rests on proving intent*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *rests on to prove intent*). A wh-clause also works: *it rests on whether the email was sent*.
**Pattern:** [an argument, a claim, an outcome] *rest on* [an assumption] &middot; [a physical thing] *rest on* [a support] &middot; [someone's eyes] *rest on* [something] &middot; *rest* [something] *on* [something]
**Variants:** *rest upon* — identical in meaning, more formal and more literary; standard in appellate opinions and in descriptions of a gaze
**Contrast:** ***Rest on* names what holds something up; *rest with* names who is responsible.** The two never swap: *the decision rests with the dean* assigns the choice to a person, while *the decision rests on the dean* would make the dean the supporting evidence. Against *depend on*: *rest on* is static support — the argument is built on it — whereas *depend on* is contingency, varying with what the other thing does. Bare *rest* in a courtroom (*the state rests*) is a fixed idiom with no complement, and *rest on your laurels* is frozen.
**Verb type:** **stative** — static support, not an event; it rejects the progressive: ✗ *the whole case is resting on proving intent*. The subject is the thing held up, never an agent.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** — the support always comes through the preposition: ✗ *the case rests proving intent*. Bare *rest* in court (*the state rests*) is a fixed idiom with no complement.
**Passive:** **no** — intransitive and stative: ✗ *proving intent is rested on by the whole case*. Compare *rest with*, equally passive-proof: ✗ *the dean is rested with*.

Be supported by something, physically or logically.

1. The entire argument *rests on* a single unnamed source.
2. The porch roof *rests on* four cedar posts.
3. Their case *rests on* proving he knew about the transfer.
4. Much *rests upon* the wording of the second clause.
5. Her gaze *rested on* the photograph a moment too long.
6. He *rested* his elbows *on* the counter and waited for an answer.

**See also:** [rest with](#rest-with) &middot; [depend on](#depend-on) &middot; [rely on](#rely-on) &middot; [stem from](#stem-from) &middot; [turn on](#turn-on) &middot; [on](../../06-prepositions/catalog/01-core-in-on-at.md#on) &middot; [upon](../../06-prepositions/catalog/01-core-in-on-at.md#upon)

### rest with

**Pronunciation:** /ˈrɛst wɪθ/ &middot; *REST with* &middot; weak /ˈrɛst wəθ/
**Русский:** быть на чьей-то ответственности — решение остаётся за кем-то
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal — bylaws, contracts, editorials, and committee minutes; in speech Americans say *is up to*
**Complement:** noun phrase naming a person or a body that holds the authority. **No gerund** (✗ *the decision rests with deciding*), no infinitive, no clause. The subject is the thing being decided — a decision, the final say, responsibility, blame.
**Pattern:** [a decision / responsibility / blame] *rest with* [a person or body] &middot; *the choice rests with* [someone] &middot; *ultimately rest with* [someone]
**Contrast:** ***With* assigns responsibility, *on* assigns support** — *the decision rests with the board* (they decide) against *the decision rests on the audit* (the audit is what it is built on). *Lie with* is the near-synonym and a shade more literary (*the fault lies with the vendor*); *be up to* is the everyday version (*it's up to you*); *reside with* is the same frame in stiffer clothes. ✗ *Rest to* and ✗ *rest in* are not this pattern.
**Verb type:** **stative** — a standing relation of authority, not an act; it resists the progressive (✗ *the decision is resting with the board*) and has no imperative (✗ *rest with the dean!*).
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** — the subject is the decision and the *with*-phrase is obligatory: ✗ *final approval rests* is incomplete. There is no direct object: ✗ *rest the board the decision*.
**Passive:** **no** — intransitive and stative both block it: ✗ *the board is rested with the decision*, ✗ *she is rested with the choice*. Use the active, or switch to *be vested in* / *be up to*.

Be the responsibility or the decision of a particular person or body.

1. Final approval *rests with* the state board, not the county.
2. The decision *rests with* her, and always did.
3. Responsibility for the delay *rests with* the contractor.
4. Where the burden of proof *rests with* the plaintiff, an ambiguity favors the defense.
5. Whatever the doctors recommend, the choice *rests with* the family.
6. Authority to waive the fee *rests with* the dean's office.

**See also:** [rest on](#rest-on) &middot; [reside in](#reside-in) &middot; [vest in](#vest-in) &middot; [belong to](#belong-to) &middot; [with](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#with)

### result in, result from

**Pronunciation:** /rɪˈzʌlt ɪn/ &middot; *rih-ZULT in* &middot; weak /rɪˈzʌlt ən/
**Русский:** in приводить к следствию; from быть следствием причины — направление противоположно
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** — *resulted in closing the plant*, *results from having no backup* — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *resulted in to close the plant*). Never a clause. *Result* is intransitive and takes no object: ✗ *result a delay*.
**Pattern:** [a cause] *result in* [an effect] &middot; [an effect] *result from* [a cause] &middot; *result in* [verb-ing] &middot; *result from* [verb-ing] &middot; *as a result of* [something]
**Contrast:** **The two prepositions point in opposite directions, and swapping them inverts the sentence.** *The storm resulted in three outages* — cause first. *Three outages resulted from the storm* — effect first. Both are true; ✗ *the storm resulted from three outages* is false. *Result in* is the formal twin of *lead to* and *cause*; *result from* is the formal twin of *be caused by* and *stem from*. ✗ *result to* is not English in either direction.
**Verb type:** **stative-relational** rather than dynamic — it asserts a causal link, so it reads as a generic truth (*higher rates result in fewer refinancings*) and takes the progressive only grudgingly (?*the cuts are resulting in delays*).
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** in both directions; the preposition is obligatory and there is no object: ✗ *result a delay*, ✗ *the storm resulted three outages*.
**Passive:** **no** — nothing to promote: ✗ *four policy changes were resulted in by the audit*, ✗ *the storm was resulted from*. The inverse is built by swapping prepositions, not by the passive: *the delays resulted from a misfiled permit*.

Produce an outcome, or be produced by a cause.

1. The audit *resulted in* four policy changes and one resignation.
2. Most of the delays *resulted from* a single misfiled permit.
3. Cutting the route *resulted in* stranding about two hundred riders.
4. The infection *resulted from* a lapse in sterilization procedure.
5. Higher rates usually *result in* fewer refinancings.
6. Nothing good has ever *resulted from* sending that email at midnight.
