**See also:** [lead to](18-phrasal-h-l.md#lead-to) &middot; [stem from](#stem-from) &middot; [contribute to](#contribute-to) &middot; [die from](#die-of-die-from) &middot; [as a result of](../../06-prepositions/catalog/06-cause-purpose.md#as-a-result-of)

### retire from

**Pronunciation:** /rɪˈtaɪr frʌm/ &middot; *rih-TIRE from* &middot; weak /rɪˈtaɪr frəm/
**Русский:** уходить на пенсию, оставлять (пост, службу, спорт)
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase naming a job, a field, or an organization — **no gerund, no clause** (✗ *retire from working*; say *retire from teaching*, where *teaching* is a noun, or just *retire*). *To* names where you go; *as* names the title you held.
**Pattern:** *retire from* [a job, a field, an institution] &middot; *retire to* [a place] &middot; *retire as* [a title] &middot; *retire* (alone) &middot; *be retired* &middot; *retire* [a number, a debt, a piece of equipment]
**Contrast:** ***Retire of* is an error.** The three live prepositions divide the sentence: *she retired **from** the district **as** an assistant principal and moved **to** Tucson*. Note the transitive senses, which take no preposition: a team *retires a number*, a company *retires debt*, an airline *retires* a fleet. And note the older, unrelated sense of *retire* meaning withdraw for the evening (*he retired at ten*), which survives mostly in period fiction.
**Verb type:** **action** — dynamic, a one-time change of status (*he retired from the fire department in March*); *be retired* is the resulting state, an adjective rather than a tense.
**Transitivity:** **both** — intransitive in the career senses (*she retired*, *retired from the board*, *retired to Tucson*), transitive in the sports and finance senses (*retire his number*, *retire the debt*, *retire a fleet*).
**Passive:** **restricted** — natural in the transitive senses: *his number was retired the following spring*, *the bonds were retired early*. No passive of the career sense: ✗ *the fire department was retired from*.

Stop working permanently — and the preposition names what you leave.

1. He *retired from* the fire department after twenty-eight years.
2. She *retired from* competitive swimming at twenty-three.
3. Two of the three founders have *retired from* the board.
4. They *retired to* a town with one traffic light and no winter.
5. He *retired as* chief of staff and stayed on as a consultant.
6. The Yankees *retired* his number the following spring.

**See also:** [escape from](#escape-from) &middot; [recover from](#recover-from) &middot; [separate from](#separate-from) &middot; [from](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#from)

### rob of

**Pronunciation:** /ˈrɑb əv/ &middot; *ROB uv* &middot; weak /ˈrɑb ə/
**Русский:** лишать кого-то чего-то — отнимать силой или несправедливо
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*robbed of seeing her graduate*) — never a to-infinitive, never a clause. The victim or the place is the direct object and the loss takes *of*: *robbed him of his savings*, ✗ *robbed his savings from him* is clumsy at best.
**Pattern:** *rob* [someone / a place] &middot; *rob* [someone] *of* [something] &middot; *be robbed of* [something] &middot; *rob* [a bank, a store] &middot; *steal* [something] *from* [someone] — the mirror verb
**Contrast:** ***Rob* takes the victim; *steal* takes the loot.** *They robbed the bank* and *they stole the money* are both correct, and ✗ *they robbed the money* and ✗ *they stole the bank* are both wrong. When you want to name both, *rob* uses *of* (*robbed the bank of four thousand dollars*) and *steal* uses *from* (*stole four thousand dollars from the bank*). The figurative sense is common and always takes *of*: *an injury robbed him of a season*, *robbed of the chance to say goodbye*.
**Verb type:** **action** — dynamic and telic, an event with a victim; the figurative use (*an injury robbed him of a season*) is equally dynamic.
**Transitivity:** **transitive**, and the object is the victim or the place, not the loot: *rob the bank*, ✗ *rob the money*. The object is obligatory (✗ *two men robbed at closing time*); the *of*-phrase is optional (*they robbed the till*).
**Passive:** **yes**, and it is the commoner form in the figurative sense — *she was robbed of a promotion by a reorganization*, *the branch was robbed at closing time*.

Take something from someone by force or by unfairness.

1. Two men *robbed* the branch on Ellis Street at closing time.
2. The injury *robbed* him *of* an entire season.
3. She was *robbed of* a promotion by a reorganization nobody explained.
4. They *robbed* the till and left the safe alone.
5. A late frost *robbed* the orchard *of* about half its crop.
6. He felt *robbed of* the chance to make his case.

**See also:** [deprive of](#deprive-of) &middot; [cheat on](#cheat-on) &middot; [escape from](#escape-from) &middot; [accuse of](#accuse-of)

### root for

**Pronunciation:** /ˈrut fɔr/ (also /ˈrʊt/) &middot; *ROOT for* &middot; weak /ˈrut fər/
**Русский:** болеть за — открыто желать успеха; разг., амер.
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** informal and American — sports, elections, and encouragement; the British equivalent is *support*
**Complement:** noun phrase — a team, a person, an outcome. **No gerund** (✗ *rooting for winning*), and no that-clause (✗ *root that they win*). An infinitive is licensed only by the person sitting between the preposition and the verb, exactly as with *wait for*: *rooting for the underdog **to** win*.
**Pattern:** *root for* [a team / a person] &middot; *root for* [someone] *to* [verb] &middot; *be rooting for* [someone]
**Contrast:** ***Root for* is cheering from outside, with nothing at stake but sympathy.** *Support* can mean money or votes as well as good wishes (*support the team*, *support the bill*); *back* puts resources behind something; *cheer for* is louder and more literal. Keep the separable particles apart from this fixed preposition: *root out* moves its object (*root out corruption*, *root it out*) and *root around in* means rummage — neither is this verb.
**Verb type:** **action** — dynamic and atelic, an ongoing attitude expressed as activity; the progressive is the default form (*we're all rooting for you*).
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** — *for* is obligatory before the team or person (✗ *root the underdog*), and any infinitive is licensed by that noun, not by the verb: *rooting for the underdog to win*.
**Passive:** **no** — the prepositional passive does not take here: ✗ *the visiting team was rooted for by half the bar*, ✗ *you're being rooted for*. Use *support* or *back*, which are transitive and passivize freely.

Openly hope that someone or something succeeds.

1. Half the bar was *rooting for* the visiting team out of spite.
2. We're all *rooting for* you on Thursday.
3. She was quietly *rooting for* the amendment to fail.
4. He *roots for* whichever team his father hated.
5. Nobody *roots for* the insurance company in a movie.
6. I'm *rooting for* her to get the fellowship.

**See also:** [vote for](#vote-for-vote-against) &middot; [stand up for](20-phrasal-s.md#stand-up-for) &middot; [care about](#care-about-care-for) &middot; [hope for](#hope-for) &middot; [wish for](#wish-for) &middot; [for](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#for)

## S

### save from

**Pronunciation:** /ˈseɪv frʌm/ &middot; *SAYV from* &middot; weak /ˈseɪv frəm/
**Русский:** спасать от — а save for откладывать на, save on экономить на
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*saved us from having to reprint it*) — never a to-infinitive. The thing saved is a direct object; *from* names the danger. Other frames take *for* (*save it for later*), *on* (*save on shipping*), and a bare object (*save money*, *save a seat*).
**Pattern:** *save* [someone] *from* [danger / verb-ing] &middot; *be saved from* [something] &middot; *save* [something] *for* [later] &middot; *save on* [a cost] &middot; *save* [someone] [trouble] &middot; *save up for* [something]
**Contrast:** ***Save from* is rescue; *save for* is postponement; *save on* is economy.** Three prepositions, three unrelated meanings, and the sentence collapses if you swap them: *save the file for Monday* and *save the file from Monday* are not variants of each other. Note the double-object frame that skips the preposition entirely — *that saved me an hour*, *it'll save you a trip* — which is the most common form in American speech. *Save up for* is the money sense with a particle.
**Verb type:** **action** — dynamic and telic; the rescue is an event with a result (*her seatbelt saved her from far worse*).
**Transitivity:** **both** — transitive with the thing saved as object, and **ditransitive** in the preposition-free frame Americans reach for most: *that saved me an hour*, *it'll save you a trip*.
**Passive:** **yes** — *she was saved from far worse by her seatbelt*, *the cat was saved from the storm drain*. The double-object frame passivizes on the person: *I was saved a trip*.

Keep someone or something out of danger, or set something aside.

1. Her seatbelt *saved* her *from* far worse.
2. That template *saved* us *from* rewriting the same memo forty times.
3. *Save* the good tomatoes *for* the sandwiches.
4. You'd *save* about eleven dollars *on* shipping by ordering both together.
5. A neighbor *saved* the cat *from* the storm drain and adopted it a week later.
6. Calling ahead *saves* you a trip.

**See also:** [rescue from](#rescue-from) &middot; [protect from](#protect-from-protect-against) &middot; [prevent from](#prevent-from) &middot; [escape from](#escape-from) &middot; [pay for](19-phrasal-m-r.md#pay-for)

### search for

**Pronunciation:** /ˈsɜrtʃ fɔr/ &middot; *SURCH for* &middot; weak /ˈsɜrtʃ fɚ/
**Русский:** искать что-то — а search a place значит обыскивать место
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** *for* takes a noun phrase naming what is missing — **no gerund** (✗ *search for getting a signal*). The **transitive** frame takes the place or person examined, with no preposition: *search the house*, *search him*. Both can appear together: *search the house for weapons*.
**Pattern:** *search for* [something missing] &middot; *search* [a place, a person] &middot; *search* [a place] *for* [something] &middot; *search through* [a container] &middot; *in search of* [something]
**Contrast:** ***You search a place, and you search for a thing.*** *They searched the car* means they went through it; *they searched for the car* means they couldn't find it. Both are correct and they describe opposite situations, which makes this one of the most consequential preposition contrasts in the whole dictionary — it is the difference between a police report and a missing-persons report. *Look for* is the everyday synonym of *search for*; *search through* emphasizes rummaging.
**Verb type:** **action** — dynamic and atelic in the *for* frame (an activity that may fail), dynamic and telic in the transitive one (*customs searched every bag*).
**Transitivity:** **both** — intransitive with *for* naming what is missing (*searched for her along the river*), transitive with the place or person examined (*search the house*, *search him*), and the two can combine: *searched the property for the equipment*.
**Passive:** **restricted** — excellent in the transitive frame: *every bag on the flight was searched*, *the property was searched for the missing equipment*. No passive of the *for* frame: ✗ *she was searched for for three days* (say *the search for her lasted three days*).

Try to find something, or examine a place thoroughly.

1. Volunteers *searched for* her along the river for three days.
2. Customs *searched* every bag on the flight.
3. Deputies *searched* the property *for* the missing equipment.
4. He spent an hour *searching for* a file that was open in another window.
5. She *searched through* the box and came up with two photographs.
6. They set out *in search of* somewhere with parking.

**See also:** [look for](#look-at-look-for-look-after-look-into) &middot; [ask for](#ask-for-ask-about) &middot; [hope for](#hope-for) &middot; [wait for](#wait-for-wait-on) &middot; [hide from](#hide-from)

### sentence to

**Pronunciation:** /ˈsɛntəns tu/ &middot; *SEN-tunss too* &middot; weak /ˈsɛntəns tə/
**Русский:** приговаривать к — назначать наказание осуждённому; юр.
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** legal, and the standard register of court reporting; the passive (*was sentenced to*) is far commoner than the active
**Complement:** the person sentenced sits between the verb and *to*, and *to* takes a noun phrase naming the punishment — a term, a fine, probation, death. **No gerund** (✗ *sentenced to serving time*). A **to-infinitive does appear**, but only in the courtroom formula *sentenced to serve 36 months*, *sentenced to pay restitution*, where *to serve* is the infinitive marker and not this preposition. The test settles it: *sentenced to five years* takes a plain noun, so that *to* is the preposition.
**Pattern:** *sentence* [someone] *to* [a term / a fine / death] &middot; [someone] *be sentenced to* [noun phrase] &middot; *be sentenced to* [a term] *for* [the offense] &middot; *sentence* [someone] *to* [verb — courtroom formula]
**Contrast:** **The offense takes *for* and the punishment takes *to*, and they never swap:** *sentenced **to** three years **for** wire fraud*. Around it stand the other stages, each with its own preposition: *charge someone **with** an offense*, *convict someone **of** it*, *sentence them **to** a term*, *fine* them (no preposition at all). *Condemn to* is now figurative or historical (*condemned to death*, *condemned to a life of paperwork*) where *sentence to* is the live legal verb.
**Verb type:** **action** — dynamic and telic, a performative act of the court, complete the moment it is uttered.
**Transitivity:** **transitive** — the person sentenced is the direct object and is obligatory (✗ *the judge sentenced to probation*); the *to*-phrase naming the punishment is nearly obligatory too.
**Passive:** **yes**, and the passive is the default in news and legal prose: *he was sentenced to eighteen months in federal prison*, *three defendants were sentenced to time served*.

Impose a punishment on someone who has been convicted.

1. He was *sentenced to* eighteen months in federal prison.
2. The judge *sentenced* her *to* probation and two hundred hours of community service.
3. Three defendants were *sentenced to* time served and released that afternoon.
4. She was *sentenced to* five years *for* the fraud and two more *for* obstruction.
5. The court *sentenced* him *to* pay $40,000 in restitution.
6. Nobody in the state has been *sentenced to* death since 2011.

**See also:** [charge with](#charge-with-charge-for) &middot; [accuse of](#accuse-of) &middot; [punish for](#punish-for) &middot; [testify to](#testify-to) &middot; [sue for](#sue-for) &middot; [to](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#to)

### separate from

**Pronunciation:** /ˈsɛpəˌreɪt frʌm/ &middot; *SEP-uh-rayt from* &middot; weak /ˈsɛpəˌreɪt frəm/
**Русский:** отделять(ся) от — разъединять или держать порознь
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase only — **no gerund, no clause**. *Into* takes the resulting parts (*separate into groups*), and the intransitive *separate* takes no complement at all (*the couple separated*). Note the pronunciation split: the verb ends /eɪt/, the adjective /ət/.
**Pattern:** *separate* [A] *from* [B] &middot; *be separated from* [something] &middot; *separate into* [parts] &middot; *separate* (intransitive) &middot; *separation from* [something]
**Contrast:** ***Separate from* divides two things; *separate into* divides one thing up.** *Separate the yolks from the whites* against *separate the class into four groups* — different structures, and using *from* for the second (✗ *separate the class from four groups*) is nonsense. Compare *divide into*, which behaves like *separate into*, and *distinguish between* / *distinguish from*, which is about telling apart rather than pulling apart. The figurative use is common in American business prose: *what separates a good manager from a bad one*.
**Verb type:** **action** — dynamic in the physical senses (*separate the yolks from the whites*); in the descriptive use (*what separates this camera from the cheaper one is the sensor*) it is a **stative** relation, which is why the progressive sounds odd there.
**Transitivity:** **both** — transitive with an object plus *from* or *into* (*separate the invoices into paid and unpaid*), intransitive with no complement at all (*they separated in April*).
**Passive:** **yes** in the transitive senses — *he was separated from his unit for two days*, *the playground is separated from the lot by a chain-link fence*. No passive of the intransitive: ✗ *they were separated in April* means something else.

Move or keep two things apart.

1. *Separate* the yolks *from* the whites while the eggs are cold.
2. A chain-link fence *separates* the playground *from* the parking lot.
3. What *separates* this camera *from* the cheaper one is the sensor.
4. He was *separated from* his unit for two days.
5. *Separate* the invoices *into* paid and unpaid.
6. They *separated* in April and reconciled by August.

**See also:** [differ from](#differ-from) &middot; [translate into](#translate-into) &middot; [escape from](#escape-from) &middot; [consist of](#consist-of)

### share with

**Pronunciation:** /ˈʃɛr wɪθ/ &middot; *SHAIR with* &middot; weak /ˈʃɛr wəθ/
**Русский:** делиться с — отдавать часть своего или иметь что-то общее
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** *with* takes the person you share with; the thing shared is a direct object: *share the file with the team*. A **gerund** can be the thing shared (*share being on call*), but no to-infinitive and no clause. *In* takes something you partake of: *share in the profits*.
**Pattern:** *share* [something] *with* [someone] &middot; *share* [something] &middot; *share in* [profits, credit, blame] &middot; *share* [a trait] *with* [someone] &middot; *have* [something] *in common with* [someone]
**Contrast:** ***Share with* gives a portion away; *share in* takes a portion of something already collective.** *She shared her notes with me* (I got a copy) against *she shared in the credit* (she got a piece of it). ✗ *share to someone* is an error, though it has spread through social-media interfaces. Note the third sense, hold in common: *the two languages share a root*, which takes a direct object or *with*, never *in*.
**Verb type:** **both** — **action** when something is handed over (*she shared her notes with anyone who asked*), **stative** when something is held in common (*he shares a birthday with his grandfather*, *the two houses share a driveway*), where the progressive is odd.
**Transitivity:** **both** — transitive with the thing shared as object and *with* naming the recipient; intransitive with *in* for taking a portion (*junior partners share in the profits*), where no object is possible.
**Passive:** **restricted** — fine in the transitive giving sense: *the notes were shared with the whole team*. No passive of *share in* (✗ *the profits were shared in*) and none of the stative sense (✗ *a driveway is shared by the two houses* is possible but reads as a description, not an act).

Give someone part of what you have, or have something in common.

1. She *shared* her notes *with* anyone who asked.
2. Would you *share* that with the rest of us?
3. The two houses *share* a driveway *with* no written agreement of any kind.
4. Junior partners *share in* the profits but not the losses.
5. He *shares* a birthday *with* his grandfather.
6. They *shared* one bathroom *with* six other tenants.

**See also:** [provide with](#provide-with-provide-for) &middot; [consist of](#consist-of) &middot; [belong to](#belong-to) &middot; [compete with](#compete-with-compete-against-compete-for) &middot; [with](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#with)

### sign up for

**Pronunciation:** /ˌsaɪn ˈʌp fɔr/ &middot; *syne-UP for* &middot; weak /saɪn ˈʌp fər/
**Русский:** записываться на — регистрироваться или подписываться на что-то
**Type:** verb + particle + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to informal — everyday American English for classes, shifts, plans, and mailing lists
**Complement:** *for* takes a noun phrase — a class, a service, a shift, a plan. A **gerund** is unusual and marked (?*signed up for cleaning the garage*); to put a verb after *sign up*, drop *for* and use the **infinitive**: *signed up **to** volunteer*. No that-clause.
**Pattern:** *sign up for* [a class, a service, a shift] &middot; *sign up to* [verb — infinitive] &middot; *sign* [someone] *up for* [something] &middot; *sign up with* [an organization]
**Contrast:** **The particle moves and the preposition does not.** *Up* slides around an object — *sign me up*, *signed the whole family up* — while *for* never leaves its noun: ✗ *sign up the class for*. Then the prepositions divide the meanings: *sign up **for*** is enrolling in something, *sign up **with*** is joining an outfit (*signed up with a new agency*), *sign **for*** is signing a receipt (*sign for the package*), and *sign on **to*** is endorsing (*signed on to the letter*). The idiom *I didn't sign up for this* means *this is not what I agreed to*.
**Verb type:** **action** — dynamic and telic, a single act of enrollment (*forty people signed up for the Saturday session in the first hour*).
**Transitivity:** **both** — intransitive with *for* (*I signed up for the two-year plan*), transitive when someone else is enrolled and the particle moves around them: *sign your kids up for swim lessons*, *sign me up*.
**Passive:** **restricted** — the causative frame passivizes naturally: *the kids were signed up for swim lessons*. The plain enrollment sense does not: ✗ *the Saturday session was signed up for*.

Enroll in something, or put your name down for it.

1. Forty people *signed up for* the Saturday session in the first hour.
2. I *signed up for* the two-year plan and regretted it by March.
3. *Sign* your kids *up for* swim lessons before the list fills.
4. She *signed up to* work the overnight shift on Christmas.
5. He *signed up for* a gym membership he has never once used.
6. Long hours are one thing, but nobody *signed up for* this.

**See also:** [apply for](#apply-for-apply-to) &middot; [qualify for](#qualify-for) &middot; [participate in](#participate-in) &middot; [commit to](#commit-to) &middot; [for](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#for)

### smile at

**Pronunciation:** /ˈsmaɪl æt/ &middot; *SMILE at* &middot; weak /ˈsmaɪl ət/
**Русский:** улыбаться кому-то — направлять улыбку на кого-то
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*smiled at being remembered*) — never a to-infinitive, never a clause. *Smile* is intransitive and the target always needs a preposition: ✗ *smile me*. A rare transitive survives in *smile a thin smile*.
**Pattern:** *smile at* [someone / something] &middot; *smile about* [something] &middot; *smile on* [someone] — favor &middot; *smile* (alone) &middot; *a smile for* [someone]
**Contrast:** ***At* aims the smile; *about* names the cause; *on* is fortune.** *She smiled at the baby* directs it; *she smiled about the whole thing later* explains it; *fortune smiled on them* is a fixed literary idiom meaning they got lucky. ✗ *smile to someone* is not idiomatic American English. Compare *laugh at*, which is usually hostile, with *smile at*, which almost never is — the same preposition, opposite social temperature.
**Verb type:** **action** — dynamic, a brief bodily act; the progressive is normal (*she was smiling at the baby*).
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** — the target always needs a preposition (✗ *smile me*), and the verb stands alone freely (*don't smile*). Only the cognate object survives transitively: *smile a thin smile*.
**Passive:** **no** for *at* — ✗ *everyone in line was smiled at*, ✗ *the photograph was smiled at*. The one fixed exception is the fortune idiom, where the passive participle is idiomatic: *the weekend was smiled on by the weather*, *a smiled-upon candidate*.

Turn a smile toward someone or something.

1. He *smiled at* everyone in line and meant it, which was unsettling.
2. She *smiled at* the photograph for a long moment before putting it back.
3. *Smile at* the camera, not at me.
4. They *smiled about* it for weeks afterward.
5. The weather *smiled on* the whole weekend for once.
6. He *smiled at* being recognized after all those years.

**See also:** [laugh at](#laugh-at) &middot; [stare at](#stare-at) &middot; [look at](#look-at-look-for-look-after-look-into) &middot; [point at](#point-at-point-to)

### specialize in

**Pronunciation:** /ˈspɛʃəˌlaɪz ɪn/ &middot; *SPESH-uh-lize in* &middot; weak /ˈspɛʃəˌlaɪz ən/
**Русский:** специализироваться на — сосредоточивать работу в узкой области
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*specializes in restoring old plaster*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *specializes in to restore plaster*). *Specialize* is intransitive: ✗ *specialize immigration law*. American spelling is *-ize*; *specialise* is British.
**Pattern:** *specialize in* [a field / verb-ing] &middot; *be specialized in* — avoid; use the active &middot; *a specialist in* [a field] &middot; *specialize* (alone)
**Contrast:** ***Specialize on* and ✗ *specialize at* are errors** — the preposition is fixed at *in*, and so is the noun's: *a specialist in labor law*. Compare *major in* (an American college term, same shape: *major in biology*), *concentrate on* (attention rather than expertise), and *be good at*, which takes *at* and never *in*. Note that the passive *is specialized in* sounds translated; American writers use the active.
**Verb type:** **stative** in practice — it describes what a firm or person characteristically does (*the firm specializes in municipal bond work*), so the progressive is odd except for a change in progress (?*they're specializing in elder law now*).
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** — *in* is obligatory: ✗ *specialize immigration law*, ✗ *specialize on bond work*. The verb also stands alone: *you can't specialize in everything*.
**Passive:** **no** — intransitive, so there is nothing to promote, and the entry flags the calque directly: ✗ *the firm is specialized in bond work*. American writers use the active, or the noun frame *a specialist in labor law*.

Concentrate your work or study on one narrow field.

1. The firm *specializes in* municipal bond work.
2. She *specializes in* restoring plaster ceilings, which is a smaller field than you'd think.
3. He *specialized in* pediatric cardiology after a residency in Cleveland.
4. That shop *specializes in* parts for cars nobody makes anymore.
5. Two of the four practices *specialize in* elder law.
6. You can't *specialize in* everything.

**See also:** [concentrate on](#concentrate-on) &middot; [work on](#work-on-work-for-work-with) &middot; [invest in](#invest-in) &middot; [participate in](#participate-in) &middot; [in](../../06-prepositions/catalog/01-core-in-on-at.md#in)

### stare at

**Pronunciation:** /ˈstɛr æt/ &middot; *STAIR at* &middot; weak /ˈstɛr ət/
**Русский:** пристально смотреть на — глядеть подолгу, не отрывая взгляда
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase only — **no gerund, no clause**. *Stare* is intransitive and always needs *at* before its target: ✗ *stare the screen*. The only bare use is absolute: *don't stare*.
**Pattern:** *stare at* [someone / something] &middot; *stare* (alone) &middot; *stare into* [space, the distance] &middot; *stare down* [someone] &middot; *be staring* [someone] *in the face*
**Contrast:** ***Stare at* is fixed** — ✗ *stare to* and ✗ *stare on* are errors. The family sorts by intensity and politeness: *look at* is neutral, *watch* is transitive with no preposition (*watch the game*), *stare at* is rude, *glare at* is hostile, *gaze at* is fond or dreamy, and *glance at* is brief. Only *watch* drops the preposition, which is why ✗ *watch at the game* and ✗ *stare the game* are both wrong.
**Verb type:** **action** — dynamic and durative, an activity you can be caught in the middle of: *two people on the platform were openly staring at her*.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** — *at* is obligatory before the target (✗ *stare the screen*), unlike *watch*, which is transitive (*watch the game*). Bare *don't stare* is the absolute use.
**Passive:** **yes**, restricted to the prepositional passive, which is genuinely idiomatic here — *she was being stared at*, *nobody likes being stared at*. Compare *watch*, which passivizes the ordinary way: *the game was watched by millions*.

Look at something fixedly for a long time.

1. He *stared at* the ceiling until about four in the morning.
2. Two people on the platform were openly *staring at* her.
3. She *stared at* the invoice as though it might revise itself.
4. Don't *stare*.
5. He *stared into* the middle distance and said he'd think about it.
6. The answer had been *staring* us *in the face* for a week.

**See also:** [look at](#look-at-look-for-look-after-look-into) &middot; [laugh at](#laugh-at) &middot; [smile at](#smile-at) &middot; [listen to](#listen-to)

### stem from

**Pronunciation:** /ˈstɛm frʌm/ &middot; *STEM from* &middot; weak /ˈstɛm frəm/
**Русский:** происходить из, восходить к — иметь своим источником
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal — common in journalism and analysis
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*stems from having no oversight*) — never a to-infinitive, never a clause. *Stem* in this sense is intransitive and never takes an object: ✗ *stem the problem* is a different verb entirely, meaning stop the flow.
**Pattern:** [a problem, a belief, a case] *stem from* [a cause] &middot; *stem from* [verb-ing] &middot; *stem* [the tide, the bleeding, the flow] — a different verb, no preposition
**Contrast:** ***Stem from* points from effect back to cause**, exactly like *result from* and *arise from*, and the opposite of *result in* and *lead to*. ✗ *stem to* does not exist. Watch the homonym: *stem the losses* means stanch them, takes a direct object, and has nothing to do with this pattern — *the charges stem from the losses* and *the charges stemmed the losses* are unrelated sentences. Journalists lean on *stem from* heavily because it asserts a cause without asserting blame.
**Verb type:** **stative** — a relation of origin, not an event; it resists the progressive (✗ *the charges are stemming from a 2019 contract*). The homonym *stem the bleeding* is a different, dynamic verb.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** — no object in this sense (✗ *stem the problem* belongs to the other verb), and *from* is obligatory: ✗ *the friction stems*.
**Passive:** **no** — intransitive and stative: ✗ *a 2019 contract is stemmed from*. Only the unrelated homonym passivizes: *the losses were stemmed by the third quarter*.

Have its origin in something.

1. The charges *stem from* a 2019 procurement contract.
2. Most of the friction *stems from* a job description nobody has updated since 2011.
3. Her interest in soils *stemmed from* a summer on a research plot.
4. The delays *stem from* having only one inspector for the whole county.
5. The dispute *stems from* a boundary survey done in 1948.
6. Almost every complaint *stems from* the same two forms.

**See also:** [result from](#result-in-result-from) &middot; [lead to](18-phrasal-h-l.md#lead-to) &middot; [result in](#result-in-result-from) &middot; [die from](#die-of-die-from)

### stipulate to

**Pronunciation:** /ˈstɪpjəˌleɪt tu/ &middot; *STIP-yuh-layt too* &middot; weak /ˈstɪpjəˌleɪt tə/
**Русский:** признавать факт по соглашению сторон — юр., в процессе
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** legal — an American meets *stipulate to* in a courtroom, a deposition transcript, or a settlement agreement; outside law the verb is used without *to* (*the lease stipulates a thirty-day notice*)
**Complement:** noun phrase naming a fact, an exhibit, or an element (*stipulated to the authenticity of the recording*), or a **gerund** (*stipulated to entering the records without a custodian*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *stipulated to admit the exhibit*). For a full proposition, drop the preposition: *stipulate that the report was authentic*.
**Pattern:** *stipulate to* [a fact / an exhibit / an element] &middot; *stipulate to* [verb-ing] &middot; *stipulate that* [clause — no preposition] &middot; *stipulate* [a condition] &middot; *enter into a stipulation as to* [something]
**Contrast:** **The two senses take opposite kinds of act.** Plain transitive *stipulate* means require as a condition — *the grant stipulates that funds be spent within a year*. With *to*, it means agreeing with the other side that something need not be proved at all: *the defense stipulated to the chain of custody*, and that issue leaves the trial. Neighbors: *concede* and *admit to* give away a point in argument, *agree to* accepts a proposal, *consent to* authorizes — none of them has the technical effect of removing an element from proof.
**Verb type:** **action** — dynamic and performative, a speech act with legal effect the moment counsel makes it (*both sides stipulated to the authenticity of the recordings*).
**Transitivity:** **both** — intransitive with *to* in the agreement sense, transitive in the requirement sense (*the grant stipulates a one-year window*, *stipulate that funds be spent within a year*).
**Passive:** **restricted** — natural in the requirement sense: *it was stipulated that the funds be spent within a year*. The *to* frame passivizes only in legal register and stiffly: ?*the chain of custody was stipulated to*; prefer the active *the parties stipulated to it*.

Agree formally, on the record, that a fact may be taken as established.

1. Both sides *stipulated to* the authenticity of the recordings.
2. The defense would not *stipulate to* the value of the stolen property.
3. Counsel, will you *stipulate to* the witness's qualifications?
4. They *stipulated to* liability and tried the damages question alone.
5. The parties *stipulated to* entering the medical records without a custodian.
6. If we *stipulate to* the timeline, the hearing takes an afternoon instead of a week.

**See also:** [agree to](#agree-with-agree-to-agree-on) &middot; [admit to](#admit-to) &middot; [testify to](#testify-to) &middot; [submit to](#submit-to) &middot; [to](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#to)

### submit to

**Pronunciation:** /səbˈmɪt tu/ &middot; *sub-MIT too* &middot; weak /səbˈmɪt tə/
**Русский:** подчиняться чему-то, подвергаться; подавать что-то куда-то — два разных глагола
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal in the yielding sense; the hand-it-in sense is everyday office and academic English
**Complement:** in the yielding sense, *to* takes a noun phrase or a **gerund** (*submitted to being searched*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *submit to be searched*). In the hand-it-in sense the thing submitted is the direct object and *to* names the recipient, always a noun phrase: *submit the form to HR*.
**Pattern:** *submit to* [authority, a test, an examination] &middot; *submit to* [verb-ing] &middot; *submit* [something] *to* [someone] &middot; *be submitted to* [a committee] &middot; *submit that* [clause]
**Contrast:** **Two verbs share one spelling, and the direct object tells them apart.** *He submitted to a drug test* is undergoing something; *he submitted the form to HR* is handing it in — only the second has an object before *to*. Among the yielding verbs: *surrender to* is giving up in a contest or to the police, *yield to* is giving way to traffic or pressure, *defer to* is bowing to someone's judgment while keeping your own position. And the lawyer's *we submit that the statute is clear* takes a clause and no preposition at all.
**Verb type:** **action** in both senses — dynamic yielding (*he refused to submit to a search*) and dynamic handing-in (*she submitted the form to HR*). Neither is stative, though the yielding sense can describe a standing arrangement: *members submit to the ethics rules when they join*.
**Transitivity:** **both** — intransitive with *to* in the yielding sense (no object: ✗ *submit a search*), transitive in the filing sense, where the thing submitted is the object and *to* names the recipient.
**Passive:** **restricted** — the filing sense passivizes constantly and is the standard form: *all bids must be submitted to the purchasing office by Friday*. The yielding sense has no passive: ✗ *a search was submitted to*, ✗ *he was submitted to*.

Yield to something, or hand something in to someone.

1. He refused to *submit to* a search without a warrant.
2. All bids must be *submitted to* the purchasing office by Friday.
3. She *submitted to* two rounds of interviews before anyone mentioned salary.
4. The paper was *submitted to* three journals and rejected by all three.
5. They would rather close than *submit to* being audited every quarter.
6. Members *submit to* the ethics rules when they join.

**See also:** [subscribe to](#subscribe-to) &middot; [stipulate to](#stipulate-to) &middot; [comply with](#comply-with) &middot; [object to](#object-to) &middot; [to](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#to)

### subscribe to

**Pronunciation:** /səbˈskraɪb tu/ &middot; *sub-SKRIBE too* &middot; weak /səbˈskraɪb tə/
**Русский:** подписываться на; разделять (взгляд, мнение)
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase only — **no gerund, no clause** (✗ *subscribe to getting the paper*). Both senses share the frame: a publication or service, and a view or doctrine.
**Pattern:** *subscribe to* [a publication, a service] &middot; *subscribe to* [a view, a theory] &middot; *subscribe* [an amount] — formal, rare &middot; *a subscription to* [something]
**Contrast:** ***Subscribe of* and ✗ *subscribe for* are errors** in ordinary use (*subscribe for shares* survives only in securities language). The second sense is the useful one for writers: *I don't subscribe to that theory* means I don't accept it, and it is a shade more formal and more distancing than *I don't believe it*. The noun keeps *to*: *a subscription to the Times*. Compare *sign up for*, the everyday American phrasal equivalent of the service sense.
**Verb type:** **stative** in both senses — holding a subscription and holding a view are states, not acts (✗ *I'm subscribing to that theory*); the inceptive act is *sign up for* or *subscribed* in the simple past.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** — *to* is obligatory: ✗ *subscribe the print edition*, ✗ *subscribe of that view*. The bare transitive *subscribe an amount* survives only in old securities language.
**Passive:** **no** ordinary passive — ✗ *the print edition is subscribed to by them*, ✗ *three databases are subscribed to*. Only the postmodifying participle survives in formal prose: *a view widely subscribed to among economists*.

Pay to receive something regularly, or accept a view.

1. They still *subscribe to* the print edition on Sundays.
2. I don't *subscribe to* the theory that everything was better in 1994.
3. The lab *subscribes to* three databases nobody uses.
4. She *subscribes to* the view that meetings should have an agenda or a cancellation.
5. He *subscribed to* four streaming services and watched one.
6. A *subscription to* the journal runs about ninety dollars a year.

**See also:** [belong to](#belong-to) &middot; [agree with](#agree-with-agree-to-agree-on) &middot; [believe in](#believe-in) &middot; [refer to](#refer-to) &middot; [apply for](#apply-for-apply-to)

### substitute for

**Pronunciation:** /ˈsʌbstəˌtut fɔr/ &middot; *SUB-stuh-toot for* &middot; weak /ˈsʌbstəˌtut fɚ/
**Русский:** заменять собой — ставить одно вместо другого
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal
**Complement:** noun phrase only — **no gerund, no clause**. The **new** item is the direct object and the **old** one takes *for*: *substitute margarine for butter* means use margarine instead of butter. The intransitive frame reverses the feel: *substitute for someone* means fill in for them.
**Pattern:** *substitute* [new] *for* [old] &middot; *substitute for* [someone] — fill in &middot; *be substituted for* [something] &middot; *a substitute for* [something] &middot; *replace* [old] *with* [new] — the mirror verb
**Contrast:** **This is the most commonly reversed pattern in the dictionary.** *Substitute X **for** Y* puts X in and takes Y out. *Replace X **with** Y* takes X out and puts Y in. So *substitute honey for sugar* and *replace sugar with honey* describe the same recipe, and getting either backwards inverts it. Because so many people reverse it, *substitute X **with** Y* has spread in speech; it is still widely criticized in edited American prose, and the safe move is to use *replace ... with* whenever you want the with-frame.
**Verb type:** **action** — dynamic in both frames: swapping one thing for another (*substitute buttermilk for the whole milk*) and filling in for a person (*she substituted for the regular teacher*).
**Transitivity:** **both** — transitive with the **new** item as object and the **old** one under *for*, intransitive in the fill-in sense, where *for* takes the person replaced.
**Passive:** **restricted** — the swap frame passivizes: *buttermilk can be substituted for the whole milk*, *a milder resolution was substituted for the one on the floor*. The fill-in sense does not: ✗ *the regular teacher was substituted for*.

Put one thing in the place of another.

1. *Substitute* buttermilk *for* the whole milk and add a little baking soda.
2. You can *substitute* almond flour *for* about a third of the wheat flour.
3. She *substituted for* the regular teacher three days a week.
4. There's no *substitute for* actually reading the contract.
5. The committee *substituted* a milder resolution *for* the one on the floor.
6. *Replace* the gasket *with* the one in the kit — same job, opposite wording.

**See also:** [prefer to](#prefer-to) &middot; [compare with](#compare-to-compare-with) &middot; [provide with](#provide-with-provide-for) &middot; [separate from](#separate-from)

### succeed in

**Pronunciation:** /səkˈsid ɪn/ &middot; *suk-SEED in* &middot; weak /səkˈsid ən/
**Русский:** суметь, добиться — после in герундий; а succeed smb сменить на посту
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal
**Complement:** **gerund** or noun phrase — *succeeded in raising the money* — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *succeeded to raise the money*, which is a different and mostly obsolete pattern about inheriting a throne). Never a clause.
**Pattern:** *succeed in* [verb-ing] &middot; *succeed in* [a field, an effort] &middot; *succeed* [someone] — follow in office, no preposition &middot; *succeed to* [a title, a throne] — formal, archaic &middot; *succeed* (alone)
**Contrast:** ***Succeed to do something* is wrong.** The verb has no infinitive frame: use *succeed **in** doing*, or switch to *manage to do*, which is the ordinary American way of saying it. The bare transitive is a different verb altogether — *she succeeded him as chair* means she took over the job — and the archaic *succeed to the throne* is the only surviving *to* pattern. Compare *fail*, which behaves in the opposite way and takes the infinitive: *failed to raise the money*.
**Verb type:** **action** — an achievement verb, dynamic and telic; the progressive is limited to the ongoing struggle (*they're succeeding in raising the money*, marked).
**Transitivity:** **both** — intransitive in this pattern (✗ *succeed the money*; *in* plus a gerund is the frame, and ✗ *succeed to raise the money* is wrong), transitive in the unrelated sense of following in office: *she succeeded him as chair*.
**Passive:** **restricted** — the office sense passivizes normally: *he was succeeded by his deputy*. The *in* frame has no passive: ✗ *raising the money was succeeded in*.

Manage to do something, or take over someone's position.

1. They *succeeded in* raising almost twice the target.
2. He never quite *succeeded in* explaining why the numbers changed.
3. Two of the eight *succeeded in* passing on the first attempt.
4. The campaign *succeeded in* registering four thousand new voters.
5. She *succeeded* him *as* chair in the spring.
6. Nobody *succeeds in* that job without a very good deputy.

**See also:** [cope with](#cope-with) &middot; [result in](#result-in-result-from) &middot; [qualify for](#qualify-for) &middot; [specialize in](#specialize-in)

### sue for

**Pronunciation:** /ˈsu fɔr/ &middot; *SOO for* &middot; weak /ˈsu fər/
**Русский:** подавать в суд, требуя чего-то — for называет иск или сумму; юр.
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral, legal-adjacent; *sue for peace* is a frozen diplomatic idiom
**Complement:** noun phrase naming either the relief sought (*sue for damages*, *sue for $2 million*, *sue for divorce*) or the ground (*sue for breach of contract*, *sue for negligence*). A **gerund** works for the ground (*sued the paper for printing the photo*) but never for the relief, and there is **no to-infinitive** (✗ *sued for to recover the deposit*).
**Pattern:** *sue* [someone] *for* [damages / an amount] &middot; *sue* [someone] *for* [a wrong] &middot; *sue for* [verb-ing] &middot; *sue over* [an episode] &middot; *sue* [someone] *in* [a court]
**Contrast:** ***For* does double duty and only context separates the two jobs:** *sued for defamation* names the claim, *sued for $3 million* names the money. *Sue **over*** names the episode that prompted the suit (*sued over the layoffs*) and is the journalist's preposition. Around it: a prosecutor *charges someone with* a crime, a private party *sues*, and *prosecute* takes no preposition. *Sue for peace* is fixed — an army sues for peace, and nobody sues for a truce.
**Verb type:** **action** — dynamic and telic, the act of filing; *be suing* is normal for a case in progress (*two shareholders are suing for $4 million*).
**Transitivity:** **both** — transitive with the defendant as object (*she sued the hospital for negligence*), intransitive when only the relief or ground is named (*the contractor sued for the balance*).
**Passive:** **restricted** — excellent when the defendant is the object: *the hospital was sued for negligence*, *the magazine was sued for running the photograph*. The relief phrase cannot be promoted: ✗ *$4 million is being sued for*.

Bring a lawsuit seeking something, or on a particular ground.

1. The contractor *sued for* the balance of the contract plus interest.
2. She *sued* the hospital *for* negligence and settled a year later.
3. Two shareholders are *suing for* $4 million in damages.
4. He *sued* the magazine *for* running the photograph without permission.
5. They filed to *sue for* divorce in a state neither had lived in.
6. Cut off from resupply, the garrison *sued for* peace.

**See also:** [charge with](#charge-with-charge-for) &middot; [accuse of](#accuse-of) &middot; [sentence to](#sentence-to) &middot; [testify to](#testify-to) &middot; [apply for](#apply-for-apply-to) &middot; [for](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#for)

### suffer from

**Pronunciation:** /ˈsʌfɚ frʌm/ &middot; *SUFF-er from* &middot; weak /ˈsʌfɚ frəm/
**Русский:** страдать от — быть подверженным болезни, недостатку или тяготе
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*suffers from having no natural light*) — never a to-infinitive, never a clause. The **transitive** frame takes a single event with no preposition: *suffer a stroke*, *suffer a defeat* — and *from* would be wrong there.
**Pattern:** [someone] *suffer from* [a chronic condition] &middot; [something] *suffer from* [a flaw] &middot; *suffer* [an injury, a loss, a defeat] &middot; *suffer* (alone) &middot; *suffer for* [one's art]
**Contrast:** ***Suffer from* is ongoing; bare *suffer* is a single event.** *He suffers from migraines* is a condition he lives with; *he suffered a concussion* happened once, and ✗ *suffered from a concussion on Tuesday* is odd. The pattern extends past medicine: *the argument suffers from a missing premise*, *the neighborhood suffers from decades of disinvestment*. Note that careful American writing increasingly avoids *suffers from* for disabilities in favor of *has*, on the grounds that *suffer* editorializes.
**Verb type:** **stative** in the *from* frame — a condition lived with, though it takes the progressive for temporary ones (*he's suffering from the flu*). The bare transitive is **action**: *he suffered a stroke in March* is a single event.
**Transitivity:** **both** — intransitive with *from* naming an ongoing condition or flaw (*the report suffers from relying on a single source*), transitive with a single event and no preposition (*suffer a defeat*, *suffer a loss*).
**Passive:** **restricted** — no passive at all for *suffer from*: ✗ *chronic pain is suffered from by one adult in eight*. The transitive object is not a true patient, so its passive is confined to formal financial and military prose: *heavy losses were suffered in the third quarter*, but ✗ *a stroke was suffered by him*.

Be affected by an illness, a flaw, or a hardship.

1. About one adult in eight *suffers from* some form of chronic pain.
2. The building *suffers from* a roof that was never right to begin with.
3. The report *suffers from* relying on a single source.
4. He *suffered* a stroke in March and was back at the shop by June.
5. Small towns along that corridor have *suffered from* forty years of decline.
6. The whole argument *suffers from* not defining its terms.

**See also:** [recover from](#recover-from) &middot; [benefit from](#benefit-from) &middot; [die of](#die-of-die-from) &middot; [cope with](#cope-with) &middot; [complain about](#complain-to-complain-about)

### suspect of

**Pronunciation:** /səˈspɛkt əv/ &middot; *suh-SPEKT uv* &middot; weak /səˈspɛkt ə/
**Русский:** подозревать в — считать виновным без доказательств
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*suspected him of skimming*) — **never a to-infinitive after *of*** (✗ *suspected him of to skim*) and never a that-clause after the preposition. The active ✗ *suspected him to be skimming* is unidiomatic too; the only live infinitive frame is the formal passive *the fire is suspected to have been deliberate*, and even there *suspected of being* is the commoner form. *Suspect* does take a that-clause with no preposition: *I suspect that she already knew*. Note the stress shift: the verb is /səˈspɛkt/, the noun /ˈsʌspɛkt/.
**Pattern:** *suspect* [someone] *of* [something / verb-ing] &middot; *be suspected of* [something] &middot; *suspect that* [clause] &middot; *suspect* [something] &middot; *a suspect in* [a case]
**Contrast:** ***Suspect of* is the private version of *accuse of*.** Suspicion is held; accusation is voiced; a charge is filed — *suspected of*, *accused of*, *charged with*, and the preposition changes only at the last step. ✗ *suspect someone for something* is an error. Note the noun frame, which uses a different preposition again: *a suspect **in** the robbery*, never ✗ *a suspect of the robbery*.
**Verb type:** **stative** — a mental state, not an act; the progressive is marked (?*I'm suspecting the thermostat*), where *investigate* and *accuse* are the dynamic neighbors.
**Transitivity:** **transitive** — the person suspected is the direct object and the *of*-phrase names the wrongdoing: *suspected him of moving money*. The object is obligatory: ✗ *investigators suspected of skimming*.
**Passive:** **yes** — the exception among stative verbs, and the passive is the commoner form: *she was suspected of leaking the schedule*, *two employees were suspected of falsifying the counts*.

Believe, without proof, that someone has done something.

1. Investigators *suspected* him *of* moving money through the parts business.
2. She was *suspected of* leaking the schedule and never asked about it directly.
3. I *suspect* the thermostat, not the furnace.
4. Two employees were *suspected of* falsifying the counts.
5. He *suspected that* the offer would be withdrawn by Monday.
6. Nobody *suspected* the quietest person in the office *of* anything.

**See also:** [accuse of](#accuse-of) &middot; [charge with](#charge-with-charge-for) &middot; [blame for](#blame-for-blame-on) &middot; [confess to](#confess-to) &middot; [admit to](#admit-to)

## T–W

### take care of

**Pronunciation:** /ˈteɪk ˈkɛr əv/ &middot; *tayk KAIR uv* &middot; weak /ˈteɪk ˈkɛr ə/
**Русский:** заботиться о; заниматься (делом) — после of герундий
**Type:** verb + noun + preposition (fixed idiom) &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*take care of getting it notarized*) — **never a to-infinitive after *of*** (✗ *take care of to get it notarized*). A separate frame, *take care **to** do something*, uses the infinitive and means be careful to: *take care to date it*. The two are not interchangeable.
**Pattern:** *take care of* [someone] &middot; *take care of* [a task / verb-ing] &middot; *take care to* [verb] &middot; *take care* (farewell) &middot; *be taken care of*
**Contrast:** ***Take care of it* handles it; *take care to do it* is cautious about it.** *Take care of the paperwork* means do it; *take care to sign the paperwork* means don't forget the signature. The idiom covers both people and jobs, which *care for* does not — *care for* is only about people and only about tending. Note the euphemism, alive and well in American crime fiction: *we'll take care of him*. And *Take care* on its own is a goodbye.
**Verb type:** **action** — dynamic in both readings, tending (*her neighbor takes care of the plants*) and handling (*I'll take care of the deposit*). Distinguish *take care **to** do*, which is a different frame.
**Transitivity:** **transitive** as a unit — the whole idiom *take care of* governs the object, and *care* is a frozen noun that takes no article or plural (✗ *take a care of it*).
**Passive:** **yes** — the prepositional idiom passivizes cleanly, and the entry lists it: *the plants are taken care of while she travels*, *everything except the labor is taken care of by the warranty*.

Look after someone, or deal with a task.

1. Her neighbor *takes care of* the plants whenever she travels.
2. I'll *take care of* the deposit on my way in.
3. Somebody needs to *take care of* getting the permit amended.
4. He *took care of* his father for the last three years of his life.
5. The warranty *takes care of* everything except the labor.
6. *Take care to* keep the original; they'll ask for it.

**See also:** [care for](#care-about-care-for) &middot; [care about](#care-about-care-for) &middot; [look after](#look-at-look-for-look-after-look-into) &middot; [cope with](#cope-with)

### take precedence over

**Pronunciation:** /ˌteɪk ˈprɛsɪdəns ˈoʊvər/ &middot; *tayk PRESS-ih-dunss OH-ver* &middot; weak /ˌteɪk ˈprɛsɪdns ˌoʊvɚ/
**Русский:** иметь приоритет перед — стоять выше при столкновении интересов
**Type:** verb + noun + preposition — a fixed idiom rather than a simple verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal — statutes, contracts, policies, and workplace prose
**Complement:** *over* takes a noun phrase or a **gerund** (*safety takes precedence over meeting the deadline*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *takes precedence over to meet the deadline*) and never a clause. The noun is frozen: no article (✗ *take a precedence over*), no plural, and no modifier beyond *clear* or *absolute*. The complement can be dropped when the other claim is understood: *the more specific provision takes precedence*.
**Pattern:** [X] *take precedence over* [Y] &middot; [X] *take precedence* &middot; *take absolute precedence over* [Y] &middot; *in order of precedence*
**Contrast:** ***Over* is the only preposition here** — ✗ *take precedence to*, and *take precedence of* is archaic British. The idiom ranks two real claims: the earlier lien takes precedence over the later one, and both exist. Its neighbors do other things: *supersede* replaces one instrument with another and takes no preposition (*this agreement supersedes all prior agreements*), *override* cancels (*the federal rule overrides the state rule*), *outweigh* compares weight rather than rank. Note the spelling trap: *precedence* is rank, *precedent* is a prior case — you *set a precedent* but *take precedence*.
**Verb type:** **stative** — a relation of rank between two claims that both exist; the progressive is odd (✗ *safety is taking precedence over the schedule*) and so is the imperative.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** in effect — *precedence* is a frozen noun, not a promotable object (no article, no plural, no modifier beyond *clear* or *absolute*), and the *over*-phrase is droppable when the rival claim is understood: *the more specific provision takes precedence*.
**Passive:** **no** — neither piece can be promoted: ✗ *precedence is taken over the schedule*, ✗ *the schedule is taken precedence over*. Use *supersede* or *override*, which are transitive and passivize: *the state rule is overridden by the federal one*.

Rank ahead of something else when the two conflict.

1. Patient safety *takes precedence over* the schedule, every time.
2. A federal statute *takes precedence over* a conflicting state rule.
3. In this house, homework *takes precedence over* screens.
4. The earlier filing *takes precedence over* the amended one.
5. Nothing *takes precedence over* getting the water back on.
6. Where two provisions conflict, the more specific one *takes precedence*.

**See also:** [comply with](#comply-with) &middot; [rest with](#rest-with) &middot; [over](../../06-prepositions/catalog/09-comparison-opposition.md#over) &middot; [subject to](../../06-prepositions/catalog/15-dependent-adjectives.md#subject-to)

### talk to, talk with, talk about

**Pronunciation:** /ˈtɔk tu/ &middot; *TAWK too* &middot; weak /ˈtɔk tə/
**Русский:** говорить — to/with с кем-то (with на равных), about о чём-то
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** *to* and *with* take a person. *about* takes a noun phrase, a **gerund**, or a wh-clause (*talked about moving*, *talked about where to live*). **None takes a to-infinitive.** *Talk* is intransitive in all of these — the exceptions are the fixed transitive uses *talk sense*, *talk shop*, *talk business*, and *talk someone into* / *out of* something.
**Pattern:** *talk to* [someone] &middot; *talk with* [someone] &middot; *talk about* [a topic] &middot; *talk to* [someone] *about* [a topic] &middot; *talk of* [something] — literary &middot; *talk over* [a decision] &middot; *talk* [someone] *into* / *out of* [verb-ing]
**Contrast:** ***Talk with* is far more common in American English than in British English**, and Americans reach for it when the exchange is mutual — *I talked with the family for an hour* implies both sides spoke. *Talk to* is the default and can tilt one-directional, which is why *I'm going to talk to him* often means a reprimand is coming and *I'm going to talk with him* rarely does. Neither is wrong; the difference is tone. *Talk about* names the subject, *talk over* means discuss thoroughly to a decision, and the literary *talk of* is now mostly a fixed phrase (*the talk of the town*). Note that ✗ *discuss about* is an error — *discuss* takes a direct object.
**Verb type:** **action** — dynamic and atelic, an activity; the progressive is completely at home (*I'm going to talk with him*, *they were talking about moving*).
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** in all three frames — the preposition is obligatory before a person or a topic (✗ *talk him*, ✗ *talk the budget*). The only transitive uses are fixed: *talk sense*, *talk shop*, *talk business*.
**Passive:** **restricted** — the prepositional passive is idiomatic when a person is affected: *he was talked to about it*, *she's been talked about all spring*. It is weak with topics: ?*moving was talked about*, where *discussed* is the natural transitive substitute.

Speak with someone, or speak about something.

| Pattern | What it means | Note |
|---|---|---|
| *talk **to*** [person] | speak to them — the default | Can imply a one-way talk or a reprimand |
| *talk **with*** [person] | speak with them, mutually | Common and neutral in American English |
| *talk **about*** [topic] | discuss the subject | *talk about the budget* |
| *talk **over*** [thing] | discuss it through to a decision | *let's talk it over* |
| *talk **of*** [thing] | literary or fixed | *the talk of the town* |
| *talk* [person] *into* / *out of* [-ing] | persuade / dissuade | *talked her into staying* |
| *discuss* [topic] | **no preposition** — ✗ *discuss about* | *discuss the budget* |

1. I need to *talk to* you about Thursday.
2. She *talked with* each family individually before the hearing.
3. They *talked about* moving for two years and then did it in a weekend.
4. He *talked* me *out of* buying it, which he was right about.
5. Let's *talk over* the numbers before we send anything.
6. Nobody wants to *talk about* the parking, and the parking is the whole problem.
7. *Talk to* your doctor before you change the dose.

**See also:** [discuss](#discuss) &middot; [argue with](#argue-with-argue-about-argue-over) &middot; [complain to](#complain-to-complain-about) &middot; [explain to](#explain-to) &middot; [think about](#think-about-think-of) &middot; [hear from](#hear-about-hear-from-hear-of)

### testify to

**Pronunciation:** /ˈtɛstəˌfaɪ tu/ &middot; *TESS-tuh-fye too* &middot; weak /ˈtɛstəˌfaɪ tə/
**Русский:** давать показания о; служить свидетельством чего-то; юр.
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** legal in the literal sense — a deposition, a hearing, a trial; the figurative sense (*the ruins testify to a wealthy past*) belongs to formal written English
**Complement:** noun phrase, **gerund** (*testified to having seen him leave*), or a wh-clause (*testifies to how much has changed*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *testified to have seen him*). A full proposition drops the preposition: *testified that he had seen him leave*.
**Pattern:** *testify to* [a fact / a quality] &middot; *testify to* [verb-ing] &middot; *testify that* [clause] &middot; *testify against* / *for* [a party] &middot; *testify about* [a topic] &middot; *testify before* [a body] &middot; *testify in* [a proceeding]
**Contrast:** **Each preposition takes a different piece of the courtroom.** *To* takes the fact you vouch for, *about* the subject matter, *against* and *for* the side, *before* the body you appear in front of, *in* the case: *she testified before the committee, for the government, about the contracts, to the authenticity of the ledgers*. Among the vouching verbs, *attest to* is the near-twin in formal writing and on signed documents, *vouch for* is the personal and spoken version, *swear to* stresses the oath, and *bear witness to* is the literary one.
**Verb type:** **action** — dynamic and performative, a speech act made under oath; in the figurative use (*the turnout testifies to how much has changed*) it shades **stative**.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** — the preposition is obligatory (✗ *testify the authenticity of the ledgers*); for a full proposition the preposition drops and a that-clause follows: *testified that he had seen him leave*.
**Passive:** **no** ordinary passive — ✗ *the authenticity of the ledgers was testified to by her*. Only the legal postmodifier survives: *the matters testified to by the witness*. Prefer the active or switch to *attest*.

State something formally as a witness — or, of a thing, be evidence of something.

1. Two employees *testified to* the accuracy of the time sheets.
2. He *testified to* having been in the building that night.
3. The condition of the roof *testifies to* years of deferred maintenance.
4. She *testified before* the committee for two hours without notes.
5. Neighbors *testified to* the family's character at sentencing.
6. The turnout *testifies to* how badly people wanted this.

**See also:** [vouch for](#vouch-for) &middot; [stipulate to](#stipulate-to) &middot; [sentence to](#sentence-to) &middot; [recuse from](#recuse-from) &middot; [to](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#to)

### thank for

**Pronunciation:** /ˈθæŋk fɔr/ &middot; *THANK for* &middot; weak /ˈθæŋk fɚ/
**Русский:** благодарить за — человек без предлога: thank you for the ride
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*thanked her for coming*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *thank you for to come*). Never a clause. The person thanked is a direct object and never takes a preposition: ✗ *thank to you*.
**Pattern:** *thank* [someone] *for* [something / verb-ing] &middot; *Thank you for* [verb-ing] &middot; *thanks to* [someone] — because of &middot; *be thankful for* [something] &middot; *no thanks to* [someone]
**Contrast:** ***Thanks to* is not the plural of *thank for*.** *Thank you for the ride* is gratitude; *Thanks to the ride, I made it* means because of it — and *thanks to* is often sarcastic (*thanks to him, we started forty minutes late*). ✗ *Thank to you* is an error in every sense. Note that *thank*, like *forgive* and *excuse*, takes the person directly, while *apologize* needs *to* — a distinction that catches learners of every background.
**Verb type:** **action** — dynamic and performative; *thank you* is the act itself, not a report of one.
**Transitivity:** **transitive** — the person is a bare direct object and is obligatory (✗ *thank to you*, ✗ *she thanked for coming*); the *for*-phrase naming the favor is optional.
**Passive:** **yes** — *she was thanked for her service at the last meeting*, *nobody was ever thanked for the extra hours*. Note that *thanks to the ride, I made it* is a different construction entirely and has no active counterpart.

Express gratitude for something.

1. *Thank* you *for* covering my shift.
2. She *thanked* every volunteer *for* showing up in that weather.
3. *Thanks for* the ride — I owe you one.
4. He never *thanked* anyone *for* anything, which people noticed.
5. *Thanks to* a rescheduled flight, we got an extra day.
6. I'm *thankful for* a landlord who answers the phone.

**See also:** [apologize to](#apologize-to-apologize-for) &middot; [congratulate on](#congratulate-on) &middot; [forgive for](#forgive-for) &middot; [excuse for](#excuse-for) &middot; [pay for](19-phrasal-m-r.md#pay-for)

### think about, think of

**Pronunciation:** /ˈθɪŋk əˌbaʊt/ &middot; *THINK uh-bowt* &middot; weak /ˈθɪŋk əbət/
**Русский:** думать — about обдумывать, of вспоминать или приходить на ум
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** Both take a noun phrase, a **gerund** (*thinking about moving*, *thought of selling*), or a wh-clause. **Neither takes a to-infinitive** (✗ *think about to move*) and neither takes a that-clause — for a clause, drop the preposition: *think that it's a mistake*. Bare *think* also takes an adjective complement in fixed frames: *think it unwise*.
**Pattern:** *think about* [something / verb-ing] &middot; *think of* [something / verb-ing] &middot; *think of* [a name, an idea] — recall or invent &middot; *think* [something] *of* [someone] — hold an opinion &middot; *think that* [clause] &middot; *think it over* &middot; *think highly of* [someone]
**Contrast:** ***Think about* is the process; *think of* is the flash.** *I've been thinking about it all week* is deliberation; *I can't think of her name* is retrieval; *who thought of that?* is invention. They overlap freely for plans (*thinking about* / *of moving*), but they part ways completely in the opinion frames: *What do you think of it?* asks for a verdict, and *think highly of*, *think little of*, and *think the world of* take only *of*. ✗ *Think to do something* is not English except in the narrow *he never thought to ask*, meaning it didn't occur to him.
**Verb type:** **both** — **action** when it means deliberate or retrieve (*I've been thinking about it all week*), **stative** when it means hold an opinion (*I think highly of her*, ✗ *I am thinking highly of her*).
**Transitivity:** **both** — intransitive with *about* / *of* plus a noun, gerund, or wh-clause; transitive when the preposition drops before a clause (*think that it's a mistake*) or in the fixed *think it unwise*.
**Passive:** **restricted** — no ordinary passive: ✗ *the offer was thought about by everyone*. But fixed evaluative passives with *of* are fully idiomatic: *she is well thought of in the department*, *it was thought of as a formality*.

Consider something, or have something come to mind.

| Pattern | What it means | Example |
|---|---|---|
| *think **about*** [thing] | consider, mull over | *I'm thinking about it.* |
| *think **of*** [thing] | have it come to mind; recall | *I can't think of his name.* |
| *think **of*** [an idea] | invent, come up with | *Who thought of that?* |
| *What do you think **of*** it? | asking for a verdict | *What did you think of the film?* |
| *think highly / little **of*** | hold an opinion of someone | *She thinks highly of him.* |
| *think **that*** [clause] | state a belief — **no preposition** | *I think it's a mistake.* |
| *think **to** do* | only: it didn't occur to him | *Nobody thought to ask.* |

1. I've been *thinking about* the offer all week.
2. I can't *think of* her married name to save my life.
3. Who *thought of* putting the printer in the stairwell?
4. What did you *think of* the second half?
5. They're *thinking about* selling and moving closer to the grandkids.
6. She *thinks highly of* him, which is more than he deserves.
7. He never *thought to* check whether the office was open.

**See also:** [worry about](#worry-about) &middot; [care about](#care-about-care-for) &middot; [forget about](16-phrasal-d-f.md#forget-about) &middot; [occur to](#occur-to) &middot; [dream of](#dream-about-dream-of) &middot; [talk about](#talk-to-talk-with-talk-about)

### touch on

**Pronunciation:** /ˈtʌtʃ ɑn/ &middot; *TUCH on* &middot; weak /ˈtʌtʃ ən/
**Русский:** затрагивать (тему) — упоминать вскользь
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral; *touch upon* is formal and academic
**Complement:** noun phrase, **gerund** (*touched on selling the building*), or a wh-clause (*touched on why the numbers moved*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *touched on to sell the building*).
**Pattern:** *touch on* [a topic] &middot; *touch briefly on* [a topic] &middot; *touch on* [verb-ing] &middot; *touch on* [wh-clause]
**Variants:** *touch upon* — identical in meaning, more formal; standard in academic and legal prose
**Contrast:** **This *on* is fixed, not a movable particle:** ✗ *touch the subject on*. And plain transitive *touch* is physical and takes no preposition, so *he touched the subject* and *he touched on the subject* are different sentences entirely. What varies among the neighbors is depth: *touch on* is a passing mention, *get into* and *go into* are thorough, *cover* is complete, *dwell on* stays too long. In a second sense *touch on* means come close to a quality (*a politeness touching on contempt*), where the neighbor is *verge on*.
**Verb type:** **action** — dynamic and telic, a brief mention within a longer act of speaking; the second sense (*a politeness touching on contempt*) is **stative** and describes a quality.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** — *on* is a fixed preposition, not a movable particle (✗ *touch the subject on*), and it is obligatory: bare transitive *he touched the subject* is a physical act and a different sentence.
**Passive:** **yes** — the prepositional passive is natural and common in this idiom: *the pension question was touched on only briefly*, *several issues were touched on and none resolved*.

Mention a subject briefly in passing.

1. The report *touches on* staffing but never gives a number.
2. She *touched on* the lawsuit for about thirty seconds and moved on.
3. His remarks *touched upon* the constitutional question without resolving it.
4. Nobody at the meeting *touched on* what happens if the grant lapses.
5. The chapter *touches on* being raised by grandparents.
6. Her politeness *touched on* the theatrical.

**See also:** [comment on](#comment-on) &middot; [refer to](#refer-to) &middot; [talk about](#talk-to-talk-with-talk-about) &middot; [about](../../06-prepositions/catalog/10-topic-reference-role.md#about) &middot; [on](../../06-prepositions/catalog/01-core-in-on-at.md#on)

### translate into

**Pronunciation:** /trænzˈleɪt ˈɪntu/ &middot; *tranz-LATE in-too* &middot; weak /trænzˈleɪt ˈɪntə/
**Русский:** переводить на (язык); выливаться во что-то — предлог into, не ✗ to
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase naming the target language or result — **no gerund, no clause**. The source takes *from*: *translated from the Portuguese*. The figurative sense takes the same *into* and a noun phrase: *translates into savings*.
**Pattern:** *translate* [something] *into* [a language] &middot; *translate* [something] *from* [a language] *into* [another] &middot; [something] *translate into* [a result] &middot; *translate as* [a rendering] &middot; *be translated by* [a translator]
**Contrast:** ***Into* names the target, *from* the source, and neither can be swapped for *to*:** ✗ *translated to Spanish* is an error in careful writing, though it is heard. Note the very common figurative use in American business and sports writing — *higher volume translates into lower margins*, *speed doesn't always translate into wins* — which is a full-fledged sense of the verb, not a metaphor anyone notices anymore. *Translate as* introduces the rendering itself: *the phrase translates roughly as "leave it alone."*
**Verb type:** **both** — **action** in the language sense (*translated from the Portuguese*), **stative-relational** in the figurative one (*higher volume translates into lower margins*), where the progressive is odd and the claim is a generalization.
**Transitivity:** **both** — transitive in the language sense (*translate the novel into Spanish*), intransitive in the figurative one (*speed doesn't always translate into wins*) and in the middle voice (*the phrase translates roughly as leave it alone*).
**Passive:** **restricted** — routine in the language sense: *the novel was translated into eleven languages*, *the report was translated from the Portuguese*. None in the figurative sense: ✗ *lower margins are translated into by higher volume*.

Put something into another language, or turn into another form.

1. The book was *translated into* eleven languages before it sold well in English.
2. She *translates* technical manuals *from* German *into* English.
3. None of that *translates into* actual policy.
4. The idiom *translates* roughly *as* "don't borrow trouble."
5. A four percent bump in traffic *translated into* almost nothing on the bottom line.
6. His stage presence never quite *translated into* film.

**See also:** [result in](#result-in-result-from) &middot; [lead to](18-phrasal-h-l.md#lead-to) &middot; [refer to](#refer-to) &middot; [adapt to](#adapt-to) &middot; [into](../../06-prepositions/catalog/04-movement-direction.md#into)

### turn on

**Pronunciation:** /ˈtɜrn ɑn/ &middot; *TURN on* &middot; weak /ˈtɜrn ən/
**Русский:** зависеть от (решающего обстоятельства); наброситься на кого-то
**Type:** verb + preposition — in the two senses below only &middot; **Register:** the *turn on a question* sense is formal and analytic, at home in legal and editorial writing; the *turn on someone* sense is neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase, **gerund** (*it all turns on proving intent*), or a wh-clause (*it turns on whether the email was sent*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *turns on to prove intent*).
**Pattern:** [a case, an argument, an outcome] *turn on* [a fact or a question] &middot; [a person or animal] *turn on* [someone] &middot; *turn on* [verb-ing] &middot; *turn on* [wh-clause]
**Contrast:** **Two different constructions share these two words, and the test is whether the object can move.** *Turn on the light* → *turn the light on* → *turn it on*: the object slides, so *on* is a **particle**, the pair is a phrasal verb, and it belongs in the discussion of phrasal verbs rather than in this file. *The case turns on one question* → ✗ *turns one question on*: the object cannot move, so *on* is a fixed **preposition** and the pattern belongs here. The same test separates two more: *turned **on** him* means attacked him, *turned him **on*** means aroused him — word order carries the whole difference. In the analytic sense the near-synonyms are *hinge on*, *depend on*, and *rest on*, of which *turn on* is the most legal.
**Verb type:** **stative** in this pattern — *the case turns on one question* states a dependency, not an act, and the progressive is marked. This is the hinge sense; the switch sense (*turn on the light*) is a dynamic phrasal verb with a movable particle and a different pattern.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** — the test is that the object cannot move: *the case turns on one question* but ✗ *turns one question on*, which is exactly what marks *on* as a preposition here rather than a particle.
**Passive:** **no** in the hinge sense — ✗ *one question is turned on by the case*. The switch sense passivizes freely (*the lights were turned on at six*), and the attack sense too (*he was turned on by the people he trusted*) — three constructions, one spelling.

Depend on one decisive point, or suddenly attack someone.

1. The whole appeal *turns on* a single sentence in the contract.
2. It *turns on* whether she knew the account was overdrawn.
3. The dog *turned on* its handler without warning.
4. Half the neighborhood *turned on* him after the vote.
5. Everything *turns on* getting the inspection done this week.
6. Elections in that district *turn on* turnout, not persuasion.

**See also:** [depend on](#depend-on) &middot; [rest on](#rest-on) &middot; [rely on](#rely-on) &middot; [turn to](#turn-to) &middot; [on](../../06-prepositions/catalog/01-core-in-on-at.md#on)

### turn to

**Pronunciation:** /ˈtɜrn tu/ &middot; *TURN too* &middot; weak /ˈtɜrn tə/
**Русский:** обращаться к (за помощью); приниматься за что-то
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase — a person, an occupation, a substance, a page, a topic — or a **gerund** (*turned to selling insurance*). **Never a to-infinitive** in this pattern: *he turned to write* is a different sentence, in which *to write* is a purpose infinitive and he physically turned around.
