**Pattern:** *turn to* [someone] *for* [help] &middot; *turn to* [an occupation, a habit, a substance] &middot; *turn to* [verb-ing] &middot; [a conversation] *turn to* [a topic] &middot; *turn to* [page 214]
**Contrast:** ***Turn to* someone seeks help; *turn on* someone attacks them** — one letter, opposite meanings. Around them: *turn into* is transformation (*turned into a decent manager*), *turn against* is a change of allegiance (*the party turned against him*), and *turn down* is a separable particle (*turned the offer down*, *turned it down*), which is why it is a phrasal verb and this is not. Watch the purpose-infinitive trap: *he turned to writing* made writing his work, *he turned to write* only says he swiveled in his chair.
**Verb type:** **action** — dynamic and telic, an act of recourse (*he turned to selling insurance*, *she turned to her sister for help*).
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** — *to* is obligatory (✗ *turn her sister for help*). Watch the purpose infinitive: *he turned to write* is a different sentence in which he swiveled around.
**Passive:** **no** in ordinary use — ✗ *her sister was turned to for help* is stiff at best; the prepositional passive survives mainly as a postmodifier (*the person most often turned to in a crisis*). Use *consult* or *ask*, which are transitive.

Go to someone or something for help, or take something up.

1. When the second round of layoffs hit, she *turned to* her sister.
2. He *turned to* teaching after fifteen years in restaurants.
3. The conversation *turned to* money about ten minutes in.
4. *Turn to* page 214 and read the second paragraph aloud.
5. A lot of people in that plant *turned to* painkillers.
6. Who do you *turn to* when the person you'd normally ask is the problem?

**See also:** [turn on](#turn-on) &middot; [resort to](#resort-to) &middot; [refer to](#refer-to) &middot; [rely on](#rely-on) &middot; [to](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#to)

### vary with

**Pronunciation:** /ˈvɛri wɪθ/ &middot; *VAIR-ee with* &middot; weak /ˈvɛri wəθ/
**Русский:** меняться в зависимости от — изменяться вместе с чем-то
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral; heavily used in technical, scientific, and consumer writing (*results may vary*)
**Complement:** noun phrase naming what the thing moves in step with — a season, a temperature, an income. **No gerund** (✗ *varies with raising the temperature*; say *varies with temperature*), no infinitive, no clause.
**Pattern:** [X] *vary with* [Y] &middot; *vary by* [a category] &middot; *vary from* [X] *to* [Y] &middot; *vary in* [a dimension] &middot; *vary from* [a norm]
**Contrast:** **Five prepositions carve five different claims.** *With* names what it moves in step with (*demand varies with the weather*); *by* names the categories it differs across (*prices vary by state*); *in* names the dimension of difference (*the panels vary in thickness*); *from … to* gives the range (*varies from ten to forty minutes*); and *vary from* alone means depart from a standard (*varies from the specification*). Against neighbors, *depend on* claims determination rather than covariation, *correlate with* is the statistical and symmetric version, and *fluctuate* takes no complement at all.
**Verb type:** **stative-relational** — it asserts covariation, not an event (*demand varies with the weather*); the progressive appears only for observed fluctuation (*the readings are varying*).
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** in this pattern — no object (✗ *vary the demand with the weather* changes verbs). The separate transitive *vary* means deliberately alter: *vary the routine*.
**Passive:** **no** — intransitive and stative: ✗ *the weather is varied with by demand*. Only the deliberate transitive passivizes: *the routine was varied to keep them alert*.

Change in step with something else.

1. The dosage *varies with* body weight.
2. Turnout *varies with* the weather more than anyone likes to admit.
3. Shipping cost *varies with* distance and *by* carrier.
4. Interest on the note *varies with* the prime rate.
5. Yields *vary with* soil temperature at planting.
6. How long an appeal takes *varies with* the circuit.

**See also:** [depend on](#depend-on) &middot; [differ from](#differ-from) &middot; [result from](#result-in-result-from) &middot; [with](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#with)

### vest in

**Pronunciation:** /ˈvɛst ɪn/ &middot; *VEST in* &middot; weak /ˈvɛst ən/
**Русский:** закреплять (власть, право, титул) за кем-то — юр./офиц.
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** legal — constitutions, trusts, corporate charters, and equity grants; an American meets it in the phrase *all legislative powers herein granted shall be vested in a Congress*, not in conversation
**Complement:** noun phrase naming the person or body that receives the power, the title, or the right. **No gerund** (✗ *vested in managing the fund*), no infinitive, no clause. The passive is the standard form: *authority is vested in the board*.
**Pattern:** [a power / a right / title] *be vested in* [someone] &middot; *vest* [a power] *in* [someone] &middot; *vest* [someone] *with* [a power] &middot; [a right] *vest* — intransitive
**Contrast:** **Two frames say the same thing with the arguments swapped:** *vest authority in the board* and *vest the board with authority*, exactly like *provide X to Y* against *provide Y with X*. Watch the intransitive: in *the options vest in four years*, *in* is the ordinary time preposition and nothing is receiving anything — that sentence answers *when*, not *who*, and is not this pattern. Among neighbors, *confer on* grants, *devolve on* is power passing downward or by default, and *reside in* and *rest with* say where authority already sits rather than how it got there. Do not read across to *invest in*, which in ordinary American English means putting money into something.
**Verb type:** **action** — dynamic and telic in origin, a legal act of conferring; the resulting *authority is vested in the board* reads as a standing state, which is why the passive dominates.
**Transitivity:** **transitive** — two frames with the arguments swapped: *vest authority in the board* and *vest the board with authority*. The intransitive *the options vest in four years* is the ordinary time preposition and not this pattern.
**Passive:** **yes**, and it is the standard form: *authority to approve waivers is vested in the dean's office*, *the power was vested in a single commissioner*. The active is comparatively rare outside statutes.

Place a power, a right, or a title formally in someone's hands.

1. All legislative powers herein granted are *vested in* a Congress of the United States.
2. Title to the property *vested in* the trust on the date of the transfer.
3. The charter *vests* final authority *in* the board of trustees.
4. Discretion to waive the penalty is *vested in* the commissioner.
5. The statute *vests* the agency *with* rulemaking power.
6. On his death, the remainder *vested in* his two daughters.

**See also:** [rest with](#rest-with) &middot; [reside in](#reside-in) &middot; [invest in](#invest-in) &middot; [belong to](#belong-to) &middot; [in](../../06-prepositions/catalog/01-core-in-on-at.md#in)

### vote for, vote against

**Pronunciation:** /ˈvoʊt fɔr/ &middot; *VOHT for* &middot; weak /ˈvoʊt fɚ/
**Русский:** голосовать за (for) или против (against)
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase naming a candidate or measure — **no gerund** (✗ *vote for raising the fee*; say *vote to raise the fee* or *vote for the increase*). *Vote* also takes a bare **to-infinitive** with no preposition (*voted to adjourn*) and a that-clause in formal minutes (*voted that the matter be tabled*).
**Pattern:** *vote for* [a candidate, a measure] &middot; *vote against* [something] &middot; *vote on* [a question] &middot; *vote to* [verb] &middot; *vote in* [an election] &middot; *vote* [someone] *in* / *out*
**Contrast:** **Four prepositions and an infinitive, each with its own job.** *Vote **for*** and *vote **against*** take sides; *vote **on*** names the question being decided, not the side (*the council votes on the ordinance Tuesday*); *vote **in*** names the election; and *vote **to** do* names the action decided. ✗ *vote to a candidate* is an error. Note the American particles: *vote someone in*, *vote someone out*, *vote down* a measure — all separable and all preposition-free.
**Verb type:** **action** — dynamic and telic, a single countable act (*every commissioner voted against the rezoning*).
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** in this pattern — the preposition is obligatory before a candidate or measure (✗ *vote the levy*), though *vote* also takes a bare to-infinitive (*voted to adjourn*) and a formal that-clause (*voted that the matter be tabled*).
**Passive:** **restricted** — ✗ *the bill was voted for by the council* is not idiomatic. The preposition-free particle frames do passivize: *the measure was voted down*, *two incumbents were voted out*. For the prepositional sense use *approve* or *reject*.

Express a choice in an election or a formal decision.

1. She *voted for* the bond measure and against everything else on the ballot.
2. Two members *voted against* the settlement and one abstained.
3. The board *votes on* the budget at the March meeting.
4. They *voted to* adjourn before anyone could raise it again.
5. He's never once *voted in* a primary.
6. The district *voted* him *out* after a single term.

**See also:** [decide on](#decide-on-decide-against) &middot; [agree on](#agree-with-agree-to-agree-on) &middot; [choose between](#choose-between) &middot; [qualify for](#qualify-for) &middot; [object to](#object-to)

### vouch for

**Pronunciation:** /ˈvaʊtʃ fɔr/ &middot; *VOWCH for* &middot; weak /ˈvaʊtʃ fər/
**Русский:** ручаться за — подтверждать надёжность по личному знанию
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral — everyday spoken English for people, and ordinary formal English for facts
**Complement:** noun phrase — a person, a fact, a quality (*vouch for the accuracy of the transcript*). A **gerund** is grammatical but stiff (*vouch for his having been there*; most speakers say *vouch for that*), and there is **no to-infinitive** (✗ *vouch for to be accurate*). The preposition is obligatory: ✗ *vouch someone*. A that-clause is marginal — say *I can vouch for the fact that…* or switch to *attest* or *swear*.
**Pattern:** *vouch for* [a person] &middot; *vouch for* [a fact / a quality] &middot; *can't vouch for* [something]
**Contrast:** ***Vouch for* puts your own credit behind someone or something.** *Testify to* does the same under oath, *attest to* does it in writing and often with a signature, and *guarantee* promises a result and takes no preposition at all (*guarantee the work*, ✗ *guarantee for the work*). *Answer for* is what comes afterward, when the thing you vouched for goes wrong.
**Verb type:** **action** — dynamic and performative, putting your own credit behind someone; *I can vouch for that* is the modal use of the same act.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** — *for* is obligatory: ✗ *vouch someone*, ✗ *vouch the accuracy of the transcript*. Compare *guarantee*, which is transitive and takes no preposition.
**Passive:** **yes**, restricted to the prepositional passive, which is fully idiomatic here — *he was vouched for by two colleagues*, *every applicant has to be vouched for*.

Say from your own knowledge that someone or something can be trusted.

1. I've known her twenty years and I'll *vouch for* her.
2. Nobody at the agency would *vouch for* the numbers in the draft.
3. I can *vouch for* the coffee; I can't *vouch for* the parking.
4. Two colleagues *vouched for* his whereabouts that evening.
5. The notary *vouched for* the identity of both signers.
6. He *vouched for* the contractor, and then spent a year regretting it.

**See also:** [testify to](#testify-to) &middot; [stand up for](20-phrasal-s.md#stand-up-for) &middot; [count on](#count-on) &middot; [rely on](#rely-on) &middot; [for](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#for)

### wait for, wait on

**Pronunciation:** /ˈweɪt fɔr/ &middot; *WAYT for* &middot; weak /ˈweɪt fɚ/
**Русский:** ждать (for); обслуживать (on); wait on в значении ждать — разг., амер.
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** *wait for* is neutral; *wait on* meaning await is informal and regional in American English
**Complement:** *for* takes a noun phrase, or a **person plus a to-infinitive** (*wait for her to finish* — this is the one place an infinitive is correct in this pattern). **A gerund after *for* is impossible**: ✗ *wait for finishing*. *On* takes a person: *wait on tables* in the serving sense. *Await* is transitive and takes no preposition at all.
**Pattern:** *wait for* [someone / something] &middot; *wait for* [someone] *to* [verb] &middot; *wait to* [verb] &middot; *wait on* [a customer, a table] — serve &middot; *wait on* [something] — informal for *wait for* &middot; *await* [something] — formal, no preposition &middot; *can't wait to* [verb]
**Contrast:** ***Wait on* has two lives in American English.** The standard sense is serving: *she waited on tables through college*, *the clerk will wait on you next*. The other — *I'm waiting on the bus* meaning waiting for it — began as Southern and Midland American usage, has spread nationally in speech and in business writing (*waiting on approval*), and is still flagged in formal edited prose, where *wait for* is safe. Note *await*, which takes a direct object and no preposition: *await further instructions*, ✗ *await for instructions*. And *wait for someone **to** do something* is one of the very few prepositional patterns that takes an infinitive, licensed by the person in between.
**Verb type:** **action** — dynamic and atelic, an activity that fills time; the progressive is its natural home (*I'm waiting for the inspector*, *she was waiting on tables*).
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** — the preposition is obligatory (✗ *wait the bus*), which is what separates it from *await*, transitive and preposition-free: *await further instructions*, ✗ *await for instructions*.
**Passive:** **restricted** — the serving sense passivizes naturally: *the customers were waited on promptly*, *you'll be waited on next*. The waiting sense does not: ✗ *the bus was waited for*, ✗ *approval is being waited on*.

Stay where you are, or delay acting, until something happens.

| Pattern | What it means | Register |
|---|---|---|
| *wait **for*** [thing] | await it | Standard everywhere |
| *wait **for*** [person] *to* [verb] | await their action — **infinitive** | Standard |
| *wait **on*** [person] | serve them | Standard |
| *wait **on*** [thing] | wait for it | Originally Southern/Midland U.S.; common in speech, avoided in formal prose |
| *wait **to** do* | delay acting | Standard |
| *await* [thing] | **no preposition** | Formal |

1. We *waited for* almost an hour and nobody ever came out.
2. She's *waiting for* the inspector to sign off.
3. He *waited on* tables at a diner on Route 9 for six years.
4. They're still *waiting on* approval from the county.
5. Don't *wait for* perfect; send the draft.
6. I can't *wait to* see the finished porch.
7. The committee *awaits* the final report.

**See also:** [look forward to](#look-forward-to) &middot; [hope for](#hope-for) &middot; [count on](#count-on) &middot; [search for](#search-for) &middot; [depend on](#depend-on)

### warn about, warn against

**Pronunciation:** /ˈwɔrn əˌbaʊt/ &middot; *WORN uh-bowt* &middot; weak /ˈwɔrn əbət/
**Русский:** предупреждать — about об опасности, against чтобы чего-то не делали
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** *about* and *of* take a noun phrase naming a danger — *of* is the more formal of the two. *against* takes a noun phrase or a **gerund** and means advise not to (*warned against signing anything*) — **never a to-infinitive after *against***. *Warn* separately takes a person plus a to-infinitive (*warned her to leave*, *warned him not to sign*) and a that-clause (*warned that the road was flooded*), neither with a preposition.
**Pattern:** *warn* [someone] *about* [a danger] &middot; *warn* [someone] *of* [a danger] — formal &middot; *warn* [someone] *against* [something / verb-ing] &middot; *warn* [someone] *to* / *not to* [verb] &middot; *warn that* [clause] &middot; *warn* [someone] *off*
**Contrast:** ***About* and *of* describe the danger; *against* prescribes avoiding it.** *He warned us about the ice* tells you the ice is there; *he warned us against driving* tells you not to. The two often coexist: *warned residents about the surge and against staying*. Note that *warn* takes the person directly — ✗ *warn to someone* — and that the negative infinitive is *warned her **not to** sign*, never ✗ *warned her to not sign* in careful prose. The noun keeps *about* / *of*: *a warning about the ice*, *a warning of severe weather*.
**Verb type:** **action** — dynamic and performative, a speech act; *about* and *of* describe a danger, *against* prescribes avoiding it.
**Transitivity:** **transitive** — the person warned is a bare direct object (✗ *warn to someone*), and the verb also takes a person plus a to-infinitive (*warned her not to sign*) or a that-clause (*warned that the road was flooded*), both preposition-free.
**Passive:** **yes** — *residents were warned about the surge*, *she was warned against signing anything*, *drivers were warned that the road was flooded*. The passive is at least as common as the active in news copy.

Tell someone about a danger, or tell them not to do something.

1. The ranger *warned* us *about* the washout on the lower trail.
2. Her attorney *warned* her *against* saying anything on the record.
3. The label *warns of* a risk that affects about one user in fifty thousand.
4. He *warned* everyone *to* leave before the tide turned.
5. Two inspectors had *warned about* the wiring the previous spring.
6. The forecast *warns of* freezing rain after midnight.

**See also:** [remind of](#remind-of-remind-about) &middot; [discourage from](#discourage-from) &middot; [protect from](#protect-from-protect-against) &middot; [explain to](#explain-to)

### wish for

**Pronunciation:** /ˈwɪʃ fɔr/ &middot; *WISH for* &middot; weak /ˈwɪʃ fɚ/
**Русский:** желать чего-то — хотеть, часто маловероятного
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** *for* takes a noun phrase — **no gerund and no to-infinitive after the preposition** (✗ *wish for going*, ✗ *wish for to go*). *Wish* separately takes a bare **to-infinitive** (*wish to speak with the manager* — formal) and a **past-tense clause** for unreal situations: *I wish I had more time*, *I wish she were here* — note the subjunctive *were*.
**Pattern:** *wish for* [a noun phrase] &middot; *wish to* [verb] — formal &middot; *wish* [someone] [something] &middot; *wish* [clause with past tense] &middot; *wish* [someone] *well* &middot; *as you wish*
**Contrast:** **The three frames are not interchangeable.** *Wish **for** a raise* is longing; *wish **to** speak* is a formal way of saying want; *I wish I **had** a raise* is counterfactual, and the past tense is doing the work rather than any preposition. ✗ *I wish that I have more time* is wrong — it must be *had*. The double-object frame is fixed and preposition-free: *wish you luck*, *wish him well*, *wish them a good trip*. And note the proverb, which is the pattern's most quoted use: *be careful what you wish for*.
**Verb type:** **stative** — a mental state of longing, not an act; the progressive is marked (?*I'm wishing for a raise*) outside seasonal formulas.
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** with *for* (✗ *wish for going*, ✗ *wish for to go*). Separately **transitive** with a bare infinitive (*wish to speak with the manager*) or a counterfactual past clause (*I wish she were here*), and **ditransitive** in the fixed frames *wish you luck*, *wish him well*.
**Passive:** **no** — stative and intransitive here: ✗ *a raise was wished for by everyone*. Only the attributive participle survives: *the long wished-for reunion*. The double-object frame does passivize: *they were wished a good trip*.

Want something, often something unlikely.

1. Everyone in the district was *wishing for* one more snow day.
2. Be careful what you *wish for*.
3. She *wished for* a quiet Christmas and got four houseguests.
4. I *wish to* register a complaint about the fee schedule.
5. I *wish* I *had* asked more questions before signing.
6. They *wished* him *well* and never called again.

**See also:** [hope for](#hope-for) &middot; [long for](#long-for) &middot; [dream of](#dream-about-dream-of) &middot; [ask for](#ask-for-ask-about)

### work on, work for, work with

**Pronunciation:** /ˈwɜrk ɑn/ &middot; *WURK on* &middot; weak /ˈwɜrk ən/
**Русский:** работать — on над чем-то, for на кого-то, with с кем-то
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** *on* takes a noun phrase or a **gerund** (*working on getting the permit*) — never a to-infinitive. *for* takes an employer, a client, or a cause. *with* takes a colleague, a client, or a material. *at* takes a place of work or an effort; *in* takes a field or an industry. None of them takes a clause.
**Pattern:** *work on* [a project / verb-ing] &middot; *work for* [an employer / a cause] &middot; *work with* [a person / a material] &middot; *work at* [a place / something requiring effort] &middot; *work in* [a field, an industry] &middot; *work as* [a role] &middot; *work out* [a solution]
**Contrast:** **Six prepositions, and each answers a different question.** *Work **on*** = what you are building or improving (*working on the second draft*). *Work **for*** = who pays you (*works for the county*). *Work **with*** = who or what is alongside you (*works with kids*, *works with mahogany*). *Work **at*** = where, or how hard (*works at the hospital*, *has to work at it*). *Work **in*** = which field (*works in public health*). *Work **as*** = which role (*works as a paralegal*). Note that *work with* a person and *work for* a person are very different claims about a hierarchy, and people are sensitive about which one you use.
**Verb type:** **action** — dynamic and atelic in every frame; the progressive is standard (*she's working on the second draft*, *he's been working for the county since 2011*).
**Transitivity:** **intransitive** — each preposition is obligatory (✗ *work the second draft* in this sense, ✗ *work the county*). The unrelated transitives *work the room*, *work the counter*, *work the dough* are a different pattern.
**Passive:** **restricted** — *work on* takes the prepositional passive well: *the second draft is being worked on*, *the roof has been worked on twice*. The others do not: ✗ *the county is worked for by her*, ✗ *the kids are worked with*.

Do a job, or apply effort to something.

1. She's been *working on* the same manuscript for six years.
2. He *works for* a firm that does nothing but municipal water.
3. I've *worked with* her on two projects and would again.
4. They're *working on* getting the variance before the weather turns.
5. She *works in* public health and *at* a clinic three days a week.
6. Marriage takes work, and he was willing to *work at* it.

**See also:** [concentrate on](#concentrate-on) &middot; [specialize in](#specialize-in) &middot; [consult with](#consult-with) &middot; [apply for](#apply-for-apply-to) &middot; [participate in](#participate-in) &middot; [with](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#with)

### worry about

**Pronunciation:** /ˈwɜri əˌbaʊt/ &middot; *WUR-ee uh-bowt* &middot; weak /ˈwɜri əbət/
**Русский:** беспокоиться о — тревожиться из-за чего-то
**Type:** verb + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase, **gerund** (*worried about missing the flight*), or wh-clause — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *worry about to miss it*). *Worry* also takes a that-clause with no preposition: *worried that the check would bounce*. Reflexive *worry yourself* and transitive *it worries me* need no preposition either.
**Pattern:** *worry about* [something / verb-ing / wh-clause] &middot; *be worried about* [something] &middot; *be worried by* [something] &middot; *worry that* [clause] &middot; [something] *worry* [someone] &middot; *Don't worry about it.*
**Contrast:** ***Worried about* is your state; *worried by* is the thing's effect on you.** *I'm worried about the numbers* describes ongoing anxiety you carry; *I'm worried by the numbers* names the numbers as what disturbed you, and is closer to *troubled by* or *alarmed by*. Both are correct and *about* is far more common. *Worry **for*** is the marginal third member — it survives in *I worry for her*, which sounds concerned on someone's behalf rather than anxious, and it will not substitute for *about* anywhere else: ✗ *I worry for the deadline*. ✗ *Worry of* is not English at all. The fixed dismissal is *don't worry about it*.
**Verb type:** **both** — **stative** in *I'm worried about the numbers*, where *worried* is an adjective describing a state; **action** in the habitual *she worries about her mother driving at night*, and in the causative *it worries me*.
**Transitivity:** **both** — intransitive with *about* plus a noun, gerund, or wh-clause, and with a preposition-free that-clause (*worried that the check would bounce*); transitive when the trouble is the subject: *the estimate worries me*, *don't worry yourself*.
**Passive:** **yes** for the causative — *I'm worried by how quickly the estimate changed*, closer to *troubled by*. But *be worried about* is an adjectival state, not a passive: it has no active counterpart ✗ *the numbers worry me about*.

Feel anxious about something.

| Pattern | What it means | Example |
|---|---|---|
| *worry **about*** [thing] | carry ongoing anxiety about it | *She worries about money.* |
| *be worried **about*** [thing] | be in that state now | *I'm worried about the numbers.* |
| *be worried **by*** [thing] | the thing is what disturbed you | *I'm worried by how fast it changed.* |
| *worry **for*** [person] | be concerned on their behalf — marginal | *I worry for her.* |
| *worry **that*** [clause] | **no preposition** | *They worried that it would rain.* |
| [thing] *worry* [person] | transitive — **no preposition** | *The cough worries me.* |

1. She *worries about* her mother driving at night.
2. Nobody at that level *worries about* the parking.
3. He's *worried about* missing the connection in Atlanta.
4. I'm *worried by* how quickly the estimate changed.
5. They *worried that* the inspection would turn up something expensive.
6. Don't *worry about* the dishes; I'll get them.

**See also:** [care about](#care-about-care-for) &middot; [think about](#think-about-think-of) &middot; [complain about](#complain-to-complain-about) &middot; [hope for](#hope-for) &middot; [forget about](16-phrasal-d-f.md#forget-about)

## Verbs that take no preposition at all

The other half of this file. Every verb below takes a **direct object with nothing in front of it**, and every one of them attracts a preposition that does not belong there — usually because the corresponding verb in Spanish, French, Portuguese, Italian, Russian, Hindi, or Arabic does take one, or because the English **noun** takes one and the verb gets tarred with it. *A discussion **about** the budget* is correct and ✗ *discuss about the budget* is not; *an approach **to** the problem* is correct and ✗ *approach to the problem* is not. Learn these as a set, because they fail as a set.

Several of them do have a real prepositional twin with a different meaning — *enter into an agreement*, *attend to a patient*, *answer for a decision*, *reach for a glass*. Those are noted in each entry, and they are the reason the errors survive: the wrong version sounds like something you have heard.

### answer

**Pronunciation:** /ˈænsɚ/ &middot; *AN-ser* &middot; weak /ˈænsɚ/
**Русский:** отвечать на что-то — прямое дополнение без предлога: answer the question
**Type:** verb + direct object (no preposition) &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** a direct object naming the question, the phone, the door, or the person — **no preposition** in that sense: ✗ *answer to the question*, ✗ *answer to my email*. The genuine *answer to* means be accountable to a person, and *answer for* means take responsibility for a thing — both are real, and both mean something else.
**Pattern:** *answer* [a question, the phone, the door, a letter] &middot; *answer* [someone] &middot; *answer to* [a boss, a board] — be accountable &middot; *answer for* [one's conduct] &middot; *answer back* &middot; *the answer to* [a question] — the noun takes *to*
**Contrast:** ***Answer to the question* is wrong for replying, and right for something else.** *He answered the question* is a reply; *He answers to the board* is a reporting line; *He'll answer for that decision* is accountability. Meanwhile the **noun** takes *to* — *the answer **to** question 4* — which is exactly why the verb attracts it. Also correct: *reply **to*** and *respond **to*** both take the preposition, so *reply to my email* and *respond to my email* are right while ✗ *answer to my email* is not.
**Verb type:** **action** — dynamic and telic (*nobody answered my question*). The *answer to* sense — be accountable — is **stative**: *the inspector general answers to Congress*, a standing reporting line rather than an act.
**Transitivity:** **both** — transitive with no preposition in the reply sense (*answer the question*, *answer the phone*, ✗ *answer to my email*), intransitive when the object is understood (*nobody answered*), and intransitive with *to* / *for* in the accountability senses.
**Passive:** **yes** in the transitive sense — *every email was answered the same day*, *my question was never answered*. No passive of the accountability senses: ✗ *Congress is answered to by the inspector general*.

Reply to something, or be accountable to someone.

1. Nobody *answered* my question, so I asked it again.
2. *Answer* the phone; it's been ringing all morning.
3. She *answered* every email the same day, which nobody asked her to do.
4. The inspector general *answers to* Congress, not to the agency.
5. Somebody is going to have to *answer for* the missing invoices.
6. The *answer to* the third question is on the back page.

**See also:** [react to](#react-to) &middot; [explain to](#explain-to) &middot; [refer to](#refer-to)

### approach

**Pronunciation:** /əˈproʊtʃ/ &middot; *uh-PROHCH* &middot; weak /əˈproʊtʃ/
**Русский:** подходить к; приступать к — без предлога: approach the house
**Type:** verb + direct object (no preposition) &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** a direct object naming a place, a person, a number, or a problem — **no preposition**: ✗ *approach to the intersection*, ✗ *approach to the problem* as a verb. The **noun** takes *to* (*a new approach to the problem*), and that is where the error comes from.
**Pattern:** *approach* [a place, a person, a figure, a problem] &middot; *approach* [someone] *about* [a matter] &middot; *an approach to* [a problem] — the noun &middot; *approach* (intransitive): *winter is approaching*
**Contrast:** **The noun takes *to*; the verb takes nothing.** *Her approach **to** the problem was unusual* is correct, and ✗ *she approached to the problem* is not. The only preposition the verb takes is *about*, naming the matter raised with a person: *he approached her about the vacancy*. Note the numeric use, which is common in American reporting — *costs approaching two million dollars* — and the intransitive one, which needs no object at all: *as the deadline approached*.
**Verb type:** **action** — dynamic, and atelic when it means draw near (*the storm approached faster than the forecast allowed for*), which is why the progressive is so common: *enrollment is approaching nine hundred*.
**Transitivity:** **both** — transitive with a bare object (*approach the vehicle*, *approach the problem*, ✗ *approach to the problem*), intransitive with no object at all (*as the deadline approached*). Only *about* attaches: *approached her about the vacancy*.
**Passive:** **yes** in the transitive senses — *the vehicle was approached from opposite sides*, *he was approached about the vacancy and told to put it in writing*. No passive of the intransitive: ✗ *it was approached by winter*.

Come near to something, or begin to deal with it.

1. Two deputies *approached* the vehicle from opposite sides.
2. Enrollment is *approaching* nine hundred for the first time since 2009.
3. She *approached* the problem the way an engineer would.
4. He *approached* the dean *about* a leave and was told to put it in writing.
5. The storm *approached* faster than the forecast had allowed for.
6. Her *approach to* teaching writing has almost no rules in it.

**See also:** [arrive at](#arrive-at-arrive-in) &middot; [reach](#reach) &middot; [enter](#enter) &middot; [react to](#react-to) &middot; [ask about](#ask-for-ask-about)

### attend

**Pronunciation:** /əˈtɛnd/ &middot; *uh-TEND* &middot; weak /əˈtɛnd/
**Русский:** посещать, присутствовать на — без предлога: attend the meeting
**Type:** verb + direct object (no preposition) &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal
**Complement:** a direct object naming an event, an institution, or a service — **no preposition**: ✗ *attend to the meeting*, ✗ *attend at the conference*. The genuine *attend to* means deal with or take care of, and *attend on* means wait on — both are real and neither means being present.
**Pattern:** *attend* [a meeting, a school, a funeral] &middot; *attend to* [a matter, a patient] — deal with &middot; *attend on* / *upon* [someone] — formal, wait on &middot; *attendance at* [an event] — the noun takes *at*
**Contrast:** ***Attend the meeting* is being there; *attend to the meeting* is handling the arrangements** — and one of those is almost never what a learner means. The noun goes its own way again: *attendance **at** the meeting*, *an attendee **at** the conference*. Compare *participate **in***, which asserts more than presence, and *be present **at***, which asserts exactly presence. In American English *attend* is also the standard verb for enrollment: *she attended Oberlin*, no preposition.
**Verb type:** **action** — dynamic; the school sense is habitual and reads almost as a state (*he attended three different high schools*), but it still takes the progressive: *she's attending Oberlin*.
**Transitivity:** **both** — transitive with a bare object for presence (*attend the hearing*, ✗ *attend to the meeting* in that sense, ✗ *attend at the conference*), intransitive with *to* for dealing with something (*the nurse attended to him*) and with *on* / *upon* for waiting on someone.
**Passive:** **restricted** — the presence sense passivizes only with a modifier or a by-phrase: *the hearing was attended by about forty people*, *the workshop was well attended*, but bare ✗ *the hearing was attended* is incomplete. *Attend to* has no passive: ✗ *the roof was attended to* is possible but stiff.

Be present at something.

1. About forty people *attended* the hearing and nine spoke.
2. He *attended* three different high schools in four years.
3. She couldn't *attend* the funeral and has never forgiven the airline.
4. The nurse *attended to* him before anyone had filled out a form.
5. *Attendance at* the workshop is optional but counted.
6. Please *attend to* the roof before the freeze.

**See also:** [participate in](#participate-in) &middot; [arrive at](#arrive-at-arrive-in) &middot; [listen to](#listen-to) &middot; [take care of](#take-care-of) &middot; [reach](#reach)

### contact

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkɑntækt/ &middot; *KON-takt* &middot; weak /ˈkɑntækt/
**Русский:** связываться с — без предлога: contact me, не ✗ contact with me
**Type:** verb + direct object (no preposition) &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** a direct object naming the person or office — **no preposition**: ✗ *contact with the office*, ✗ *contact to me*. The same is true of *call*, *phone*, *email*, *text*, and *write* with a person: ✗ *phone to her*, ✗ *email to me*. The **noun** takes *with* (*in contact with the family*), which is where the error starts.
**Pattern:** *contact* [someone] &middot; *call* / *phone* / *email* / *text* [someone] &middot; *write* [someone] — American; British adds *to* &middot; *be in contact with* [someone] &middot; *get in touch with* [someone] &middot; *reach out to* [someone]
**Contrast:** ***Contact with someone* is a noun phrase, not a verb phrase.** *I contacted her* is right; ✗ *I contacted with her* is not. The whole group behaves the same way: *call me*, *email me*, *text me*, *phone the clinic* — all bare objects. **The one genuine American-British split is *write*:** Americans say *write me when you get there*, and British English requires *write to me*. Both are correct in their own varieties, and *write to* is safe everywhere. When you want a preposition, use the noun frames: *in contact with*, *in touch with*.
**Verb type:** **action** — dynamic and telic, a single completed act of reaching someone (*she contacted three references*).
**Transitivity:** **transitive**, and the object is obligatory: ✗ *I contacted yesterday*, ✗ *I contacted with her*. The whole family behaves this way — *call me*, *email me*, *text me*, *phone the clinic* — and American *write me* joins them, where British English requires *write to me*.
**Passive:** **yes** — *three references were contacted and one wrote back*, *the family has been contacted*. The noun frame supplies the preposition instead: *they've been in contact with the family since Tuesday*.

Get in touch with someone.

1. *Contact* the registrar's office before the deadline, not after.
2. She *contacted* three references and heard back from one.
3. *Call* me when you land.
4. He *emailed* everyone on the list twice in one afternoon.
5. *Write* me when you get settled.
6. They've been *in contact with* the family since Tuesday.

**See also:** [reach](#reach) &middot; [explain to](#explain-to) &middot; [consult with](#consult-with) &middot; [talk to](#talk-to-talk-with-talk-about) &middot; [hear from](#hear-about-hear-from-hear-of)

### discuss

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈskʌs/ &middot; *dih-SKUSS* &middot; weak /dɪˈskəs/
**Русский:** обсуждать что-то — без предлога: discuss the plan, не ✗ discuss about
**Type:** verb + direct object (no preposition) &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** a direct object, a **gerund** (*discussed selling the building*), or a wh-clause (*discussed how to pay for it*) — **and no preposition before any of them**: ✗ *discuss about the budget*, ✗ *discuss on the budget*, ✗ *discuss of the budget*. There is no that-clause: ✗ *discuss that we should move*.
**Pattern:** *discuss* [a topic] &middot; *discuss* [verb-ing] &middot; *discuss* [wh-clause] &middot; *discuss* [something] *with* [someone] &middot; *a discussion about* / *of* [something] — the noun does take a preposition
**Contrast:** ***Discuss about* is the single most common preposition error in English**, and it comes from the noun: *a discussion **about** the merger* is perfectly correct, so the preposition leaks back into the verb where it has no business. The only preposition *discuss* takes is *with*, and it names the other person, not the topic: *discuss the merger with counsel*. If you want a preposition on the topic, change the verb — *talk about it*, *speak about it*, *comment on it*, or *have a discussion about it*.
**Verb type:** **action** — dynamic and atelic, an activity that occupies time (*we discussed the budget for two hours and settled nothing*).
**Transitivity:** **transitive**, and the object is obligatory and preposition-free: ✗ *discuss about the budget*, ✗ *discuss on the budget*, ✗ *we discussed for two hours*. The only preposition is *with*, naming the other person, never the topic.
**Passive:** **yes** — *the budget was discussed for two hours*, *how the shortfall would be covered was never discussed*. The noun keeps its preposition: *a discussion about the merger*.

Talk something over with someone.

1. We *discussed* the budget for two hours and settled nothing.
2. They *discussed* selling the building and voted to refinance instead.
3. Let's *discuss* it *with* the whole team on Monday.
4. The board never *discussed* how the shortfall would be covered.
5. She *discussed* her options *with* an attorney before responding.
6. Nothing in that email is worth *discussing*.

**See also:** [talk about](#talk-to-talk-with-talk-about) &middot; [comment on](#comment-on) &middot; [consult with](#consult-with) &middot; [argue about](#argue-with-argue-about-argue-over)

### emphasize

**Pronunciation:** /ˈɛmfəˌsaɪz/ &middot; *EM-fuh-size* &middot; weak /ˈɛmfəˌsaɪz/
**Русский:** подчёркивать, акцентировать — без предлога, не ✗ emphasize on
**Type:** verb + direct object (no preposition) &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal
**Complement:** a direct object or a **that-clause** — **no preposition**: ✗ *emphasize on the deadline*, ✗ *emphasize about safety*. The **noun** takes *on* (*emphasis **on** safety*) and the phrasal frame is *place* / *put emphasis on*, which is where the error comes from. *Stress* behaves identically: *stress the deadline*, ✗ *stress on the deadline*.
**Pattern:** *emphasize* [something] &middot; *emphasize that* [clause] &middot; *stress* [something] &middot; *stress that* [clause] &middot; *place* / *put emphasis on* [something] &middot; *with an emphasis on* [something]
**Contrast:** ***Emphasize on* is wrong, and *an emphasis on* is right**, which is the whole trap in one sentence. The same split governs *stress* (verb, no preposition; noun, *on*), *highlight*, *underscore*, and *underline*, all transitive. If you want the preposition, use the noun: *the training places heavy emphasis on de-escalation*. Note that *emphasize* also takes a clause directly — *she emphasized that attendance was voluntary* — with no *on* anywhere in sight.
**Verb type:** **action** — dynamic and performative, a speech act (*the memo emphasized the deadline three separate times*).
**Transitivity:** **transitive**, object or that-clause obligatory and always preposition-free: ✗ *emphasize on the deadline*, ✗ *emphasize about safety*. *Stress*, *highlight*, *underscore*, and *underline* all behave identically.
**Passive:** **yes** — *the deadline was emphasized three separate times*, *it was emphasized that attendance was voluntary*. The preposition belongs to the noun: *heavy emphasis is placed on writing*.

Give special importance to something.

1. The memo *emphasized* the deadline three separate times.
2. She *emphasized that* nothing had been decided yet.
3. The program *emphasizes* fieldwork over coursework.
4. He *stressed* the difference between a recommendation and a requirement.
5. The curriculum places heavy *emphasis on* writing.
6. I want to *emphasize* how narrow the finding actually is.

**See also:** [concentrate on](#concentrate-on) &middot; [point to](#point-at-point-to) &middot; [comment on](#comment-on) &middot; [insist on](#insist-on) &middot; [explain to](#explain-to)

### enter

**Pronunciation:** /ˈɛntɚ/ &middot; *EN-ter* &middot; weak /ˈɛntɚ/
**Русский:** входить в — без предлога: enter the room; enter into о договоре
**Type:** verb + direct object (no preposition) &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** a direct object naming a place, a state, or a stage — **no preposition in the literal sense**: ✗ *enter into the room*, ✗ *enter to the building*. The figurative *enter into* is a genuine and separate pattern for agreements and processes: *enter into a contract*, *enter into negotiations*.
**Pattern:** *enter* [a room, a building, a country] &middot; *enter* [a contest, a phase, one's thirties] &middot; *enter into* [an agreement, negotiations, a discussion] &middot; *enter* [data] *into* [a system] &middot; *enter* (intransitive, stage direction)
**Contrast:** ***Enter into the room* is wrong; *enter the room* is right.** The confusion is fed by the fact that *enter into* is correct for abstractions — you *enter into* a contract, an alliance, a partnership, or a debate. The rule of thumb: if you could physically walk in, drop the preposition. Note also *enter* meaning key in, which takes *into* or *in*: *enter the figures into the spreadsheet*. And the intransitive stage direction *Enter Hamlet* is the fossil that gives the verb its theatrical feel.
**Verb type:** **action** — dynamic and telic in every sense: crossing a threshold, joining a contest, signing an agreement, keying in data.
**Transitivity:** **both** — transitive and preposition-free in the literal sense (*enter the building*, ✗ *enter into the room*), intransitive in the stage direction (*Enter Hamlet*). *Enter into* is a separate frame for abstractions (*enter into a contract*), and *enter X into Y* for data.
**Passive:** **yes** — *the building was entered through the loading dock*, *the figures were entered into the second field*. The abstraction frame gives a genuine prepositional passive, common in legal prose: *the consent decree was entered into rather than tried*.

Go into a place, or become party to an agreement.

1. He *entered* the building through the loading dock.
2. Three hundred people *entered* the contest and two read the rules.
3. The company *entered into* a consent decree rather than go to trial.
4. She *entered* her thirties without any of the alarm she'd been promised.
5. *Enter* the confirmation number *into* the second field.
6. The negotiations the union *entered into* lasted eleven weeks.

**See also:** [arrive at](#arrive-at-arrive-in) &middot; [reach](#reach) &middot; [approach](#approach) &middot; [participate in](#participate-in) &middot; [into](../../06-prepositions/catalog/04-movement-direction.md#into)

### lack

**Pronunciation:** /ˈlæk/ &middot; *LAK* &middot; weak /ˈlæk/
**Русский:** не иметь, недоставать — без предлога: lacks detail, не ✗ lacks of detail
**Type:** verb + direct object (no preposition) &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal
**Complement:** a direct object naming what is missing — **no preposition**: ✗ *lack of experience* as a verb, ✗ *lack in experience*. The **noun** takes *of* (*a lack of experience*) and the **adjective** takes *in* (*lacking in experience*), and those two are the source of every error made with the verb.
**Pattern:** *lack* [something] &middot; *be lacking* &middot; *be lacking in* [a quality] &middot; *a lack of* [something] &middot; *for lack of* [something]
**Contrast:** **Three parts of speech, three different constructions, and only one of them takes *of*.** *The proposal **lacks** detail* (verb, bare object), *the proposal shows **a lack of** detail* (noun, *of*), *the proposal is **lacking in** detail* (participial adjective, *in*). ✗ *The proposal lacks of detail* mixes the first two and is wrong. Note that *lack* is stative and resists the progressive: ✗ *is lacking experience* is odd where *lacks experience* is right, though *is lacking* on its own (meaning absent) is fine: *what's lacking is a budget*.
**Verb type:** **stative** — the entry says so outright: it names an absence, not an act, and it resists the progressive (✗ *the plan is lacking a mechanism* where *the plan lacks a mechanism* is right). Bare *be lacking* meaning absent is fine: *what's lacking is a budget*.
**Transitivity:** **transitive**, with a bare object that is obligatory and takes no preposition: ✗ *the proposal lacks of detail*, ✗ *the plan lacks in enforcement*. The noun takes *of* (*a lack of detail*) and the participial adjective takes *in* (*lacking in detail*).
**Passive:** **no** — one of the classic stative refusals: ✗ *a mechanism for enforcement is lacked by the plan*, ✗ *confidence has never been lacked by her*. Use *the plan lacks it*, or turn it around: *a second exit is missing*.

Not have something that is needed or expected.

1. The plan *lacks* any mechanism for enforcement.
2. She has never *lacked* confidence.
3. The building *lacks* a second exit, which is why it can't be permitted.
4. What the argument *lacks* is a single piece of evidence.
5. It's a careful report, though *lacking in* imagination.
6. The case was dropped *for lack of* evidence.

**See also:** [consist of](#consist-of) &middot; [deprive of](#deprive-of) &middot; [suffer from](#suffer-from) &middot; [provide with](#provide-with-provide-for) &middot; [for lack of](../../06-prepositions/catalog/06-cause-purpose.md#for-lack-of)

### marry

**Pronunciation:** /ˈmɛri/ &middot; *MAIR-ee* &middot; weak /ˈmɛri/
**Русский:** жениться на, выходить замуж за — без предлога: marry her, не ✗ marry with
**Type:** verb + direct object (no preposition) &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** a direct object naming the person — **no preposition**: ✗ *marry with her*, ✗ *marry to him*. The **adjectival** frame is where the preposition lives, and it is *to*, never *with*: *married to a surgeon*, ✗ *married with a surgeon*. *Get married to* follows the same rule.
**Pattern:** *marry* [someone] &middot; *marry* (intransitive) &middot; *get married* &middot; *get married to* [someone] &middot; *be married to* [someone] &middot; *marry* [a couple] — what the officiant does &middot; *married with children* — a different *with* entirely
**Contrast:** ***Married with her* is one of the most persistent errors in English**, because Spanish *casarse con* and French *se marier avec* both use the equivalent of *with*. English uses no preposition for the verb and *to* for the state: *He married her in 1998* and *He's been married to her since 1998*. The one place *with* is correct is a completely different structure — *married with two kids* means married and having two kids, where *with* attaches to the children, not the spouse. Note also the officiant's transitive use: *a justice of the peace married them*.
**Verb type:** **action** — dynamic and telic, a single event (*she married a man she met in a bus station*). *Be married to* is the resulting state and is adjectival, not a tense of the verb.
**Transitivity:** **both** — transitive with the spouse as a bare object (*marry her*, ✗ *marry with her*, ✗ *marry to him*), transitive with the couple as object when the officiant is the subject (*a justice of the peace married them*), and intransitive (*her sister married young*).
**Passive:** **restricted** — the officiant frame passivizes and is the live passive: *the couple were married by a friend who got ordained online*. The state is not a passive at all: *he's been married to her since 1998* has no active *✗ she marries him*.

Become someone's husband or wife.

1. She *married* a man she met in a bus station in Lincoln.
2. They *got married* in a courthouse and had the party a year later.
3. He's been *married to* the same woman for thirty-four years.
4. Her sister *married* young and stayed married.
5. The couple were *married by* a friend who got ordained online.
6. They're *married with* three kids and a dog nobody planned on.

**See also:** [introduce to](#introduce-to) &middot; [belong to](#belong-to) &middot; [separate from](#separate-from) &middot; [share with](#share-with) &middot; [to](../../06-prepositions/catalog/02-core-relational.md#to)

### oppose

**Pronunciation:** /əˈpoʊz/ &middot; *uh-POHZ* &middot; weak /əˈpoʊz/
**Русский:** выступать против, противостоять — без предлога, не ✗ oppose to
**Type:** verb + direct object (no preposition) &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal
**Complement:** a direct object naming what you are against — **no preposition**: ✗ *oppose to the merger*, ✗ *oppose against the bill*. A **gerund** can be that object, still with no preposition: *opposed extending the deadline*. The preposition appears only once the verb has become the **adjective** *opposed*, and then it is *to* plus a noun or a **gerund**: *opposed to the merger*, *opposed to extending the deadline*, never ✗ *opposed to extend the deadline*.
**Pattern:** *oppose* [a plan, a bill, a nominee] &middot; *oppose* [verb-ing] &middot; *be opposed to* [noun phrase / verb-ing] &middot; *as opposed to* [noun phrase] — a fixed comparative phrase
**Contrast:** ***Oppose to* is wrong and *opposed to* is right** — one letter apart, and the difference is a part of speech. The verb is transitive like *support*, its opposite: *she supports the levy*, *she opposes the levy*, neither with a preposition. Once you reach for *be*, you have switched to the adjective and the *to* becomes obligatory — and it is a preposition, so a verb after it takes *-ing*, which puts *opposed to* in the *look forward to* family. Compare [object to](#object-to), which is a verb that does take *to*, so *object to the merger* and *oppose the merger* say the same thing with opposite structures. Note the fixed phrase [*as opposed to*](../../06-prepositions/catalog/11-complex-multiword.md#as-opposed-to), which means rather than and behaves as a complex preposition of its own.
**Verb type:** **both** — mostly **stative**, naming a position held (*every county commissioner opposed the rezoning*), and **action** where it means actively fight something (*they're opposing the permit at every hearing*).
**Transitivity:** **transitive**, with a bare object or a gerund and no preposition: ✗ *oppose to the merger*, ✗ *oppose against the bill*; *opposed extending the deadline* is correct. The *to* belongs to the adjective *opposed*, which then takes a noun or *-ing*: *opposed to extending the deadline*, ✗ *opposed to extend*.
**Passive:** **restricted** — the true passive is fine with a by-phrase: *the rezoning was opposed by every county commissioner*. But *the union is opposed to reopening the contract* is the adjective, not a passive — it has no active counterpart ✗ *something opposes the union to reopening*.

Be against something, and act against it.

1. Every county commissioner *opposed* the rezoning.
2. She *opposes* raising the retirement age.
3. The union is *opposed to* reopening the contract midterm.
4. He *opposed* the nomination for reasons he never explained.
5. Two of the three trustees are *opposed to* the sale.
6. They want a flat fee *as opposed to* an hourly rate.

**See also:** [object to](#object-to) &middot; [disapprove of](#disapprove-of) &middot; [protest](#protest) &middot; [agree with](#agree-with-agree-to-agree-on) &middot; [argue against](#argue-with-argue-about-argue-over) &middot; [opposed to](../../06-prepositions/catalog/15-dependent-adjectives.md#opposed-to)

### protest

**Pronunciation:** /prəˈtɛst/ &middot; *pruh-TEST* &middot; weak /prəˈtɛst/
**Русский:** протестовать против — в амер. англ. без предлога: protest the ruling
**Type:** verb + direct object (no preposition) &middot; **Register:** neutral — the transitive form is characteristically American
**Complement:** a direct object naming what you object to, or a **that-clause** (*protested that he had never been told*) — **in American English, no preposition**: *protest the ruling*, *protest the war*. British English requires *protest against* the ruling. *Protest to* names the person you complain to, and is correct in both varieties.
**Pattern:** *protest* [a decision, a policy, a verdict] — American &middot; *protest against* [something] — British, and also used in the U.S. &middot; *protest to* [an official] &middot; *protest that* [clause] &middot; *protest* (intransitive) &middot; *under protest*
**Contrast:** **This is a genuine transatlantic split, and the American form is the bare transitive.** *Thousands protested the ruling* is standard in the United States and reads as an error to some British ears, where *protested against the ruling* is required. Both are current in American writing, with the bare form dominant in news copy. Note the third pattern, which is neither: *protest **to** the commissioner* names the person you complain to. And *protest that* introduces a denial (*he protested that nobody had told him*), which is where *protest too much* comes from.
**Verb type:** **action** — dynamic and atelic in the demonstrating sense (*students protested the tuition increase for most of the spring*), dynamic and performative in *he protested that nobody had told him*.
**Transitivity:** **both** — transitive with a bare object in American English (*protest the ruling*, where British English requires *protest against the ruling*), intransitive when the crowd simply protests, and with *to* for the official you complain to.
**Passive:** **yes** in the American transitive — *the closure was protested by several hundred people*, *the charge was protested in writing and half of it reversed*. No passive of *protest to*: ✗ *the commissioner was protested to*.

Object publicly to something.

1. Several hundred people *protested* the closure outside the county building.
2. Students *protested* the tuition increase for most of the spring.
3. She *protested* the charge in writing and got half of it reversed.
4. In Britain they would say the crowd *protested against* the bill.
5. He *protested to* the commissioner and was told to file a form.
6. He *protested that* nobody had ever explained the deadline to him.
7. She signed it *under protest*.

**See also:** [complain to](#complain-to-complain-about) &middot; [object to](#object-to) &middot; [argue with](#argue-with-argue-about-argue-over) &middot; [comply with](#comply-with) &middot; [vote against](#vote-for-vote-against)

### reach

**Pronunciation:** /ˈritʃ/ &middot; *REECH* &middot; weak /ˈritʃ/
**Русский:** достигать, добираться до; дозвониться до — без предлога: reach Denver
**Type:** verb + direct object (no preposition) &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** a direct object naming a place, a person, a level, or a decision — **no preposition**: ✗ *reach to the summit*, ✗ *reach at the office*. The genuine *reach for* means extend a hand toward something, and *reach out to* means make contact — both are real and both mean something else.
**Pattern:** *reach* [a place, a person, a figure, an agreement] &middot; *reach for* [something] &middot; *reach out to* [someone] &middot; *reach into* [a bag, a pocket] &middot; *within reach of* [something] — the noun takes *of*
**Contrast:** ***Reach the airport*, not ✗ *reach to the airport*.** The verb is transitive for arriving, contacting, and attaining: *reach Denver*, *reach me at this number*, *reach a verdict*, *reach ninety degrees*. *Reach **for*** is the physical gesture (*reached for the salt*), and *reach out to* is the American business idiom for making contact — now so common that it has largely displaced *contact* in email. Compare *arrive at* and *arrive in*, which do take prepositions, and *get to*, which takes *to*: three ways of saying the same thing with three different requirements.
**Verb type:** **action** — an achievement verb, dynamic and telic (*we reached the trailhead about twenty minutes before dark*, *the jury reached a verdict on the second day*).
**Transitivity:** **transitive** with a bare object in the arriving, contacting, and attaining senses: ✗ *reach to the summit*, ✗ *reach at the office*. The prepositional frames are separate verbs of gesture and contact: *reach for the railing*, *reach into a pocket*, *reach out to someone*.
**Passive:** **restricted** — routine with abstract objects and with people: *a verdict was reached on the second day*, *she could not be reached for comment*. Weak with plain destinations: ?*the trailhead was reached before dark* belongs to expedition prose, not ordinary speech.

Arrive at something, get in touch with someone, or attain a level.

1. We *reached* the trailhead about twenty minutes before dark.
2. You can *reach* me at the office until five.
3. The jury *reached* a verdict on the second day.
4. Temperatures *reached* a hundred and four in the valley.
5. He *reached for* the railing and missed it.
6. Somebody from the office will *reach out to* you this week.

**See also:** [arrive at](#arrive-at-arrive-in) &middot; [approach](#approach) &middot; [contact](#contact) &middot; [enter](#enter)

### resemble

**Pronunciation:** /rɪˈzɛmbəl/ &middot; *rih-ZEM-bul* &middot; weak /rɪˈzɛmbəl/
**Русский:** быть похожим на — без предлога: resemble his father, не ✗ resemble to
**Type:** verb + direct object (no preposition) &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal
**Complement:** a direct object — **no preposition**: ✗ *resemble to her mother*, ✗ *resemble with her mother*. *Resemble* is stative and does not take the progressive: ✗ *is resembling her mother*. The noun takes *to*: *a resemblance to her mother*.
**Pattern:** *resemble* [someone / something] &middot; *bear a resemblance to* [someone] &middot; *take after* [a relative] — the idiomatic alternative &middot; *look like* [someone] — the everyday one
**Contrast:** ***Resemble to* is an error** imported from French *ressembler à* and Spanish *parecerse a*. The verb takes a bare object; only the **noun** takes *to* — *a striking resemblance to her mother*. In American speech the verb is rare: people say *she looks like her mother* or *she takes after her mother*, and *resemble* is reserved for writing. Note the useful passive-free construction *X and Y resemble each other*, which avoids choosing a direction.
**Verb type:** **stative** — the entry says it directly: a relation of likeness, not an act, and it refuses the progressive: ✗ *she is resembling her mother*.
**Transitivity:** **transitive**, with a bare object that is obligatory: ✗ *resemble to her mother*, ✗ *resemble with her mother*. The noun is where *to* lives: *a striking resemblance to her mother*.
**Passive:** **no** — the textbook case of a transitive stative that refuses promotion: ✗ *her mother is resembled by her*, ✗ *the rendering was resembled by the building*. The symmetric active is the way around it: *the two dialects resemble each other*.

Look or be like something else.

1. She *resembles* her mother more every year.
2. The finished building *resembled* the rendering in almost no respect.
3. His second novel *resembles* the first one a little too closely.
4. Nothing on the plate *resembled* what the menu had described.
5. The two dialects *resemble* each other more than either resembles the standard.
6. The child bears a strong *resemblance to* his grandfather.

**See also:** [compare to](#compare-to-compare-with) &middot; [differ from](#differ-from) &middot; [separate from](#separate-from)
